# Unspoken Sermons: A Modern Paraphrase

*Unspoken Sermons, by George MacDonald, restated paragraph by paragraph in modern English. This paraphrase follows the order and content of the original without adding interpretation. Drafted with the help of AI.*

## First Series

*These Ears of Corn. gathered and rubbed in my hands upon broken Sabbaths, I offer first to my Wife, and then to my other Friends.*

### THE CHILD IN THE MIDST

*The passage:* Jesus came to Capernaum and asked the disciples in the house what they had argued about on the road. They kept silent, for they had argued about which of them was greatest. He sat down, called the twelve, and said that whoever wants to be first must be last of all and servant of all. Then he set a child among them, took him in his arms, and said that whoever receives one such child in his name receives him, and whoever receives him receives the one who sent him.

Mark's account is the fuller, but Matthew's record enriches it and makes its lesson plainer.

"Unless you are converted and become like little children, you will not enter the kingdom of heaven. Whoever humbles himself like this little child is greatest there. Whoever receives one such child in my name receives me. But whoever makes one of these little believers stumble would be better drowned in the sea with a millstone round his neck."

These passages record a lesson against ambition and rivalry. But I write not to set out that lesson but for a truth about God in which his great argument reaches its height.

He took a little child, possibly Peter's, since Mark says this was at Capernaum, "in the house." Peter's very faults were those of a childish nature, so we might expect his child to have the childlike face and bearing the lesson needs.

There are children who are not childlike. One of the saddest and commonest sights is the face of a child so full of worldly wisdom that both the human childishness and the divine childlikeness are gone. For the *childlike* is the divine, and the very word leads me on my way. But I must first remove a difficulty that turns us toward one of the grandest truths yet away from the truth the Lord had in view here.

The difficulty is this. Would the *Son of man* pick out the beautiful child and leave the common one unnoticed? Even publicans do as much. Does a mother's heart not cling closest to the deformed of her little ones? And shall Christ, as we believe him, choose by the look of the eye? Would he turn from the child born in sin and taught iniquity, whose pinched face shows hunger, daring and love of praise combined into the cunning of avaricious age, and take up the child of honest parents like Peter and his wife, who could not help looking better? That would not be he who came to seek and to save the lost. Let the man who loves his brother say which child, in his highest moments of love to God, he would clasp to his bosom of refuge, when he is nearest to that ideal humanity in which *a man* shall be a hiding-place from the wind. Would it not be the evil-faced child, because that child needed it most? Yes; in God's name, yes. Who that has read of the lost sheep or the found prodigal will deny it is the divine way? It will often *appear* otherwise, since the childlike child is easier to save and may *come* first. But heaven rejoices most over the sheep that wandered farthest, perhaps born on the wild hillside and never in the fold. For such a prodigal the elder brother in heaven prays: "Lord, think about my poor brother more than about me, for I know thee, and am at rest in thee. I am with thee always."

Why then do I say the child was probably Peter's, and certainly looked childlike because it was? Because no amount of evil *is* the child. No evil, in face, habits or heart, can make a child cease to be a child or destroy the divine idea of childhood that moved in God when he made that child in his image. God speaks of the essential, judges by the real, and is God of the undying.

I grant this heartily. If the Lord's aim had been to teach love of neighbour, the ugliest child might have served best. Whoever receives any child, most plainly a repulsive one, because it is God's offspring and his own brother, receives the Father; whoever gives a cup of cold water to a little one refreshes the Father's heart. Any human being, especially a wretched outcast, would do as well as a child to show that love. So something more is intended: the lesson lies not in the *humanity* but in the *childhood* of the child.

If the disciples could have seen that the essential childhood was meant, not a blurred and half-obliterated one, even a selfish child might have served, though no better than a childlike one. But the child was used as a sign of the truth in his childhood, so that the eyes as well as the ears might carry it to the heart. What mattered then was not that he be beautiful but that he be childlike: the qualities that wake in us the love peculiar to childhood, which is just the perception of the childhood, had at least to glimmer from the face of the *chosen type*. Would an unchildlike child, such as we sometimes see, in purple and lace in a great house or in dirt and rags in a squalid close, have suited the Lord when he said his listeners must become like this child, and the lesson was the divine nature of childlikeness? Beside his words "this little child," "one of such children," "these little ones that believe in me," such a child would have been ludicrous but for the horror of it. Even the pity a good heart would feel for him would have turned it from the lesson.

Let me show more fully that the lesson lay in the childhood, not the humanity. The disciples had been disputing who should be greatest, and the Lord wanted to show them that such a dispute has nothing to do with his kingdom. So he set a child before them as a specimen of his subjects. It was in virtue of his childhood, not his humanity, that the child represented a subject, and what he showed was the nature of the kingdom, not its scope. They could enter only by becoming little children, by humbling themselves; ruling is excluded where childlikeness is the one essential quality. The question was no longer who should rule but who should serve: not who should look down from the conquered heights of authority, even sacred authority, but who should look up, honouring humanity and ministering to it, until humanity is persuaded of its own honour as a temple of the living God. That lesson lay in the child's *childhood*.

But now to my chief aim, for this lesson sprang from a yet higher truth. Nothing is required of man that is not first in God; we must be perfect because God is perfect. The child is set before us in the gospel to reveal God to all souls, that they may be saved by knowing him and becoming like him. He shares the blessing of this passage who, in giving the cup of water or the embrace, meets the essential childhood and embraces the *childish* humanity of the child, not he who embraces it out of love to humanity, or even love to God as its Father. Seeing the childhood as divine is what shows the disciple how vain is the strife for place in the great kingdom.

For it is *in my name*. That means *as representing me*, and so *as being like me*; the Lord could not have anyone received in his name who could not more or less represent him. And since he had just told the disciples to become like this child, telling them to receive *such* a child in his name must imply something common to them all, in which the child, Jesus and the disciples meet. What can that be but spiritual childhood? *In my name* does not mean *because I will it*. A bare declaration of his will would find ten thousand to obey, even to suffering, for one able to receive so vital a truth about his character; but he wants obedience to the *truth*, the Light of the World, truth beheld and known. The full meaning, which alone makes the passage a whole, is a revelation from resemblance, from fitness to represent and so reveal. He who receives a child in Jesus' name perceives where Jesus and the child are one: he must see not only the *ideal* child, the loveliness of true childhood, in the child he receives, but that the child is like Jesus, or rather that the Lord is like the child, and is embraced by every heart childlike enough to embrace a child for his childness. I do not say only those conscious of this share the blessing. But a lofty knowledge of blessedness belongs to embracing a child as the visible likeness of the Lord; for the blessing is the God-known truth itself, that the Lord has the heart of a child, and the man who perceives it knows he is blessed because it is true.

But the argument about *in my name* is incomplete until we follow our Lord's words to their higher stage: whoever receives me receives him who sent me. The link between the first and second is probably the same as between the second and third. I do not say it must be; I aim not at logical certainty but at showing the idea I am approaching. If once my reader beholds it and cannot receive it, logic would do little for him, and he can easily suggest other connections, though none so symmetrical. How does he who receives the Son receive the Father? Because the Son is as the Father; whoever perceives the essential in Christ has the essence of the Father, and is one with it by recognition and worship. And I think the first link is the same: he who sees the essential in this child, the pure childhood, sees the essence of Christ, grace and truth, in a word, childlikeness. The child is not therefore perfect as Christ is, but it is the same in kind, and so, manifest in the child, it reveals what is in Jesus.

Then to receive a child in the name of Jesus is to receive Jesus; to receive Jesus is to receive God; therefore to receive the child is to receive God himself.

I can show that this was in our Lord's heart as he spoke from another golden thread in the shining web of his words.

What is the kingdom of Christ? A rule of love, truth and service, in which the king is the chief servant. The kings of the earth hold dominion, but it shall not be so among his followers; the Son of Man came to minister; his Father works, and so he works. The great Workman is the great King, labouring for his own, so whoever would be greatest there and nearest the King must be servant of all. It is *like king like subject*: no rule of force, of one kind over another, but of *kind*, of *nature*, of *God*. If we must become children to enter it, the spirit of children must pervade it from lowly subject to lowliest king. Luke adds that he who is least among them will be great; Matthew, that whoever humbles himself like this child is greatest. Hence the sign between king and subject: the subject kneels to earthly kings, but the heavenly king takes his subject in his arms.

To glance back: to receive the child because God receives it, or for its humanity, is one thing; to receive it because it is like God, or for its childhood, is another. The first does little against ambition; alone, it might only widen its scope by admitting all men to the arena. The second strikes at the root of emulation: the moment even service is done for honour and not for service's sake, the doer is outside the kingdom. And the childhood we receive in Christ's name is humanity; childhood is humanity's deepest, divine heart, so in the child we receive all humanity. The lesson, though about childhood, returns upon our race, which we receive with wider arms and a deeper heart. Nothing is lost, and there is no heartlessness in insisting the child was lovable and childlike.

If heaven holds a picture of that teaching, we shall see a dim childhood shining from the faces of the disciples around the Son of God with a child in his arms: dim in the men, trustfully clear in the child, triumphant in the Lord, with all his wisdom and truth upholding that radiant serenity of faith in his Father. Truly, O Lord, this childhood is life. When thy tenderness has made the world great, all men, children like thee, will smile in the face of the great God.

Now to the highest point: whoever receives him receives the one who sent him. To receive a child in God's name is to receive God himself. How? As he alone can be received, by knowing him as he is; to know him is to have him in us. So let us receive this revelation in the Lord's own words.

God is represented in Jesus because he is like Jesus; Jesus is represented in the child because he is like the child. Therefore God is represented in the child because he is like the child. God is childlike. To see this truly is to receive God in the child.

Having reached this point, I have done with argument. If the Lord meant this, and it is true, whoever can receive it will, and whoever has ears will hear; the truth carries its own conviction.

Still, the word of one who has seen this truth may help the dawn of a like perception in those who face the east, for men may see dimly and want to see more. So let us brood a little over the idea, and see whether it commends itself to that spirit which, one with the human spirit where it dwells, searches the deep things of God. The true heart may at first be shocked, as Peter was when he said, "That be far from thee, Lord," but after a season it will receive the truth and rejoice.

Let me ask, then: do you believe in the Incarnation? If so, was Jesus ever less divine than God? I answer for you: never; lower, but never less divine. Was he not a child, then? You say, "Yes, but not like other children." But did he not look like them? If he looked like them and was not like them, it was a masquerade. I say he was a child, whatever more he might be. God is man, and infinitely more. Our Lord became flesh but did not *become* man; he took the form of man because he was man already. And he was, is, and ever shall be divinely childlike; he could never have been a child if he were ever to cease being one, for in him the transient found nothing. Childhood belongs to the divine nature. Obedience is as divine as Will, and Service as divine as Rule, for both are a doing of the truth, and the love in them is the same. Fatherhood and Sonship are one, save that Fatherhood looks down lovingly and Sonship looks up lovingly. Love is all, and God is all in all. He is ever seeking to get down to us, to be the divine man to us, and we are ever saying, "That be far from thee, Lord!" In our unbelief we guard a divine dignity he is too grand to think about. More pleasing to God, it needs little daring to say, is the audacity of Job, who flings open the door of the presence-chamber and, like a troubled, perhaps angry, but faithful child, cries to one whose perfect Fatherhood he has yet to learn: "Am I a sea or a whale, that thou settest a watch over me?"

Let us dare to climb the height of divine truth to which the Lord's words lead.

Does it not lead us here: that God's devotion to his creatures is perfect, that he thinks not of himself but of them, and wants nothing for himself but finds his blessedness in the outgoing of blessedness?

Ah, it is a terrible glory---must it also be a lonely one? We will come near with our human answer, giving ourselves up in the faith of Jesus. He gives himself to us; shall we not give ourselves to him, and to one another, whom he loves?

For when is the child the ideal child to us? Is it not when he gently takes his father by the beard and turns that face up to his brothers and sisters to kiss, when even the lovely selfishness of seeking love has gone and the heart is absorbed in loving?

In this, then, God is like the child: he is simply and altogether our friend and father, more than friend, father and mother, our infinite, love-perfect God. Grander and stronger than human imagination can conceive in poet-thinking or kingly action, he is more delicate than human tenderness can conceive in husband or wife, more homely than the human heart can conceive in father or mother. He has not two thoughts about us. All with him is simplicity of purpose and end: that we should be as he is, think his thoughts, and have his blessedness. It is so plain that everyone shall see it. He is utterly true and good to us, and nothing shall withstand his will.

How terribly the theologians have misrepresented God by the measure of the low and showy human qualities rather than the lofty and simple! Nearly all picture him as a great King on a grand throne, thinking how grand he is, making it the business of his being and the end of his universe to keep up his glory, and wielding the bolts of a Jupiter against those who take his name in vain. They would not admit it, but follow out what they say and it comes to this. Brothers, have you found our king? There he is, kissing little children and saying they are like God; at table with a fisherman's head on his bosom, heavy at heart that even the beloved disciple cannot yet understand him well. The simplest peasant who loves his children and his sheep is a true type of our God beside that monstrosity of a monarch.

The God who is ever uttering himself in the changing profusion of nature; who takes millions of years to form a soul that will understand him and be blessed; who is never in haste; who welcomes the simplest thought of truth or beauty as the return on seed sown in the old fallows of eternity, and rejoices in a faltering moment's answer to the age-long cry of his wisdom in the streets; the God of music, painting and building, the Lord of Hosts, the God of mountains and oceans; whose laws go out from one unseen point of wisdom and return without the loss of an atom; the God of history working in time toward christianity: this God is the God of little children, and he alone can be perfectly, abandonedly simple and devoted. The deepest, purest love of a woman has its wellspring in him. Our longings can no more exhaust the Godhead's treasures than our imagination can measure them. No thought, joy or hope of any of his creatures passes unseen; and while one remains unsatisfied, he is not Lord over all.

Therefore, with angels and archangels, with the spirits of the just made perfect, with the little children of the kingdom, with the Lord himself, and for all who do not know him, we praise his name in itself, saying *Our Father*. We do not draw back because we are unworthy, hard-hearted or careless of good. It is his childlikeness that makes him our God and Father; the perfection of his relation to us swallows up all our imperfections, for our childhood is born of his fatherhood. That man is perfect in faith who can come to God in utter dearth of feeling and desire, without a glow or an aspiration, weighed down with low thoughts, failures, neglects and forgetfulness, and say, "Thou art my refuge, because thou art my home."

Such a faith does not lead to presumption. The man who can pray this prayer knows better than anyone that God is not mocked, that he is not a man who changes his mind, that tears and entreaties will not move him to break one of his laws, and that giving a man what is out of harmony with truth and right because he asked would be to damn him, to cast him into the outer darkness. He knows the childlike, imperturbable God will let no man out of that prison till he has paid the uttermost farthing.

And if he forgets this, the God he belongs to does not forget it, or him. Life is not a series of chances with a few providences sprinkled between to prop up a failing belief, but one providence of God, and before long life itself will remind him, perhaps in agony of soul. When he prays for comfort the answer may be dismay, terror and the Father's countenance turned aside, for love itself, for love's sake, turns its face from what is not lovely; and he will read on the dark wall of his imprisoned conscience the words, awful and glorious, *Our God is a consuming fire*.

### THE CONSUMING FIRE

*The passage:* Our God is a consuming fire.

Nothing is inexorable but love. Love that yields to prayer is imperfect and poor, and what yields is its alloy, not love. If at the voice of entreaty love conquers displeasure, that is love asserting itself, not giving up its claims. Love does not grant a boon unwillingly, still less answer a prayer to the hurt of the one who prays. Love is one, and changeless.

For love loves unto purity. It always has in view the absolute loveliness of what it beholds. Where that is incomplete, love spends itself to make the beloved more lovely, that it may love more; it strives for perfection so that itself may be perfected in the object. As love first created humanity, so human love, in proportion to its divinity, goes on creating the beautiful for its outpouring. Nothing is eternal but what loves and can be loved, and love climbs ever toward the consummation when such shall be the universe.

Therefore all that is not beautiful in the beloved, all that comes between and is not of love's kind, must be destroyed.

And our God is a consuming fire.

If this is hard to understand, it is as simple, absolute truth is hard to understand. It may take centuries of ages of strife and aspiration before a man sees a truth; once he does, it is so plain he wonders how he lived without it. He did not understand sooner only because he did not see; to see a truth, to know it, to understand it and to love it are all one. Many a motion toward it, misery for want of it, cry of conscience against neglecting it and dim longing for it as an unknown need comes first, before the night of dreams yields to the sun of truth. But once beheld, it is for ever. To see one divine fact is to stand face to face with essential eternal life.

For this vision of truth God has worked for ages of ages. For this simple condition, this apex of life, where a man wonders like a child that he cannot make others see as he sees, the whole labour of God's science, history and poetry---from the time the earth gathered itself into a lonely drop of fire from the red rim of the driving sun-wheel to the time Alexander John Scott worshipped him from its face---has been evolving truth upon truth, in lovely vision and torturing law, never lying, never repenting. For this his patience will labour while any soul's child-heart is not yet born; for this the divine destruction has gone forth, that his life might be our life, and in us too might dwell that same consuming fire which is essential love.

Let us look at the apostle's words crowned with this lovely terror: "Our God is a consuming fire."

The apostle says we receive a kingdom that cannot be moved, so let us have grace to serve God acceptably with reverence and godly fear, for our God is a consuming fire. The kingdom is immovable because it is of the eternal world, of being and truth. We are to serve the Consuming Fire with divine fear: not the fear that cringes and craves, but the bowing of all thoughts, delights and loves before him who is the life of them all and will have them pure. He will shake heaven and earth (verse 27) that only the unshakeable may remain; he is a consuming fire that only what cannot be consumed may stand forth eternal. His nature is so terribly pure that, like fire, it destroys all that is not pure, and so demands such purity in our worship. The fire will not burn us if we do not worship thus; it will burn us until we do, and go on burning within us after all foreign to it has yielded, no longer with pain, but as the highest consciousness of life, the presence of God. When evil, which alone is consumable, has passed from the dwellers in that kingdom, man's nature will look God's in the face and his fear will be pure; a holy fear springs from knowing the nature, not from a sense of the power. Since what cannot be consumed is one within itself, in such a soul fear of God will be one with the homeliest love, and make a man flee not from him but from himself, to him, in terror lest he wrong God or his neighbour. The first words after this, on how to serve God acceptably, are "Let brotherly love continue." To love our brother is to worship the Consuming Fire.

The symbol of *the consuming fire* seems to have been suggested to the writer by the fire on the mountain of the old law, part of God's revelation to the Israelites. It was not the first such revelation: the symbol of God's presence, before which Moses had to put off his shoes, was a fire that *did not consume the bush in which it burned*. Both were revelations of terror. But the same symbol in a New Testament writer should mean more, not than it meant before, but than it was before used to express, for it could not express more than they could perceive. What could a nation of slaves, with the rust of chains eaten into their souls, who would have gone back to bondage rather than miss the food they liked best, see in that fire but terror and destruction? They had no state of mind to think of purification by fire, and if they had, the suffering would soon have overwhelmed the thought. Nor would they have listened to any teaching not supported by terror. Fear was what they were fit for; they had no worship for any being they did not have to fear.

Was this show on Sinai, then, a device to move obedience such as bad nurses use with children, a hint of vague and false horror? Was it not a true revelation of God?

If it was not a true revelation it was none: either the story is false or the display was a political trick of Moses. Those who can read Moses' mind will hardly believe the latter, and those who understand the scope of the revelation see no reason for the former. That a thing would be politic as deception does not exclude another source; some believe so little in an ordered world that fitness itself is for them a reason for unbelief.

At all events, if God showed them these things, he showed them what was true, a revelation of himself. He puts on a face, not a mask. He will not speak out of flaming fire if there is nothing in him for it to reveal, nor terrify even brutish children with a lie.

It was a revelation, but a partial one; a true symbol, not a final vision.

No revelation can be other than partial. If a man must be told all the truth for a true revelation, then farewell to revelation, and to sonship; what other than a partial revelation can the highest spiritual condition receive of the infinite God? But it is not untrue for being partial. For a lower receiver a more partial revelation may be truer than a fuller one, which might reveal nothing to him. Only a revelation that precluded growth, chaining the man to its incompleteness, would be false. The true revelation rouses the desire to know more by the truth of its incompleteness.

Here was a nation at its lowest: could it receive anything but a partial revelation of fear? How could the Hebrews not be terrified at what opposed all they knew of themselves, who thought it good to honour a golden calf? Such as they were, they did well to be afraid. Acknowledging a terror *above* them, flaming on an unknown height, was better than worshipping the idol below. Fear is nobler than sensuality and better than no God or a god made with hands, and in it lay hidden the sense of the infinite. The worship of fear is true though very low, not acceptable to God in itself yet precious even to him, for he regards men not as they merely are but as they are growing, or can grow, toward the image after which he made them. A thousand stages, each almost valueless alone, are of inestimable worth as gradations of an infinite progress; a condition that in decline would show a devil may in growth show a saint. So the revelation, neither final nor complete, but calling forth the best they could give and making higher revelation possible, may have been a true one.

But this very revelation of fire is true in a higher sense to the rejoicing saint as to the trembling sinner; the saint sees farther into its meaning. The symbol needed not to be superseded, only unfolded. While men take part *with* their sins, feeling that without them they would not be themselves, how can they understand that the lightning word is a Saviour, piercing to the dividing between the man and the evil, slaying the sin and giving life to the sinner? Is it comfort to be told that God loves them so that he will burn them clean? To them the cleansing can only be torture; they do not want to be clean, and cannot bear to be tortured. Can they, or can we wish them to, do other than fear God, even with the fear of the wicked, until they learn to love him with the love of the holy? To them Sinai is crowned with signs of vengeance. And is not God ready to do to them as they fear, though with another feeling and a different end than they can suppose? He is against sin; so far as they and sin are one he is against their desires, aims, fears and hopes, and so is altogether and always *for them*. That thunder, lightning and tempest, that blackness torn with the trumpet, that visible horror billowed with the voice of words, was a faint image for slaves' senses of what God feels against vileness and selfishness, meant to leave the stupid people, fearing somewhat to do as they would, a little room for grace to grow in them, until they saw that evil, not fire, is the fearful thing, and would gladly rush up into the trumpet-blast of Sinai to escape the flutes around the golden calf. Had they understood this, they would have needed no Sinai. It was a true, and of necessity a partial revelation: partial in order to be true.

Even Moses, the man of God, was not ready for the revelation in store, though from love to his people he prayed God to blot him out of his book of life. If that means he offered himself as a sacrifice *instead* of them, it shows why he could not be glorified with the vision of the Redeemer: he thought to appease God, not seeing that God is as tender as himself, the Reconciler and Redeemer, who cares for no atonement but the sacrifice of the heart. Nor could he see that it was the kindest thing God could do to tell him that he who had sinned should suffer. But I doubt that was what he meant. It seems rather a divine despair: he would not survive the children of his people, and cared nothing for a love that would save him alone and send those calf-worshipping thousands to the dust. Either way, how much could Moses have understood had he seen the face and not the back of the form that passed the cleft of the rock amid the thunderous vapours of Sinai? Suppose it had turned on him: the face of him who was more man than any man, through which the divine emotion would in ages to come show itself to men; the face of him who bore and was yet to bear their griefs and sorrows, who now bears ours; the face of the Son of God, who, instead of accepting a creature's sacrifice to satisfy his justice or support his dignity, gave himself utterly to them and so to the Father by doing his lovely will, and suffered unto death not that men might not suffer but that their suffering might be like his and lead them to his perfection. Would Moses have lived? Would he not have died, not of splendour or sorrow (terror was not there) but of the sight of the incomprehensible? Or if mystery had not slain him, would he not have gone about dazed, with no business left in the world, God being to him altogether unknown? A full revelation would be not only no revelation but the destruction of all revelation.

"Then may it not hurt to say that God is Love, all love and nothing other? It is not enough to answer that it is true; too much revelation may hurt by dazzling and blinding."

There is a great difference between a mystery of God that no man understands and one laid hold of, even by one man. The latter is already a revelation, and passing through his mind it will be presented, perhaps so *feebly*, that it will not hurt his fellows. Let God conceal as he will (though I believe he is ever destroying concealment, wanting to reveal everything), the light any man has received is not to be hid under a bushel. In sowing the seed God will not withhold his hand for thorns and stony places and waysides; even a bird may carry the matter, the good seed may overcome the thorns, and what withers on stony ground may leave a deeper soil for the next seed. Besides, only those with ears to hear can receive the doctrine. The selfish man could not believe it, and would misinterpret it if he could. The loving soul, oppressed by wrong teaching or partial truth claiming to be the whole, will hear, understand and rejoice.

When we say God is Love, do we teach men their fear of him is groundless? No. As much as they fear will come upon them, possibly far more. But beyond their fear is a divine fate they cannot withstand, for it works along with the human individuality that the divine individuality created in them. The wrath will consume what they *call* themselves, so that the selves God made appear, with tenfold consciousness of being, bringing all that made the blessedness of the life they tried to live without God. They will know that now first they are fully themselves. The avaricious, weary, selfish, suspicious old man will have passed away; the ever young self will remain. What they *thought* themselves will have vanished; what they *felt* themselves, though they misjudged it, will remain, glorified in repentant hope. What is immortal in God remains in man; the death in them is consumed.

It is the law of Nature, that is, of God, that all that is destructible shall be destroyed. When what is immortal buries itself in the destructible, hearing only from without and nothing from the eternal doors within, it cannot know its own immortality. The destructible must be burned out of it before it can *partake* of eternal life; when the fire of eternal life possesses a man, the destructible is gone and he is pure. Many a man's work must be burned that he may be saved "so as by fire." The lordships and Rabbi-hoods of the world go away in smoke, and the man who acquiesces is saved, for the fire has destroyed the destructible, the vantage point of the deathly, which would destroy body and soul in hell. If he still clings to what can be burned, the burning goes deeper till it reaches the roots of the falsehood that enslaves him, possibly by looking like the truth.

The man who loves God and is not yet pure courts the burning of God, and it is not always torture; the fire sometimes shows itself only as light, yet still purifies. The consuming fire is the original, active form of Purity, which is Love, the creative energy of God. Without purity there is no creation and no persistence: what is not pure is corruptible, and corruption cannot inherit incorruption.

The man whose deeds are evil fears the burning, but it will not come the less for his fearing or denying it. Escape is hopeless, for Love is inexorable. Our God is a consuming fire. He shall not come out till he has paid the uttermost farthing.

If the man resists the burning of God, a terrible doom awaits him, and its day will come: he who hates the fire of God shall be cast into the outer darkness. What sick dismay will seize him! However little a man cares about God, he does not exist without him; God is here, upholding, warming, delighting and teaching him, and giving him himself though he knows it not. But when God withdraws as far as that can be without the man's ceasing to be, and the man feels himself abandoned, hanging in ceaseless vertigo on the verge of the gulf of his being, without support, refuge, aim or end (for the soul has no weapons to destroy herself), with no inbreathing of joy and nothing to make life good, then he will listen in agony for the faintest sound of life from the closed door. If the moan of suffering humanity ever reaches that outcast of darkness, he will be ready to rush into the very heart of the Consuming Fire to know life once more, to change this terror of sick negation for that region of painful hope. Imagination cannot mislead us into too much horror of being without God, that one living death. Is not this to be worse than worst Of those that lawless and incertain thoughts Imagine howling?

But with this divine difference: the outer darkness is only the most dreadful form of the consuming fire, the fire without light, the darkness visible, the black flame. God has withdrawn but not lost his hold; his face is turned away but his hand is still on the man. His heart has ceased to beat into the man's, but he keeps him alive by his fire, which goes on searching and burning in him as in the highest saint not yet pure as he is pure.

But at length, O God, wilt thou not cast Death and Hell into the lake of Fire---even into thine own consuming self? Death shall then die everlastingly,

| And Hell itself will pass away,
|  And leave her dolorous mansions to the peering day.

Then indeed wilt thou be all in all. For then our poor brothers and sisters, every one, will have been burnt clean and brought home---O God, we trust in thee, the Consuming Fire. If their moans, myriads of ages away, would turn heaven for us into hell, shall a man be more merciful than God? Shall mercy alone of all his glories not be infinite? Shall a brother love a brother more than the Father loves a son, more than the Brother Christ loves his brother? Would he not die yet again to save one brother more?

As for us, we will now come to thee, our Consuming Fire. Thou wilt not burn us more than we can bear, but thou wilt burn us. And though thou seem to slay us, yet will we trust in thee even for what thou hast not spoken, if by any means at length we may attain the blessedness of those *who have not seen and yet have believed*.

### THE HIGHER FAITH

*The passage:* Jesus told Thomas that he had believed because he had seen, and that those who have not seen and yet have believed are blessed.

The aspiring child is often checked by the dull disciple, who has learned his lessons so imperfectly he never got beyond his school-books, and perceives the principle of none of his fragmentary rules. The child comes with his heart full: some outburst of feeling, spark of hope, or wide imagination that draws nature and humanity into religion---for to the child the Father's doings fill all space, and he has not learned to divide God from nature, Providence from grace, love from benevolence. The dull disciple answers: "God has said nothing about that in his word, so we have no right to believe anything about it. It is not revealed." He cannot suspect that what is hidden from him may be revealed to the babe; with the authority of years and ignorance he forbids the child, for he believes in no revelation but the Bible, in which all revelation lies buried, to be exhumed and reassembled into a rigid skeleton of metaphysical and legal contrivance for letting God's love have its way unchecked by the other perfections of his being.

But the man who would live through the whole divine form of his being, not confined to one broken corner and leaving the rest to demons, will meet a thousand questions the Bible never mentions. Has he nothing to do with them? "Leave them," says the dull disciple. "I cannot," says the man; "the peace of mind without which I cannot act depends on the answers, and my conduct must match them." "Leave them till God chooses to explain." "No. Questions imply answers. He has put the questions in my heart and holds the answers in his. I will wait, but not till I have knocked; I will be patient, but not till I have asked; I will seek until I find. He has something for me. My prayer shall go up to the God of my life."

Sad indeed if the Bible had told us *everything* God meant us to believe. But the Bible is wronged here: it nowhere claims to be *the* Word, *the* Way, *the* Truth. It leads us to Jesus, the ever unfolding Revelation of God. All the treasures of wisdom and knowledge are hid in Christ, not in the Bible except as it leads to him. They are hid in him, are they not, that they may be revealed to us when we need them, his holding them the mediatorial step toward their unfolding in us? Is he not the Truth to men, the High Priest who answers all the troubled questions of their dim humanity? For his heart contains of good, wise, just, the perfect shape.

Didymus answers, "What we know not now, we shall know hereafter." Questions that come here must be inquired into here, and if not answered here, then there, till they are. More is hid in Christ than we shall ever learn; but those who begin to inquire first will soonest be gladdened with revelation, and he will be best pleased with them, for his disciples' slowness troubled him of old. To say we must wait for the other world to know the mind of him who came to this world to give himself to us seems to me the foolishness of a worldly and lazy spirit. The Son of God *is* the Teacher of men, giving them his Spirit, which is to a man the mind of Christ and manifests the deep things of God. The great heresy of the Church today is unbelief in this Spirit: most of the Church does not believe the Spirit has a revelation for every man individually, as different from the Bible's as food becoming brain and nerve is from bread and meat. Filled with the mind of Christ, we would know the Bible had done its work and passed away for us, that the Word of God might abide. The Bible's one use is to make us look at Jesus, that through him we may know his Father and our Father, his God and our God. Till then, let us hold it dear as the moon of our darkness, by which we travel toward the east, not as the sun from which her light comes, toward which we haste, so that walking in the sun himself we need no more the mirror of his absent brightness.

This doctrine of the Spirit is not my end now, though without it all religion would be vain, St Paul's and Socrates' alike. I want to show that a man will please God better by believing some things not told him than by confining his faith to what is expressly said---said to arouse in us the truth-seeing faculty, the spiritual desire, the prayer for the good things God gives to those who ask.

"But is this not dangerous? Will a man not be taught to believe, even to pray for, what he likes best? Will he not grow arrogant in his confidence?"

If the Spirit strives with our spirit and God teaches men, we may leave those dreaded results to him. A man of the Lord's company is safer with him than those who secure their safety by hanging on the outskirts and daring nothing. If he is not taught of God in what he hopes for, God will let him know, giving something other than he prays for. If he can pray for anything not good, the answer will come in the flames of the consuming fire, which will soon bring him to some of his spiritual senses; better to be so sharply tutored than to go at a snail's pace. And as for arrogance, I have seen nothing breed it faster or more offensively than the worship of the letter.

To whom should a man whom God has made look for what he likes best, but God? If we have seen that God is our Father, as the Lord taught, let us go on to understand that he is far more: his nearness is beyond the highest idea of father, and fatherhood is but a step toward the Godhood for those who can receive it. What a man likes best *may* be God's will, the voice of the Spirit striving *with* his spirit; and if it is not, there is the consuming fire. The danger is not in asking God for what is not good, nor in hoping to receive it, but in not asking him, in not having him of our council. Nor will not daring to inquire his will spare us from acting in matters we call unrevealed; and for such a disposition of mind the book itself is unlikely to hold much revelation.

The whole matter may safely be left to God.

But I doubt if a man *can* ask anything bad of God. One who has begun to pray is child enough to know bad from good when it comes so near, and dares not pray for *that*. If you point to David praying such fearful prayers against his enemies, read them by the man and his history: when his greatest enemy was delivered into his hands, this man of burning eastern heart contented himself with cutting off the skirt of his garment instead of taking an eastern's vengeance. What he craved was justice and right, though his prayers took a wild form of words; God heard him and gave him what contented him. In a good man, "revenge is," as Lord Bacon says, "a kind of wild justice," and easily satisfied. The heart's desire upon such a one's enemies is best met when hate is changed into love and compassion.

But it is about hopes rather than prayers that I wish to write.

What should I think of my child if he limited his faith in me, and hope from me, to the few promises he had heard me utter! The faith that limits itself to God's promises shares that paltry character: good enough for a Pagan, but for a Christian miserable and wretched. Those who rest in it would be more comfortable with God's bond than his word, which they regard not as the outcome of his character but as a pledge of his honour. They try to believe the truth of his word but do not understand the truth of his Being; they trust his oath, but in *himself* they do not believe, for they know him not. Little wonder they distrust those swellings of the heart that are his drawings of the man toward him, as sun and moon heave the ocean heavenward. Brother, sister, if this is your faith, you must not stop there. Come out of this bondage of the law to which you give the name of grace, for little gracious is in it. You will yet know the dignity of your high calling and the love of God that passes knowledge. He is not afraid of your presumptuous approach; you are afraid to come near him. He is not watching over his dignity; you fear being sent away as the disciples would have sent away the children. You think so much about your souls and fear so much to lose your life that you dare not draw near the Life of life, lest it consume you.

Our God, we will trust thee. Shall we not find thee equal to our faith? One day we shall laugh at ourselves for expecting so little; thy giving will not be limited by our hoping.

O thou of little faith! "In everything"---I quote one who knew his master Christ and in his strength opposed apostles to their faces because they believed only a little in God: only for themselves, not their fellows; for the chosen few, for whom they had God's ancient *word*, not for the multitude of nations, the millions of hearts God had made to search after him. "In everything," says St Paul, "by prayer and supplication, with thanksgiving, let your requests be made known unto God." For this *everything* nothing is too small: that it troubles us is enough, for there is some principle in it worth God's notice, since he made us so that it troubles us. And nothing can be too great. When the Son of man comes and finds too much faith on the earth, may God in his mercy slay us. Meantime we will hope and trust.

Do you count it great faith to believe what God has said? To me it is little faith, and, alone, worthy of reproach. To believe what he has not said is faith indeed, and blessed, for it comes of believing in HIM. Can you not believe in God himself? Is it not so hard to believe what he has said that even that is almost beyond you, because you are not sure he said it? If you believed in God it would be easy to believe the word; you would not ask whether he had *said* it, but know he meant it.

Let us dare something, then, and not always be unbelieving children. The Lord, not forbidding those who insist on seeing before they believe, blesses those who have not seen and yet believed, who trust him more than that. Blessed are those to whom a wonder is not a fable, a mystery not a mockery, a glory not an unreality, who are content to ask, "Is it like Him?" A dull-hearted, unchildlike people is always putting God in mind of his promises. The promises are good to reveal what God is; if they think them good as binding God, let them have it so, for the hardness of their hearts. They prefer the Word to the Spirit; it is theirs.

Even such people will leave us, some if not all, to "the uncovenanted mercies of God." We desire no less and hope for no better: the mercies beyond our height, depth and reach. We know in whom we have believed, and look for what has not entered the heart of man to conceive. Shall God's thoughts be surpassed by man's, his giving by man's asking, his creation by man's imagination? No. Though we climb to the height of our Alpine desires and ascend the spear-pointed Himmalays of our aspirations, still we shall find the depth of God's sapphire above us, the heavens higher than the earth, and his thoughts and ways higher than ours.

Ah Lord! be thou in all our being, not in the Sundays of our time alone, nor the chambers of our hearts alone. We dare not think that some things are not for thy beholding, some questions not to be asked of thee. Are we not utterly thine? What a man speaks not to his fellow, we speak to thee. Our very passions we hold up to thee, saying, "Behold, Lord! Think about us; for thus thou hast made us." We would not escape our history by fleeing into the wilderness, hiding our heads in the sands of forgetfulness, the repentance that comes of pain or the lethargy of hopelessness. We take it as our very life in our hand and flee with it to thee. Triumphant is the answer thou holdest for every doubt; perhaps we could not yet understand it even if thou spoke it "with most miraculous organ." But thou shalt at least find faith on the earth, O Lord, if thou comest to look now: the faith of ignorant but hoping children, who know they do not know and believe that thou knowest.

And our brothers and sisters who cleave to what they call thy word, thinking to please thee so, are in thy holy safe hands, who hast taught us that whoever speaks against the Son of man will be forgiven, though not he who blasphemes against the Holy Ghost.

### IT SHALL NOT BE FORGIVEN

*The passage:* Whoever speaks a word against the Son of man will be forgiven; whoever blasphemes against the Holy Ghost will not be.

Whatever belongs to thought and feeling is expressed in words only imperfectly: thought and feeling are infinite, and human speech, though far-reaching and delicate, embodies them only approximately and suggestively. Spirit and Truth are like Una and the Red Cross Knight; Speech is the dwarf lagging behind with the lady's "bag of needments."

Our Lord did not set out to construct a system of truth in intellectual forms. He spoke The Truth, in its relation to him at the moment, out of a region of realities that can only be suggested in intellect and speech. His words invade our darkness in flashes of life, stinging us with light to will our awakening and cry for the light he gives, not in lightning words only but in indwelling presence and power.

How does the truth fare, then, with those who, lacking glow and insight, build intellectual systems on the words of our Lord or his disciples? A child would understand Plato better than they understand St Paul. The meaning in those great hearts is too great for theirs; the sense they find must be small enough to pass their narrow doors. Without sympathy to interpret them, words can mean almost anything the hearer likes. How will a man bent at best on saving his own soul understand the apostle who was ready to be banished from Christ so that his brethren might enter? To men who are not simple, simple words are the most inexplicable riddles.

If we must search for what our Lord means, we must equally refuse any interpretation that seems unlike or unworthy of him. He himself asks why we do not judge for ourselves what is right. Through ignorance or misunderstanding we may refuse the verbal form of a true interpretation, but not its spirit and truth, since we could not have seen those without being able to recognize them as Christ's mind. Prejudice or misapprehension may make us refuse the true reading, but we are still bound to refuse and wait for more light. To accept as his will what contradicts what we already worship in him is to bring discord into the harmony meant to unite and heal our hearts.

"Is it for us," says the objector, who by some sleight of will believes in the word apart from its meaning, "to judge the character of our Lord?" I answer that he requires this very thing of us: that we do him no injustice. He would dwell with us if we opened our chambers to him. How shall we receive him if, avoiding judgment, we hang some daub of authority or tradition on our walls as his likeness? Judging unrighteously by it while flattering ourselves that we do not judge, we may shut our doors on the Master himself as an impostor because he is unlike the picture in our oratory. And if we do not judge, humbly and lovingly, who will judge for us? Better to refuse even the truth for a time than to take into our creed what our heart cannot receive, and bring hesitation into our prayers, a jar into our praises, misery into our love. If it is the truth, we shall one day see it differently and love it as lovely, for all truth is lovely. "Not to the unregenerate mind," you say. But at least to the mind that can love Christ Jesus; let us follow that part of us and trust its judgments, hoping for its growth in the light of the Lord, who is that Spirit. Better, I say again, to refuse the right *form* than by accepting it in misapprehension to refuse the truth in it. Which is liker the sin against the Holy Ghost? To mistake the Son of man's meaning may fill a man with sadness. But to care so little for him as to receive as his what the noblest part of us rejects as low, selfish or wrong is far more like the unforgivable sin, for it is a sin against the truth itself, not merely its embodiment in him.

Words depend for their full meaning on their speaker. An utterance may seem commonplace until you learn it came from one you know to be always thinking, feeling and acting; knowing the mind behind it, you know the scale to read it by. So God's words cannot mean just what man's do. "Can we not understand them, then?" We can---*more* than men's words. Whatever a good word means from a good man, it means infinitely more from God, and the thought it expresses rises higher in us as we grow able to understand him, that is, as we become like him.

I am less anxious to show what the sin against the Holy Ghost is than what the nonforgiveness means, though I think we can understand both. I cannot admit that anything in the Bible is too mysterious to look into, for the Bible is a *revelation*, an unveiling. Into much of it I can see only a little way, but that way is the way of life, for the depth of its mystery is God. And even if the duty were doubtful, it would be reason enough to inquire into passages like this that they often torture minds, chiefly those of holy women and children. I knew a child who believed she had committed the sin against the Holy Ghost because she had misused a pin at her toilette. Do not rebuke me for bringing a child's diseased fancy into a weighty theological matter: "Despise not one of these little ones." Would the theologians were as near the truth as children. *Diseased fancy!* The child knew, *and knew that she knew*, she was doing wrong because she had been forbidden; *her fear had rational ground*. How would Jesus have received the darling's confession? *He* would not have told her she was silly and not to mind. Might he not have said, "I do not condemn thee: go and sin no more"?

To reach the position needed for our end, I will ask what divine forgiveness means, and to do that I will first ask what human forgiveness means; for if the Incarnation means anything, we must climb through the Human to the Divine.

Going back to the Greek or English word for a primary idea is of little use: the one means *a sending away*, the other *a giving away*. It is enough to look at the feelings associated with what is called *forgiveness*.

A man will say: "*I* forgive, but I cannot forget. Let the fellow never come in my sight again." This reaches only to remitting the penalties the wronged believes he can claim.

*But there is no sending away of the wrong itself from between them*.

Another will say: "He has done a mean thing, but he has the worst of it himself in being capable of it. I despise him too much to want revenge. I forgive him. I don't care."

Here again the wrong is not sent away from between them; there is no *remission* of the sin.

A third: "I suppose I must forgive him; if I do not, God will not forgive me."

This man is a *little* nearer, since he recognizes a ground of sympathy, though only that of common sin.

One more will say: "He has wronged me grievously. It is dreadful to me, and more dreadful to him, that he did it. He has hurt me, but nearly killed himself. I will spare him further injury from it as far as I can. I cannot yet feel the same toward him, but I will try to make him acknowledge the wrong and so put it away from him; then perhaps I can feel as before. For this I will show him every kindness I can, not forcing it on him but seizing fit opportunities---not to magnify myself through bounty, I hope, but because I love him so much I want to love him more by reconciling him to his true self. I would destroy this deed that has come between us; I send it away, and would have him destroy it too, by abjuring it utterly."

Which comes nearest to the divine idea of forgiveness---nearest, though with the gulf between, as the heavens are higher than the earth?

For the Divine creates the Human and exceeds it in creative power. It is the divine forgiveness that, originating itself, creates ours, and so can do far more: take up all our wrongs, small and great, with their rightful griefs and sorrows, and carry them away from between God and us.

Christ is God's Forgiveness.

Before drawing nearer to this great sight, let us look at human forgiveness in a more definite case, between father and son. God is so much more to us and nearer than any father, yet fatherhood is the last height of the human stair from which our understanding can see him afar and our hearts first know he is nigh, even in them.

Wrongdoing has various kinds and degrees, needing various kinds and degrees of forgiveness. A child's outburst of anger scarcely needs forgiveness: the parent need only influence him toward self-restraint and rouse his will against it, and no wall is built between them. But suppose the father found in him a habit of sly cruelty to his younger brothers or the animals; how differently he would feel! Could his forgiveness be the same? Would the different evil not require a different *form* of forgiveness---the kind of punishment fittest to restrain, and finally root out, the wickedness? Could any other kind be true love? Passing by the offence might spring from poor human kindness, never from divine love, and would not be *remission*. Forgiveness can never be indifference. Forgiveness is love toward the unlovely.

Look closer at how a father might feel. One child the father would clasp to his bosom the moment the fault was committed, knowing that love in its natural working would destroy the fault and the child would soon be weeping. His hatred of the sin would burst out in pitying tenderness toward a child so wretched as to have done it, and so destroy it. Such a fault interrupts no sweet affections; the child is forgiven at once. But another child, treated on the same principle, would be quite different. If he were guilty of baseness, meanness, selfishness, deceit, or gloating over the evil he brought on others, the father might *say* to himself, "I cannot forgive him. This is beyond forgiveness"---and go on saying it while striving all the time to let forgiveness find its way and lift him from the gulf. His love might even grow greater because of the wandering and loss, for love is most divine when it loves by *needs* and not *merits*. But the forgiveness would still be in the making, drawing nigh to the sinner. Only when his opening heart received the divine flood of destroying affection, and his own affection burst out to meet it and sweep the evil away, could it be finished, and the son be said to *be* forgiven.

God forgives us every day, sending our sins with their fogs and darkness from between him and us. Witness his sun and rain, the filling of hearts with food and gladness: he loves those who love him not. When some sin has clouded our whole horizon and hidden him, he, forgiving us before we are forgiven and that we may be, sweeps a path for his forgiveness to reach our hearts, that by causing repentance it may destroy the wrong and make us able to forgive ourselves. Some are too proud to do that until God's forgiveness has drowned their pride in tears and made their heart again like a little child's.

Seen as the perfecting of a work ever going on, the contact of God's heart and ours despite and in destruction of the wrong between, forgiveness is always behind God's love. His love is the prime mover, ever seeking to perfect forgiveness, which needs the human condition for its completion. God loves where he cannot yet forgive---where forgiveness in the full sense is impossible because no contact of hearts is possible, the wrong between not having begun to yield to the besom of his holy destruction.

Some things between the Father and his children, as between a father and child, may in a sense be made comparatively light of---not in themselves, for away they must go---since, though evils or sins, they leave room for God's Spirit to dwell in the heart, forgiving and cleansing. When a man's evil is fading and he is growing better and better, forgiveness is coming into him more and more, perfect in God's will and having its perfect work in the man. When he has cast away his sin with his whole nature, there is no room left for forgiveness, since God dwells in him and he in God. With Nathan's "Thou art the man," God's forgiveness laid hold of David; his heart was humbled to the dust, and waking from his moral lethargy he found he was still with God: "When I awake, I am still with thee."

But two sins, not of individual deed but of spiritual condition, *cannot be forgiven*---that is, they cannot be excused, passed by or made little of even by God's tenderness, because they let no forgiveness into the soul, allow no good influence to work beside them, and shut God out altogether. The man guilty of them can receive none of the renewing, saving influences of God's forgiveness. God is outside him in every sense except his creating relation, by which, thanks be to God, he still keeps hold of him against the man's will. One of these sins is against man, the other against God.

The first is unforgivingness to our neighbour: shutting him out of our mercies and love, and so from the universe as far as we are part of it. It is murder, and may be infinitely worse than killing a man, which can be a moment's passion where this is the heart's choice. To hate, and brood on the feeling that in our microcosm kills the image of the hated, is the worst, *spiritual* murder. We listen to our hurt pride or hurt affection (only the latter, without the former, thinks no evil) to the injury of the evil-doer, quenching the relations of life between us and closing the passages of return. This is to shut out God, the Life. How can we receive the forgiving presence while we shut our brother out of our share of the final restoration, and refuse to let God be All in all? If God appeared and said "I forgive you" while we were unforgiving to our neighbour, his forgiveness would do us no good: it would not touch us while we were uncured of our unforgivingness, and would even hurt us, for we would think ourselves safe while disease ate out our heart. Tenfold forgiveness lies in the words "If ye forgive not men their trespasses, neither will your heavenly Father forgive your trespasses." They are kindness indeed. God holds the unforgiving man with his hand but turns his face away; if, wishing to see his Father's face, the man turns his own toward his brother, the face of God turns and seeks his, for then he may look on God and not die. With our forgiveness to our neighbour flows in the consciousness of God's forgiveness to us; even trying to forgive makes us able to believe God can forgive us. No man who will not forgive can believe God is willing, even wanting, to forgive him, or that the dove of God's peace hovers over a chaotic heart, fain to alight but finding no rest for the sole of its foot. For God to say to such a man "I cannot forgive you" is love as well as necessity. If God said "I forgive you" to a man who hated his brother, and (impossibly) the voice reached him, he would hear "You may go on hating. I do not mind. You have had great provocation and are justified." God does take the provocation into account; but the more provocation and excuse, the more reason, if possible, that the hater be delivered from the hell of his hate and God's child made the loving child he meant. The man would think God loved the sinner but forgave the sin, which God never does. Every sin meets its due fate: inexorable expulsion from the paradise of God's Humanity. God loves the sinner so much that he can forgive him no other way than by banishing the demon that possesses him and lifting him out of the mire of his iniquity.

No one supposes that a man who has once refused to forgive his brother is condemned to endless unforgivingness. The meaning is that while he stays in that mood God cannot be with him as friend: not that God will not be his friend, but a friendship all on God's side must take forms the man cannot recognize as friendship. Forgiveness, as I said, is not love merely but love *conveyed as love* to the erring, establishing peace toward God and forgiveness toward our neighbour.

To return to our text: does the refusal of forgiveness in it condemn a man to irrecoverable impenitence? Strange righteousness, to decree that because a man has done wrong so often that he *is* wrong, he shall remain wrong for ever! Do not tell me the condemnation is only negative, leaving him to the consequences of his own will or withdrawing the Spirit he despised. God will not shelter behind such jugglery of logic; he is neither schoolman nor theologian but our Father in heaven, and he knows this would be the very unforgivingness for which he refuses to forgive man. The only tenable ground for such a doctrine is that God *cannot* do more: that Satan has overcome, and Jesus, among his brothers and sisters in God's image, has been weaker than the adversary. What shall I say to a doctrine of devils which holds that even if a man did repent, God would not or could not forgive him?

Let us look at "*the* unpardonable sin," as this mystery is called, and see what we can understand of it.

All sin is unpardonable: no compromise is possible; we shall not come out until clean, having paid the uttermost farthing. But the special unpardonableness of these two sins lies in shutting God out from his *genial*, especially spiritual, influence on the man. Perhaps I said this too strongly of the first: perhaps God's love may have some part even in the man who will not forgive his brother, though it must decrease and die away if he continues so; perhaps resentment might for a while leave room for some divine influence, though one or the other must soon yield. But the man who denies truth, who consciously resists duty, who says there is no truth or that the truth he sees is not true, who calls the good of Satan or the bad of God while knowing which is which, denies and shuts out the Spirit and cannot be forgiven. Without the Spirit no forgiveness can enter the man to cast out the satan, and without the Spirit to witness with his spirit he could not know himself forgiven even if God appeared and said so. Full forgiveness is when a man feels God forgiving him, and that cannot be while he opposes the very essence of God's will.

As far as we can see, those this was spoken of resisted the truth with some perception that it was the truth: men not led astray by passion, nor wholly blinded by prejudice, nor moved to condemn one form of truth by love of another, but so set, from selfishness and love of influence, against one they saw to be good that they denied the goodness of what they knew was good, to put him down, because he spoke against them and was ruining their authority with the people by declaring them no better than they knew themselves to be. Is this not to be Satan, to be in hell, damned? Was this *condition* not unpardonable? How could God's pardon reach the essential humanity within through such a mass of falsehood? Crying as it was for forgiveness, these men had almost separated their humanity from themselves and sided with the powers of darkness. Forgiveness while they were such was impossible; they must come out of it, else there was no word of God for them. But the very word that told them of their unpardonable state was the form the voice of mercy could take in calling them to repent. They must hear and be afraid. I dare not think they refused the truth knowing all it was; but I think they refused it knowing it was true, moved not by wild passion but by cold self-love, envy, avarice and ambition, setting their whole natures knowingly against the light. The sin against the Holy Ghost must be of this nature. "This is the condemnation"---not the sins men have committed but the condition of mind in which they choose to remain---that light has come into the world and men loved darkness rather than light, because their deeds were evil. In this sin I see no single act alone, though it must find expression in many, but a wilful condition of mind,

| As far removed from God and light of heaven,
| As from the centre thrice to the utmost pole.

For this there could be no excuse that even a little light might work beside it: light could find no entrance, for light was just what such a mind was set against, almost because it was what it was. The condition was utterly bad.

But can a man really fall into such a spiritual condition?

That is my chief difficulty. But I think it may be, as wiser people than I have thought. I find it hard to believe, yet I think it must be so. But I do not believe it is a fixed, final condition, any more than that of the man who does not forgive his neighbour. If it is a worse offence, is it too bad for God's forgiveness?

But can God do anything more with such a man? How is he ever to get out of this condition? If God's Spirit is shut out of his heart, how is he to become better?

The Spirit of God is known by witnessing with our spirit. But may there not be other powers and means of the Spirit preparing for this highest office? God who made us can never be far from any man who draws breath; he must be in him, if not in his heart. May not some terrible convulsion from the centre of his being, some earthquake from the hidden gulfs of his nature, so shake such a man that the still small voice that follows tempest and earthquake may be faintly heard through all the deafness of his death? May there not be a fire even such a man can feel? Who shall set bounds to the consuming of our God's fire, and the purifying in it?

The only argument I can think of that would weigh with me against this is that a man who had so sinned, once brought to acknowledge it, would feel a revulsion so terrible that life would never be endurable again, and the kindest thing God could do would be to put him out of being, since it had been better for him never to have been born. But he who could make such a man repent could make him so sorrowful, lowly and glad he had repented that he would wish to live for ever to repent and worship the glory he now saw. When a man gives up self, past sins no longer oppress him. It is enough for the good of life that God lives, that the All-perfect exists and we can behold him.

"Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do," said the Divine, excusing his murderers not after it was over but while dying by their hands. Then Jesus had already forgiven them. The Father must have heard, for he and the Son are one; and when he succeeded in answering the prayer, his forgiveness in the murderers' hearts broke out in sorrow, repentance and faith. A dreadful sin, but easy for our Lord to forgive. All that excuse for the misled populace---Lord Christ be thanked; that was like thee! But must we believe that Judas, who repented to agony, so that his high-prized life and self became worthless in his eyes and met no mercy from his own hand, could find none from such a God? I think that when Judas fled from his hanged and fallen body he fled to the tender help of Jesus and found it---I say not how. He was now in a more hopeful condition than ever before, for he had never repented. I believe Jesus loved Judas even while he kissed him with the traitor's kiss, and was his Saviour still. If any remind me of the words "It had been good for that man if he had not been born," I have not forgotten them, though I offer only a conjectural explanation: Judas had got none of the good of the world he was born into. He lived an evil life out of harmony with the world and its God, and its love was lost on him. Brought to the very Son of God and living with him as friend, he loved him less, not more, than himself. So it had all been useless, and was all to try over again, in some other way, perhaps inferior, in some other world, in a lower school; he had to be sent down the scale of creation, which is ever ascending toward its Maker. But I will not, cannot believe, O my Lord, that thou wouldst not forgive thy enemy when he repented and did thee right, or that thy holy death was powerless to reach Judas. Have we not heard of those taught of thee who could easily forgive their betrayers in thy name? And if thou forgivest, will thy forgiveness not find its way at last in redemption and purification?

The clause before my text says: "He that denieth me before men shall be denied before the angels of God." Does that mean "You are mine but not my sort; you denied me; away to the outer darkness"? No. Whoever speaks against the Son of man shall be forgiven, for he may be only the truth revealed *without* him; but he must be ashamed before the universe of the loving God, and may need the fire that burns and does not consume.

But he who speaks against the Spirit of Truth, the Son of God revealed *within* him, is beyond that Spirit's teaching now. How can he be forgiven? Forgiveness would touch him no more than a wall of stone. Let him know what it is to be without the God he denied. Away with him to the Outer Darkness! Perhaps *that* will make him repent.

My friends, I offer this only as a contribution to understanding our Lord's words. If we ask him, he will lead us into all truth. Let us not fear to think, for he will not take it ill.

But what I have said must be at least part of the truth.

No discovery in his words can tell us more than *we* have discovered and seen to be true; the best of his disciples can only help us see for ourselves. Beyond our discoveries lie depths within depths of truth we cannot understand yet shall ever go on understanding. Even now we sometimes glimpse regions from which we receive no word to bring away.

That some things have become so much simpler to us, and great truths have come out of what once looked common, is ground for hope that this will go on through the ages. Our advance from ignorance measures only a small part of the distance between our childishness and his manhood, our love and his, our dimness and his vision. To him may we all come ere long, all children, more children than ever, to receive from his hand the *white stone, and in the stone a new name written, which no man knoweth saving he that receiveth it*.

### THE NEW NAME

*The passage:* To the one who overcomes, Christ will give a white stone with a new name written on it, which no one knows except the one who receives it.

Whether or not the Book of the Revelation was written by the author of the Gospel according to St John, the two share at least one element: mysticism.

I use *mysticism* for a way of embodying truth common, in various degrees, to almost all the New Testament writers. Defining it would take an essay; I offer one suggestion. A mystical mind has seen that truth is best expressed through the symbolism of nature and the customs that arise from human needs, and pursues its thought about truth so embodied by working with the symbols themselves in logical forms. This is the highest way of conveying the deepest truth, and the Lord often used it, as in the whole passage ending, "If therefore the light that is in thee be darkness, how great is the darkness!"

The mysticism of St John's Gospel is of the simplest and therefore noblest kind. No dweller on this planet can imagine a purer, loftier method of embodying truth. Other worlds may have higher modes, or may not; of all ours, these forms best illustrate the highest. John's own mysticism apparently let him report our Lord's words with sufficient accuracy; they seem to me always recognizably different from those of any other New Testament writer, chiefly in the simplicity of their poetical mysticism.

The mysticism of the Revelation is more complicated, more gorgeous, less poetic, and occasionally, I think, arbitrary or near it; it *reminds* one of Swedenborg. Setting aside historical and literary criticism, on which I have no right even to an opinion, I suggest that this difference of tone may be just what we should expect when the recorder of a mystical teacher's sayings goes on to embody his own thoughts and inspirations: when the revelation flows no longer from the Master's lips but through the disciple's own heart, soul and brain. Surely not even the most idolatrous Bible-worshipper will assert that the Spirit of God could speak as freely by the lips of the wind-swayed, rebukable Peter, or of the Thomas who could believe his own eyes but neither his brethren's word nor his Master's nature, as by the lips of Him who was blind and deaf to everything but the will of him that sent him.

Truth is truth, whether from Jesus or Balaam. But in its deepest sense *the truth* is a condition of heart, soul, mind and strength toward God and our fellow, not an utterance or even a *right* form of words; so truth in words is in a sense the person speaking. Many utterances of truth in the Revelation carry the conviction that the writer was no mere "trumpet of a prophecy" but spoke what he knew and testified to what he had seen.

In this passage about the gift of the white stone I think we find the essence of religion.

What the writer had in mind about the white stone matters comparatively little. I take it to belong more to the arbitrary and fanciful than to true mystical imagery, though fancy, glorified, serves the mystic's imagination. I doubt he attached any essential meaning to it. I will not allow that he meant so poor a thing as a voting pebble, white because the receiver is chosen; the word also means a jewel, and a mystical writer is likelier to refer to nature than to a political custom. Different minds will take his meaning differently; *I* think he sees purity in its whiteness and indestructibility in its substance. But I care chiefly for the stone as the vehicle of the name, the form in which it passes from God to the man, and what that involves. If my reader will not accept this as St John's meaning, I hope to set it forth so that he sees it true in itself, and I will leave the interpretation to its fate.
