I say in brief that the giving of the white stone with the new name is the communication to the man of what God thinks about him. It is the divine judgment, the solemn holy doom of the righteous man, the "Come, thou blessed," spoken to the individual.

To see this we must first understand what a name is, in its perfect idea. Since the mystical energy of a holy mind here speaks of God as giving something, we must understand that the essential thing is meant, not any of its accidents or imitations.

*An ordinary name in this world has nothing* essential in it. It is a label by which one man and a scrap of his history are told from another. The only significant names are those that popular judgment, prejudice or humour bestows, for ridicule or honour, on a few of the many, each founded on some external characteristic: a predominant peculiarity of temper, some excellence or the reverse, something done well or ill, or singularly enough, to mark him off from other men. As far as they go these are real names, for they express individuality in some poor measure.

The true name expresses the character, nature, being and *meaning* of the person who bears it: his own symbol, his soul's picture, the sign that belongs to him alone. Who can give a man this? God alone, for no one else sees what the man is, or could express the sum and harmony of it in a name-word. To whom is it given? To him that overcometh, when he has overcome. Does God then not know what a man will become? As surely as he sees the oak he put there lying in the heart of the acorn. Why does he wait till the man has overcome before settling his name? He does not wait; he knows it from the first. But as repentance comes because God pardons, yet the man becomes aware of the pardon only in the repentance, so God gives the stone with the name only when the man has become his name, for then first can he understand what it signifies. The blossom determines the name; God foresees it from the first because he made it so; but the tree of the soul, before it blossoms, cannot understand what blossom it will bear, or know what the word meant that named its own unarrived completeness. The name cannot be given until the man *is* the name.

God's name for a man must be the expression, in a mystical word of the language all overcomers understand, of his own idea of the man: the being he had in thought when he began to make the child and kept in thought through the long process of creation. To tell the name is to seal the success: to say, "In thee also I am well pleased."

But we are still in the region of symbol. Even if such a form were actually observed between God and the overcomer, it would be a symbol still, only an acted one. We must look deeper, and shall soon find that the mystic symbol centres on the personal, individual relation of every man to his God. That every man has affairs, and his first affairs, with God stands to the reason of anyone who attaches any meaning to the words Maker, Father, God. Were we children of a day, given one holiday by someone, there would be something to think, feel and do because we knew it; our nature would suit our fate, and we could worship and die. But that would be the praise of the dead, not the living, for death would be the deepest and lasting thing; we would have come out of nothingness, not out of God, who could be only our Maker, not our Father, our Origin. But God cannot be the God of the dead; he must be the God of the living, since knowing that we died would freeze the heart of worship, and we could not say Our God. To him who offers this God of the living his own self in sacrifice, who has brought his individual life back to its source and knows he is *one* of God's children, *this* one of the Father's making, God gives the white stone. To him who climbs the stair of all his God-born efforts and God-given victories to the height of his being---to look face to face on his ideal self in the Father's bosom, God's *him*, realized through the Father's love in the Elder Brother's devotion---God gives the new name written.

But I leave this, because what follows intensifies this individuality. The name is one "which no man knoweth saving he that receiveth it." So each man has not only an individual but a peculiar relation to God: made after his own fashion and no one else's, he will receive, when perfected, a new name no one else can understand. So he can worship and understand God as no one else can; another may understand God more or better, but none as he does. God give me grace to be humble before thee, my brother, that I drag not my simulacrum of thee before the judgment-seat of the unjust judge, but look to thyself for the revelation of God thou alone canst give. As the fir-tree lifts itself with a far different need from the palm, each man lifts a different humanity to the common Father, and to each God gives a different response. With every man he has a secret, the secret of the new name. In every man is an inner chamber of peculiar life that only God can enter. I do not say it is *the innermost chamber*, but no brother, nay, no sister, can come into it.

It follows that there is also a chamber (O God, humble and accept my speech) in God himself, which none can enter but the one, individual, peculiar man, and out of which he must bring revelation and strength for his brethren. This is what he was made for: to reveal the secret things of the Father.

By his creation each man is isolated with God; by his peculiar making each can say "*my* God," and come to him alone and speak with him face to face as with a friend. There is no *massing* of men with God; gathered men are a spiritual *body*, not a *mass*, for in a body every part is individual and so fit to be part of it.

See what significance the symbolism now takes on. Each of us is a distinct flower or tree in God's garden, precious for his own sake to the one who is even now making us, watered, shone upon and filled with life for the sake of the flower, the completed being that will at last blossom out of him to the great gardener's glory and pleasure. Each has within him a secret of the Divinity and is growing toward its revelation to himself, and so toward receiving the divine according to his measure. Each moment he is true to his true self, some new shine of the white stone breaks on his inward eye and some fresh channel opens for the coming glory of the flower, the conscious offering of his whole being in beauty to the Maker. Each man is of worth in God's sight; life and action, thought and intent, are sacred. And what an end lies before us: a consciousness of our own ideal being flashed into us from God's thought! For this our paltry self-consciousness, self-admiration and self-worship may well give way; to know what he thinks of us will pale out all our thoughts about ourselves. St Paul had drawn near to this when he who began the race with a bitter cry for deliverance from the body of his death could say he no longer judged his own self.

"But is there not the worst of all dangers in such teaching, spiritual pride?" Must we refuse the spirit for fear of the pride, when there is no deliverance from pride except the spirit? Pride springs from supposed success in the high aim; with attainment comes humility. And here there is no room for ambition, the desire to be above one's neighbour, for no comparison is possible: no one knows what the white stone contains but the man who receives it. There is room for endless aspiration toward the unseen ideal, but none for ambition, which would only be higher than others where aspiration would be high. Relative worth is not only unknown but, to the children of the kingdom, unknowable; each esteems the other better than himself. How shall the glowing rose rejoice against the snowdrop rising with hanging head from the snow? Both are God's thoughts, dear to him, needed for the completeness of his earth and the revelation of himself. "God has cared to make me for himself," says the victor with the white stone, "and has called me what I like best, for my name must be what I would have it, seeing it is myself. What matter whether I am a grass of the field or an eagle of the air, a stone for his temple or a Boanerges to wield his thunder? I am his idea, his making, perfect in my kind and in his sight, full of him, revealing him, alone with him. Let him call me what he will; the name shall be precious as my life."

He will no longer worry what his neighbour thinks of him; it is enough that God thinks about him. To be something to God---is that not praise enough? To be a thing God cares for and would have complete for himself, because it is worth caring for---is that not life enough?

Nor will he thus be isolated from his fellows, for what we say of one we say of all, and it is as *one* that a man has claims among them. Each will feel the awe of his neighbour's dark and silent speech with God, regard him as a prophet, and look to him for what the Lord has spoken. Each, like a high priest returning from his Holy of Holies, will bring some gospel of truth that his neighbours will understand. Each will see in the other a marvel of revelation, a son or daughter of the Most High come forth to reveal him afresh. In God each will draw nigh to each.

Yes, there will be danger, as everywhere; but he gives more grace. And if the man who has striven up the heights should fall from them into the deeps, is there not the consuming fire of God, which burns and does not destroy?

To anyone who has not already had some speech with God, or at least felt some aspiration toward the fount of his being, all this must seem foolishness. So be it.

But, Lord, help them and us, and make our being grow into thy likeness. If through ages of strife and growth, yet let us at last see thy face and receive the white stone from thy hand. To that end give us day by day our daily bread, fill us with the words that proceed out of thy mouth, and help us lay up *treasures in heaven, where neither moth nor rust doth corrupt*.

### THE HEART WITH THE TREASURE

*The passage:* Do not store up treasures on earth, where moth and rust destroy and thieves break in and steal. Store up treasures in heaven, where nothing destroys them and no thief can reach them. For wherever your treasure is, your heart will be there too.

To understand our Lord's words is the business of life, because it is the main road to understanding The Word himself. To receive him is to receive the Father and so to have Life in ourselves; and that original Life, higher, deeper and simpler than any other, is the business of life.

The Word is that by which we live, namely Jesus himself, and his words show something of him in shadow and suggestion. Any utterance worthy of being called *a truth* is food for human beings; how much more The Word, who gives no abstract laws of our being but the living relation of soul and body, heart and will, strength and joy, beauty and light, to Him who gave birth to them all. The Son came to *be*, before our eyes and in our hearts, what he made us for, so that we might see *the truth* in him and cry out for the living God, who in the highest sense is The Truth: not as understood but as understanding, living, being, doing and creating the truth. "I am the truth," said our Lord, and only those who are in some measure like him in being the truth can understand the Word. Let us try.

No doubt the Saviour would sometimes have spoken differently had he come to Englishmen instead of Jews. But his lessons would have been the same, for even when asked about a passing matter, he stated the great human principle in it, and that does not change as circumstances change. With the added experience of Christian ages, we should find his words easier to understand than his first hearers did.

What is the force of his *For*: "For where your treasure is, there will your heart be also"? Its meaning is not obvious on the surface; it must be sought, because it is deep and simple. But it is so complete, and works so directly on the conscience through its poetry, that once understood there is nothing more to say and everything to do.

"Why not lay up treasures on earth?"

"Because there the moth and rust and the thief come."

"So we would lose them!"

"Yes."

"Does the Lord then mean the *reason* is that such treasures are transitory and corruptible?"

"No. He adds a *For*: 'For where your treasure is, there will your heart be also.'"

"Of course the heart will be where the treasure is; but what has that to do with it?"

This: what is with the treasure must fare as the treasure fares. The heart that haunts a treasure-house where moth and rust corrupt will suffer the same ravages and be rusted and moth-eaten itself.

Many a man and woman, fair and flourishing to look at, goes about with a rusty, moth-eaten heart inside that form of strength or beauty.

"But this is only a figure." True. But is the reality less than the figure, or more? Do *the rust and the moth* mean more than disease, and *the heart* more than the heart? It means a deeper heart: your own self, not your body; the self that suffers not pain but misery, whose end is not comfort but blessedness, even ecstasy; the inmost chamber where the divine fountain of your being springs. God regards that heart, though you may never have known it was there, even when its writhing under moth and rust sends a dull pain to the outer heart. If God sees it corroded by cares and riddled by the worms of ambition and greed, that is your heart, for God sees things as they are. One day you will be forced to feel your heart as God sees it, and to know that the cankered thing within you is the centre of your being.

Nor does the lesson apply only to worshippers of Mammon, who give their best energies to accumulating wealth. It applies equally to anyone who worships the transitory: who seeks the praise of men more than the praise of God, who would make a show by wealth, taste, intellect, power, art or any kind of genius, and so gather golden opinions to be treasured in an earthly storehouse.

Nor only to these, but to those whose pleasures are more plainly transitory still, the pleasures of the senses in every direction, lawful or unlawful, if the joy of being is centred in them. The hurt is not that such pleasures are false, like the deceptions of magic; they are real pleasures. Nor is it that they pass and leave a fierce disappointment; that is so much the better. The hurt is that the immortal, the infinite, made in the image of the everlasting God, is housed with the fading and corrupting and clings to them as its good, until it is infected with their diseases. These are more terrible in it in proportion to its higher kind: what is mere decay in the one becomes moral vileness in the other, and what fits the one for the dunghill casts the other into outer darkness. It creeps into a burrow in the earth, and its budded wings wither and drop away, when it should have roamed the open plains and high table-lands, spreading young wings to the sun and strengthening them in further flights until they could bear the God-born into the presence of its Father in Heaven. Therein lies the hurt.

The man whose heart is sound because it haunts the treasure-house of heaven may be *tempted of the devil*, but he will first be *led up of the Spirit into the wilderness*.

### THE TEMPTATION IN THE WILDERNESS

*The passage:* The Spirit led Jesus into the wilderness to be tempted by the devil. After forty days of fasting he was hungry, and the tempter said, "If you are the Son of God, command these stones to become bread." Jesus answered that it is written, "Man shall not live by bread alone, but by every word that proceedeth out of the mouth of God." The devil set him on the temple's pinnacle and told him to throw himself down, since it is written that angels will bear him up. Jesus answered that it is also written, "You shall not tempt the Lord your God." Then the devil showed him all the kingdoms of the world from a high mountain and offered them if Jesus would fall down and worship him. Jesus said, "Get away, Satan! It is written: You shall worship the Lord your God, and serve him only." Then the devil left him, and angels came and ministered to him.

This narrative must have one of two origins: either invention, like many tales told of our Lord in the early centuries of Christianity, or our Lord himself, since by the story no one was present at his Temptation but the wild beasts.

As to invention, the story bears no sign of it. A man who could see what is embodied here would not have dared to invent such an embodiment. If you doubt this, compare it with the best of the stories resting only on the apocryphal gospels, such as the grand account of the Descent into Hell in the Gospel according to Nicodemus.

| If it have not this origin, there is but the other that it can have---
| Our Lord himself. To this I will return presently.

Let us approach from another side. Think how much God must know that we do not. Think what an abyss of truth our Lord was, from whose divine darkness, through that countenance, voice and tender hands, broke every holy radiance of human meaning, and in whose understanding, imagination and heart lay all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge. Must he not have known and rejoiced in things no human words could tell? His human form was a veil over the face of the truth, which by dimming the glory revealed it in part; what could be conveyed had to be conveyed so, and an infinite More lay behind. And even of what could be partly revealed, could he speak to his Father and to his disciples in the same words? What he said to God on the mountain-tops may have been as far beyond his disciples as God's voice from heaven was to the crowd, who heard only thunder.

No attempt is made to convey even the substance of the battle of those forty days. A conflict that for forty days absorbed all the human needs of *The Man* in the cares of the Godhead could not be put into forms we understand; in itself it could not be intelligible to us. Only at the end of the forty days does the divine event begin to dawn from the depths of eternal thought, becoming human enough to be spoken in human forms, though still only as a dark saying for those who have ears to hear and a heart to understand. The mystery is not left behind, and the speech cannot yet be clear.

Just as the approaching event comes within human ken, dimly seen from afar by his God-upheld intelligence, his humanity seizes on the Master and he is hungry. Hunger is the first sign that he has come back to us and that the strife is nearing its human result. On what a sea of endless life our poor necessities float, not the poorest of them separate from the divine! Coming out of the storms of divine thought and feeling into the shallows by the human shore, bearing the treasures won in the strife, our Lord is at once hungry; and from this moment the temptation is human and we can in some measure understand it.

But could it even then have been conveyed in intellectual forms, to those who had only vague, error-mingled notions of him? No. His inward experiences could be conveyed only in a parable, the form he chose for far plainer things; it is the first form in which truth can be embodied, and the fullest. Would he who takes the passage as simple narrative believe what it then plainly says: that a visible demon came to our Lord and, himself prince of worldly wisdom, thought that quoting Scripture like the priests would persuade a good man to tempt God, and promised power to make him abandon every claim on the human race by worshipping one he knew to be the adversary of Truth, Humanity and God? How could Satan be so foolish? Or if he could, how could such a temptation tempt our Lord, and how would victory over it be a victory for the race?

Told as a parable, it is as full of meaning as it would be bare as narrative.

Our Lord told them this parable, then, and so conveyed more of the truth about his temptation than any other form could. Yet I do not think we have it exactly as he told it. A man hears only what he can hear and in retelling tells only what he laid hold of; his effort to reproduce the impression is open to countless altering influences. That does not make the reproduction false. The mighty life-bearing worlds, needing abysses of space for their courses, dwindle to the eye into seeds of light sown on a blue plain, and the thunder of their adoration is faint in our ears; yet they are lovely, uttering speech and teaching knowledge. So this story may not be just as the Lord told it, and yet hold in its mirror as much truth as we can receive, with scope for a lifetime's discovery. The modifying influence of human channels may be essential to God's way of revealing; we learn the laws of the heavens only by first seeing the stars from afar.

Now arises the question on whose answer the whole elucidation depends: *How could the Son of God be tempted?*

If anyone says he was not moved by those temptations, then they were no temptations to him and he was not tempted; his victory meant no more than that of a man who, tempted to bear false witness against his neighbour, refrains from robbing him. It would mean nothing for human need, struggle and hope, and we would have to reject the whole as crude invention, a mere stage-show, a lie for the sake of a fancied truth, a doing of evil that good might come, and not to be received as a divine message however many fragments of truth its mud might hold.

But if these were real temptations, am I not in a greater difficulty? How could the Son of God be tempted with evil, which to him must appear in its true colours of discord and deformity? And how then could he be the Son of a Father who cannot be tempted with evil?

The answer is the centre and essential germ of the whole interpretation: he was not tempted with Evil but with Good, with inferior forms of good pressing in while the higher forms held aloof, biding their time, which is God's time. I do not believe the Son of God could be tempted with evil, but I believe he could be tempted with good, to yield to which would have been evil in him, ruin to the universe. But does not all evil come from good?

Yes, but it has come *from* it; it is no longer good. A good corrupted is no longer a good, and such could not tempt our Lord. Revenge may originate in a sense of justice, but it is revenge, not justice, an evil thing, for it would be fearfully unjust. The Lord could not have felt tempted to take vengeance on his enemies, but he might have felt tempted to destroy the wicked from the face of the earth, not to destroy them for ever; to that I do not think he could have felt tempted.

But the matter itself will illustrate what I mean. Let us look at the individual temptations in the parable.

The idea behind St Matthew's arrangement seems to me superior to St Luke's. In the two accounts the closes, each profoundly significant, are remarkably different.

Now let us follow St Matthew, and see how the devil tempted him *to* evil, but not *with* evil.

First, he was hungry, and the devil said, *Make bread of this stone*.

The Lord had been fasting forty days, a fast impossible except in intense mental absorption. Let no one glorify it by calling it miraculous. Such fasts are on record of holy men; and as the Lord was more of a man than his brethren, he could be withdrawn farther into his spiritual humanity from his physical nature, whose workings would be that much slower, so that his fast could outlast any other. This, I believe, was all, and it is the simplest and so the divinest way to see the fast. At the end, then, it was not hunger alone that made food tempting but the exhaustion of the whole system, which, acting on a mind already worn out by its own tension, must have so deadened it (speaking from the experience of his brethren) that for the time it could see or feel nothing of the spiritual and could only *believe in* the unfelt, the unseen. What a temptation! There is no sin in wishing to eat or in honestly procuring food. It rises even to an awful duty when a man knows that eating will restore the lost vision of the eternal and make him capable of hope as well as faith, gladness as well as confidence. Why then should he not eat, using the power in him? Because that power was his not to take care of himself but to work the work of him who sent him, and not even to honour his Father except as his Father chose, who is more honoured by common wonders than by miracles. It was God's business to take care of him, and his to do what the Father told him. To make that stone bread would take the care out of the Father's hands and turn the divinest thing in the universe into the merest commonplace of self-preservation.

In nothing was he to be beyond his brethren, save in faith. He had no refuge, any more than they, but the Father's love and care; any other, miraculous power or what you will, would be hell to him. God is refuge. God is life. "Was he not to eat when it came in his way? And did not the bread come in his way when his power met what could be changed into it?"

Regard that word *changed*; the whole matter lies in it. Changed from what God had made it, into what he did not make it, because the Son was hungry and the Father would not feed him with convenient food! The Father did not give him a stone when he asked for bread; Satan brought the stone and told him to provide for himself. The Father said, That is a stone; the Son would not say, That is a loaf. No creative *fiat* shall contradict another; Father and Son are of one mind. The Lord could hunger, could starve, but would not change into another thing what his Father had made one thing. \[Footnote: There was no such change in feeding the multitudes; the fish and bread were fish and bread before. I think this significant for the true nature of a miracle: in these miracles, and I think in all, there was only a hastening of appearances, the doing in a day of what may ordinarily take a thousand years, for with God time is not what it is with us. Hastening a process does not interfere with cause and effect or make it more miraculous. The wonder of the growing corn is to me greater than the feeding of the thousands; it is easier to understand creative power going forth at once than through the countless, lovely, seemingly forsaken wonders of the corn-field. To the merely scientific man this is nonsense; the time will come, I think, when he will see in it a higher reason and loftier science, however incorrectly indicated here.\]

The answer he gave the devil shows the root at once: "Man shall not live by bread alone, but by every word that proceedeth out of the mouth of God." Yes, even by the word that made that stone that stone. It is life to him to leave a stone what the Father made a stone; it would be death to alter one word that He had spoken.

"Man shall not live by bread alone." There are other ways to live. A man will live by the word of God: by what God says to him and means between Him and him, by the truths of being only the Father can reveal to his child, by the communion of love between them. Without bread he will die, as men say, but will not find that he dies; he will find only that the tent which hid the stars is gone and he can see the heavens, or that the earthly house melts away and he has a palace-home about him, another and loftier word of God clothing upon him. So the man lives by the word of God even in refusing bread God does not give him; instead of dying he rises into a higher life of the same kind.

I have been speaking of the consciousness of existence, not of that higher spiritual life on which all other life depends, and which cannot exist for a moment except from the heart of God. A man who tries to live by bread and not by the word from that heart may think he lives, but he begins to die or is dead. Our Lord says in effect, "I can do without the life that comes of bread; without the life that comes of my Father's word I die indeed." So he does not think twice. That God's will be done is all his care; that done, all will be right with him, whether he thinks of himself or not. The Father does not forget the child so busy trusting him that he does not even pray for himself.

In the higher aspect of this first temptation, that a man cannot feel the things he believes except under conditions of physical well-being that depend on food, the answer is the same: a man lives not by his feelings any more than by bread, but by the Truth, the Word, the Will, the uttered Being of God.

I am ashamed to be forced to write what is only milk for babes. What I must say is that by *the Word of God* I do not mean *The Bible*. The Bible is *a* Word of God, the chief of his written words, because it tells us of The Word, the Christ; but everything God has done and given man to know is a word of his, a will of his, and as such a necessity to man: receiving it is his life. Since God's utterances are a whole, the smallest is essential. But by *the word* of the God who is God because he *speaks* to men, I must understand in the deepest sense every revelation of Himself in the heart and consciousness of man, so that the man knows God is there, nay, here. Even Christ is not The Word of God in the deepest sense *to a man* until he is this Revelation to him, until the Spirit that is the meaning in the Word has come to him, until the speech is not a sound as of thunder but the voice of words; for a word is more than an utterance, it is a sound to be understood. No word is fully a Word *of* God until it is a Word *to* man, until he recognizes God in it; that is what it is spoken for. God's words, like the sands and stars, cannot be numbered, but the end of each is to reveal God. Nor can a man know any of them is God's word except as it comes thus to him. It is *to* him that it may be *in* him, and till it is *in* him he cannot *know* it was *to* him. God must be God *in* man before man can know that he is God, or that he has rightly received any word of his. \[Footnote: No doubt the humble spirit will receive the testimony of everyone he reveres and look in the direction indicated for a word from the Father; but till he receives it in his heart he cannot know what the word spoken of is.\]

If we live by any will of God, any truth in him, we live by it tenfold when it becomes a word to us; his will becomes ours, and so we live by God. But once the word is understood, a man must live by faith in what God is, not by his feelings even about God. When he can no longer *feel* the truth he does not therefore die. He lives because God is true, and knows he lives because, having once understood the word, he knows God is truth. He believes in the God of former vision, and lives by that word when all is dark.

The Enemy's second attempt: "If God is to be so trusted, try him. I would like to see the result. Show yourself his darling. Here is the word for it: angels will have charge of you; not a stone shall hurt you. Take him at his word. Throw yourself down and strike the conviction into me that you are the Son of God. For you know you do not look like what you say you are."

Again the Lord meets him with a written word, not as a logical proof to confute Satan, but as giving even Satan the reason for his conduct. Satan quotes Scripture as verbal authority; our Lord answers with a Scripture by whose truth he regulates his conduct.

This answer holds the same principle as the first: to the Son of God the will of God is Life. It was a temptation to show the powers of the world that he was the Son of God, above the laws of Nature as the Eternal Son, and so stop the raging of the heathen. It would be but to show them the truth. But he was the *Son*: what was his *Father's* will? That was not the divine way of convincing the world of sin, righteousness and judgment. If the Father told him to cast himself down, the pinnacle would point bare to the sky; if the devil threw him down, let God send angels or, if better, let him be dashed to pieces. He will never forestall the divine will. The Father shall order what comes next; the Son will obey. In the path of his work he will turn aside for no stone, and there let angels bear him if need be; but he will not choose the path because there is a stone in it. He will not choose at all. He will go where the Spirit leads.

This throws light on our Lord's saying that with faith and no doubt we could tell a mountain to be cast into the sea and it would be done. Good people, John Bunyan among them, have been tempted to tempt the Lord their God on the strength of it, as Satan did with the Psalm; happily the assurance they called faith generally failed them in time. Faith is that which, knowing the Lord's will, goes and does it, or, not knowing it, stands and waits, content in ignorance as in knowledge because God wills, neither pressing into the hidden future nor careless of knowledge that opens the path of action. Its noblest exercise is to act without certainty of the result when the duty is certain, or even when a course seems very probably duty. \[Footnote: In the latter case a man may be mistaken and his work burned, but by that very fire he will be saved. Nothing saves a man more than the burning of his work, except doing work that can stand the fire.\] To put God to the question in any way other than "What wilt thou have me to do?" is an attempt to compel God to declare himself or hasten his work. This probably was the sin of Judas. It is presumption like making a stone into bread: forcing God to act where he has created no need, or making a case in which he will seem to have forfeited his word if he does not act. The man acts in God's face to see what he will do, instead of from within by the divine will. Man's first business is "What does God want me to do?" not "What will God do if I do so and so?" To tempt a human parent so would be impertinence; to tempt God so is the same vice at its highest, the fruit of a state of mind worse than all the so-called cardinal sins, spiritual pride, which attributes God's love not to man's being and need but to some excellence in the individual that makes the Father love him better than his fellows and smile at his faults. Not thus did the Son of God regard his relation to his Father. The faith that removes mountains is confidence in God that comes from seeking nothing but his will. A man so faithful would die of hunger sooner than say to the stone, *Be bread*, and take the scoffs of the unbelieving without reply sooner than say to the mountain, *Be thou cast into the sea*, even if he knew it would be torn up at the word, unless he first knew God would have it so.

And so I come to how this could be a real temptation to the Son of Man. It would be good to confound his adversaries and force on them the conviction that he was the God-supported messenger he declared himself. Why should he have Adversaries a moment longer, standing between him and the willing hearts that would believe if they could? The answer was plain to our Lord and is plain now: it was not the way of the Father's will. It would not fit the gradual development of life and history by which the Father breeds free, God-loving wills. It would be violent and theatrical, poor in nature and result, not God-like. His history as Son of Man shall be The Man's history; shall it begin with an exception? Yet it might well tempt one who longed to do all he could for men. He was the Son of God: why should the sons of God not know it?

This temptation was an epitome and type of those to come, against which for forty days he had been making himself strong, revolving truth beyond our reach. We shall understand it better by looking at his life. As he refused to make stones bread, he never worked a miracle to help himself; as he refused to cast himself from the temple to convince Satan, he steadily refused the sign the human Satans demanded, though they offered conviction as a bribe. How easy it seems it would have been to confound them and strengthen his followers! But such conviction would stand in the way of a better conviction in his disciples and do his adversaries only harm. Neither could truly be convinced by a show; it could only prove his power. It might prove the presence of a God, but would it prove that God, or bring him nearer to those who could not see him in the face of his Son? To say *Thou art God* without knowing what the *Thou* means is of no use; God is a name only unless we know *God*. Our Lord did not care to be acknowledged so.

On the same principle he would not do, where unbelief prevailed, the very miracles that partly revealed his character to those who had faith. He often avoided cities and crowds and declined mighty works because of unbelief. Apart from the loving help they gave the distressed, revealing him as Redeemer from evil, I doubt he would have worked a single miracle, or expected much from them for onlookers. A mere marvel is soon forgotten, and long before that many have begun to doubt the senses that brought it. Inward sight alone convinces of truth, never signs and wonders, which can convey only a probability that he who shows them knows what he speaks of. The vision of the truth itself, beyond signs and wonders, is the power of God, is salvation. This vision was in the Lord's face and form to the pure in heart, but not in his signs to those who sought them. Yet it is easy to see how the temptation might for a moment work on a mind that longed to begin its labours with the credentials of its truth. How the true heart longs to be received by its brethren and known in its truth! But no. The truth must show itself in God's time, in and by the labour. The kingdom must come in God's holy human way: not by a stroke of grandeur but by years of love, centuries of seeming bafflement, aeons of labour. The Lord *will* be bound by the changeless laws which are the harmony of the Father's being and utterance. He will *be*, not seem; he will be, and thereby, not therefore, seem. Yet even on him the idea of asserting the truth in holy power must have dawned in grandeur. The thought was good; to yield to it would have been the loss of the world, nay, worse: the God of obedience fallen from his throne, and all is blackness.

But let us not forget that the whole is a faint parable, faint beside the grandeur of the reality, as the ring and the shoes are poor types (yet how dear!) of the Father's absolute love for his prodigal children.

Now the third temptation. The first was to help himself in his need; the second, perhaps, to assert the Father; the third, to deliver his brethren.

To deliver them in the way of men, still from the outside. Indeed the whole Temptation may be seen as the contest of the seen and the unseen, the outer and inner, the likely and the true, the show and the reality. And as in the others, the evil in this last lay in its being a temptation to save his brethren instead of doing the Will of his Father.

Could it be other than a temptation to think he might lay a righteous grasp on the reins of government, leap into the chariot of power and ride forth conquering and to conquer? Glad visions rose before him: the prisoner breaking jubilant from the cell of injustice; the widow lifting her bowed head before the devouring Pharisee; weeping children shouting at the sound of the chariot wheels, before which oppression and wrong withered, and behind which the fir-tree sprang instead of the thorn, the myrtle instead of the brier. What glowing dreams of holy vengeance and human blessedness, all from his hand, would crowd such a brain, not like the castles in the air of an aspiring youth, who builds at random because he cannot make them real, but consistent and grand because he knew them within his reach. Could he not mould the people at his will, and, transfigured in snowy garments, call aloud in Jerusalem, "Behold your King"? His nation's fierce warriors would start at the sound, and the nation would rush to a grand, holy war (not a crusade; we should not have had *that* word) against the tyrants of the race who trod on their brethren and would not let them even look to the heavens. Ah! but when were his garments white as snow? Not when he looked to such a conquest, but when, on a mount like this, he spoke of the decease he should *accomplish* at Jerusalem! Why should this be "the sad end of the war"? "You shall worship the Lord your God, and him only shall you serve." Not even your own visions of love and truth, O Saviour of the world, shall guide you to your goal, but the will of your Father in heaven.

But how would he, thus conquering, be a servant of Satan? How would this be a falling down and worshipping (acknowledging the worth of) the lord of misrule and its pain?

I will not inquire whether such an enterprise could be accomplished without the worship of Satan, without the trickery, double-dealing, tact, flattery and finesse of every ambitious leader and self-serving conqueror; on any supposition of our Lord leading his country's armies these are impossible, and such a career cannot be imagined for him. But I ask whether to know better and not do so well is not serving Satan; whether to lead men on in God's name toward the best when the end is not the best is not serving Satan; whether to flatter their pride by making them conquerors of their nation's enemies instead of their own evils is not serving Satan. To desert the mission of the God who knew men could not be set free that way, and sent him to be a man, a true man, the one man among them, so that his life might become theirs and they be as free in prison or on the cross as on a hillside or a throne; to give men over instead to the lie that anything other than spirit and truth is the worship of the Father, anything other than love the fulfilling of the law, anything other than the offering of their best selves the service of God, anything other than obedient harmony with the primal love, truth and law, freedom: would that not have been to fall down and worship the devil? Not all the sovereignty of God, as the theologians call it, delegated to the Son, could have wrought the kingdom of heaven in one corner of the earth. Nothing but the Son's obedience unto death, the absolute *doing* of God's will because it was the truth, could redeem the prisoner, the widow, the orphan; and it would redeem them by redeeming also the conquest-ridden conqueror, the stripe-giving jailer, the unjust judge, the devouring Pharisee himself with his insatiable moth-eaten heart. The earth should be free because Love was stronger than Death. So fierceness and wrong and hypocrisy and God-service should play out their weary play. He would not pluck the branches of the tree but lay the axe to its root. It would take time, but the tree would be dead at last and cast into the lake of fire; his Father had time to spare. It would take courage, strength, self-denial and endurance; his Father could give him all. It would cost pain of body and mind, agony and torture; he was ready to take it on himself. It would cost him many hopeless sights: tears he must not wipe, sighs he must not turn to laughter, the dead he must let lie, Rachel weeping for her children and refusing comfort, his brothers and sisters crying like children over broken toys while he does not mend them; he must go on to the grave and they not know that thus he was setting all things right for them. His work must be one with, and complete, God's Creation and History. The disappointment and sorrow and fear he could and would bear. The will of God should be done. Man should be free, not merely man as he thinks of himself, but man as God thinks of him: the divine idea set free in the divine bosom, the man on earth seeing his angel face to face, growing into the likeness of the divine thought, free not in his own fancy but in the absolute divine fact of being. The great, beautiful and perfect will of God *must* be done.

"Away, Satan! For it is written: You shall worship the Lord your God, and serve him only."

When Peter tried to stop him as he set his face steadily to meet death at Jerusalem, he gave Peter the same kind of answer, calling him Satan too.

"Then the devil left him, and angels came and ministered to him."

So says St Matthew. They brought him the food he had waited for, walking in the strength of the word. He would have died if it had not come now.

"And when the devil had ended all the temptation, he departed from him for a season," says St Luke.

Then Satan ventured once more. When?

Was it then, in the agony of the last faint, when the Lord cried out, "Why hast thou forsaken me?" when, having done the great work and laid it aside clean and pure as the linen cloth ready to enfold him, another cloud than that on the mount overshadowed his soul, and out of it came a voiceless persuasion that after all was done God did not care for his work or for him?

Even in those words the adversary was foiled, and for ever. For when he seemed to be forsaken, his cry was still, *My God! my God!*

### THE ELOI

*The passage:* "My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?"

I would not dare approach this most awful utterance ever shaped by human breath, if I did not feel that, holding both germ and blossom of final devotion, it holds the deepest practical lesson the heart has to learn. The Lord, the Revealer, hides nothing that can be revealed, and does not warn off the foot that treads in naked humility on the ground of his terrible conflict with Evil, when the smoke of battle rose between him and his Father and, for one terrible moment before he broke the bonds of life and went weary and triumphant to his Father's arms, hid God from his Son's eyes. He lets us meditate the one thought that slew him at last, when he fled to the Father to know that he loved him: Satan had come once more with a last temptation, to tell him that though he had done his part, God had forgotten his; that though he had lived by the word of God's mouth, that mouth had nothing more to say to him; that though he had refused to tempt God, God had left him to be tempted beyond bearing; that though he had worshipped none other, God did not care. The Lord hides nothing of his sacred sufferings, for truth is light and would be light in men's minds. Let us draw near and kiss those feet that bear for ever the scars of our victory; in them we clasp the safety of our suffering, sinning brotherhood.

We should approach the sufferings of our Lord with the holiest fear, and not think they were less because he was more. The more delicate the nature, the more alive to all that is lovely, true and right, the more it feels the antagonism of pain and the inroad of death on life, that breach in the harmony of things whose sound is torture. He felt more than man could feel, and was worn out sooner. His sufferings were awful when they began to invade the region about the will, when the struggle to keep consciously trusting God sank into darkness, and the Will of The Man put forth its last effort in that cry after the vanishing Father: *My God, my God, why hast thou forsaken me?* Never before had he been unable to see God beside him. Yet never was God nearer, for never was Jesus more divine. He could not see or feel him near, and still it is "*My* God" he cries.

So the Will of Jesus, just when his faith seems about to yield, finally triumphs. With no *feeling* to support it and no beatific vision to absorb it, it stands naked and tortured in his soul as he stood scourged before Pilate, and, pure and simple and surrounded by fire, declares for God. The sacrifice ascends in the cry *My God*. It came not out of happiness, peace, hope or even suffering; it was a cry *in* desolation, but out of Faith, the last voice of Truth, speaking when it can only cry. The horror of that moment is unfathomable; it was blackness of darkness. Yet he would believe and hold fast: God was his God still. In the cry came the Victory, and all was soon over. Of the peace that followed, the peace of a perfect soul, large as the universe, pure as light, ardent as life, victorious for God and his brethren, he alone can know the breadth and length and depth and height.

Without this last trial, the Master's temptations would not have filled the human cup; there would be a region we must pass through where we might call on our Captain-Brother and find no voice or hearing; he would have avoided the fatal spot! The desert temptations came to a young, strong man with his road before him and God around him, and drew their force from the exuberance of his conscious faith. "Dare and do, for God is with you," said the devil. "I know it, and therefore I will wait," returned the king of his brothers. Now, after three years of divine action, with his course run, his frame tortured until the ruling brain falls whirling down the gulf of fainting, and friends fled, the enemy comes again: "Despair and die, for God is not with you. Death, not Life, is your refuge. He never was with you. He was the God of Abraham, and Abraham is dead. Whom do you make yourself?" "My God, my God, why hast thou forsaken me?" the Master cries. For God was his God still, though he had forsaken him: forsaken *his vision* that his faith might glow out triumphant; forsaken *himself*? no; come nearer than ever, even as (with a deeper import) the Lord withdrew from his friends' bodily eyes to dwell in their profoundest being.

I do not think our Lord's deepest trial was in the garden, when he prayed that the cup might pass and then that the Father's will be done. For that will was then present with him, and he lived and acted in it. Now the foreseen horror has come; he drinks the dread cup and the Will has vanished from his eyes. Were it visible in his suffering, his will could bow with tearful gladness under its grandeur. But now his will is left alone to drink the cup of The Will in torture. In the sickness of this agony it rises perfect at last, unsupported, a naked consciousness of misery hung in the waste darkness of the universe, and declares for God in defiance of pain, death, apathy, self, negation and the blackness around it, calling aloud on the vanished God.

This is the Faith of the Son of God: God withdrew, as it were, so that the Son's perfect Will might go forth to find the Father's Will.

Is it possible that even then he thought of the lost sheep who could not believe God was their Father, and cried for them the word they might say, knowing for them that *God* means *Father* and more, and knowing now as never before the fearfulness of being without God and without hope? I dare not answer.

But what has this Alpine apex of faith to do with creatures who call themselves Christians, creeping in the valleys, hardly aware of the mountains above except to stumble over the pebbles washed down by glacier streams? I will tell you. We remain creeping Christians because we look at ourselves and not at Christ, gazing at the marks of our own soiled feet instead of up at the snows of purity where Christ's soul climbed. Each of us, putting his foot in the Master's footprint and so defacing it, turns to see how far his neighbour's matches what he still calls the Master's, though it is only his own. Or, having committed a petty fault, such as only a petty creature could, we mourn the defilement to ourselves and the shame before friends, children or servants, instead of hurrying to confess and make amends to our fellow and then, forgetting our paltry self and its disgrace, lifting our eyes to the glory that alone will quicken the true man in us and kill the peddling creature we wrongly call our *self*. The true self is that which can look Jesus in the face and say *My Lord*.

When the inward sun shines and the wind of thought stirs glad feelings among the flowers of fancy, it is easy to look up and say *My God*. It is easy, when outward failure has braced the mental nerves to fresh effort, to turn to God and trust him, since honest exertion gives an ability as well as a right to trust. It is easy in pain, short of certain bounds, to hope in God for deliverance or pray for strength. But what is to be done when all feeling is gone, when a man does not know whether he believes or loves, when art, poetry and religion are nothing to him, so swallowed up is he in pain, depression, disappointment or temptation? It seems to him then that God does not care for him, and certainly he does not care for God. If still humble, he thinks he is so bad that God cannot care for him, and believes for the time that God loves us only because and while we love him, rather than always, because he is our God and we live only by his love. Or he does not believe in a God at all, which is better.

So long as we have nothing to do with God except in the sunshine of the mind, when we feel him near, we are poor creatures, willed upon, not willing: flowering reeds, perhaps, pleasant to behold, but blown by the wind; not bad, but poor.

And how do we generally act then? Do we sit mourning the loss of our feelings, or make frantic efforts to rouse them? Or, ten times worse, relapse into temporary atheism and yield to the temptation? Or, being heartless, stay careless, conscious only of evil thoughts and low feelings but too lazy to rouse ourselves against them? We know we must be rid of them some day, but meantime, never mind; we do not *feel* them bad, or anything else good; we are asleep and know it, and cannot be troubled to wake.

God does not, by instant gift of his Spirit, make us always feel right, desire good and aspire after him. So either he will not or he cannot. If he will not, it must be because it would not be well. If he cannot, he would not if he could; else a better condition than God's is conceivable to God's own mind, one in which he could save his creatures better than he can. The truth is this: he wants to make us in his own image, *choosing the good* and *refusing* the evil. How could he do that if he were *always* moving us from within, as he does at divine intervals, towards holiness? God gives us room *to be*; he "stands away from us" that we may act from ourselves and exercise the pure will for good. I do not mean that we can do anything of ourselves without God. If we choose the right at last, it is all God's doing, and only the more his for being ours, in a far more marvellous way than if he had kept us so full of holy impulses that we never had to choose. For to this point he has been educating and leading us, that we may choose him and his will and so be tenfold more his children, of his own best making, in the freedom of a will found our own first in its loving sacrifice to him, than in the most ecstatic worship flowing from divine impulse without this *willing* sacrifice. God made our individuality as well as, and a greater marvel than, our dependence: made our *apartness* from himself, that freedom might bind us dearer to him with a new and inscrutable marvel of love. The Godhead is still the making root of our individuality, and the freer the man, the stronger his bond to the one who made his freedom. He made our wills and is striving to make them free, for only in the perfection of our individuality and the freedom of our wills can we be altogether his children. This is full of mystery, but can we not see enough in it to be very glad and peaceful?

Only in an act that declares for the Truth, for God, in spite of impulse or weakness, does the will spring into absolute freedom and true life.

See, then, what lies within our reach whenever we are lapt in the folds of night. The highest condition of the human will is attainable. I do not say the highest condition of the Human Being, which surely lies in the Beatific Vision, the sight of God; but the highest condition of the Human Will, as distinct, not separated, from God, is when, not seeing God or seeming to grasp him at all, it yet holds him fast. It cannot continue so, for the man would die not finding God; but with the will thus asserting itself he has passed from death into life, and the vision is near. Then first, free in asserting its freedom, the individual will is one with the Will of God; the child is restored to the father; the brotherhood of the race rises from the dust; and our Lord's prayer is answered, "I in them and thou in me, that they may be made perfect in one." Let us then arise in God-born strength whenever we feel the darkness closing, or find it closed around us, and say, "I am of the Light and not of the Darkness."

Troubled soul, you are not bound to feel, but you are bound to arise. God loves you whether you feel or not. You cannot love when you will, but you are bound to fight the hatred in you to the last. Do not try to feel good when you are not good, but cry to Him who is good. He does not change because you change. He has an especial tenderness for you because you are in the dark, and his heart is glad when you arise and say, "I will go to my Father." For he sees you through all the gloom through which you cannot see him. Will his will. Say to him: "My God, I am very dull and low and hard; but you are wise and high and tender, and you are my God. I am your child. Forsake me not." Then fold the arms of your faith and wait quietly until light goes up in your darkness. Fold the arms of your Faith, but not of your Action: think of something you ought to do, and do it, if it be only sweeping a room, preparing a meal, or visiting a friend. Heed not your feelings: do your work.

As God lives by his own will, and we in him, so he has given us power to will in ourselves. How much better we should fare if, finding our heads bowed away from the good and no inclination to seek the source of our life, we still *willed* upwards toward God, rousing the life he has given us from his own heart to call on him who can fill the emptiest heart, rouse the deadest conscience, quicken the dullest feeling and strengthen the feeblest will!

Then, if ever the time should come, as perhaps it must for each of us, when all sense of well-being has vanished, the earth is a sterile promontory and the heavens a dull and pestilent congregation of vapours, neither man nor woman delights us, God himself is but a name and Jesus an old story, and a Death far worse than "that phantom of grisly bone" grips our hearts and, having slain love, hope and faith, forces existence on us only in agony, then, even then, we shall be able to cry out with our Lord, "My God, my God, why hast thou forsaken me?" Nor shall we die then, I think, without taking up his last words as well: "*Father, into thy hands I commend my spirit.*"

### THE HANDS OF THE FATHER

*The passage:* "Father, into your hands I commend my spirit."

Neither St Matthew nor St Mark records any words of our Lord after the *Eloi*. With St Luke they tell of a loud cry and the giving up of the ghost; St Luke alone puts between them "Father, into your hands I commend my spirit," and he omits the Eloi. St John records neither, nor the loud cry; only that after the vinegar Jesus said "*It is finished*," bowed his head and gave up the ghost.

Will the Lord ever tell us why he cried so? Relief at the touch of death? Victory? Gladness that he had endured to the end? Or did the Father look on him in answer to his *My God*, and the blessedness make him cry aloud because he could not smile? Or was it the last wrench of pain before repose? Perhaps all in one. No books or words of thinking men could express as much as lay unspoken in that cry. Now he had made his Father Lord not by the might of making and loving alone, but by right of devotion and deed of love. Now inward sonship and glad sacrifice should be born in men's hearts, for the divine obedience was perfected by suffering. He had been among his brethren what he would have them be, doing for them what he would have them do for God and each other. God was henceforth inside and beneath them as well as around and above, suffering with and for them, giving them all he had, his very life-being, what he best loved and best was. And the mighty story ends with a cry.

So the cry meant *It is finished* and *Father, into your hands I commend my spirit*. Every highest human act gives back to God what he first gave us: "You gave me this; here is your gift; I send my spirit home." Every act of worship holds up to God what he has made us: "Look, Lord, feel with me in this bounty of yours, my being. I am your child and can thank you only by lifting up the overflow of your life and crying, It is yours; it is mine. I am yours, and therefore I am mine." The vast operations of the spiritual and physical worlds alike are a turning again to the source.

Commending his spirit at the close of his life only summed up what our Lord had done all his life. He had offered himself all the years, and so had lived the divine life. Every morning when he went out before day, every evening when he lingered on the night-lapt mountain after his friends had gone, he offered himself to his Father in loving words, high thoughts and speechless feelings; and between times he did the same in deed, toward his fellows, in loving word, helping thought and healing action. The way to worship God while daylight lasts is to work; the only "divine service" is helping our fellows.

I do not offer this commending of our spirits as a duty, which would turn our highest privilege into a burden. I want to show it is the simplest, blessedest thing in the human world.

The Human Being may say: "Am I going to sleep, to be helpless and thoughtless, perhaps weak of will and scant of conscience in my dreams? Father, into your hands I commend my spirit; I give myself back to you; refresh me, make me over again. Am I going into the day's turmoil, with its temptations to be less honourable, faithful, kind or diligent than the Ideal Man would have me? Father, into your hands. Am I about to do a good deed? Of all times then, Father, into your hands, lest the enemy have me now. Am I about to do a hard duty, refusing a friend's request or urging a neighbour's conscience? Father, into your hands. Am I in pain, with illness shutting out the glad visions of a healthy brain and bringing troubled, untrue ones? Take my spirit, Lord, and see that it has no more to bear than it can bear. Am I going to die? You know from your Son's cry how terrible that is, and if it does not come to me as terribly as to him, think how poor I am to bear it beside him. I do not know what the struggle means, for of the thousands who pass through it daily not one enlightens his neighbour left behind; but shall I not long with agony for one breath of your air, and not receive it? Shall I not be torn asunder with dying? I will question no more: Father, into your hands I commend my spirit. It is your business, not mine. You will know every shade of my suffering and care for me with your perfect fatherhood, which makes my sonship and wraps it round. As a child I could bear great pain with my father's arm about me; how much nearer to my soul can your hands come, with a comfort I have never imagined, for how shall my imagination overtake your swift heart? I care not for the pain so long as my spirit is strong, and into your hands I commend that spirit. If your love, which is better than life, receives it, surely your tenderness will make it great."

So may the Human Being speak to himself.

Think, brothers and sisters: we walk in the air of an eternal fatherhood. Every uplifting of the heart is a looking up to The Father, and graciousness and truth are around, above, beneath and *in* us. When we are least worthy, most tempted, hardest and unkindest, let us still commend our spirits into his hands; where else dare we send them? How an earthly father would love a child who crept into his room with an angry, troubled face and sat at his feet, saying, "I feel so naughty, papa, and I want to get good"! Would he say, "Go away, be good, and then come to me"? Shall we think God would send us away if we came so, or be displeased we came, even angry as Jonah? If we, being evil, know how to give good gifts to our children, will God not give us his Spirit when we ask? Will no heavenly dew fall cool on the hot anger, no raindrop on the dry selfishness, no sunlight on the cloudy hopelessness? Bread at least will be given, not a stone; water, not vinegar mingled with gall.

Nor is there anything we can ask for ourselves that we may not ask for another. We may commend any brother or sister to the common fatherhood, and there will be moments when, filled with the Lord's spirit, nothing will ease our hearts of their love but commending all our brothers and sisters to the one Father. We shall never know the rest in the Father's hands, the rest of the Holy Sepulchre, which the Lord knew when the agony of death was over and he entered the regions where there is only life, and so all that is not music is silence (for all noise comes of the conflict of Life and Death), until the fatherhood is revealed to us in the love of the brothers. He cannot be our father except as he is theirs, and if we do not see him as their father we cannot know him as ours. We shall never know him aright until we rejoice for our race that he is *the* Father. If we do not love our brother whom we have seen, how can we love God whom we have not seen? To rest at last even in those hands into which the Lord commended his spirit, we must already have learned to love our neighbour as ourselves.

### LOVE THY NEIGHBOUR

*The passage:* You shall love your neighbour as yourself.

Our Lord quotes God's words to Moses in Leviticus: do not avenge or hold a grudge against your people, but love your neighbour as yourself. He never thought of being original; the older the saying, the better, if it utters the truth he wants to utter. In him it becomes fact: the *Word* was made *flesh*, and his words are no more words but spirit and life.

St Paul twice quotes these words, and St James once, always presenting love as the fulfilling of the law.

Is the converse true: is the fulfilling of the law love? Paul says love works no ill to its neighbour, so love is the fulfilling of the law. But does *working no ill* make love? Love will fulfil the law; the law will not fulfil love. A man who keeps the law is a lover of his neighbour, but he keeps the law because he loves, not the reverse. No heart will be content with the law for love.

"But at least the law can fulfil itself, though it does not reach love."

I do not believe it. The law cannot be kept toward a neighbour unless he is loved. It is infinite, reaching to such delicacies of action that the man who tries hardest is most aware of defeat. We are made for love, not law. Love is law because it is infinitely more: of a higher region, and the creator of law. Without love not one *shall-not* would have been spoken. Once spoken, they appear as justice, even in lower forms like prudence and self-preservation, but love spoke them first. Without love, what sense of justice could we have? Each would be full of his own wants, tearing things to himself. I do not say *conscious* love breeds justice, but without love in our nature justice would never be born; a sense only of *our own* rights is not justice. Even poor, withered forms of love, far below justice now, are of speechless worth, for they will grow into what will supersede justice because it will necessitate it.

Of what use then is the law? To lead us to Christ, the Truth: to waken a sense of what our deepest nature, God's presence *in* us, requires, and to show us by failure that our purest effort of will cannot lift us even to abstaining from wrong to our neighbour. Who that does not love his neighbour can be sure never to bear false witness against him by word, look, tone, gesture or silence? Who can judge his neighbour aright but the one whose love makes him refuse to judge? So we are told to love, not judge: love is the only justice we are capable of, and perfected it comprises all justice. To refuse our neighbour love is the greatest wrong we can do him, but of that afterwards. To fulfil even the commonest law we must rise to a region above law, which is spirit and life and makes the law: to keep the law toward our neighbour we must love him. We are made not for law but for grace, or faith, another much-misused word; made on too large a scale for any pure relation to mere justice, if that exists, for it is an abstract idea that will not stay abstracted. The law comes to make us long for the divine condition in which love is all, for God is Love.

The fulfilling of the law is the practical form love takes, and neglecting it proves lovelessness; it is how a man's *will* must at once begin to be love. But the story of the good Samaritan shows that what our Lord meant by love of neighbour is not fulfilling the law toward him but the condition of being that results in fulfilling the law and more. "Who is my neighbour?" said the lawyer. The Lord taught him that everyone to whom he could be or for whom he could do anything was his neighbour; each of the race who comes within touch of one tentacle of our nature is. Which prohibition of the law does the tale illustrate? None. The love that is more than law, and makes breach of it impossible, lives in this endless story, coming out in active kindness, in recognition of kin, of *kind*, of nighness, of *neighbourhood*, in tenderness and loving-kindness: the Samaritan heart akin to the Jewish, Samaritan hands neighbours to Jewish wounds.

You shall love your neighbour as yourself.

So direct and complete is this parable that one is almost ashamed to say more. If a man in the company had put our question, "But I may keep the law and yet not love my neighbour," would the Lord not have answered, "Keep the law in the spirit, in the truth of action, and you will soon find, Jew, that you love your Samaritan"? Yet he wants us to follow our questions when they rise. He will not check us with a scornful word of heavenly wisdom; he knows even his words will not fit every question of the willing soul, and his spirit will reply to what remains. Not every man finds his neighbour in need of help, and he would gladly hasten the slow results of opportunity by true thinking. So we should be ready for further teaching from the Spirit who is the Lord.

"But how," says a man who is willing to recognize universal neighbourhood but cannot even fulfil the bare law toward the woman he loves best, "am I to rise into that empyrean of love?" Trying at once to love his neighbour, he finds the empyrean no more reachable in itself than the law was. As he could not keep the law without first rising into love of neighbour, so he cannot love his neighbour without first rising higher still. The whole system of the universe works on this law, the driving of things upward toward the centre. No immediate exercise of will can make a man love his neighbour. Only the man fulfilled of God, from whom he came and by whom he is, can love as himself the neighbour who also came from God. The mystery of individuality and the relation that follows is deep as the beginnings of humanity, and can be solved only by one who has practically solved the holy necessities of his origin. In God alone can man meet man; in him alone the converging lines of existence touch and do not cross. When the life of Christ, the Head, courses through the atom that the man is of the slowly revivifying body, then the love of the brothers is there as conscious life. From Christ through the neighbours comes the life that makes him part of the body.

It *is* possible to love our neighbour as ourselves. Our Lord *never* spoke hyperbolically, though many unconsciously treat his words so, to persuade themselves they believe them. We may see it is possible before we attain it, for our perception of truth is always ahead of our condition; no man can see it perfectly until he is it, but we must see it that we may be it. A man who knows he does not yet love his neighbour as himself may believe in such a condition, and see that nothing else is the goal of human perfection or the end to which the universe is speeding, propelled by the Father's will. Let him labour on and not faint at the thought that God's day is a thousand years: his millennium is also one day, this day, for we have him, The Love, in us, working even now the far end.

Though only when a man loves God with all his heart will he love his neighbour as himself, there are mingled processes along the way. Suppose a man who believes our Lord meant what he said sets out to obey. He thinks of his neighbours generally and tries to feel love for them, and at once they sort themselves into classes. With some there is no difficulty, since he already loves them: not because they *are* but because by friendly qualities, by showing themselves lovable, that is, loving, they have moved his feelings as wind moves water, without any action of his own. He feels this is nothing much to the point, though he would be farther from the goal if he had none such to love. He recalls the Lord's words, "If you love those who love you, what reward have you?" and fixes on someone of a second class. This man is no enemy (we have not reached that class yet) but dull, uninteresting, unlovable in a negative way. With all his effort the goal is as far off as ever.

Naturally, in his failure, the question arises, "Is it my duty to love him who is unlovable?" Certainly not, if he is unlovable. But that begs the question.

So the man falls back on the primary foundation of things and asks, "How, then, is the man to be loved by me? Why should I love my neighbour as myself?"

We must not answer "Because the Lord says so." It is because the Lord says so that the man is looking for help to obey; no man can love his neighbour merely because the Lord says so. The Lord says so because it is right, necessary and natural, and the man wants to feel it so. Though the Lord would be pleased with a man for doing a thing because he said it, he would show his pleasure by making him ever more dissatisfied until he knew why, for no one can obey a command in the deepest sense until he sees its reasonableness. I do not say the man should put off obeying until he sees it; that is outside my present supposition. It is beautiful to obey the rightful source of a command, more beautiful to worship the radiant source of our light, and for the sake of obedient vision the Lord commands us. Then our heart meets his: we see God.

Let me represent as a conversation what might pass in the man's mind on the two sides of the question. "Why should I love my neighbour?"

"He is the same as I, and therefore I ought to love him."

"Why? I am I. He is he."

"He has the same thoughts, feelings, hopes, sorrows and joys as I."

"Yes, but why should I love him for that? He must mind his; I can only deal with mine."

"He has the same consciousness as I. As things look to me, so they look to him."

"Yes, but I cannot get into his consciousness, nor he into mine. I feel myself, not him. My life flows through my veins, not his. The world shines into my consciousness, not his. I wish I could love him but do not see why. I am an individual; he is an individual. My self must be closer to me than he can be; two bodies keep me apart from his self. I am isolated with myself."

Here lies the mistake. The thinker supposes a duality in himself that does not exist, and so judges individuality a separation. On the contrary, it is the sole possibility and very bond of love; *otherness* is the essential ground of affection. But in spiritual things such a unity is presupposed that wherever something that ought to be there does not exist, its empty place looks like a separating gulf. The negative looks positive, and where a man does not love, not-loving seems rational. No one loves because he sees why, but because he loves; no human reason can be given for the highest necessity of divinely created existence, since reasons run from above downwards. A man must just feel this necessity, and then questioning is over; it justifies itself. He who has not felt it has nothing to argue about, only a phantom he made in a vain effort to understand. Love cannot be argued about in its absence, for nothing near enough resembles it to admit treatment by the algebra of reason or imagination, and the very talking raises a mist between the mind and the vision. But let a man once love, and all the difficulties that seemed opposed to love become so many arguments for loving.

Let a man find another fallen among thieves and be a neighbour to him, pouring oil and wine into his wounds, binding them, setting him on his own beast and paying at the inn; let him do it merely from duty, with his Jewish superiority undiminished. Such is the virtue of obeying an eternal truth even in poor measure, of putting into actuality what he has not seen in theory, of doing the truth without believing it, that even if the truth gives no glimmer after the deed he will be ages nearer it than before, for he will go on loving that Samaritan neighbour a little more than his Jewish dignity justifies, and will not question the reasonableness of it. How much more, if he is a man who would love his neighbour if he could, will the higher condition be found, unsought, in the action! For man is a whole, and as soon as he *unites himself* by obedient action, the truth in him shines from the new whole. His action is his response to his maker's design, his own part in creating himself, his yielding to the All in all, to whose harmonious life his whole being then lies open. When will begins to aspire it soon finds that action must precede feeling, so that the man may know the foundation of feeling itself.

For those who recognize no authority as ground for tentative action, a doubt, a suspicion of truth ought to be ground enough for putting it to the test.

The whole system of divine education about the relation of man and man aims at a man's loving his neighbour as himself. It cannot be learned by itself or shown by argument, any more than the difference between right and wrong can be defined in other terms. "But that difference," it may be objected, "is self-evident to every mind, whereas loving one's neighbour is *not* seen as a primary truth; most who hope for eternal blessedness through him who taught it do not really believe it, but think the paramount obligation is to take care of oneself even at risk of forgetting one's neighbour."

But the race has got as far as recognizing right and wrong, so most men are born able to distinguish them. It has not lived long enough for its latest offspring to be born perceiving the truth of love to the neighbour. The individual sees it only after long submission to the education of life; and once seen, it is believed.

The whole constitution of human society exists to teach the two truths by which man lives, Love to God and Love to Man. Of the parental relation, which belongs to the first truth, I say only that we come into the world as we do to look up to the love over us and see in it a symbol, poor yet the best we can have, of the divine love. \[Footnote: It might be put more deeply by saying that God made human affairs after his own thoughts, so they are the best teachers of love to him and to our neighbour; that is nobler and truer than seeing them as a scheme invented by the divine intellect.\] Thousands more would find it easy to love God if they had not such miserable types of him in self-seeking, impulse-driven, purposeless, faithless beings as their only father and mother, to whom their children are no dearer than her litter to the unthinking dam.
