What I want to speak of, concerning the second great commandment, is brotherhood and sisterhood. Why am I given a brother of the same father and mother, to see his helpless infancy, grow up on the same nurture, share sunsets, bed and games, quarrel and vow revenge and endless enmity, and then, unable to resist the brotherhood within, go arm in arm and forget it within the hour? Is it not that Love may grow lord of all between us, so that I feel toward him what has no words, a love in which the divine self rushes out in utter self-forgetfulness to live in contemplating the brother, stronger than death, glad, proud and satisfied? If love stops there it is ruined and the brotherhood lost: he who does not love his brother for deeper reasons than common parentage will cease to love him at all. Love that does not keep enlarging its borders, spreading, including and deepening, will shrivel and die. I have had my mother's sons that I may learn universal brotherhood. Between me and the most wretched liar who ever died for a murder he would not confess there is a bond infinitely closer than one father and mother: that we are sons and daughters of God, born from his heart, the outcoming offspring of his love, a bond closer than all other bonds in one. No man ever loved his own child aright who did not love him for his humanity, his divinity, forgetting his origin from himself. My mother's son is my brother by this greater bond too, but if I recognize it at all I recognize it for my race. The greater excludes not the less, thank God; it makes the weaker bonds stronger and truer, and does not forbid that, where all are brothers, some should be those of our bosom. My brother according to the flesh is my first neighbour, so that while our hearts are tender we are very nigh whether we will or no, and may learn *brotherhood*; our love to each other is the throbbing of the great brotherhood's heart and could come only from the eternal Father, not our parents. My second neighbour is anyone I come in contact with, anyone with whom I have any human dealings: not only the man I dine with, the friend I share thoughts with, the man whose misery my compassion would lift, but the tailor, the printer, the cabman, the beggar to whom, for brotherhood's sake, I may have to refuse, even the man who condescends to me. With each there is a chance to do a neighbour's part, if only by speaking truly, acting justly and thinking kindly, and these deeds help toward the love born of righteousness. All true action clears the springs of right feeling and lets their waters rise. A man must not choose his neighbour but take the one God sends; in him, whoever he is, lies hidden or revealed a beautiful brother. The neighbour is just the man next to you at the moment, whom any business has brought you in contact with.

Thus love will spread in wider and stronger pulses until the whole race is sacredly lovely to the man. Drink-debased, vice-defeatured, pride-puffed, wealth-swollen, vanity-smeared, they will still be brothers, sisters, God-born neighbours; any rough-hewn semblance of humanity will in time move him to reverence and affection. Some find this harder, their first impulse being to repel rather than receive, but learn they may and must. Even they may grow until an unknown face awakens a yearning of affection that rises to pain because it has no expression, and they can only give the man to God and be still.

And now come in all the arguments out of which the man vainly tried to build a stair to the heights of love. "Ah brother, you have a soul like mine," he will say. "Out of your eyes you look, and sights, sounds and odours visit your soul as mine, with wonder and tender comforting. You too love the faces of your neighbours; you are oppressed with sorrows and uplifted with joys. Perhaps you do not know as well as I that gladness surrounds your grief, light your darkness, peace your tumult. I will love you. I cannot come very near you; I will love you the more. Perhaps you do not love your neighbour but think only how to get from him; how lonely you must be, shut in a poverty-stricken room with the bare walls of your selfishness and the hard couch of your dissatisfaction! I will love you the more. You shall not be alone with yourself. You are not me; you are another life, a second self; therefore I can, may, and will love you."

When the human face is to a man the human face divine, and his neighbour's hand a brother's hand, he will understand what St Paul meant in wishing himself accursed from Christ for his brethren. He will no longer understand those who, far from feeling love of neighbour essential to their being, expect to be freed from its law in the world to come, confining it for God's glory to the circle of their heaven and regarding hell from the battlements of safety: "Hark! their moans. But do not weep, for they are our neighbours no more." Paul would be wretched before God's throne if he thought one man beyond mercy, as much for God's glory as for the man's sake. And what of the man Christ Jesus? Who that loves his brother would not, upheld by Christ's love and a dim hope of help in the far-off time, rise from the company of the blessed and go down into despair to sit with the last, the only unredeemed, the Judas of his race, and be more blessed in the pains of hell than in the glories of heaven? Who, among the golden harps and white wings, knowing that one miserable brother from the old-world time when men were taught to love their neighbour as themselves was howling unheeded far below, would not feel he had no choice but to gird his loins and go down, awful as it was, into the smoke and darkness and fire, travelling the weary road to the far country to find his brother? Who, that had the mind of Christ and the love of the Father?

But it is a wild question. God is, and shall be, All in all. Father of our brothers and sisters, you will not be less glorious than we, taught of Christ, can think you. When you go into the wilderness to seek, you will not come home until you have found. It is because we do not hope for them in you, not knowing you or your love, that we are so hard and heartless to the brothers and sisters you have given us.

One word more: love of our neighbour is the only door out of the dungeon of self, where we mope and mow, striking sparks, rubbing phosphorescences from the walls and blowing our own breath in our own nostrils, instead of coming out to the sunlight of God and the sweet winds of the universe. A man thinks his consciousness is himself, but his life consists in the inbreathing of God and the consciousness of the universe of truth. To have, know and enjoy himself he calls life, whereas if he would forget himself his life in God and his neighbours would be tenfold. The region of man's life is spiritual; God, his friends, neighbours and brothers are the wide world in which alone his spirit has room. Himself is his dungeon. If he does not feel it now he will one day, as a living soul would feel being imprisoned in a dead body, wrapped in sevenfold cerements and buried in a stone-ribbed vault. His life is not in knowing that he lives but in loving all forms of life. He is made for the All, for God, who is the All, is his life; his delights, like those of the Ideal Wisdom, are with the sons of men, and his health is in the body of which the Son of Man is the head. The whole region of life is open to him; nay, he must live in it or perish.

Nor will a man thus lose the consciousness of well-being. Far deeper and more complete, God and his neighbour will flash it back on him, pure as life. No more will he agonize "with sick assay" to generate it in the light of his own decadence. He shall know the glory of his own being in the light of God and his brother.

But he may have begun to love his neighbour hoping soon to love him as himself, and yet start back affrighted at another word of our Lord, seeming a harder law, though it is no other, for without obeying it the former cannot be attained. He has not yet learned to love his neighbour as himself whose heart sinks at the word, *I say unto you, Love your enemies*.

### LOVE THINE ENEMY

*The passage:* You have heard it said, Love your neighbour and hate your enemy; but I say, love your enemies, bless those who curse you, do good to those who hate you, and pray for those who abuse and persecute you, that you may be children of your Father in heaven, who makes his sun rise on the evil and the good and sends rain on the just and the unjust. If you love only those who love you, or greet only your brethren, what more do you do than the publicans? Be perfect, therefore, as your Father in heaven is perfect.

Is this not too much to expect? A man may come to do good to those who hate him, but when will he pray for those who abuse and persecute him? When he is a child of his Father in heaven. Then he will love his neighbour as himself even if the neighbour is his enemy; in the Leviticus passage our Lord and his apostles quote, neighbour and enemy are one: do not avenge or bear a grudge against your people, but love your neighbour as yourself, for I am the Lord.

See how gloriously Jesus interprets the earlier scripture: "I am the Lord" becomes "that you may be perfect, as your Father in heaven is perfect."

Is it reasonable to love our enemies? God does, so it is the highest reason. Is it reasonable to expect man to become capable of it? Yes, on one ground: the divine energy is at work in man to make his doing divine, as his nature is. For this our Lord prayed that all may be one, as the Father is in him and he in the Father, that they may be one in us. Nothing could be less likely to human judgment, yet he knows it will one day come.

Why should we love our enemies? The deepest reason cannot be put in words, for it lies in the absolute reality of their being, where they are of one nature with us, the divine nature; we see into it only as into a dark abyss. But we can sketch its form if we let our hearts' thoughts move over the face of the deep.

"Are our enemies men like ourselves?" Yes, but not by virtue of their enmity, their wrongs, cruelty, heartlessness, injustice or misrepresentation. *Humanum est errare* is a truism with a germ of truth: *errare* shows that there is a way so truly human that to leave it is to *wander*, and the wandering is not the humanity. The words *humane* and *humanity* point to a shadow of that loving-kindness which, perfected in the divine fashion, will include even our enemies. We do not call human sacrifice, torturing captives or cannibalism humanity; not by such deeds are they men. Their humanity lies deeper, in the divine essence in them, and that pure essential humanity, deeper than the region of hate and independent of it, is what we are to love. It originates the claim of neighbourhood; neighbourhood only decides the occasion for it. Is it in every enemy? Else there would be nothing to love. Then we *must* love it, come between us and it what may.

But how can we love someone cruel and unjust to us, who sears with contempt or cuts off with wrong every tendril we put out; who is mean, carping, uncertain, self-righteous, self-seeking and self-admiring; who can even sneer, the most inhuman of human faults, far worse in essence than murder?

These things cannot be loved; the best man hates them most, and the worst cannot love them. But are these the man? Is there not within each a divine element of brotherhood and sisterhood, something lovely and lovable, perhaps slowly dying under the heat of vile passions or the colder fear of selfishness, but there? Shall that divine something, which once awakened will loathe these unlovely things tenfold more than we do, have no recognition from us? The very presence of this fading humanity is what makes it possible for us to hate; had an animal hurt us we should only kill. We hate the man because we are prevented from loving him; we push over the verge of creation, *we damn*, because we cannot embrace, and embracing is the necessity of our deepest being. Instead of telling ourselves that there is our enchained brother, our disfigured sister, captive of the devil, whom our love would sooner free, we recoil into hate that fixes them there, sacrificing their lovable reality to the outer falsehood of Satan's incantations, murdering them to get rid of them. Yet within the most obnoxious lies that which, if it could show itself as it will one day, would compel our devotion. We are told to love not the unlovely but the brother or sister who is unlovely. Shall we leave our brother to his desolate fate? Shall we not say, "With my love at least you shall be compassed about, since you have not your own lovingness to wrap you; and when yours comes out to meet mine we shall be one in the indwelling God"?

Let no one say I speak only in a figure. I do; but many things we see most vividly are more truly expressed by a right figure than by clear logical outline. My figure means a truth.

If anyone says, "Don't make vague distinctions. There is the person; can you deny he is unlovely? How can you love him?" I answer: with the evil cast out he will be yet more the *person*, his real self. What you now dislike is separable from him, is not he; it makes him less himself, working death in him until he risks ceasing to be a person at all. In his right mind he will be a person indeed, and you could not go on hating him. Begin to love him now and help him into the loveliness that is his. His personality, though clouded and defiled, lies deeper than the wrong, and is injured, perhaps destroyed, only so far as the wrong has reached it.

Those who will not acknowledge the claim of love may acknowledge the claim of justice. Some would shrink from doing injustice to people they would equally shrink from loving. But if, as I believe, justice is impossible without love, much more can it not coexist with hate. The pure eye for another's claims goes only with a loving heart; the man who hates can hardly be delicate in doing justice to his neighbour's love, predilections and peculiarities. It is hard enough to be just to friends; how shall our enemies fare? Justice demands that we think rightly of our neighbour as surely as that we neither steal his goods nor bear false witness. Man is made not for justice from his fellow but for love, which is greater than justice and by including it supersedes it. *Mere* justice is a fiction of analysis; it exists between man and man only relative to human *law*. Justice, to be justice, must be much more than justice. Without love, the law of our condition, we can no more render justice than a man can walk a straight line in the dark. No man even indifferent to his brother can recognize his humanity's claims; the indifference is itself an injustice.

I have assumed the fault lies with the enemy, to build on the primary rocks. But each man must ask, "Is my neighbour really my enemy, or am I his, and so take him to be mine? Awful thought! If he is mine, am I not his? Am I not refusing to acknowledge the child of the kingdom in his bosom, and so killing it in mine?" Let us claim no more indulgence than we give him. Such honesty ends in severity at home and clemency abroad: we are accountable for the ill in ourselves and must kill it, and for the good in our neighbour and must cherish it. Only God can kill the bad in him; we cherish the good by being good to it across all the evil fog between our love and his good.

Nor should we forget that this fog is often misapprehension and mistake, giving rise to indignation, resentment and regret. Scarcely anything is just as it seems, but at the core is truth enough to dispel all falsehood and show life unspeakably divine. O brother, sister, across this weary fog, dim-lit by the faint torches of our truth-seeking, I call to the divine in you, which is mine, not to rebuke you or say "Why do you hate me?" but to say, "In God's name I love you." I will wait until the true self looks out of your eyes and knows the true self in me.

In the working of the Divine Love on the race, my enemy is doomed to become my friend. One flash of truth toward me would destroy my enmity; one hearty confession of wrong and our enmity passes away, and from each comes the brother who was inside the enemy all along. For this The Truth is at work. In faith, let us love the enemy now, accepting God's work in reversion, as it were, and clasp our brother fast against the wiles of the wicked enchantment that would persuade us he is some horrible thing, hateful and hating.

But what if *we* are in the wrong, and hate because we have injured? Then let us cry to God as from the throat of hell, as under a spiritual incubus, as possessed by an evil spirit, as one buried alive in the sepulchre of our evil consciousness, that he would pity us, the chief of sinners, and lift us from the fearful pit and miry clay. Nothing will help but the Spirit of the Father and the Brother casting out and revealing. It will be with tearing and foaming, a terrible cry and a lying as one dead, that such a demon goes out. But what a vision will then rise in the purified soul!

"Be perfect, as your Father in heaven is perfect." "Love your enemies, and you shall be children of the Highest." It is the divine glory to forgive.

Yet, some say, a time will come when the Unchangeable will cease to forgive; when it will no more belong to his perfection to love his enemies; when he will look calmly, and have his children look calmly, at the rising smoke of the everlasting torments of our strong brothers, our beautiful sisters! Nay, alas, the brothers are weak now, the sisters ugly now!

O brother, believe it not. "O Christ!" the redeemed would cry, "where are you, our strong Jesus? Come, our grand brother. See the suffering brothers and tormented sisters down below! Come, Lord of Life, Monarch of Suffering, redeem them. For our part, we will go down into the burning and see whether we can carry through the howling flames a drop of water to cool their tongues."

Believe it not, my brother, lest it quench forgiveness in you and you go down with those brothers to the torment unforgiven; whence, were God not better than the phantom you call God, you should *never* come out, but whence you shall come out when you have paid the uttermost farthing, having learned of God in hell what you refused to learn on the gentle earth: what the sunshine and rain could not teach you, nor the seasons, nor morning and evening, nor the human face divine, nor the word near you in your heart and mouth, the story of Him who was mighty to save because he was perfect in love.

O Father, you are All-in-all, perfect beyond the longing of your children, and we are altogether yours. You will make us pure and loving and free. We shall stand fearless in your presence because perfect in your love, and your children will be of good cheer, infinite in love of each other and eternal in your love. Lord Jesus, give us the heart of a child, that we may arise from the grave of our dead selves and die no more, but see face to face *the God of the Living*.

### THE GOD OF THE LIVING

*The passage:* God is not a God of the dead but of the living, for to him all are alive.

It is a recurring perplexity in our Lord's teaching that he is too simple for us. While we puzzle over the design of Solomon's earring on some gold-plated temple door, he speaks of the foundations of Mount Zion, and of the earth beneath. If we suppose he was making a verbal argument against the Sadducees ("I *am* the God of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob; therefore they *are*"), we will wonder that no Sadducee had the courage to answer: "God only meant to introduce himself to Moses as the God who had protected his fathers while they lived. They found me faithful; so will you."

But no such reply occurred even to the Sadducees of that day, for their eastern minds could see argument beyond logic. Shall God call himself the God of the dead, of those once alive whom he could not or would not keep alive? Is that Godhood? A changeless God over an ever-born, ever-perishing torrent of life, each atom of which cries *My God!* and passes straight into the Godless cold! "Trust me, for I looked after your fathers once. I will be good to you for seventy years or so, and when you are no more and the world goes on without you, I will still call myself your God." God does not change; once God, always God. If he has once told a man "I am your God," and that man has died the death of the Sadducee's creed, then we may say God is the God of the dead.

"But why should he not be the God of the dead in that sense, if while they lived here he was the faithful God of the living?" What Godlike relation can the ever-living, changeless God have to creatures who share nothing of his life, who have death at their core, who are not worth keeping alive? To let them die would be to change, to renounce his Godhood; and if they are not worth keeping alive, his creating is a poor thing, and he is less divine than even his dying creatures have imagined him. But our Lord says, "All live unto him." With him death is not. Your life, O Lord, sees our life; you think of us eternally more than we think of you. If you forgot us for a moment, death would be. The beloved pass from our sight, not from yours. What we call death is only a form in men's eyes; it looks final, and nothing beyond seems probable. But if God could see us before we were, and make us after his ideal, our passing from our friends' sight is no argument that he beholds us no longer. However great the change, it is not against reason to hope that God saw Abraham after Isaac ceased to see him, Isaac after Jacob ceased, and Jacob after some Sadducees doubted there ever was a Jacob. He remembers them, that is, carries them in his mind, and he of whom God thinks, lives. The Living One, whose Godhood lies in giving life, cannot name himself *Their God* after the dead; so they must be alive. If he remembers his loving thoughts of them, would he not have kept them alive if he could? And if he could not, how could he create them? Is it easier to call into life than to keep alive?

"But if they live to God, they are aware of God and conscious of their own being. Why then is a resurrection needed?"

For their relation to the other children of God in mutual revelation, and for a fresh revelation of God to all. But what is meant by the resurrection of the body? "With what body do they come?"

Surely we are not required to believe that the same body is raised. That is against science, common sense, and Scripture; St Paul puts it quite otherwise. It is childish to argue it. Who could wish his material body, which has died over and over since he was born and never stays made of the same matter for an hour, to be fixed as his changeless possession just as it is at death, and kept in worthless identity for ages? At death the body will be to a man's consciousness no more than an old garment thrown aside at night for a new and better one in the morning. To want to keep it argues a sensual materialism excusable only in pagans who, with only a thin, dreamy, funeral existence to hope for in their Elysian fields, might long for the thicker bodily life they had known above. A Christian might as well want all the hair ever cut from his head restored to his risen head.

Yet the Resurrection is a gladdening doctrine, like a silver trumpet, and as needful as breath to our longing souls. Let us know what it means, and we shall see how precious it is.

Let us first ask what this body is for. It is the means of revelation to us, the *camera* in which God's eternal shows are set forth. Through it we meet Nature and our fellow men and their revelations of God, and learn the lessons of passion, suffering, love, beauty, and science. Through it we are trained outward from ourselves and driven inward to find God in our deepest selves. There is glory and might in this vital evanescence, this ever-uptossed rainbow of tangible humanity, and it is no less God's making than the spirit it clothes.

We cannot yet have learned all we are meant to learn through the body. How much of even this world's teaching can the most diligent man exhaust before he must leave it? Is all that remains to be lost? Who that has loved this earth can doubt that the spiritual body St Paul speaks of will be a higher channel of such revelation? The meek, who have inherited the earth, see all matter radiant with spiritual meaning, and would not sigh over mere animal pleasure, would, I think, be least willing to be without a body, to be unclothed without being clothed upon. Who, after centuries of glory in heaven, would not rejoice to see the meek snowdrop once more? Whom, among the golden choirs, would an old sunset not move to a song the ancient dwellers on earth would hush their harps to hear?

All this revelation, however, would make only *a* body necessary, not *this* body, and would ill meet the fulness of the word *Resurrection*. We need a body not only to convey revelation to us but to reveal us to others. Our inward thoughts and feelings need garments that make the unseen world within us manifest to our brothers and sisters; otherwise each is left in human loneliness. If my body served this use before, the new body must be like the old: the same body, glorified as we are, with all that distinguished each of us more visible than ever. The accidental, nonessential, unrevealing, and incomplete will be gone; what made the body what it was to those who loved us will be there tenfold. Is this not the resurrection of the body, the same body though not the same dead matter? Every eye shall see the beloved, every heart cry, "My own again! More mine because more himself than ever I saw him!" On earth we say, "He is not himself today," or "She looks her own self," and that is when the heart is glad and the face radiant. For we carry a better likeness of our friends in our hearts than their faces, except at precious seasons, show us.

Who will dare call anything less than this a resurrection? Oh, how the letter kills! Some can believe the dirt of their bodies will rise as it went down to the grave, yet doubt whether they will know their friends. And they call *that* believing in the resurrection!

What! Shall a man love his neighbour as himself, and be content not to know him in heaven? Better to lose our own consciousness. What! Shall God be the God of earth's families, and the love he created in us for parents, brothers, sisters, wife and child go longing for ever, or, far worse, die out of our hearts? Shall God be God, and this be the end?

Ah, my friends! What will resurrection or life be to me, how shall I go on loving God as I learned to love him through you, if he cares so little for this human heart as to take from me the gracious visits of your faces and forms? True, I might gaze at Jesus now and then, but he would not be so good as I had thought. And how should I see him if I could not see you? God has not taken you from me to bury you in the abyss of his own being, where I could not follow and find you, lost myself in the same gulf. No, our God is a revealing God. He will raise you from the dead that I may behold you, looking out of the same eyes of eternal love and truth, holding out the same strong hands of brotherhood and sisterhood to me, who knew and loved you. I shall not care that the matter of the forms I loved long ago has gone to mingle with the workings of God's science on some far-off world, or that the muscle now sending the ichor through your veins is not made of the particles that once fed the brain, the eye, the arm, so long as it is yourselves before me, beloved, my own loving souls of old, with garments of revealing after their old lovely fashion. The new shall be dear as the old, because it reveals the old love. And in the changes that must come when the mortal puts on immortality, shall we not feel that the nobler our friends are, the more they are themselves? The more each one's idea is carried out in perfect beauty, the more they are like what we thought them in our most exalted moods, in rare moments of deepest communion, and through the veil of their imperfections when we loved them truest.

Lord, always give us this Resurrection, like your own in the body of your Transfiguration. Let us see, hear, and know, and be seen, heard, and known, as you do. Give us glorified bodies to reveal the glorified thoughts that will then inhabit us, when not only will you reveal God, but each of us will reveal you.

And for this, Lord Jesus, come, you the child, the obedient God, that we may be one with you, and with every man and woman you have made, in the Father.

## Second Series

::: center
THESE ALSO AFTER EIGHTEEN YEARS TO MY WIFE
:::

::: center
CORAGGIO, BORDIGHERA
:::

*January 1885*

### THE WAY

*The passage:* If you want to be perfect.

For many deep reasons, not least the fragmentary records, whoever reads them without the candle of the Lord (the light of truth in his inward parts) will fall into a thousand errors and, worse, fail to see the life struggling there to reveal itself: the Son of Man's own thought, feeling, and intent, that by which he lived and which he poured out for us. Yet this life, his inner being shown through his outer life as far as the spiritual eye can see, is the one thing the reader has to do with.

Readers who do not *study* the Greek Testament should use the revised version for the incident I want to unfold. Had I not known and rejoiced in it before the revision, I would owe the revisers endless gratitude for the true reading of St Matthew's report of the youth who came to our Lord. Whoever does not welcome the change cannot see its preciousness.

In the revised version, St Matthew's opening of the conversation is very different from St Mark's and St Luke's. There is no need to reject either; they blend perfectly, and I rejoice to have both. Together they give a complete conversation, which I read thus; let my fellow students see how naturally these differing, not opposing, reports combine.

| \'Good Master,\' said the kneeling youth, and is interrupted by the
| Master:---

| \'Why callest thou me good?\' he returns. \'None is good save one, even
| God.\'

Not daring to reply to this, the youth leaves it and turns to his purpose in addressing the Lord.

"What good thing shall I do," he says, "to have eternal life?"

But again the Lord takes hold of the word *good*:

"Why do you ask me about what is good?" he replies. "There is One who is good. But if you want to enter into life, keep the commandments."

"Which?"

"You shall not kill, you shall not commit adultery, you shall not steal, you shall not bear false witness, honour your father and mother; and, you shall love your neighbour as yourself."

"I have kept all these. What do I still lack?"

"If you want to be perfect, go, sell what you have and give to the poor, and you will have treasure in heaven; and come, follow me."

Let us look at the story.

As Jesus went out of a house (St Mark x. 10 and 17), the young man ran to him, knelt in the way, and called him "Good Master."

The Lord's interruption reveals the whole attitude of his being. At every moment, as much as in Gethsemane or any hour men call a crisis, his whole thought and delight was in the will and being of his Father, the joy that made life life to him. Jesus needed no pause in action to send him home. His heart's home was the Father's heart, the mystery of his gladness, claiming every degree of delight from calm content to ecstasy. His life was hid in God. He saw every truth and grandeur of life as it was, undistorted by ambition or disappointment, and beheld and loved them from the Father's bosom. He did not come for himself, to establish his power over men, but that they might know the Father who was his life. That the Father should have all the sons of men, his children like himself, in his bosom was the one thought of his heart, whatever it cost him. He did not care to hear himself called good; he was there to let men see the goodness of the Father in whom he gloried, and for that he entered the weary dream of the world, where the glory was so dulled and clouded. "You call *me* good! You should know my Father!"

The Lord's greatness consisted in his Father being greater than he: he who calls into being is greater than he who is called. The Son is always the Son, not of himself but by the Father; he does not live by his own power, as the Father does. All that is his is the Father's, given to him, and his goodness is of the Father's goodness. So when the word *good* reaches the Son's ears, his heart lifts it at once to the Father of all. His words do not deny goodness in himself; but he was not its origin, nor did he care about it except to be good, which was a matter of course to him. He would spend life and death to make his Father's goodness known! The Son no more thought of his own goodness than an honest man thinks of his honesty. A good man seeing goodness thinks of his own evil; Jesus had none to think of, yet he thinks only of the Father's goodness. "Why do you call me good? None is good but one, God."

Checked thus, the youth turns to the question that brought him running: what good thing shall he do to have eternal life? Whatever he meant by that, it was something he needed and lacked, to be gained, he saw, on some path of good. But he thought to gain by a doing what he wanted as *a being*: he would have as a possession what must possess him.

The Lord cared neither for isolated truth nor for orphaned deed, but for truth in the inward parts, the good heart, the mother of good deeds. He cared for no speculation in morals or religion; he cared about good men, not notions of good things, nor even good actions except as the bodies in which the live actions of love and will in the soul take shape. Could one word from him have settled all philosophy's questions about the supreme good and absolute truth, I venture to say he would not have spoken it. But he would die to make men good and true, and his whole heart would answer the cry of the sad publican or despairing pharisee, "How am I to be good?"

When the Lord says, "Why do you ask me about what is good?", we must not stress *me*, as if he refused the question as he had declined the epithet. He was the proper person to ask; but the question was the wrong one: the good thing was a small matter, the good Being all in all. [In a footnote he says that, as it stands, the passage is hard to read without stressing *me*, which spoils the sense, and that "Why do you ask me concerning the good thing?" would be better.] "Why ask me about the good thing? There is one living good, in whom the good thing and all good is alive and ever at work. Ask not about the good thing but about the good Person, the origin of all good." We have to do not with this good thing and that, but with the power from which comes even our power to speak the word *good*, with him to whom no one can look without the need to be good waking in his heart. To think about him is to begin to be good. To do a good thing is to do a good thing; to know God is to be good. He cares less to make us do all things right than to make us hunger and thirst after a righteousness which, once ours, will leave us no need to ask what is good, for we shall refuse the evil and choose the good by a motion of the will that is at once necessity and choice. He refers the youth again to his Father.

Note that the youth's question is not about doing something he knows to be right; to that the Lord would allow no question, for the one thing he insists on is the *doing* of what we know we ought. But the youth is looking for some unknown good thing, and the Lord sends him back to doing what he knows.

A man must have something to do in the matter, and may well ask such a question. The Lord declines only its form, to help the youth to what he really needs. He has hinted that the answer lies in God himself, but the youth cannot yet receive that; so he starts farther back: "If you want to enter into life, keep the commandments." If the commandments had nothing to do with entering into life, why were they ever given? This is his task.

Then the road to eternal life is the keeping of the commandments! Had the Lord *not* said so, what man of common moral sense would dare say otherwise? What else can the way into life be but doing what the Lord of life tells the creatures he would have live for ever? It is the beginning of the way. A man who had kept all the commandments would not therefore have eternal life in him; yet without keeping them there is no entering into life. Keeping them is not life, but so much of the way to it, and closely and essentially related to it.

The Lord says nothing about the first table of the law. Why does he not tell this youth, as he told the lawyer, that to love God is everything?

He had given him a glimpse of the essence of his own life, but the youth was not ready for it. To love God with all our heart, soul, strength, and mind is to know God, and to know him *is* eternal life, the end of the whole saving matter and the eternal beginning of all things. But the youth was not capable of it. To begin with that would be like telling someone who asks how to reach the top of a mountain, "Just set your foot on that shining snow-clad peak, high there in the blue." It would have been to mock him, who could not yet see or believe that this was eternal life, or look at life even from afar. How many *Christians* can, and how many know or care that they cannot? So the Lord answers with something he can do: keep the commandments. And when he asks "Which?", he names only those about his neighbour, ending with the highest and hardest.

"But no one can perfectly keep a single commandment of the second table, any more than of the first."

Surely not, or why were they given? But does *keep* mean nothing unless qualified by *perfectly*? Is there no keeping but perfect keeping?

"None that God cares for."

There I think you utterly wrong. That nothing but perfect keeping will *satisfy* God I hold with all my heart; but that he cares for no other is one of the enemy's lies. What father is not pleased with his little one's first tottering step? What father would be satisfied with anything but the manly step of the full-grown son?

When the Lord names the commandments he means, the youth answers that he *has* observed them from his youth up. The Lord takes his word: he looked on him and loved him. Was he deceived, or did he believe the youth had kept them perfectly? There must be a keeping, anything but perfect, which is yet acceptable to the heart of him from whom nothing is hid. In that way the youth had kept them, and however he failed of perfection, he had not missed the end for which they were given. For the immediate end of the commandments was never that men should succeed in obeying them, but that, finding they could not do what yet must be done, and that the more they tried the more was required, they should be driven to the source of life and law, to seek from him such reinforcement of life as would make fulfilling the law possible, even natural. In the youth it had been wrought: his observance gave him no satisfaction, and he desired eternal life, of which the law says nothing; keeping the law had developed a hunger no law could fill. Must not the imperfection of his keeping, even in the lower sense in which he read the commandments, have helped reveal how far they were beyond him, and how their implicit demands rose into the infinitude of God's perfection?

Having kept the commandments, the youth was ready for a further lesson; the Lord, who came to seek and to save, would not leave him where he was. He saw him in sore need of perfection, which the commonplace Christian thinks he can best do without and the elect hunger after eternally. Perfection, the Father's perfection, is eternal life. "If you want to be perfect," said the Lord. What an honour, to be supposed desirous of it, and what an enormous demand! The one thing lacking was that he sell all he had, give it to the poor, and follow the Lord. Could this be all that lay between him and entering into life? God only knows what the victory of such obedience might at once have wrought in him. Much more would be needed before perfection, but the next step would have been the step into life: in the very act there would have been born in him that whose essence is eternal life, needing only process to grow into the glorious consciousness of oneness with The Life.

There was nothing like this in the law. Was it not hard? Hard to let earth go for heaven, to turn his back on Mammon and follow Jesus, to lose his rich friends? Let him call it hard who does not know the Lord, who has never thirsted after righteousness or longed for eternal life!

The youth had got so far, and so pleased the Master, that he would give him the highest favour: to be with him, to walk and rest with him and go from him only to plead with men as a mediator between God and men, doing for him what he did for his Father. He would set him free at once, a child of the kingdom, an heir of eternal life.

I do not suppose the youth was one whom ordinary people would call a lover of money, or covetous, or even wanting more. I imagine he was like most good men of property: he valued his possessions as a good. In another man he would have regarded such possession almost as a merit, valuing a man more who had *means* and less who had none, like most of my readers. They have no notion how entirely they will one day have to change their judgment, or have it changed for them. Well for them if they change it themselves!

From this false way of thinking, and the folly and unreality that go with it, the Lord would deliver the young man. As he was, he was a slave, for a man is in bondage to whatever he cannot part with that is less than himself. The Lord could have taken the possessions by his own will, but he wished to do it by the young man's will, which would be a victory for both. So the youth would enter freedom and life, delivered from mammon by the Lord's lovely will in him, one with his own, and escape the corruption that is in the world through lust, the desire or pleasure of *having*.

The young man would not.

Was the Lord then premature? Was the demand a test rather than a word of deliverance, a next step too high for the child? I do not believe it. It was the very next lesson for which he was ready; he could respond, give birth by obedience to the redeemed and redeeming will, and so be free. Do you say, "But he would not obey"? Then it was time, I answer, that he should refuse, and know what manner of spirit he was of, and meet the sad searchings of heart that must follow. A time comes to every man when he must obey, or refuse, *and know it*.

Shall I then be supposed to mean that the refusal was final, that he was lost, that the door of life was closed against him? Verily, I have not so learned Christ. That the lesson was not lost I see in this, that he went away sorrowful. Was such sorrow in an earnest youth likely to grow less or more? Many have looked on his face and turned back, or kept company with him for years and denied him; but their weakness does not measure God's patience. Perhaps this youth was never one of the Lord's while he was on earth, but perhaps when he saw that the Master himself cared nothing for the wealth he had told him to cast away, that instead of ascending his fathers' throne he let the people do with him what they would and left the world as poor as he had lived in it, then he became one of those who sold all they had and laid the money at the apostles' feet. Meanwhile his soul was heavy: by the gravity of his riches the world held him and would not let him rise. He counted his weight his strength, and it was his weakness. Moneyless in God's upper air he would have had power indeed. Money is the power of this world, a power for defeat and failure to him who holds it, a weakness to be overcome before a man can be strong; yet many decent people fancy it a power of the world to come! It is a little power, no mighty thing for redemption, and to those who have it the saddest obstruction. To make this youth capable of eternal life, the first thing clearly was to make a poor man of him, the more clearly because he went away sorrowful! He would doubtless gladly have devoted his wealth to the Master's service, and gone with him *as a rich man* to spend it for him; but to part with it to be free for his service, that he could not, *yet*!

And how would he go on with keeping the commandments? Would he not see his shortcomings and their requirements more plainly, and feel keeping them more imperative than ever, yet impossible without something he lacked? The commandments can never be kept while there is a strife to keep them: the man is overwhelmed by the weight of their broken pieces. It takes a clean heart to have pure hands, the power of a live soul to keep the law: a power of life, not struggle; the strength of love, not the effort of duty.

One day the truth of his conduct must dawn on him with absolute clearness, and bitter must be the discovery: he had refused eternal life, turned his back on The Life! Humbled and ashamed, yet with the consolation of repentance, he would return to the Master and bemoan his unteachableness. Some, like St Paul, can say, "I did wrong, but in ignorance; my heart was not right, and I did not know it"; the remorse of such must differ from that of one who, brought to where he could embrace the truth, turned from it and refused to be set free. To him will come a time, God only knows when, to see his deed *with the knowledge that he was dimly seeing it so even when he did it*. All those months, or days, or hours, or moments, he might have been following the Master, hearing his words, looking through his eyes into the very gulfs of Godhead!

The sum of the matter is this. He had begun early to climb the eternal stair, and had climbed by every keeping of the commandments. But because he was *well to do* (a phrase of unconscious irony) he felt well to be, quite, but for that lack of eternal life. His possessions gave him a standing and consequence, and value in his own eyes. He looked up to himself because of his *means*, forgetting that means are only tools, and poor ones. To part with his wealth would be to sink to the level of his inferiors! He could devote it, but he could not cast it from him, nor devote himself; he could not make himself naked as a little child and let his Father take him. How could a rich man believe he would be of more value without his money, that casting it away would make him one of God's Anakim, that God's battle could be better fought without such an aid? But the Master had repudiated money to do his Father's will, and the disciple must be as his master. Had he done as the Master told him, he would soon have understood. Obedience is the opener of eyes.

There is this danger to every good youth in keeping the commandments, that he will think of himself more highly than he ought. He may be right about the facts and wrong in his self-regard, thinking himself a fine fellow when he is only an ordinarily reasonable youth trying to do the first thing necessary to a man's name or honour. One who has begun to recognize duty is still only a tottering child on the path of life. He is on the path, and the Father's arms are stretched out to receive him; but he is not therefore a wonderful being or a model of wisdom, not the admirable creature his remaining folly would persuade him he is, in his worst moments, that is when he feels best; he is just one of God's poor creatures. What share this besetting sin of the *good young man* had in this one's failure we need not inquire; but he may well have thought the Master undervalued his work as well as his wealth, and was less than fair to him.

To return to the summing up:

The youth, climbing the stair of eternal life, had come to a landing where not a step more was visible. On the cloud-swathed platform he stands, looking in vain for further ascent. What he thought he wanted I cannot tell. I can hardly think his idea of eternal life was only the poor idea of living for ever that commonplace minds grasp at, its mere shadow, taken for granted by all devout Jews: when a man is one with God, what should he do but live for ever? Without oneness with God, continued existence would be to me all but the unsurpassable curse (the unsurpassable itself being a God other than the God I see in Jesus). Whatever his idea, it must have held, perhaps only in solution, all his notions of God and man and righteousness. While he stands alone and helpless, behold the Son of Man! It is God himself come to meet the climbing youth, take him by the hand, and lead him up his own stair, the only stair by which ascent can be made. He shows him the first step through the mist. His feet are heavy; they have golden shoes. To go up that stair he must throw aside his shoes and walk barefoot into eternal life. Rather than stride free-limbed up the everlasting stair to the Father's bosom, he will keep his precious shoes! It is better to drag them about on the earth than part with them for a world where they are useless!

But how miserable his golden vessels, embroidered garments, and stately house must have seemed when he went back to them from the Lord's face! Surely it was not long before, in shame and misery, he cast all from him, as Judas cast away the thirty pieces of silver, in the agony of everyone who wakes to find he has preferred money to the Master! For although no man can be saved without being freed from his possessions, it is only *hard*, not impossible, *for a rich man to enter into the kingdom of God*.

### THE HARDNESS OF THE WAY

*The passage:* "Children, how hard it is!" (St. Mark x. 24.)

I suspect nearly every thoughtful young rich man feels the Lord treated this young man harshly. He asks why the rich should find it hard to enter the kingdom of heaven and fare differently from everyone else in the world to come, taking a fact of nature for an arbitrary decree born of some prejudice in God or some objection to his creatures' enjoyment. He fails to see that this is the law: they will fare like everyone else, when what they want is to fare as rich people. I can't imagine heaven's kingdom being easy for a rich man to enter: every earthly kingdom lets the rich in, and puts them first if they are rich enough. A rich man isn't bound to belong to Satan's kingdom, but he is especially welcome there, while heaven welcomes him just as it welcomes anyone.

I suspect too that many a rich man resents this story's claim on everything he has, and that many people, rich or not, can't believe the Lord really meant to take the poor fellow's money. To a man born rich, wealth seems natural and essential to well-being; a man who made his own feels it is his through labour by day and worry by night. Each feels entitled to what he holds, so it seems hard that this right should collide with the necessity of entering heaven's kingdom. Why can't he have the best of both worlds? He would compromise if he could, serving Mammon a little and God a lot, and gaining something of heaven at the least cost to the world. But he doesn't mean the best that comes from making the lower serve the higher, giving up this world's treasure for heaven's. What he wants of heaven isn't its best, and what he won't give up of the world is its worst.

I can easily picture an honest young man, brought up in Christian ways, reasoning like this. "Does this story apply to everyone? Must I give up everything if I become a Christian? That was easy then, and I'd have done it if the Saviour himself had asked. But wealth meant something different socially then; now I'd give up far more. Nor do I love money as he was in danger of doing; the Jews have always worshipped Mammon. I try to do good with mine. Besides, I'm a Christian already, so why ask the same of me as of a young Jew? If everyone with a conscience about money gave it all up, the irreligious would hold the power unopposed and the world would go to the devil. The Bible often mentions rich men but never tells another to part with everything. Ananias was struck dead for pretending to give all, not for keeping some, and Peter said the land was his own until sold, and the money his afterwards. How would the Lord have been buried without the rich Joseph? He did say, 'If you want to be perfect, go and sell what you have,' but I can't be perfect; it's hopeless, and he doesn't expect it." It would be more honest to say, "I don't want to be perfect; I'm content to be saved." Such people don't care about being perfect as their Father in heaven is perfect, only about being what they call *saved*, and little think that perfection is salvation: the two are one. "And besides," he concludes in triumph, "the text says, 'How hard it is for those who trust in riches to enter the kingdom of God!' I don't trust in my riches. I know they can't save me."

Suppose I were speaking with such a young man. I would state a truth only as a siege to win his surrender to the law of liberty; if I couldn't persuade him, I'd keep quiet. I wouldn't labour to instruct the keenest mind; I'd rather learn. Only persuading the heart, will and actions deserves a man's full energy, first for his own manhood, then his neighbour's: the beam out of his own eye before the speck out of his brother's, if that speck is more than the end of his own beam. Making someone happy as a lark *might be* doing him serious wrong; making him wake up, rise, look up and turn is worth the life and death of the Son of the Eternal.

So I say to the young man:

"Have you been keeping the commandments?"

"I wouldn't dare say that," I imagine him answering. "I should know better than that young man how much the commandments demand."

"But does that answer mean," I press, "that you think the Lord a hard master and so have tried less to please him? Or that you have strained toward his perfect standard and seen that the law can't be fulfilled? Surely you've noticed that the shocking command to give up everything came to someone who had kept the commandments for years. Have you kept them as he did, so that it could come to you? Dissatisfied with your keeping and longing to be perfect, have you gone on your knees to the Master to learn more of the way to eternal life? Or are you so content with yourself that you've never hungered and thirsted for God's righteousness, the perfection of your being? If so, take comfort: the Master doesn't ask you to sell what you have and give to the poor. *You* follow him, *you* preach good news with him, when you don't care about righteousness! You're not the company he wants, and he won't ask for your purse; it's nothing to him whether you give or keep. Is he to be obliged for money to someone outside his kingdom, false and ignoble? Bring him a true heart and an obedient hand, which he gave his life's blood for."

"Please don't be harsh. I admit I haven't been what I should, but I want to repent and enter into life. Don't take my reluctance to throw everything away, without knowing it's required, as disregard for higher things."

"Then once more: *go and keep the commandments*. It hasn't come to your money yet; you aren't yet a child in the kingdom, caring for your father's arms and not just the shelter of his roof. Let the commandments tell you how to use your money; it is pitiable presumption to wonder whether you must sell it all. Wait until keeping them has shown you the great reward of loving what's right, then that for all your effort you're only an unprofitable servant; until you know that only those who need no law can keep it, and would rather cease to be than live the poor, selfish life that is all you can call your own; until you sense something beyond your mind's thought but not your heart's longing, something not yours, seemingly never to be yours, without which your life is worthless. When all this brings you to the Master crying, 'What must I do to inherit eternal life?', he may say, 'Sell all you have, give to the poor, and come follow me.' Obey then and you'll be most honourable; refuse and you'll be most pitiable. Until then you'd be no comfort to him and no pleasure to his friends. For the young man, selling all and following him would have meant accepting a patent of nobility from God; it isn't offered to you. If one of the disobedient gave up every straw he owned in hope of that honour, he'd just be sent back to keep the commandments in the easier circumstances of poverty.

"Does that comfort you? Alas for you! Your relief is that the Lord has no need of you, doesn't require your money, and doesn't offer you himself instead! You haven't sold him for thirty pieces of silver, but you're glad not to buy him with all you have. How do you differ from the young man in the story? He was invited to do everything, to share the divine nature; you never had the chance to refuse, because you aren't fit to be invited. People like you can never enter the kingdom. You wouldn't know you were in heaven if you were there."

"But I don't trust in my riches. I trust in the merits of my Lord and Saviour, in his finished work and the sacrifice he offered."

"Yes, yes! You'll trust in anything but the Man himself, who tells you it's hard to be saved. All the merits of God and his Christ can't give you eternal life; only God and his Christ can, and they can't, and wouldn't if they could, unless you keep the commandments. Knowing the living God *is* eternal life; what you need is not his merits but him. As for trusting in riches, who with even half a conscience or half a head could imagine that anyone relying on them to get him in could enter? A Jew confident in his wealth might hope it signalled God's favour that wouldn't fail him, or be too self-satisfied to imagine being lost; but *trusting in his riches*? No. Saying he doesn't trust in them to take him into life is the last refuge of the money-worshipper, the man who needs wealth for his peace. The man who thinks of nothing else does trust in it, in a fearful sense; yet hundreds who do will say, 'I don't trust in my riches; I trust in...' some stock phrase."

"You're forgetting yourself. You're criticizing the Lord's own words: he said, 'How hard it is for those who trust in riches to enter the kingdom of heaven!'"

"I'm not. I believe our Lord never said those words. The two oldest manuscripts, the Sinaitic and the Vatican, read, 'Children, how hard it is to enter the kingdom of God!' I'm glad Westcott and Tischendorf prefer that reading, though Tregelles and the Revisers don't. I take it to be the Lord's. Some copyist with a rich man's mind, uneasy at the Lord's view of money and eager like you to compromise, added a marginal note: it isn't having riches but trusting in them that makes entering hard! *Hard*? For a man who trusts in riches it is eternally impossible; it is the man who merely has them for whom it is hard. Is the Lord supposed to teach that a man who trusts in riches can enter, that this is impossible for men but possible with God? He never said it. Mr. Facingbothways's annotation crept into the text and stands in the English version. Our Lord didn't explain away his hard words; he let them stand as the burning fire meant to purge us. Where simplicity meets simplicity they are understood; the double heart must mistake. It is hard for a rich man, simply because he is rich, to enter the kingdom of heaven."

Some comfort themselves that God will take the difficulty into account, another form of the notion that a rich man, having had his good things here, must have them there too. They'll certainly get fairness, but what good is that if they don't enter the kingdom? It is life they need; existence without it can't be endured, yet they think they can do without eternal life if only they live for ever! Those who know what eternal life is count it the one terror to live on without it.

Take the Lord's words, then, as he spoke them: "Children, how hard it is to enter the kingdom of God!" He first has them reflect on how hard it is for anyone to enter, then restates the rich man's difficulty more strongly: "It is easier for a camel to go through a needle's eye than for a rich man to enter the kingdom of God." It always has been and will be hard to enter; it is hard even to believe one must be born from above, passing into a new, unknown consciousness. The law-abiding Jew and the ceremonial Christian shrink from self-annihilation, from the life of grace and truth, from the high clear air of heavenly delight, and from the all-embracing love that fulfils the law and sets it aside; they can't accept a state of being as itself eternal life. What is hard to believe in is hard to enter. How hard? As hard as the Master could put it: "If any man comes to me and does not hate ... his own life also, he cannot be my disciple." A rich man finds this harder still, since so much is tied to his life to swell his sense of self that casting it off as the ugly shadow of the self God made is far harder.

Nobody knows how hard it is to enter the kingdom of heaven except those who have tried hard and not stopped trying. Don't tell me one can pass at once into full assurance; I want not the certainty of eternal life but eternal life. Whatever other preachers say, in St Paul the spirit and flesh often fought. Only those who want life simply as eternal, and struggle every day and soon every hour, know how hard entering is; they see that no moment is life without the presence that strengthens. I don't deny anyone's testimony of peace and unspeakable joy as the instant result of the new birth; I say only that if by salvation they mean less than absolute oneness with God, it's no salvation to me, and I wouldn't be content with it with every joy of heaven thrown in. If they aren't righteous as he is righteous, they aren't saved, however glad; they are only on the way. If they don't love their neighbour, not "as themselves" (a phrase hard to understand and not Christ's) but as Christ loves him, I can't count them entered into life, though life may have begun to enter them. The disciples little knew what the Lord's "how hard it is to enter the kingdom" would ask of them.

New demands keep coming to the Christian; the Spirit's ever-fresh calling is what works in the children of obedience. When he thinks he has arrived, he is in danger. When the mountain he has long climbed suddenly shows a distant peak, radiant white and nearly lost in heaven, that no human foot seems able to reach, then there is hope: it proves he has been climbing, since he sees what he couldn't before. He may know little of what he is, but he learns again and again that he isn't in the world as Jesus was; and the very wind that gives him courage as he climbs is the hope that one day he will be like him, seeing him as he is.

Possessions are *things*, and things, except as something to conquer and a means of spiritual gain, readily turn against the better life. Whoever depends on anything but life itself for his sense of well-being is a slave, hanging on something less than himself. A person isn't perfect unless he could sit calmly content with every *thing* taken from him, aware of a well-being untouched, still possessing all things as a child of the Eternal. Things, our bodies first, are given to train us in independence of them and true possession of them: we must own them, not be owned. They are meant to mediate, as lower shapes of unseen things that belong to the unshakeable world of silence and being, not speech and showing. Those unseen things take form in time and space not to exist, since they exist in eternal Godhead, but to be known by those in training for the eternal, and God's sons and daughters must possess them. Instead people grasp at the forms, treating the visible as what is to be owned and loving bodies rather than souls. Some good people can hardly believe that if the young man had given up his wealth, the Lord wouldn't have told him to keep it; they think heaven's treasure a poor substitute. "Isn't wealth power?" they ask. It is, and so is a wolf hiding in a robe: the power of a brute machine whose levers and valves its owner hardly understands. Most readers of the story agree with the young man at his worst moment, as he turned away, except that they aren't sorrowful.

Nobody can really possess things who can't do without them, who wouldn't be perfectly content in the knowledge that the cause of his being is present in his consciousness and with him. To be clear: anyone with God's presence is content. What I mean is that only someone who has the Father, and is eternally content in him alone, can possess a sunset, a field of grass, a gold mine or a person's love as God means him to. Whoever has God has all things, as their maker has them. To every man, woman and child I say: if you aren't content, it's because God isn't with you as you need him, more than your body ever needed food or air, more than your soul ever hungered for joy or peace.

We must get free of the tyranny of *things*. See how urgent it is. However tightly the young man clings to his wealth, God will do what he can: his child won't be left in the hell of possession. When the angel of death comes, where are the things that haunted the poor soul? The world and all in it slip from his hands and feet, taking his body, eyes and ears, and every purse and coffer that deluded him into thinking he owned anything.

"If death frees a man from the rule of things, why hurry it? Shouldn't the young man wait for the Inevitable to free him?"

Not so! I presume that only then does the man of things see their tyranny. Abstaining first shows a man his craving's strength; having nothing left may first show him how much he made a necessity of things. If he then fights them, casting out of his soul what death tore from his hands, he will first know the full passion of possession, the slavery of prizing the worthless part of the precious.

"Then what good is Death? He removes the sting but leaves the poison!"

This: it's not the fetters that chafe but the fetters that soothe that eat into the soul. When the golden fetters he loved to look at are gone, though they held him fast to his dungeon wall, he will first feel the soreness of their loss, in a weary indifference to earth and sea, space and stars. When it dawns on him that they were a horror and a disgrace, the good of saving death will appear, and he will begin to understand that having never was well-being: we don't live by possessing but possess by living. So losing what he thought he had moves him, hardly toward deliverance but in its favour: it may feel like the start of slavery when it is the start of freedom. No soul was ever freed without feeling its slavery; nothing but itself can enslave a soul, nothing outside itself free it.

Take the drunkard, freed from his body but keeping his craving, unable to indulge it. With no means left to drown thought, he finally has time to think; surely a fearful hope dawns on him then! But he isn't saved until, by God's power and his own obedient effort, he would refuse the temptation even for an eternity of drunkenness with all its delights and none of its penalties.

So death may give a new opportunity, with some hope for the multitude who count themselves Christians yet are possessed by *things* as by a legion of devils: respected in their church, thought blameless, kind and generous, believers in Jesus's redemption, yet whose care is always to heap up more, adding house to house and field to field, burying themselves deeper in the ash-heap of *Things*.

But things rule not only the rich man; people with no money are slaves too, if lack of it makes them unhappy. The man forever digging his grave is little better than the one already rotting in it, and the money one has and the other wants causes the same enduring stupidity in each. To both comes the word: "*How is it that you do not understand*?"

### THE CAUSE OF SPIRITUAL STUPIDITY

*The passage:* Why is it that you do not understand?

After feeding the four thousand with seven loaves and a few small fishes on the east side of the Sea of Galilee, Jesus crossed the lake and was met by certain Pharisees whose attitude drove him back to the boat and across again. On the way the disciples realized they had only a single loaf, probably bought by one of them while the Lord dealt with the Pharisees. Jesus, still occupied with the leaders' antagonism and wanting to destroy their influence on his disciples, began to warn them, using a figure they had heard before, leaven as a hidden but pervading energy. Now he tells them to beware of the leaven of the Pharisees. The disciples, their minds on their lack of provisions, thought of bread at once, concluded he spoke of leaven because it was missing, and perhaps imagined a warning about defilement from Pharisaical cookery: "It is because we have taken no bread!" A leaven like the Pharisees' was already at work in their hearts; for the sign the Pharisees sought in mockery of unbelief, they themselves had had a few hours before, and had already forgotten all that made it valuable.

It is to the man who is trying to live, who obeys the Master's word, that the word unfolds itself. Understanding the outside of things, we think we have them; but the Lord puts his things in suggestive shapes that give no satisfactory meaning to the mere intellect and unfold themselves only to the conscience and heart as the new creation of reality advances in a man. For life, that is, action, is the only condition into which the light of the Living can penetrate; only life can assimilate life. See how the disciples here fooled themselves!

See how the Lord calls them to their senses. He does not tell them where they are wrong; he attacks the cause in themselves, always of infinitely more consequence than any mistake: the one is a live mistake, an untruth in the soul, the other a dead blunder born of it. So the connection between their blunder and his exhortation is not on the surface. Often he speaks not to the words but to the thought; here he speaks to the whole mode of thinking, the inward condition of the men.

He sets out to rouse in them a sense of their lack of confidence in God, the cause of their blunder. He reminds them of the two miracles of the loaves and the fragments left over; one was not a day behind them, and already they were doubting! He makes them go over the particulars, not to refresh their memories but to make their hearts dwell on them; for they had forgotten the central revelation, the eternal fact of God's love and care. They knew the number of men, loaves, and baskets of fragments each time, but forgot the Love that had so broken the bread that its remnants twenty times outweighed its loaves.

Having questioned them like children and listened to the answers of children, he turns the light of their thoughts on themselves and demands, with an argument that overleaps its own logic's links, "Why do you not understand?" Then they understood, and knew he did not speak of the leaven of bread but of the teaching of the Pharisees and Sadducees. He who trusts can understand; he whose mind is at ease can discover a reason.

How otherwise than by quelling their anxiety could "How many baskets did you take up?" and "Why do you not understand?" have made them see he did not mean the leaven of bread? What had the bread miracles to do with it?

The lesson he would have had them learn from the miracle, the only lesson worthy of it, was that God cared for his children and could, did, and would provide for their needs. They had not learned it. The miracle's love proved his mission better than its power: it was a throb of the Father's heart. The ground of the Master's upbraiding is not that they did not understand him but that they did not trust God, that after all they had seen they still troubled themselves about bread. Because we easily imagine ourselves in want, we imagine God ready to forsake us. The miracles of Jesus were the ordinary works of his Father, wrought small and swift that we might take them in. Their design was to show what God is: not that Jesus was God but that his Father was, and was what Jesus showed, for no other kind of God could be worth believing in. The Son's mission was to reveal his Father as he is, that men may know him and, knowing, trust him. It would be a small boon for God to forgive men and not give himself; less than God as he is cannot comfort men for the essential sorrow of their existence. Jesus came to give them God, who is eternal life.

Those miracles of feeding gave to their eyes, hands, and mouths the lesson his words gave their ears when he told them not to seek what they would eat or drink, since their Father knew their need, and to seek first the kingdom of God and his righteousness and all these things would be added. So little had they learned it that they remembered the loaves but forgot the Father, as men in their theology forget the very *Theou logos*. Forgetting, they were troubled about provision for the day, and the moment leaven was mentioned thought of bread. *What else could he mean? The connection was plain!* The miracle had set forth God's heart toward them, the loving care without which he would not be God. Had they learned this lesson, their hearts would not have been so uneasy as to mistake his word; had they but said that, though they had one loaf, they had him who makes all the loaves, they would never have made their blunder.

The answer to the Lord's reproach is plainly this: their minds were so full of care about the day's bread that they could think of nothing else; the mere mention of leaven threw them floundering afresh in the bog of their unbelief. When he reminded them of what they had seen, and so of what God is and of the foolishness of their care, the moment their fear was taught to look up they began to see what his former words must have meant.

The disciples' care was for the day, not the morrow; and *morrow* stands for any point of the future. The next moment is as much beyond our grasp and as much in God's care as a hundred years away; in neither can we do anything, in both God is doing everything. Only those claims of the morrow that must be prepared today belong to today's duty; the moment that coincides with work to be done is the moment to be minded; the next is nowhere till God has made it.

Their lack of bread seems to have come not from neglect but from the suddenness of the re-embarkation; and had there been a want of foresight, the Lord did not come to reprove that but the primary evil of life without God, the root of all evils. Minor virtues, prudence among them, would thus almost be superseded. If a man forgets a thing, God will see to that; man is not lord of his memory or intellect. But he is lord of his will and action, and is to blame when, remembering a duty, he puts it off and so forgets it. If a man does the immediate duty of the moment, I suspect wonderfully little forethought will be needed; only that forethought is right which determines duty and passes into action. Work done is of more consequence for the future than the foresight of an archangel.

With the disciples as with the rich youth, it was *Things* that prevented the Lord from being understood. Because of possession the young man had no suspicion of the grandeur of the call with which Jesus honoured him; he thought himself hardly dealt with to be offered a patent of Heaven's nobility, he was so very rich! His soul was crowded with the meanest idols, among which his spirit crept upon its knees, wasting on them the gazes that belonged to his fellows and his Master. The disciples had left all and followed the Lord; but neither had they got rid of *Things*. The paltry solitariness of a loaf was enough to hide the Lord from them. Surely if he had told them to go all day without food for his sake, they would not have minded! But they lost sight of God, as if he did not see or did not care.

In the former case it was the possession of wealth, in the latter the lack of more than a loaf, that made them incapable of receiving the Lord's word: the evil principle was precisely the same. If it be *Things* that slay you, what matter whether things you have or things you have not? The youth did not trust God as having given; the disciples did not trust God as ready to give. We are like them when in *any* trouble we do not trust him. It is hard on God when his children will not let him give, when they carry themselves so that he must withhold his hand lest he harm them. To take no care that they acknowledge whence their help comes would leave them worshippers of idols, trusters in that which is not.

Distrust is atheism, and the barrier to all growth. Lord, we do not understand you because we do not trust your Father, who is whole-hearted to us as never yet was mother to her first-born! While we who are evil would die to give our children bread, we are not certain the only Good will give us anything we desire! The things of your world so crowd our hearts that there is no room for the things of your heart, which would raise ours above all fear and make us merry children in our Father's house! How many a whisper of the watching Spirit we miss through brooding over a need not yet come! Tomorrow makes today's whole head sick, its whole heart faint. You do not so, Lord; were you such as we, we would have good cause to be troubled! But you know it is difficult, with *things* pressing on every sense, to believe that where we can see no hand directing, a will nearer than any hand is moving them from within to fulfil his word! Help us to obey, to resist, to trust.

The care filling your mind at this moment, or waiting only till you lay the book aside to leap on you, that need which is no need, is a demon sucking at the spring of your life.

"No; mine is a reasonable care, indeed an unavoidable one!"

"Is it something you have to do this very moment?"

"No."

"Then you are allowing it to usurp the place of something required of you this moment!"

"Nothing is required of me at this moment."

"Yes, there is: the greatest thing that can be required of man."

"Pray, what is it?"

"Trust in the living God. His will is your life."

"He may not will that I have what I need!"

"Then you only think you need it. Is it a good thing?"

"Yes, it is a good thing."

"Then why doubt you will have it?"

"Because God may choose to have me go without it."

"Why should he?"

"I cannot tell."

"Must it not be to give you something instead?"

"I want nothing instead."

"I thought I was talking to a Christian!"

"I will consent to be called nothing else."

"Do you not know, then, that when God denies a child of his anything he values, it is to give him something *he* values?"

"But if I do not want it?"

"You are none the less miserable for not having it. Instead of his great possessions the young man was to have the company of Jesus and treasure in heaven. When God refused to deliver a certain man from a sore evil, though he asked three times, he gave him instead his own graciousness, consoling him in person."

"Ah, but that was St Paul!"

"True; what of that?"

"He was one by himself!"

"God deals with all his children after his own father-nature. No scripture is of private interpretation, even for a St Paul. It sets forth God's way with man. If you are not willing that God should have his way with you, then in God's name be miserable, until your misery drives you to the Father's arms."
