"I do trust him in spiritual matters."

"Everything is an affair of the spirit. If God has a way, it is the only way. Every little thing in which you would have your own way has a mission for your redemption; and he will treat you as a naughty child until you take your Father's way for yours."

There will be this difference between the rich who love their riches and the poor who hate their poverty: at death the heart of the one will still be crowded with things and their pleasures, the heart of the other relieved of their lack; the one has had his good things, the other his evil. But the rich man who held his *things* lightly, a channel and no cistern, ever forsaking his money, starts in the new world beside the man who accepted, not hated, his poverty. Each will say, "I am free!"

For the only air in which the soul can breathe and live is the present God and the spirits of the just: that is our heaven, our home. Cleansed of greed, jealousy, vanity, pride, possession, and all the forms of the evil self, we shall be God's children in the fields of that heaven, none desiring to be before another or to cast him out; for ambition and hatred will then be seen to be one spirit. "What you have, I have; what you desire, I will; I give to myself ten times in giving once to you." But let me be practical: you are ready to be miserable over trifles and do not believe God good enough to care for your care; I would reason with you to rid you of troubles that hide from you the thoughts of your God.

The things readiest to be done, those lying on the very table of a man's mind, are in general the most neglected, even by the thoughtful. May a man become strong in righteousness without learning to speak the truth to his neighbour, or climb the last flight of the stair without setting foot on the lowest step? Truth is one: he who does it in the small thing is of the truth; he who postpones the small thing near him for the great thing farther off is not. Consider the parallels between ourselves and the disciples maundering over one loaf with the Bread of Life beside them. We too dull our understandings with trifles and waste heavenly time with hurry; to those who possess their souls in patience come the heavenly visions. When I trouble myself over a trifle, hunting the house for some little lost article not from need but from dislike of loss, or fretting over a borrowed book not returned while thousands on my shelves could give me treasure that neither moth, rust, nor thief touches, am I not like the disciples? Am I not a fool whenever loss troubles me more than recovery would gladden? God would have me wise and smile at the trifle; this losing of things is his mercy, teaching us to let them go. Or have I forgotten a thought that seemed a truth revealed to my heart? I keep trying to call it back, feeling a poor man till it is recovered, though it might be far more lost in a notebook I would never look into again. I forget that God cares about live truths, not things set down in a book or memory or embalmed in the joy of knowledge, but things that lift up the heart and are active in an active will. Had I faith in God, the maker of thought and memory, I would know that if it was a truth it must come again; it is in God, and so, like the dead, not beyond my reach: kept for me.

"These are foolish illustrations, not worth writing!"

If such things do not happen, mentioning them is foolish; if they do, he is foolish who treats them as if they did not. I choose them for their smallness, appealing to all who keep house about the size of trouble that suffices to hide the word and face of God.

With every haunting trouble, great or small, go to God and ask him, the God of your life, to deliver you, his child, from what is unlike him and antagonistic to your nature. If your trouble is such that you cannot appeal to him, the more need! Where one cannot go to God, something is specially wrong. If you let thought for tomorrow or next year distress you, if the chatter of the public peering purblind into the sanctuary of motive annoys you, or you greatly heed the judgment of men, capable or incapable, you open your windows to the mosquitoes of care, to drown with their buzzing the voice of the Eternal!

If you tell me that but for care the needful work of the world would be ill done, I ask, "What work is better done by the greedy or anxious than by the free, fearless soul? If the work is not God's, care may help it, for its success is loss. Is he worthy the name of man who for fear of starvation does better work than for the joy that his labour is not in vain in the Lord? You are not likely to get rich that way, but you will not block the gate of heaven against yourself."

Ambition in every shape has to do with *Things*, with outward advantages for the satisfaction of self-worship; it is the form of pride that usurps the place of aspiration. The sole ambition of God is to rise above oneself; all other is of the devil. Yet it is nursed in many a soul that thinks itself devout, filling it with petty cares and disappointments that shut out the glory of God. We must get rid of the love of men's praise, the desire of fame, the pride that takes offence, the puffing-up of knowledge, and every other form of self-worship. We must be free. A man whom another enslaves may be free as God; but God will not enter, or sup with, a man who is a slave in himself, for he cannot be his friend; he will not eat in a lumber-room, though the lumber be thrones and crowns. *Cannot*, I say rather than *will not*: men full of things would not partake with God were he by them all day.

Nor will God force any door. He may send a tempest to burst doors and windows and shake the house to its foundations; but not so will he enter. The door must be opened by a willing hand before the foot of Love will cross the threshold. He watches to see the door move from within. Every tempest is an assault in the siege of love; the terror of God is the other side of his love, love outside the house that would be inside, that knows the house is no home, only a tent, until the Eternal dwells there. *Things* must be cast out to make room for their souls, the eternal truths which in things find shape and show.

But who is sufficient to cast them out? A man who attacks the army of bats and demon-snakes infesting the place of the Holy finds the task too great: the temple will not be cleansed by him, and the very dust he raises is full of corruptive forces. Let those who would do what they cannot remember, with hope, that he who knows all about our being once told a parable to the effect that people ought always to pray and not lose heart.

### THE WORD OF JESUS ON PRAYER

*The passage:* People ought always to pray.

Because we cannot do what we would as we would, we are driven to look for help, and that gives us a new starting point. Every difficulty points to something our theory of life has not yet taken in, and checks our tendency to leave the narrow path. But there is a reality of being in which everything is easy and plain: oneness with the Lord of Life. To pray for that is the first thing, and every difficulty hedges us toward it. If I set out the reasonableness of prayer, remember it is for action, not speculation: prayer is a thing to be done, and it matters nothing whether you agree with me if you do not pray. I want to help someone pray, not to explain how a man might pray and still be a reasonable soul.

First, the parable. It is one of many in which the Lord takes a story about a very common or bad person to argue, from the lesser to the greater, about the way of the All-righteous. Did the unrighteous judge, to save himself annoyance, punish a man who had not offended him for the sake of a woman he cared nothing about? And shall not the living Justice avenge his praying friends, while he bears patiently with their enemies over the injuries done them? That is how I take the phrase the Revision rightly renders "and he is long-suffering over them." He will avenge them speedily; yet "when the Son of Man cometh, shall he find faith on the earth?"

Here is a word of the Lord about prayer, and it is a comfort that he recognizes the difficulty: we need encouragement to go on praying, it looks as if we were not heard, and no wonder we are ready to faint. In his parable the suppliant must go again and again to the one who can help her, and wins only at last. Yet he allows no actual delay on God's part, so he plainly knows how things must look to those he wants to keep praying. He teaches us to go not by the look of things but by the reality behind it; one necessity of God's own willed nature outweighs a whole army of appearances. It looks as if he does not hear you: never mind; he does, he must; go on as the woman did, and you too will be heard. She is heard at last because of her much going; God hears at once and will avenge speedily. The judge cared nothing for the woman; those who cry to God are his chosen, as their very crying shows. He made them to cry, and they cry: will he not hear them? They exist to pray, that there may be communion between them and him. A gulf of indifference lay between the woman and the judge; God and those who seek him are closer than hands clasped in love. This is bold, given the delay the parable admits. Why then does he seem to check himself with a sigh: "Howbeit when the Son of Man cometh, shall he find faith on the earth?" After all he had said and done, and time given for the holy leaven to work, would he find men trusting the Father? Would they be beyond the tyranny of appearances, children enough to believe that God was hearing and working for them, though his ways are so wide and his goal for them so high that they could detect no movement toward it? The sigh never meant that God might do more than he was doing, but that the Father would have a dreary wait before his children would trust him; it recognizes man's slowly yielded part in faith, and his blame for not trusting.

He seems here to refer to his second coming. About its time he refused information; about its manner he said it would be unexpected; about our duty he insisted that we be ready, faithful and at our work. Those who say "Lo here, lo there" about the signs of his coming think to be too keen for him; told to watch lest he find them neglecting their work, they stare about, watching lest he succeed in coming like a thief! If we gave ourselves to obedience instead of speculation, what a difference would soon be seen in the world! To the multitude of so-called religious questions the Lord would answer, "Strive to enter in at the strait gate"! Many eat and drink and talk and teach in his presence; few do what he says. Obedience is the one key of life.

I would meet difficulties, not answer objections: remove stumbling-blocks from the path of the man who would pray, and help him pray. If we live not by our own will but another's, there is reason in prayer, and only then. To the man who refuses that other will I have nothing to say; the hour may come when he wishes there were someone to pray to, but now I cannot help him.

If there is a God and I am his creature, some communication between us may be, should be, must be open. If someone allows a God not good enough to care for his creatures, I grant it would be foolish to pray to him. But the notion that, with all the good impulses in us, we are the offspring of a cold-hearted devil is so horribly inconsistent that I would ask that man what cold-hearted disregard for truth makes him capable of supposing it. God's terrors, or if not his terrors then his sorrows, will yet speak to such a man; the divine something in him will love, and the love be left moaning.

Suppose I find myself like one away from home, even in a kind of prison. I can rule neither the world I live in nor my own thoughts and desires. I cannot quiet my passions, order my likings, determine my ends, will my growth, control my memory, or love or hate where I would; I am no king over myself. I cannot supply my own needs, nor always tell which of my seeming needs are impostors. My being is too much for me: I can neither understand it, be satisfied with it, nor better it. May that not well give me pause, the pause that ends in prayer? I cannot account for my existence, had no hand in it, and cannot end it. I can do nothing to make up to those I love, or those I hate, for the evils I have done them. In my worst moments I disbelieve in my best, and in my best I loathe my worst; there is in me no wholeness; life is no good to me, for I scorn myself. Can it be strange, then, that I think there ought to be somewhere a being who accounts for himself and for me, and makes the round of my existence just; one whose presence in my being is imperative, not merely to supplement it but to make my existence a good to me? A life not rounded in itself, but dependent on what it cannot know, cannot be a known good to itself; it longs for a *logos* to be the interpreting soul of its *cosmos*, a *logos* it cannot have. To know God present, where he is the essential life, must be absolutely necessary to that life. He who is made in God's image must know him or be desolate: the child must have the Father! My soul's desolation without him bears witness. It cannot act from itself except in God; what seems acting from itself without him is only yielding to impulse, and all within is disorder and spasm. A cry behind me and a voice before tell me I must rise above my present self, perhaps above all my possible self, and I do not see how. I am an unknown *I* shut up in a world of consciousness, itself unknown; surely this world of my unwilled, compelled existence cannot be unknown or impenetrable to him from whom I am! Is it not his thinking in which I think, his consciousness by which I am conscious? What passes in me must be known to him far more thoroughly than to me. Though I flew toward the dawn or settled at the farthest sea, his hand would lead and hold me. If I speak to him, however low, or only think to him, surely he, my original, in whose life and will alone I think of him, hears and knows and acknowledges! Shall I not tell him, then, that he has troubled me by making me, that I am unfit to be what I am, that my being is not yet a good to me, that I need a law to account for it in righteousness and show me how to *be* a good and not an evil? Shall I not cry to him to comfort me, to move his breath on the waters of the Chaos he has made, to be my rest and strength, to deliver me from my sins and make me clean and glad? Such a cry is of the child to the Father; if there is a Father, he will hear, and let the child know he hears. Every need of God that lifts up the heart is a seeking of God, a begging for himself, the profoundest prayer, and the root and inspirer of all other prayer.

If it is reasonable for me to cry thus, God cannot but hear; a being who could not hear or would not answer prayer could not be God.

"But granting all this, is what you call a necessary truth an existing fact? You say, 'It must be so'; I say, 'What if there is no God?' Convince me that prayer is heard, and I shall know. We know the wind blows; why should we not know that God answers prayer? Why should the question need reasoning about at all?"

I reply: what if God does not care to have you know it at second hand? There is some testimony, and might be more were such things not so personal and delicate to talk about. But no testimony can be conclusive, since like a reported miracle it can always be put aside, and the conviction so gained is of little value.

Proof is scarcely possible for inward experience, and the better part of the evidence is of that kind: the testifier may be truthful yet utterly self-deceived. Take King David: old poetry, in the most indefinite language in the world! Many psalms are a cry as true as ever came from depth of pain or height of deliverance; yet it may all have been only the waves of the psychical aether! I lay nothing on testimony now, not valuing the bare assent of the intellect. The only assurance worth having, though the most incontestable evidence came from a thousand quarters, is that gained by personal experience, which a man can least readily receive from another or pass on as evidence. Even the evidence of Jesus Christ could not take its place. A truth matters enormously for the life, that is, the heart, conscience, and will; little as a fact for the understanding. God may hear every prayer ever offered, and a man may believe he does, and be none the better, so long as he has no prayers to be heard and no answers to receive. Reader, if you are in trouble, try whether God will not help you; if you are in no need, why ask about prayer? He knows little of himself who does not know he is wretched, miserable, poor, blind, and naked; but until he suspects a need, how can he pray? For one who does not want to pray I would not lift a straw to defeat him in the argument whether God hears; let him think what he will; it matters nothing in heaven or on earth, and whether in hell I do not know.

I am almost ashamed to mention the so-called scientific challenge to prove the efficacy of prayer by the results of simultaneous petition; science itself should show it absurd. A God who could be moved one way or another like that is not worth believing in, nor is he the God Jesus Christ believed in, who said he knew. I cannot believe in a God who would fail to hear a single prayer, even the feeblest or worst; but a God who would grant every request of every man or company of men would be an evil God, no God but a demon. A God who hangs in the thought-atmosphere like a windmill, waiting till enough men combine to send up prayer with force to turn his arms, is absurd. God waits to be gracious, not to be tempted. A man who proposes such a test has no worthy idea of a God and might well disbelieve in any; better that than believe in an unworthy one.

"But I want to believe in God. I want to know there is a God who answers prayer. There was a time when I believed. I prayed to him in great and sore trouble of heart and mind, and he did not hear me. I have not prayed since."

How do you know that he did not hear you?

"He did not give me what I asked, though the welfare of my soul hung on it."

In your judgment. Perhaps he knew better.

"I am the worse for his refusal. I would have believed in him if he had heard me."

Until the next desire came that he would not grant, and then you would have turned your God away. A desirable believer! A fine brother to him who thought nothing fit to give the Father less than his all! You would accept no decision against your desire; that ungranted, there was no God, or not a good one. This only I will say: God has not to consider his children only at the moment of their prayer. If a man is not ready for love's severity, he may be given what he wishes so that he comes to wish he had not been; but to give a child what he wants merely because he wants it, with no further purpose for his good, would be the cruelty of a devil. Yet every prayer is heard, and the real soul of the prayer may require, for its real answer, that it not be granted in the form requested.

"To have a thing in another shape might be equivalent to not having it at all."

If you knew God, you would leave that to him. He is not mocked, and he will not mock. He knows you better than you know yourself, and deals with you as the child of eternal ages, not of a day. You shall be satisfied if you will let him have his way with the creature he has made; the question is between your will and his. He is not one of those who give readiest what they prize least; he gives only his best, or what prepares for it. Not many years may pass before you confess, "You are a God who hears prayer and gives a better answer." You may see that the desire of your deepest heart would have been frustrated by getting what then seemed its embodiment. That God should listen as a loving father and deal with the request with perfect tenderness is enough for me; it is little that I go without what I pray for. If any answer not coming from love, and not for the final satisfaction of the one who prayed, would be unworthy of God, and love and knowledge watch over the wayward, ignorant child, then the trouble of seemingly unanswered prayer begins to abate, and a lovely hope takes its place. To hear is not necessarily to grant, God forbid! but it is necessarily to attend to, and sometimes to refuse.

Paul says he besought the Lord three times to remove a certain thing, and was told that Christ's grace was enough for him, for power is made perfect in weakness. God had something better for Paul than granting his prayer: he would make him strong, so that the power of Christ would descend and remain on him, stronger than his suffering, a sharer in the energy of God. If we have God, we can do without the answer to any prayer.

"But if God is so good, and knows all we need far better than we do, why should it be necessary to ask him for anything?"

What if he knows prayer to be the thing we need first and most? What if the main object of prayer in God's idea is to supply our endless need of himself, and our smaller needs are good because they drive us to him? Hunger may drive the runaway child home, and he may or may not be fed at once, but he needs his mother more than his dinner. Communion with God is the soul's one need beyond all others; prayer begins it, and some need is the motive of the prayer. If gratitude and love followed at once on the supply of our needs, and God our Saviour were the one thought of our hearts, it might be unnecessary to ask. But we take our supplies as a matter of course, as if they came from nothing, or the earth, or our own thoughts, not from a heart of love and a will which alone is force. So we must be made to feel some of our wants, that we may seek him who supplies them all and find his every gift a window to his heart of truth. So begins a communion with God, which is the sole end of prayer and of existence itself. We must ask that we may receive; but God's end in making us pray is not that we should receive what we ask for our lower needs, for he could give us everything without that. To bring his child to his knee, God withholds, that man may ask.

For the high necessities of our nature, God requires us to ask, by shutting us up to prayer, in order that he may be able to give. For how can he give to the soul what it cannot receive? The ripeness for receiving is the asking; the blossom-cup of the soul, ready to be filled with heavenly dews, is its prayer. When the soul's hunger for light and truth has waked its higher energies and roused the will to action, its only fitness for receiving the things of God, that action is prayer. Then God can give, and be toward the man as he would be; for the glory of God is to give himself. We thank you, Lord Christ, for it is by your pain alone that we rise toward knowing this glory of your Father and our Father.

Even for lower things: what might be unfit to do for a man who does not recognize the source of his life may be fit in the highest sense to grant when he comes to that source to ask; and even with an individual desire of one who mostly recognizes the Father, it may be well to give to him asking what would not benefit him unasked. For the real good of any gift, the giver must be in the gift, as God always is, for he is love; and the receiver must know and receive the giver in it. Every gift of God is a harbinger of his greatest and only sufficing gift, himself. No gift unrecognized as from God is at its own best; so many things God would gladly give us, even things we need because we are, must wait until we ask, that we may know where they come from. When in all gifts we find him, in him we shall find all things.

Sometimes one praying feels: "Were it not better to abstain? If this thing is good, will he not give it? Would he not be better pleased if I left it all to him?" I think it comes of a lack of faith and childlikeness, perhaps as a fear that, asking for what was not good, the prayer might be granted. Paul has no such thought; he says to cast all our care on God, who cares for us, and in everything to make our requests known to him. It may even come of ambition for spiritual distinction. In every request, heart and soul and mind ought to supply the low accompaniment, "Thy will be done"; but making any request brings us near to him, into communion with our Life, and helps us to think of him in all our affairs and give thanks in everything. Anything large enough for a wish to light on is large enough to hang a prayer on, and the thought of him to whom it goes will purify the desire; to say "Father, I should like this or that" would at once show us a bad wish and make us turn from it. So the Lord carried himself toward his Father when he said, "If it be possible, let this cup pass from me." But there was something he cared for more than his own fear, his Father's will: "Nevertheless, not my will, but thine be done." He has no fear that God will be displeased at his saying what he would like, nor does he regard his Father's plans as so fixed that his prayer could not alter them. The true son-faith comes with boldness, fearless that the Father will do anything but what is right fatherly and full of loving-kindness. We must not think to please him by any asceticism, even of the spirit; we must speak straight out to him. The Father may will otherwise, but his grace will be enough for the child.

There could be no riches but for need. God himself is made rich by man's necessity: by that he is rich to give, and through it we are rich by receiving.

Prevailing by entreaty over an unwilling God is a heathen notion, held by those who think him a hard master, or like the unjust judge. Nothing so quenches prayer as the notion of unwillingness in the ear that hears; when prayer is dull, nothing makes it flow like the thought that God wants to give us everything. We are told to come boldly to the throne of grace, to obtain mercy and find help in time of need. We shall be refused if that is better; but what is good our Father will give with divine good will. The Lord spoke his parable "to the end that they ought always to pray, *and not to faint*."

### MAN\'S DIFFICULTY CONCERNING PRAYER

*The passage:* They ought to pray, and not to faint.

"How should any design of the All-wise be altered by prayer of ours?" How are we to believe such a thing?

By reflecting that he is the All-wise, who sees what lies ahead and will not block his own path. The objection springs from the poorest idea of God in relation to us: it supposes he has plans for our part of creation irrespective of us. What is the whole system of things for but our education? Does God care more for suns and planets and ordered harmonies than for his children? I venture to say he cares more for oxen than for those. He lays no plans irrespective of his children; since he means them to be free, active, living things, he keeps space for them: room to struggle out of the chrysalis, to undergo the change that comes with the waking will, and to enter on the divine sports and labours of children in their Father's house. He may keep his plans in a measure unfixed, waiting the free desire of the individual soul. The first course of his children's education is meant to bring them to pray: shall the system appointed to that end be so hard and fast that he cannot answer the prayer it exists to produce? Often the prayer itself is God's end; but how will the further end, oneness of the child's heart with the Father's, be reached if the child knows the Father cannot answer? *Will not* may be for love, but a self-imposed *cannot*? How could he be Father who would keep no room for his children's babbled prayers? There must be a Godlike region of choice, as there is a human, however little we can conceive it. It would be a glory in such a system that its suns themselves wavered and throbbed at the pulse of a new child-life.

What perfection in a dwelling would it be if everything were fitted like the pigeon-holes of a cabinet? What stupid perfection would leave no room to change the plan when the facts change, even by the mighty change that his child is praying! See God's freedom in his sunsets, never one like another, and in his skies and ever-changing earth: all moves not by dead law but by the vital law of liberty, ordered from within. A fine perfection, where a thing had only one way! I may move my arm as I please: shall God be unable to move his? For himself he might desire no change, but he is God for his growing creatures, and change is the necessity of their existence. They need a mighty law of liberty, with not one atom of chance in it. Creation's one idea is the begetting of a free, divine will in us: shall that will, praying with the Father's, find itself manacled by foregone laws, and not rather be a new-born law, working new things? No man is so tied by divine law that he cannot modify his work; shall God not modify his? Law is but a mode of life-action, and Life's slave. Is God only the prisoned steam in the engine, ordered by valve and piston, or an indwelling, willing power, as a man's soul in his body dimly typifies? If you say he made things to go and left them, I say that if his machine interfered with his answering a single child's prayer, he would sweep it away, not to bring back chaos but to make room for his child. But order is divine and cannot obstruct its own higher ends; free order, neither chaos nor law senseless and unpossessed, is the home of Thought. If you say there can be but one perfect way, I answer that the perfect way to a certain crisis can hardly be the perfect way beyond it; and since the crisis depends on a will, not all can be exactly prepared. God is not occupied with a grand toy of worlds, suns, forces and waves; these are his workshops and tools for bringing out righteous men and women to fill his house of love. Would he have let his Son die for a law of nature? They exist only for the birth of sons and daughters, who must be born again from above. Those whose doom, to grow or perish, implies their inability to lay hold of the perfect must be given many a modification of the ideal; the best *means* cannot be the ideal Best. Truth embodied for the low cannot equal that for the higher, else it will fail; but as the low ascend, their revelation will ascend also.

That God cannot modify his plans without changing a single law of his world is to me absurd. If we can change, God can change, or he is less free than we: his plans, I say, not his principles or ends, God forbid! He changes them in a divine fashion, above ours as the heavens are higher than the earth. Since in his miracles Jesus did only in miniature what his Father does in the great, I will take from them an instance of answer to prayer that bears importantly on my subject. Poor was the making of wine in the stone water-pots compared with its making in the vine, whose live roots gather water from the earth; but it is precious as the interpreter of that, and as the outcome of our Lord's sympathy with ordinary human rejoicing. It is more precious to me for another element in its origin: the regard of our Lord for a wish of his mother. Alas, how often it is misunderstood!

His mother had suggested that here was an opportunity to appear in his greatness, the potent purveyor of wine for the failing feast. It was not in his plan, as his words show; the Lord never pretended anything, to enemy or mother. He lets her know they have different notions of his work: "What to me and thee, woman? My hour is not yet come." But there was that in his look and tone from which she knew her desire, scarcely half-formed into a request, was granted. What am I to conclude, worthy of the Son of God and the Son of Mary, but that at his mother's prayer he made room in his plans for what she desired? He did for her what he would himself rather have left alone. He did not always do as she would have him; here he could, since it did not interfere with his Father's will. Was the perfect son to be the only son of man who did nothing to please his mother but what fitted his own plan? Not so could he be the living heart of the children's great response to the Father, or the grand family become real. Alas for the son who would not willingly do for his mother something he would rather not do! Had it turned from his Father's will, he would not have done it, for that would have been to answer her prayer against her. The Son could change his intent and spoil nothing; so can the Father, for the Son does nothing but what he sees the Father do.

Granting that God may answer those who pray for themselves, what of prayer for others? It would surely be selfish to pray only for ourselves; but the question is of the use of the act, so let us pray for all we can. But here the ground is changed, if the fitness for answer lies in the praying of the one who prays: the attitude needed for reception belongs not to those *for* whom prayer is made but to him *by* whom it is made. What fitness, then, in praying for others? Will God give to another, for our asking, what he would not give without it, or, if it could be done without the person's own part, would he not do it without a second person? If God were a tyrant whose heart anxious love might soften, or one who could be informed and reasoned with, or one whose interest a showing of character, need, or claim might awaken, prayer for another would have plain reason, though prayer to one so unworthy would be degrading. But if we believe that God is the one unselfish, good being in the universe, whose one design is to make his children perfect as he is perfect; that he is always giving himself to us for our life; that he does his best for *every* man (which once I heard a bishop decline to acknowledge); and that he knows every man's needs and, for love's sake, will not spare one pang that may purify one of his children, how can we think he will alter his way with one because another prays for him?

I do not know how to answer the argument. I can only, feeling the difficulty, say again: "Yet I believe I may pray for my friend, for my enemy, for anybody! There must be some genuine good and power in one man's prayer for another to the maker of both, just because their maker is perfect." I shall not bring authority to bear, for authority can at best make us believe reason is there, not see it. The difficulty is the same when we hear the Lord himself pray to his Father for those the Father loves, with a special love from the deeps of divine approval, because they have received his Son. Those who believe in Jesus will be satisfied that reason and right lie in what he does, but that is not understanding. Though I cannot explain, I can show some ground on which, even untaught, a man left alone with his heart might pray for another.

If God has made us to love and long to help like himself; if there are people for whom we would give our lives to lift them out of the evil gulf of their ungodliness; if the love in us would, for the easing of the love he kindled, give them a gift; if amid a sore need to bless we find utter impotence, the fire burning and unable to get out; and if all our hope lies in God: what can we do but go to him with this our own difficulty and need? Where the natural refuge is, there must be help; the best argument that he has help is that we have need. If I can be helped through my friend, I think God will take the thing up and do what I cannot, helping my friend that I may be helped, perhaps helping me to help him. In praying for another we pray for ourselves, for the relief of the needs of our love; so this comes under the former kind. Would God give us love, the root of power, and leave it helpless in us? Where he could not alter, he could perhaps at least expedite something, in view of some help we might then give. If he desires that we work with him, that work surely helps him!

Some things are argued for by the very possibility of supposing them; but I think I can go farther and imagine at least the *where*, if not the *how*, of help for another in answer to prayer: the divine conditions in which it is born.

God is ever seeking to lift us into sharing his divine nature; God's kings, those who have borne witness to the truth with Jesus, share his glory even on the Father's throne. See the grandeur of the Holy One's creative love: nothing less will serve it than that his children, through his suffering and theirs, share the throne of his glory! Does such perfection bind him with difficulties, or set him freer to do what he would amid opposing forces? If his glory is in giving himself, and we must share it by giving ourselves, why should we not begin here and now? If he wills that other children be brought to his knee and fireside by the help of the children born from above through his eternal Son, why should he not have kept some margin of room in which their prayers may work for those they have to help, who are of the same life? I cannot tell how, but may not those prayers in some way increase God's opportunity for working his best will, the good ones adding to his means of helping the not yet good? One way is clear: the prayer will react on the mind that prays, which will shine brighter and enlighten more. But there must be more in it. Prayer in its perfect idea is a rising into the will of the Eternal: may not the Father's help become one with the child's prayer, and for the prayer of him he holds in his arms go forth for him who wills not yet to be lifted to his embrace? God cannot bring his children to his bosom at once, and not at all except through his own suffering and theirs. But will not any good parent find some way of granting the prayer of the child who says, "Papa, this is my brother's birthday: I have nothing to give him, and I do love him so! Could you give me something to give him, or give him something for me?"

"Still, could not God have given the gift without the prayer? And why should the good of anyone depend on the prayer of another?"

I can only answer with a question: why should my love be powerless to help another? We must not tie God to our measures of time, or think he has forgotten a prayer that we, seeing no answer, have forgotten. Death is not an impervious wall; the prayers go through it. We may have some to help in the next world because we have prayed for them in this: will it not be a boon to them to have an old friend to serve them? What I venture to say is this: if in God we live and move and have our being, and the very possibility of loving lies in our existing in the live air of love, God himself, then we are nearer each other than by any bodily nearness; and if prayer is a pulse that sets this atmosphere in motion, it brings us closer than the parts of our body are by their nerve-telegraphy. Surely in the Eternal hearts are never parted! Surely the system of things would be incomplete in relation to love, its best thing, if love had no help in prayer. If I love and cannot help, does my heart not move me to ask him to help who loves and can, him without whom I would neither love nor care to pray? Will he answer, "Child, do not trouble me; I am already doing all I can"? Then who that loved would not be content to be nowhere in the matter? But how if the limitless Love, the self-forgetting devotion that demanding all gives all, should say, "Child, I have been doing all I could; but now you are come, I shall be able to do more! Here is a corner for you, my little one: push at this thing to get it out of the way"? How if he should say, "Pray on, my child; it goes through me in help to him. We are of one mind about it; I help and you help. I shall have you all safe home with me by and by! Only we must work, and not lose heart. Let your light so shine that men glorify me by knowing I am light and no darkness"? What then? Oh that lovely picture by Michelangelo, with the young ones and the little ones come to help God to make Adam!

But the answer to prayer may come in a shape that seems a refusal, even in an increase of what we seek deliverance from. I know of one who prayed to love better: a sore division came between, out of which at length rose a dawn of tenderness.

Our vision is so circumscribed, our theories so small, our faith so continually fitted to our dwarf intellect, that there is endless room for rebellion against ourselves. We must not let our poor knowledge limit our intellect, our intellect our faith, or our faith our divine hope; reason must humbly watch over all, reason, the candle of the Lord.

Some argue for prayer not from any answer to be looked for but from the good gained in the spiritual attitude of praying. Some who believe in no ear to hear or heart to answer will yet pray; they say it does them good; they pray to nothing at all, but get spiritual benefit.

I will not contradict their testimony. So needful is prayer to the soul that the mere attitude of it may encourage a good mood. To pray to what is not is in logic a folly; yet the good they say comes of it may rebuke the worse folly of their unbelief, for it indicates that prayer is natural, and how could it be natural if inconsistent with the very mode of our being? Theirs is a better way than that of those who believe there is a God but not that he will answer, yet pray to him; that is more foolish and more immoral than praying to the No-god, and mocks him, themselves, and the truth.

On the other hand, though God give no assent to the individual prayer, and the prayer be for something not good enough to be a gift of God, the soul that prays will get good of it, if only in being brought a little nearer the Father and making way for coming again. Prayer reacts for good on the praying soul irrespective of answer. But to pray for the sake of the prayer, regardless of there being no one to hear, would indicate to me a nature morally false and not merely illogical, did I not suspect a vague apprehension of a Something diffused through all existence, and some shadowiest communion with it.

There are moods of such satisfaction in God that a man may feel nothing is left to pray for and he has only to wait for what the Lord will work; moods of such hungering that petition is crushed into an inarticulate crying; and a communion with God that asks for nothing, yet asks for everything. This last is the very essence of prayer, though not petition. A man may believe, not in God, but that there is a God, and yet not desire communion with him; but he who prays and does not faint will come to recognize that to talk with God is more than to have all prayers granted: it is the end of all prayer, granted or refused. And he who seeks the Father more than anything he can give is likely to have what he asks, for he is not likely to ask amiss.

Even those who ask amiss may sometimes be answered. The Father will never give a stone to the child who asks for bread; but I am not sure he will never give a stone to the child who asks for a stone. If the Father says, "That is a stone; it is no bread," and the child answers, "I am sure it is bread; I want it," may it not be well he should try his bread?

Now for another point in the parable, where I think I can help: the Lord's apparent recognition of delay in answering prayer. The structure of the parable takes delay for granted, yet he says, "He will avenge them speedily!"

The reconciling conclusion is that God loses no time, though the answer may not be immediate.

He may delay because it would not be safe to give us at once what we ask; we are not ready. To give before we could truly receive would destroy the heart and hope of prayer, and cease to be our Father. The delay itself may bring us nearer our help, deepen the desire, perfect the prayer, and ripen the receptive condition.

Again, time may be needed to work out the answer, not from any straitening in God but from our own condition and capacity, or our friend's. God is limited by regard for our best; our best implies education, in which we must have a large share, and being human it takes time. Perhaps the better the gift, the more time it needs. To give us a spiritual gift God may have to begin far back in our spirit, in regions unknown to us, and do much that we can be aware of only in the results; our consciousness is to our being as the flame of the volcano to the world-gulf it issues from, and God works in that gulf, behind our consciousness. He may be coming toward our consciousness from behind with his own presence, the one thing we most earnestly cry for, long before we are aware that he has answered and is visiting his child. To avenge speedily must mean to make no delay beyond what is necessary, to begin the moment it is possible. Because the Son of Man did not appear for thousands of years after men began to cry for a Saviour, shall we imagine he did not come the first moment it was well he should? To come sooner would have delayed his kingdom, not hastened it. God does not put off like the unrighteous judge, until irritated by the needy; he will hear while they are yet speaking; before they call he will answer.

The Lord uses words without anxiety about their misuse by those who do not search for his will in them; the word *avenge* may be kept from the parable without its special meaning there, yet it suggests a remark or two.

Of course no prayer for revenge that would gratify the selfishness of our nature, which God's fire must burn out of us, need think to be heard. Be sure, when the Lord prayed his Father to forgive those who crucified him, he uttered his own wish and his Father's will at once: God will never punish according to the abstract abomination of sin, as if men knew what they were doing. "Vengeance is mine," he says; rightly understood, we might as well pray for God's vengeance as for his forgiveness. That vengeance is to destroy the sin, to make the sinner abjure and hate it; no vengeance that seeks or effects less is satisfying. The man himself must turn against himself, and so be for himself. If nothing else will do, then hell-fire; if less will do, whatever brings repentance and self-repudiation is God's repayment.

Friends, if any prayers are offered against us, if God's vengeance is cried out for because of some wrong you or I have done, God grant us his vengeance! Let us not think we shall get off!

But perhaps the Lord was thinking here not of persecution or any human wrong but of the troubles that most trouble his true disciple. That comforts those whose foes are within them, for then he recognizes the evils of self against which we fight not as parts of ourselves but as our foes, on whom he will avenge the true self at strife with them. No evil is, or could be, of the essential being of the creature God made! What is not good, however associated with our being, is against it, its enemy. When we fight, he will avenge. Till we fight, evil has dominion over us, to make us miserable; no one can be other than miserable under the yoke of a nature contrary to his own. Comfort yourself, then, who find your own heart and soul too much for you: God will avenge his own elect. He is not delaying; he is at work for you. Only pray, and do not faint. Ask, and it shall be given you. Seek most the best things; to ask for them is to have them; the seed of them is in you, or you could not ask.

But from whatever quarter our troubles come, outside world or inside, let us still pray. In his own right way, the only way that could satisfy us, God will answer our prayers with help. He will avenge us of our adversaries, and that speedily. Only let us be adversaries to no man, but fountains of love and forgiving tenderness to all. And from no adversary, whether on the way with us or haunting the secret chamber of our hearts, let us hope to be delivered till we *have paid the last farthing*.

### THE LAST FARTHING

*The passage:* You will by no means come out from there until you have paid the last farthing.

The parables have a wonderful quality that the Lord himself points to but we seldom grasp: the mere intellect cannot understand them. They speak to the conscience and the will, not to the intellect and imagination. They are strong and direct but not definite. They are meant not to explain anything but to rouse a man to say, "I am not what I ought to be; I do not do what I ought to do!" The wise may give endless rambling interpretations, while the child who uses the parables to walk the one path keeps receiving light from them. The Lord's words, like those of all true teachers, are most obscured by those who interpret rather than do them, and theologians have hidden the gospel more than any adversary. Christ came for our will, not our understanding. He who does what he sees will understand; he who wants to understand rather than do will stumble on and talk foolishness, lacking what could understand such things. Only those who walk by what they find can understand the gospel and its parables. The speaker never meant anyone else to know intellectually what would harm him so known, since he would imagine he had grasped and even possessed it. When the pilgrim of the truth reaches the region of the parable, he finds its interpretation. It is not a fruit or jewel to store, but a well springing by the wayside.

Let us look at what the Lord himself said about his parables. I take Matthew xiii. 14, 15, because it is plainer and quotes Isaiah in full, after the Septuagint; the other Gospels should be read in its light (Mark's is so compressed it could be falsely read, and John seems to blame the devil directly for the blinding). The point is that those who close their deeper eyes by insincerity shall not be able to use even the superficial eyes of the understanding in the matter. Whether this follows by necessity or as a special punishment, it is God's will and comes from the live Truth: they shall not see what is not for such as they. It is the punishment of the true Love, and constantly fulfilled. If I know anything of God's truth, the objectors to Christianity I know do not; their arguments, though not false in themselves, miss the matter. They see what they argue against, but not what they think they argue against.

This removes the difficulty that the parables are plainly for teaching the truth, yet the Lord speaks of them as for concealing it. They are for the understanding of the practical man only, who does what he knows and seeks to understand vitally. They reveal to the live conscience, not to the keenest intellect, though they may rouse the conscience with glimpses of truth where a man is near waking. Ignorance may be both a punishment and a kindness, since all punishment is kindness, the best of which the man is then capable: "Because you will not do, you shall not see; it would be worse for you to see, being unwilling to do." They are punished by having the way closed; their own doing falls on them. To tell them things plainly would only harden them, since they would not act on them. They get parables, lanterns of the truth, clear to those who walk by their light and dark to those who will not; the latter would have the light in their eyes, and it would only blind them. Their punishment is to be in the wrong and stay there until they come out of it: "You choose the dark; you shall stay in it till its terrors make you cry out." God puts a seal on a man's will, either as his great punishment or his mighty favour: "Ye love the darkness, abide in the darkness"; "O woman, great is thy faith: be it done unto thee even as thou wilt!"

I do not know what special meaning lies in the magistrate, judge, and officer, beyond perhaps a general sense of the final approach coming by steps; but I think I know what "agree on the way" and "the uttermost farthing" mean. The parable appeals to the common sense of its hearers in every affair of righteousness. Settle the claim against you; compulsion waits behind it. Do now what you must do one day. Since payment cannot be escaped, escape the prison that enforces it. Do not drive Justice to extremities. You cannot escape the eternal law of things; yield of yourself, and do not make God compel you.

To the honest man, or the man who would be honest, this word is gracious. To the untrue it is a terrible threat; to him who is of the truth it is sweet as the most loving promise. He who is of God's mind rejoices at the word of the changeless Truth; the voice of the Right is his salvation. If God were not inexorably just, the feeblest lover of right would have no stay: "You are true, O Lord; one day I also shall be true!" "Render the right, cost what it may" is dreadful to those whose life is a falsehood; but those who love righteousness more than life would pay the last farthing. It is a joy deep as peace to know God is determined on such payment, determined to have his children clean as snow, so that with his help they not only make up for any wrong but at length, by eternal choice of good, become incapable under any temptation of doing what God would not do.

Men often cherish, without giving it form, the evil fancy that there is some way out of strict justice, some way to escape doing *all* that is required. There is none; such a way would be worse than the road to the everlasting fire, for it would end in eternal death. There is no heaven with a little hell in it, no keeping some of the devil in our hearts or pockets. Out Satan must go, every hair and feather! Do not think to be delivered from the necessity of *being* good by being made good. God is the God of the animals in a far lovelier way, I suspect, than many dare think, but he will not be God of a man by making a good beast of him. You must be good; neither death nor good company will make you so, though if you are willing and try, all helps will be given. A robe of imputed righteousness is the poorest of legal cobwebs spun by spiritual spiders. To me it seems an invention of well-meaning dulness to soothe insanity, though it has served as an escape from worse imaginations. John seems to point at it: he that does righteousness is righteous even as Christ is righteous. Christ is our righteousness not so that we escape punishment, still less being righteous, but as the live, potent creator of righteousness in us, so that, receiving his spirit, we resist unto blood, striving against sin, and know in ourselves how lovely righteousness is and how mean, ugly, and unnatural unrighteousness is. He *is* our righteousness, and it is no fiction, pretence, or imputation.

What keeps men from seeing righteousness and unrighteousness as they are is that they have been told many things are righteous or unrighteous which are neither. Righteousness is just fairness, from God to man and from man to God and to man: giving every one his large, mighty due. He who does this, and no one else, is righteous. Any system that persuades men there is any salvation but becoming righteous as Jesus is righteous, that a man can be made good as a good dog is good without his own willed share, or that he is saved by having his sins hidden under imputed righteousness, is, so far as it does so, of the devil and not of God. Thank God, even error does not injure the true of heart. They grow in the truth, and as love casts out fear, truth casts out falsehood.

I read in this parable that a man had better make up his mind to be righteous and fair, and pay what he owes in every relation of life, wherever one man may demand of another or complain of unfair play. Arrange matters with those who have anything against you while you are still together and things can be arranged; *you will have to*, under harder circumstances than now. Putting off is no use. There are means of compelling you.

"In this affair, however, I am in the right."

"If so, very well, for this affair. But I have reason to doubt whether you are capable of judging righteously in your own cause. Do you hate the man?"

"No, I don't hate him."

"Do you dislike him?"

"I can't say I *like* him."

"Do you love him as yourself?"

"Oh, come! come! no one does that!"

"Then no one is to be trusted when he thinks, however firmly, that he is all right and his neighbour all wrong in any matter between them."

"But I don't say I am all right and he is all wrong; there may be something to urge on his side. What I say is that I am more in the right than he."

"This is not fundamentally a question of things but of your spiritual condition and action toward your neighbour. If you were all right toward him, you could do him no wrong. Whatever the dispute, you owe him something you do not pay, as surely as you think he owes you something he will not pay."

"He would take immediate advantage of me if I owned that."

"So much the worse for him. Until you are fair to him, it does not matter to you whether he is unfair to you or not."

"I beg your pardon, it is just what does matter! I want nothing but my rights. What can matter to me more than my rights?"

"Your duties, your debts. You are all wrong. It matters little *to you* whether he gives you your rights; it is life or death to you whether you give him his. Whether or not he pays what you count his debt, you will be compelled to pay all you owe him. If you owe him a pound and he owes you a million, you must pay the pound whether he pays the million or not. If, owing you love, he gives you hate, you, owing him love, must still pay it. A love, justice, or praise withheld from you, a judgment passed on you without judgment, will not absolve you of a love unpaid, a justice undone, a praise withheld, a false judgment passed. These uttermost farthings, to say nothing of debts the world itself counts grievous wrongs, you must pay, whether he pays you or not. We have a good while to pay, but a crisis comes, always sooner than those expect who are not ready, when the unyielded demand will be followed by prison.

The same holds for every demand of God: refusing to pay makes an adversary who will compel you, for your own sake. If you say "I will not," he will see to it. There is a prison whose doors, we know, open only when the last farthing is paid.

The main debts God demands are those at the root of all right, which we owe in mind, soul, and being. Whatever in us could make an adversary or keep us from doing God's will or agreeing with our fellow must be yielded. Every relation to God and our fellow must be heartily acknowledged and met as real. Smaller debts, if any is small, follow.

If the man acknowledges the debt and would pay but cannot, the universe will be taxed to help him. If he accepts God's will, he is the Father's child, the Father's whole power and wealth are his, and the uttermost farthing will easily be paid. If he denies the debt, or acknowledges it and does nothing, then at last the prison! God in the dark can make a man thirst for the light who in the light sought only the dark. The cells may differ in darkness but not in this: every door opens only to payment. There is no day but God's will, and he who is of the night cannot roam the day forever; unfelt and unprized, the light must be taken from him that he may know what darkness is. When the darkness is perfect, when he is without the light he spent the light in slaying, then he will know darkness.

I think I have seen from afar something of the final prison, the innermost cell of the debtor of the universe; I will try to convey what it may be.

It is the vast outside, the ghastly dark beyond the gates of the city of which God is the light, where evil dogs range, silent as the dark, for there is no sound any more than sight. The time of signs is over; all were misused, and nothing is left by which to believe. The man wakes from the final struggle of death in absolute loneliness, worse than any deserted childhood. Nothing outside his consciousness reaches him: all is dark and dumb, no motion, no wind, no dream of change, no scent from a far field, no sign of God. God has so far withdrawn that the man is conscious only of what God has withdrawn from. In the live world he cared for nothing but himself; now, in the dead world, he is in God's prison, his own separated self. He would not believe in God because he never saw him; now he doubts there is such a thing as a man's face, or that he ever saw one rather than dreamed it. He never came near enough to human beings to know what one was, so may well doubt that they ever were, or that he was one.

Next after doubt comes reasoning on the doubt: "The only one must be God! I know no one but myself: I must be God!" Poor helpless dumb devil, his own glorious lord god! He will imagine himself that resistless force which, without his will or knowledge, is the law by which the sun burns and the stars keep their courses. His fancy will breed a thousand fancies, running riot like mice in a deserted house, and he will call it creation, and *his*. With no reality to set beside them or correct them by, and God's measured order and sweet graces nowhere for him, what he thinks will be his realities, for lack of what God thinks. Soon misery will beget on imagination a thousand shapes of woe, which he cannot rule or tell from real presences: a world of miserable contradictions and cold-fever dreams.

But no human imagination could adequately picture being left without a shadow of God's presence. If God gave it, man could not understand it; he knows neither God nor himself by understanding. No man who cares least about God was ever left in this world as God could leave him. I doubt any man from whom God had withdrawn could go on following his wickedness.

The most frightful thing that could befall a man, to his own consciousness, is to have to live an existence with which God had nothing to do. It could not be, for caused being ceases when the causation ceases; we live and do always in God, never out of him. But suppose the man so left that he seems utterly alone, yet, alas, with himself, with not the smallest interchange of thought or contact of existence possible. I believe he would be glad of contact with the worst-loathed insect, a shape of life beyond his own huge, void, formless being! He would be ready to worship his worst enemy, could he be aware of him; I imagine some such feeling in the devils' prayer to enter the swine. The misery would be not only the absence of all being but his own, but the fearful, endless, unavoidable presence of that self. Without the correction, reflection, and support of other presences, being is not merely unsafe but a horror, for anyone but God, who is his own being. For one whose idea is God's and the image of God, his own being is far too fragmentary and imperfect to be good company. It is the lovely creatures around us, in whom God gives himself, that save us, until we know him, from the frenzy of aloneness, which is Self, Self, Self. The man who minds only himself must at last go mad unless God interferes.

Is there any way out of the misery? Will the soul that could not believe in God with all his lovely world around testifying of him believe when shut in its own lonely, weary all-and-nothing? For a time it would try to believe it was nothing, a paltry dream that dreamed itself, and to hope the dream might dream it was no more. Self-loathing, for no sin and from no repentance or vision of better, would begin and grow to a misery no soul can tell. Yet if a being can feel self-disgust, is there not some room for hope, as much as a pinch of earth in a rock's cleft gives a pine? There must be hope while there is existence; where there is existence there must be God, and God is for ever good. But alas, the distance from the light! Such a soul is at the farthest verge of life's negation. No, not the farthest: a man is nearer heaven in deepest hell than just before he begins to reap the reward of his doings, for he is ready to receive the smallest show of the life that is as an unspeakable boon. All his years in the world he took the gifts of sun and air, earth and sea and human face divine as things that came because that was their way; now the poorest thinning of the darkness he would hail as men of old the glow of a descending angel, a messenger from God. He would not think of God, for it takes long; but hope, not yet seeming hope, would begin to dawn, and the thinner darkness would be a cave of light, a refuge from the horrid self of which he used to be so proud.

A man may think it impossible ever to think so unpleasantly of himself! But he has only to let things go, and it will be his real, natural way of thinking. All I have said is imaginary; but imagination is made to mirror truth, its images are more or less after the model of things that are, I suspect it is the region whence prophecy issues, and when we are true it will mirror nothing but truth. I deal here with the same light and darkness the Lord, Paul, John, Peter, and Jude dealt with. Ask yourself whether the faintest dawn, even of physical light, would not be welcome to such a soul as a refuge from the justly hated self.

And the light would grow across the awful gulf between the soul and its haven, repentance, which is the first pressure of the bosom of God. In the faint twilight the man would feel another thought beside his, another thinking Something near his dreary self, perhaps the man he had most wronged, hated, and despised, and would be glad someone was near: the man he had most injured and was most ashamed to meet would be a refuge from himself, oh, how welcome!

So I might imagine a thousand steps up from the darkness, each a little nearer the light; but ah, the weary way! He cannot come out until he has paid the uttermost farthing! Yet repentance once begun may grow more and more rapid. If God once gets a willing hold, if with but one finger he touches the man's self, swift as possibility he will draw him into the light. For the forlorn, self-ruined wretch was made to be a child of God, partaker of the divine nature, heir of God and joint heir with Christ. Out of the abyss he cast himself into, refusing to be heir of God, he must rise and be raised, setting out to the heart of God, the one goal and home of the human race, or the last glimmer of humanity will die from him. Whoever will live must cease to be a slave and become a child of God. There is no half-way house where ungodliness may be dallied with and not prove fatal. Whether few or many are cast into such a prison as I have imagined, there is no deliverance for a human soul, in it or out of it, but in paying the last farthing, in becoming lowly, penitent, self-refusing, and so receiving sonship and learning to cry, *Father*!

### ABBA, FATHER!

*The passage:* The spirit of adoption, by which we cry, "Abba, Father."

To cry *Father*! from a full heart is the hardest and gladdest thing in the world. I want to help whoever I can to call on the Father so.

Every form of systematic Christian teaching has things that check this outgoing of the heart, and some make it impossible. The more delicate the affections, the more easily they are chilled and blown aside; even the suspicion of a cold reception paralyzes them. Such a cold wind at the very gate of heaven (thank God, *outside* it!) is the so-called doctrine of *Adoption*. When a heart hears and believes, or half believes, that it is not God's child from the first of its being but may possibly be adopted, its love sinks in a cold faint: where is its own father, and who is this who would adopt it? In childhood this doctrine was to me a repellent cloud-phantom that took maturer thought to dissipate. But to stand against its hideousness takes not much knowledge or insight, only love that will not be denied, and courage to question the phantom.

A devout and honest scepticism on God's side, not to be put down by anything called authority, is needed by anyone who would know the liberty with which Christ makes free. What good men believe deserves respect, but no teaching, whoever the teachers, must come between the soul and the Father, who himself teaches his children. To accept authority may even be to refuse what it would teach, which stays misunderstood for lack of the natural doubt and inquiry we were meant to go through.

As no scripture is of private interpretation, so no feeling exists in one human heart alone; so I conclude many have groaned like me under this doctrine's supposed authority. Refusing to look up to God as Father is the one central wrong of the human affair, and being unable to, the one central misery; whatever clears a difficulty from the way of recognizing the Father undermines every difficulty in life.

"Is God not my Father," cries the child's heart, "that I need to be adopted? Adoption can never satisfy me. Am I not his to begin with? Is he my Father only by a legal contrivance? There may be much love in adoption, but accepting it from anyone makes me the child of another! Adoption by God would be blessed if another had given me being; but if he gave it, adoption is not reception but repudiation. 'O Father, am I not your child?'"

"No; but he will adopt you. He will not acknowledge you his child, but he will call you his child, and be a father to you."

"Alas," cries the child, "if he is not my father, he cannot become my father. A primary relation cannot be added later. That might matter little with earthly fatherhood, but for the very origin of my being, if he is only a maker and not a father, I am only a machine, not a child, not a man! It is false to say I was created in his image!

"It avails nothing to answer that we lost our birthright by the fall. I need not argue that *I* did not fall with Adam; I have fallen many a time, and a shadow on my soul, which I or another may call a curse, always comes between my heart and the blue of every sky. But can any repudiation, even God's, undo the fact of an existing origin? And it is not only that he made me: by whose power do I go on living? When he cast me out, did I begin to draw my being from myself, or from the devil? In whom do I live and move and have my being? I cannot fail to be the creature of God."

"But creation is not fatherhood."

"Creation in the image of God is. And if I am not in God's image, how can his word mean anything to me? 'He called them gods to whom the word of God came,' says the Master himself. To be fit to receive his word is to be of his kind. However defaced his image in me, it is his image still, one that can hear his word. What makes me evil and miserable is that the thing spoiled in me is the image of the Perfect; nothing can be evil but in virtue of a good hypostasis. Nothing can make me not the child of God. If someone says, 'Look at the animals: God made them, but you do not call them his children,' I answer, 'I am to blame; they are not! I cling to my blame: it is the seal of my childhood.' Two things I am sure of: God is to them 'a faithful creator,' and the sooner I put in force my claim to be his child, the better for them, for they too are fallen, though without blame."

"But you are evil: how can you be a child of the Good?"

"Just as many an evil son is the child of a good parent."

"But in him you call a good parent, there yet lay evil, and that accounts for the child being evil."

"I cannot explain. God let me be born through evil channels. But however I became an unworthy child, I cannot have ceased to be a child of God, as a child is always the child of the man he comes from. Is not my heart's very complaint at the word *Adoption* proof of it? Is it not the spirit of the child crying, 'Abba, Father'?"

"Yes; but that is the spirit of adoption; the text says so."

"Away with your adoption! I could not even be adopted if I were not such as adoption could reach, of the nature of God. Can a man adopt a dog? I must be of a nature for the word of God to come to, and so far of the divine nature, of the image of God! Had God dropped me and held no communication with me, I grant I could never have cried thus or cared when told I was not his child. But he has never repudiated me, and does not now desire to adopt me. Why should it grieve me to be told I am not a child of God, if I am not? If you say, 'Because you have learned to love him,' I answer that adoption would satisfy one who was not but would be a child; I cannot do without a father, and no adoption can give me one."

"But what is the good of all you say, if the child is such that the father cannot take him to his heart?"

"Ah, nothing, I grant, so long as the child does not desire to be taken to the father's heart; but the moment he does, it is everything to him to be indeed the child of the one his soul thirsts for. However bad I am, I am the child of God, and therein lies my blame. I would not lose my blame; in it lies my hope, the pledge of what I am meant to be and shall one day be, the child of God in spirit and in truth."

"Then you dare to say the apostle is wrong in what he so plainly teaches?"

"By no means; I say that our English presentation of his teaching is very misleading here. It is not for me to judge the learned and good men who revised the New Testament; I can only wonder why they kept the word *adoption*. Only Paul uses it in the New Testament. Liddell and Scott give 'Adoption as a son,' a mere submission to popular theology, citing only the New Testament. The relation of the word \[Greek: *niothesia*\] to \[Greek: *thetos*\], 'taken,' or rather '*placed* as one's child,' is, I presume, the sole ground for that translation; even plentiful and invariable usage could not justify it here, in face of what Paul elsewhere shows he means. The Greek word *might* be variously meant, though I find no use of it earlier than Paul; the English can mean but one thing, and not what Paul means. 'The spirit of adoption' Luther translates 'the spirit of a child'; *adoption*, *kindschaft*, or *childship*

Of two things I am sure: that by *niothesia* Paul did not intend *adoption*; and that if the Revisers had felt the word come between God and their hearts as I have, they could not have let it stay in their version.

Once more: Paul's word does not imply that God adopts children not his own, but that a second time he fathers his own, who are born a second time, from above; that he will be tenfold, infinitely, their father, and have them back into the very bosom they issued from, that they might learn they could live nowhere else, and be one with himself. It was for this that, in his Son, he died for them.

Let us look at the passage where he reveals his use of the word, in his epistle to the Galatians, iv. 1-7.

Paul writes that so long as the heir is a child he differs nothing from a slave though lord of all, being under guardians and stewards until the time his father set. So we too, as children, were in bondage under the rudiments of the world; but when the fullness of time came, God sent his Son, born of a woman under the law, to redeem those under the law, that we might receive the adoption of sons. Because we are sons, God sent the Spirit of his Son into our hearts, crying, Abba, Father; so we are no longer slaves but sons, and if sons, heirs through God.

How could the Revisers choose this last reading, "an heir through God," and keep *adoption*? The passage makes it as plain as Paul could that the word means raising a father's own child from tutelage and subjection to others, a state he says is no better than a slave's, to the position and rights of a son. None but a child could become a son; the idea is a spiritual coming of age: *only when the child is a man is he really and fully a son*. So it is in the earthly relation: many good children of good parents never know them or feel toward them as children might until they have grown and left home, or lost them. To be a child is not necessarily to be a son or daughter. Childship is the lower condition in the upward process toward sonship, the soil it grows from, without which it were impossible. God, like an earthly parent, cannot be content with children only; he must have sons and daughters, children of his soul, spirit, and love, not merely in that he loves them, or they love him, but in that they love like him, as he loves. For this he does not adopt them; he dies to give them himself and raise his own to his heart; he gives them a birth from above, born again out of himself and into himself, for he is the one and the all. They are not his real sons and daughters until they think, feel, and judge as he does, are at home with him and without fear before him because they love the same things and seek the same ends. For this we are created; it is the one end of our being and includes all others. Only unbelief can make men believe that God has cast them off and said they never were his children, while he spends himself to make us the children he designed, who would take him for their Father! He is our father all the time, but until we respond as true children he cannot let all the father out to us; there is no place for the dove of his tenderness to alight. Nothing will satisfy him, or do for us, but that we be one with our father! Because we are the sons of God, we must become the sons of God.

Some readers, alas, may find no cheer or dawn in the word *Father*, and feel no tremble of even a vanished emotion at it. It is hardly likely to be their fault. Children seldom love up to the mark of reason, and some are inexplicable to the parent who loves them, yet if the parent has been ordinarily kind, even a son grown worthless will sometimes feel in better moments a dim reflex of childship, a faintly pleasant or sorrowful remembrance of the father around whose neck his arms once clung. In my own childhood my father was the refuge from all the ills of life, even sharp pain. To the son or daughter who takes no pleasure in the name *Father* I say, "Interpret the word by all you have missed in life. Every time someone might have been to you a refuge from the wind, a covert from the tempest, the shadow of a great rock in a weary land, a father might have been a father indeed. If you have found a man or woman such a refuge, you have seen a shadow of the perfect, the back of the only man, the perfect Son of the perfect Father. All that human tenderness can give in nearness and readiness of love, and infinitely more, must be true of the perfect Father, the maker of fatherhood, the Father of all earth's fathers, especially those who have shown a father-heart."

This Father would make sons and daughters indeed: sons and daughters not merely by having come from his heart but by having returned to it, choosing to be what he is. He will have them share his being and nature, strong where he cares for strength, tender and gracious as he is, angry where and as he is angry. Even in the small matter of power, he will have them able to do whatever his Son Jesus could on earth, whose life was the perfect man's, whose works were perfected humanity's. Everything must at length be subject to man, as it was to The Man. When God can do what he will with a man, the man may do what he will with the world; he may walk on the sea like his Lord, and the deadliest thing cannot hurt him. The Lord said that whoever believes in him will do the works he does, and greater.

|         God, whose pleasure brought
| Man into being, stands away
| As it were, an handbreath off, to give
| Boom for the newly-made to live.

He has made us, but we have to be. All things were made *through* the Word, but what was made *in* the Word was life, the light of men.
