Those who live by this light, as Jesus lived, by obedience to the Father, share in their own making; the light becomes life in them, the life first born in Jesus; by obedience they become one with the godhead: "As many as received him, to them gave he power to become the sons of God." He does not *make* them sons of God but gives them power to become so: in choosing and obeying the truth, man becomes the true son of the Father of lights.

The Galatians passage is enough to show that *adoption* does not fit Paul's idea. While we merely obey the law God has laid on us without knowing the Father's heart that it comes from, we are slaves, not necessarily ignoble; but when we come to think *with* him, when the son's mind and action are the Father's, then the son is *of* the Father, and we are the sons of God. Both this passage and the one from Romans at the head of this sermon have *Abba, Father*, showing that he uses the word \[Greek: uiothesia\] in the same plain sense in both. Of the other passages where he uses it (he alone does, and may have coined it), one is in this same eighth chapter of Romans, which I keep for last. Another is in the next chapter, verse four, where he speaks of the \[Greek: viothesia\], literally the *son-placing* (the placing of sons in their true place), as belonging to the Jews. "Whose is the \[Greek: viothesia\]" cannot mean that they had already received it, or that it was theirs more than the Gentiles'; only that as the elder-brother-nation they had the foremost claim and would naturally first receive it, their best men always being nearest it. It must be wrought out first in those prepared for it; those were the Jews; of the Jews was the Son, bringing the sonship to all. Theirs was the \[Greek: viothesia\], as theirs was the gospel: to the Jew first, then the Gentile, though many a Gentile would have it before many a Jew. Those, and only those, who out of a true heart cry "*Abba, Father*," whether of some paltry little so-called church other than the body of Christ, or of none, are the sons and daughters of God.

Paul uses the word also in Ephesians i. 5. The authorized version has "predestinated us unto the adoption of children"; the revised has "foreordained us unto adoption as sons"; there is little to choose, and neither gives Paul's meaning. Whatever is gained by "of children" or "as sons" added to translate the word for which "adoption" alone serves elsewhere, the advantage goes only to the wrong interpretation.

Children we were; true sons we could never be, save through The Son. He brothers us. He takes us to the Father's knees, and beholding his face we grow sons indeed. We could never have known the Father's heart or felt it possible to love him as sons but for him who cast himself into the gulf between us. Even had we never sinned we could not have reached the sonship foreordained in him without him; we should have been little children loving the Father, but far from the sonhood that understands and adores. Those led by the Spirit of God are sons of God; anyone without the Spirit of Christ is none of his; and if we lack each other's spirits, we do not belong to each other. There is no unity but having the same spirit, and there is but one spirit, that of truth.

It remains to note yet another passage.

Paul never meant anything he wrote to contribute to a system of theology. One sign is that he uses this word, perhaps coined by himself, in different and apparently opposing, though never contradictory, senses; his meanings always vivify each other. His ideas are so large they strain his words, but the honest heart, which alone he addresses, is in no danger of misunderstanding, though the ignorant and unsteadfast still wrest them. He speaks of sonship now as the Israelite's possession, now as his who has learned to cry *Abba, Father*; and in the passage I consider last, Romans viii. 18-25, as yet to come, and as concerning our bodily rather than spiritual condition. This use, though unlike any other, is entirely consistent with the rest.

The 23rd verse says that we ourselves, who have the first fruits of the spirit, groan within ourselves, "waiting for adoption, the redemption of our body."

The ideas in this verse and the main use are plainly associated. Putting a son in his true place has outward relations as well as inward reality; the outward arises from the inward and reveals it. When the child, his time under tutors past, took his place as a son, he would naturally change his dress and manner of life; so when God's children cease to be slaves doing right from law and duty and become sons doing right from the essential love of God and neighbour, they too must exchange the garments of slavery for the robes of liberty, lay aside the body of this death, and appear in bodies like Christ's, with whom they inherit of the Father. But many who have learned to cry *Abba, Father* are yet far from the liberty of the sons of God: sons, no longer children, yet groaning as in bondage! The apostle is not working out an idea but writing a letter with burning heart; yet he gives lines enough for us to work it out, and this is how it takes shape:

We are the sons of God the moment we lift up our hearts seeking to be sons, the moment we begin to cry *Father*. But as the world must be redeemed first in a few men, so the soul is redeemed first in a few of its thoughts, wants, and ways, and the new creation takes long to finish. Having taken millions of years to bring the world to where a few of its inhabitants desire God, shall the creature of this new birth be perfected in a day? The process may now go tenfold faster, since the new factor of man's fellow-working, for which all the previous means existed, is developed; but its end is far below the horizon of man's vision:

The apostle speaks of the thing now as to come, now as done, when it is only begun; our ways of thought are like that. A man's heart may leap for joy the moment a strong hand grasps his hair amid the sea-waves; he may cry, "I am saved!" and be safe, but not saved. So we are sons when we begin to cry Father, but far from perfected sons. While there is in us the least taint of distrust, the least lingering of hate or fear, we have not received the sonship; we lack the life that raised Jesus' body; we have not attained to the resurrection of the dead, by which word in Philippians iii Paul means, I think, what he here means by the sonship, which he puts in apposition with the redemption of the body:

So long as our outward condition is not that of sons royal and divine; so long as the garments of our souls, these mortal bodies, are mean, torn, and stained; so long as we groan under sickness, weakness, weariness, old age, forgetfulness, and all heavy things, we have not received the sonship in full; we are only getting ready one day to creep from our chrysalids and spread the great heaven-storming wings of the psyches of God. We groan, burdened, waiting for the sonship, that is, the redemption of the body: its uplifting to be a fit house and revelation of the indwelling spirit, nay, like Christ's, of the deeper indwelling God. We shall always need bodies to manifest us to each other, bodies that present the soul with absolute truth. So the revealing of the sons of God in verse 19 is the redemption of the body: it is redeemed when made a fit revelation of them, as it was meant to be and always, imperfectly, was. This will be when truth is strong enough in the sons of God, for the soul makes the body. When we are sons of God in heart and soul, we shall be so in body too: "we shall be like him, for we shall see him as he is."

I care little to speculate on the kind of this body; two things only are needful to believe: that it will show the same self as before, and that it will show the being truly, without the defects of the former bodily revelation. Through their bodily presence we shall know our own infinitely better and find endlessly more delight in them. We must believe this or distrust the Father of our spirits. Until the body is redeemed, the \[Greek: uiothesia\] is only on the way, and it can come only by our working out the salvation he is working in us.

This redemption of the body, its deliverance from all that is amiss, unfinished, weak, or worn out and prevents the revelation of the sons of God, Paul calls not the *adoption* but the \[Greek: niothesia\], sonship in full manifestation. It is the slave left in the sons and daughters of God that has let the word *adoption* mislead them!

To see how the whole utterance hangs together, read verses 18 to 25, noticing the 19th: the earnest expectation of the creation waits for the revealing (*the outshining*) of the sons of God. When the sons of God show as they are, taking the appearance and place that belong to their sonship, sitting with *the* Son of God on their Father's throne, they shall in fact be lords of the lower creation, bestowers of liberty and peace upon it; then shall the creation, subjected to vanity for their sakes, find its freedom in their freedom and its gladness in their sonship. The animals will glory to serve them. Let the heartless scoff! The heart that cries *Abba, Father* cries to the God of the sparrow and the oxen; hope cannot go too far in hoping what that God will do for the creation that now groans and travails in pain because our higher birth is delayed. Shall not the judge of all the earth do right? Shall my heart be more compassionate than his?

If my interpretation does not satisfy a reader, I pray him not to spend his strength disputing my faith but in making sure of his own progress toward freedom and sonship. Only the child of God can judge truly; right opinion on the most momentous questions will deliver no man. The only cure for any ill in me or about me is to become the son of God I was born to be. Until Christ is born in me and I am revealed a son of God, pain and trouble will endure, and God grant they may! Call this presumption, and I can only widen my assertion: until you yourself are the son of God you were born to be, you will never find life a good thing. If I presume for myself, I presume for you; but I do not presume. Jesus Christ and his love-slave Paul both represented God as a Father perfect in love, grand in self-forgetfulness, supreme in righteousness, devoted to the lives he has uttered. I will not believe less of the Father than I can conceive of glory after the lines he has given me, after the radiation of his glory in the face of his Son. He is the express image of the Father, by which we, his imperfect images, are to read and understand him: imperfect, we have yet perfection enough to spell toward the perfect.

It comes to this, then, after the apostle's grand theory: the world exists for our education; it is the nursery of God's children, served by troubled slaves, troubled because the children are themselves slaves, children but not good children. Beyond its own will or knowledge, the whole creation works to develop the children of God into the sons of God. When at last the children have arisen and gone to their Father, clothed in the best robe, with ring and shoes, shining out in their predestined sonship, then shall the mountains and hills break forth before them into singing, and all the trees of the field clap their hands. Then shall the wolf dwell with the lamb, the leopard lie down with the kid, the calf, the young lion, and the fatling together, and a little child lead them. Then shall the fables of a golden age, which faith invented and unbelief threw into the past, unfold their essential reality, and the tale of paradise prove a truth by becoming a fact. Then shall every ideal show itself a necessity, aspiration though satisfied put forth longer wings, and the hunger after righteousness know itself blessed. Then first shall we know what was in the Shepherd's mind when he said, "*I came that they may have life, and may have it abundantly*."

### LIFE

*The passage:* Jesus says he came so that people may have life, and have it abundantly.

In a word, he came to supply all our lack, from the root outward; for what do we need but more life? The infant needs it, and gets it in abundance from his mother's breast. The old man, weak in limb and low in pulse, needs more of the life that seems to be ebbing. Weary with feebleness he calls on death, but it is life he wants; only the death encroaching in him desires death. He longs for rest, but death cannot rest, and neither can weakness: it takes strength as well as weariness to rest. How different is the strong man's weariness after overlong labour from the sick man's, who cries in the morning, "Would God it were evening!" and in the evening, "Would God it were morning!" Low-sunk life imagines itself weary of life, but it is death it is weary of. No soul that knew what life is ever cried out for its opposite. Why does the worn-out suicide seek death? Really to escape it---the death of homelessness, hunger and cold, of failure and disappointment, of exhausted passion, of madness, of a household he cannot rule, of crime and fear of discovery. He seeks darkness as a refuge from the death that possesses him; what he calls his life is a dream of horrible phantoms.

"More life!" is the unconscious prayer of all creation, groaning and travailing for the redemption of its lord, the son who is not yet a son. Can the dumb cry not be read in some animals' faces, some flowers' looks, many an aspect of Nature? All things are possible with God, but not all are easy. For him *to be* is easy, since there he deals only with his own perfect will; to create in the grand fashion in which he is now creating us is not. It must be hard, as divine history shows, to create what is not himself yet is like himself. The problem is to separate from himself, far enough that it can turn and look at him, what must still depend on him utterly. It must be able to choose him and say, "I will arise and go to my Father," and so develop the highest *Divine* it is capable of: the will for the good against the evil, the will to be one with its source and complete the circle by returning---to live consciously and willingly from that source, and be the thing the maker thought of when he willed it.

I imagine this separation from himself, such that will in the creature becomes possible, to be so difficult that God must begin inconceivably far back among the infinitesimal beginnings---eternal miles beyond the farthest discovery in *protoplasm*, let alone anything resembling man. There he sets in motion the division from himself that issues in individuality, consciousness, and conscious choice, at last pure because it chooses the right, the true, the divinely harmonious. But individuality is only a means; the end is oneness, impossible without it. Where there is only one there is no unity, no delight of love, no harmony, no good in being. Two at least are needed, and the more individuals, the richer and diviner the unity.

God is life, and the will-source of life. In the outflowing of that life I know him; and when I am told he is love, I see that without love he could not create. I know nothing deeper in him than love, nor believe there can be. The being of God is love, therefore creation, and I imagine he has been creating from all eternity. As he saw it was not good for man to be alone, he has never been alone himself: the Father has always had the Son, whose existence I imagine an easy outgoing of the Father's nature, while making beings like us I imagine the eternal labour of a God. Speaking humanly, as we must, I imagine God has never been content to be alone even with the Son of his love, the prime and perfect idea of humanity, but has from the first willed and laboured to give existence to other creatures who should share his blessedness. He is developing them into likeness to that Son, distant and small for long but for ever growing. Perhaps none of them yet, though unspeakably blessed, has any idea of the blessedness in store.

Let no one think that saying God undertook a hard labour in willing that many sons and daughters should share his nature lessens his glory! The greater the difficulty, the greater the glory of him who does what he undertook, with no half-success, but absolute satisfaction for innumerable radiant souls. He knew what it would cost: not only energy of will, and the separation from himself that is only creation's first step (itself perhaps painful), but suffering beyond our imagining, which only God could bear, in bringing out the God-life in the individual soul. That suffering is ever renewed, since the soul itself thwarts him and forces him to use only the best means its resistance leaves him, not the absolutely best. Man finds it hard to get what he wants because he does not want the best; God finds it hard to give because he would give the best and man will not take it. What Jesus did was what the Father is always doing; the suffering he endured was the Father's from the foundation of the world, reaching its climax in the Son. God provides the sacrifice, and the sacrifice is himself; he has always sacrificed himself for his creatures, for it lies in the essence of creating them. The worst heresy, next to dividing religion from righteousness, is to divide the Father from the Son and show the Son doing what the Father does not do. If Jesus suffered for men, it was because his Father suffers for men; he only came close to them through a body and senses, to bring their spirits close to his Father and theirs, giving them life and losing what could be lost of his own. The God and Father of Jesus Christ could never be satisfied with less than giving himself to his own! The unbeliever may easily imagine a better God than common theology offers; but no loving heart can reflect the length and breadth and depth and height of the love of God shown in his Son. The whole history is a divine agony to give divine life to creatures; its outcome will be radiant life, whose flower is unspeakable joy. Every child will look in the Father's eyes and be received with an infinite embrace.

The life the Lord came to give exceeds the highest undivine man's by far more than his exceeds the least human animal's, and there is more and more of it, to eternity, for each who will receive it. The Father has given the Son to have life in himself; that life is our light, and the life in us is what makes us see. The Christian's whole growth is the more and more life he receives. At first his religion may hardly differ from a prudent wish to save his soul; at last he loses that soul in the glory of love, and so saves it, and self becomes only the cloud on which God's white light divides into unspeakable harmonies.

"In the midst of life we are in death," said one; it is more true that in the midst of death we are in life. Life is the only reality; death is a shadow, a negation that owes the very idea of itself to what it denies. If God were not, there would not even be nothing, for nothingness owes its idea to existence.

One form of the question between matter and spirit is which came first and caused the other: did things without thought cause thought, or thought without things cause things? To those who cannot doubt that thought came first, it is plain that death can cure nothing and life must cure everything: the ills of existence come from its imperfection, not from itself, and we need more of it. We who *are* have nothing to do with death; what mourns mourns only from lack of enough being. We are vessels of life not yet full of its wine; where the wine does not reach, the clay cracks and aches. Who would pour out the wine that is there, instead of filling the vessel to the brim! Life is the law, the food, the necessity of life---everything. Many mistake the joy of life for life itself, and languish with a thirst at once poor and inextinguishable, yet even that thirst points to the one spring. These love self, not life, and self is only life's shadow; taken for life and set at the man's centre, it becomes a live death in him, a devil worshipped as a god, the worm of the death eternal that he clasps to his bosom as his one joy!

The soul full of harmonies has more life than the soul consumed with cares; the sage than the clown; the poet than the man whose life flows out that money may come in; the man who loves his fellow than the one who tries to exalt himself above him; the man who strives to be better than the one who longs for the praise of many. But the man to whom God is all in all, who feels his life-roots hid with Christ in God, who knows himself heir of all wealth and worlds and ages, and of power essential and in itself, has begun to be alive indeed.

In all the troubles of life let us remember that our one lack is life, the life-making presence in us. When most oppressed and weary of life, as our unbelief puts it, it is really the inroad of death we are weary of. When most inclined to sleep, let us rouse ourselves to live, and shun the false refuge of a weary collapse, a hopeless yielding to things as they are. The life in us is what is discontented; we need more of it, not more of the cause of its discontent. He has the victory who, in pain and weakness, cries out not for death or the repose of forgetfulness but for strength to fight---more power, more consciousness of being, more God in him; who, when sorest wounded, says with Sir Andrew Barton in the old ballad:

| Fight on my men, says Sir Andrew Barton,
|   I am hurt, but I am not slain;
| I\'ll lay me down and bleed awhile,
|   And then I\'ll rise and fight again;

---and says it with no silly notion of playing the hero (what have creatures not yet barely honest to do with heroism!), but because to fight so is the truth and the only way.

Suppose that in the extreme of our exhaustion an angel came, as to Elijah asleep in the desert, and showed us the waiting bread and water. Would we answer in faint unwillingness, "I am weary unto death! The battle is lost, or not worth gaining! The world is too much for me! Let me be gathered to my fathers and be at rest!"? I should be loath to think that if the enemy came roaring upon us in recognizable shape we would not, like the red-cross knight, stagger to meet him, heavy sword in nerveless arm. But when effort has failed, it takes more faith to rise and eat the food that will bring back more effort, travail and weariness. The true man trusts in a strength that is not his, that he does not feel and does not always even desire---a power that seems far off yet is at the root of his very fatigue and need of rest, rest as far from death as labour is. To trust in God's strength in our weakness; to say, "I am weak: so let me be: God is strong"; to seek from him, as the natural cure for all that is amiss, power to live even when weary---this is the victory that overcomes the world. To believe in God our strength despite all seeming denial, out of weakness, unbelief, numbness and lethargy; to believe in the wide-awake real through the stupefying dream; to will to wake when the whole being thirsts for godless repose---these are the broken steps up to the high fields where repose is a form of strength, strength of joy, and joy of love. "I am weak," says the true soul, "but not so weak that I would not be strong; not so sleepy that I would not see the sun rise; not so lame that I would not walk! Thanks be to him who perfects strength in weakness, and gives to his beloved while they sleep!"

If we will let our God and Father work his will with us, there is no limit to the enlargement of our existence. We have no conception how vast a consciousness we could be made capable of. Many can recall a moment when life seemed richer than ever before, some mostly in dreams; can a soul, awake or asleep, hold a bliss greater than the living God can seal, perpetuate and enlarge? Surely God could at any moment, by a word to a soul, by breathing afresh into the secret caves of its being, give it a sense of life before which the most exultant earthly triumph would pale to ashes! If a sunlit sea crowded with sails ever filled your soul as with a new gift of life, think what your sense of existence must be when he whose thought has only fringed its garment with such a show makes his home with you, and lets you feel, thinking the gladness of a God inside your being, that he carries you as a father in his bosom!

I have been speaking as if life and the consciousness of it were one; but consciousness of life is only the outcome of life. The real life is that which is of and by itself, life because it wills itself; this can only be God. But in us there ought to be a life corresponding to his, one that wills itself and so shares in its own being. There is an original act possible to man which must begin the reality of his existence: he must live in and by willing to live. A tree lives, with perhaps some vague consciousness known by but not to itself, only to the God who made it; I trust such lower life is on the way to thought and blessedness, in that process of separation from God in which living souls are created. But it is not life in the Bible's high sense: true life knows and rules itself, and eternal life is life come awake. Even the most exalted animals' life is not such, whatever it may become, though I refuse to believe their fate fixed as we see it. No one would call the dog's existence, looking strange lack out of wistful eyes, one to be satisfied with; as little can I call the human pleasure, refinement and common endeavour around me life. What in them is true dwells amid unchallenged corruption, which demands repentance, labour and prayer to destroy it. Most men and women seem to me to live a life in death, in unwhited sepulchres, withering forms possessed by spirits that slumber and babble in their dreams. That they do not feel it so is nothing; the sow wallowing in the mire may rightly call it her way of being clean, but theirs is not the life of the God-born. The day must come when they will hide their faces with the shame the good man now feels at the memory of living like them. Nothing is worthy of the name of life but the life eternal, God's life, shared in his degree by the man made in his image to be alive as he is alive. Its outcome and light is righteousness, love, grace and truth, but the life itself, like God, will not be defined: it is a power, the formless cause of form. It shows itself to the soul hungering after righteousness, which cannot show it to another except in the shining of its own light. The ignorant soul understands by eternal life only an endless elongation of consciousness; what God means is a being like his own, beyond decay or death, so essential that it has no relation to nothingness, having come out of the heart of Life, the fountain of being. It partakes of the divine nature and, like the Eternal himself, has nothing in common with what can pass or cease: God owes his being to no one, and his child has no lord but his Father.

This eternal life consists for man in absolute oneness with God and all divine modes of being. It is a love as deep as it is universal, as conscious as it is unspeakable, that can no more be reasoned about than life itself---a love whose presence is its all-sufficing proof and whose absence is an annihilating defect. He who lacks it cannot believe in it: how should death believe in life, though all the birds of God sing jubilant over the empty tomb! What must be the delight of such a being, the splendour of a consciousness, when immortal, uncreated life flows in silent fullness from the heart of hearts, in the great day of God and the individual soul!

What then is our practical relation to the original life---what have we to do toward attaining the resurrection from the dead? If we did not and could not make ourselves, how can we, now that we are made, do anything at the unknown roots of our being? What conscious unity can there be between the self-existent God and beings who live at another's will, who cannot refuse or cease to be, and can assert their relation to life only by refusing to be content with what is not life?

The self-existent God is that other by whose will we live, so the links of the unity must already exist and need only be joined. The link by which to close the circle of oneness with the Father must lie in the deepest part of man's nature, and there we find it: the *will*, the deepest, strongest, divinest thing in man, and so, I presume, in God, for so we find it in Jesus Christ. Only in the relation of the two wills, God's and his own, can a man come into vital contact with the All-in-all---not by a one-sided unity of complete dependence, but by a willed harmony of dual oneness. When a man entirely says, "Not my will, but thine be done," and so wills the will of God as to do it, he is one with God as a true son with a true father. When he wills that his being be conformed to its origin, the life in his life, and sets himself *to live the will of that causing life*, then, receiving God, he becomes in that act a partaker of the divine nature, a true son and heir; by a son's obedience he receives the very life of the Father. Obedience joins the links of the eternal round, as the other side of the creative will: will is God's will, obedience is man's will, and the two make one. The root-life, knowing well the thousand troubles it would bring on him, has created and goes on creating other lives so that, though incapable of self-being, they may by willed obedience share the bliss of his essential being. If we do the will of God, eternal life is ours---not mere continuity of existence, which in itself is as worthless as hell, but a being one with the essential Life, and so within God's reach to fill with the endless outgoings of his love. Our souls shall be vessels ever growing and ever filled with more of the life proceeding from the Father and the Son, from God the ordaining and God the obedient. We can never know the delight of that life until we have it; but even now it may seem to the holy fancy too glorious to bear, as if we must die of very life to awake to a higher life and be filled with a wine our souls were too weak to hold. To be aware for one moment of such pure, simple love toward even one of my fellows as I trust I shall one day have toward each would bring a sense of life my imagination can now only feebly shadow---a mighty glory of consciousness! It would not be always present, for my love, not my glory in that love, is my life. For we are made for love, not for self. Our neighbour is our refuge; *self* is our demon-foe. Every man is the image of God to every man, and as we love him we shall know it. The precious thing to a human soul is, and one day shall be known to be, every human soul. And if it is so between man and man, must not the glory of existence be endlessly redoubled in the creature's infinite love---for all love is infinite---to the infinite God, the one great life, than whom there is no other, only lovely shadows of him!

Reader, to whom my words seem inflation and foolish excitement: you may care nothing that I seem to myself to speak words of truth and soberness. But what if they seem otherwise to you not merely because you are not whole, but because you do not thirst after harmony, are not of the truth, have not yet begun to live? How should the reveller, coming out worn and wasted from haunts where the violent seize joy by force and find her perish in their arms, sing with the sons of the morning when the ocean of light bursts from the fountain of the east? As little can you, with your mind full of petty cares or pettier ambitions, understand the groaning and travailing of creation. You may honestly desire to save your own wretched soul, but you can as yet know little of your need of him who is *the first and the last and the living one*.

### THE FEAR OF GOD

*The passage:* John says that when he saw him he fell at his feet as though dead, but the Lord laid his right hand on him and said, Do not be afraid; I am the first and the last and the Living One.

Fear is not found only in religion's first beginnings. While love is imperfect there is room for torment; only love, which alone can fill a heart, casts out fear. What holds in the beginnings of religion holds, in some degree, until the religion, that is the love, is perfected.

The unknown that is yet known to exist will always be more or less formidable. When it is known as immeasurably greater than we and as making demands on us, the more vaguely we grasp the demands the more room there is for anxiety, and a guilty conscience can raise anxiety to terror. The kind and degree of terror depend on the mind that dwells on the Supreme. It needs no exalted idea of God: those fear him most who imagine him like their own evil selves, only stronger, free to work his arbitrary will on them. Thinking him only a little higher than themselves brings no unity with him---who are so far apart as those on the same level of hate and distrust? Power without love and dependence without righteousness wake a worship without devotion, a loathsome flattery. Where the notion of God is better but the conscience troubled, his goodness does little to remove apprehension; the same sense of evil and offence that gave rise to the bloody sacrifice still works in most who call themselves Christians. Naturally, man's first emotion toward the being he calls God, yet knows so little, is fear.

Where fear can exist, it is well that it should, causing continual uneasiness until nothing less than love casts it out. For one who does not know God and cannot be satisfied with himself, fear of God is as reasonable as it is natural, and serves powerfully to develop his true humanity. Neither the savage nor the self-sufficient sage, whether we call him degenerate or undeveloped, is rightly human; the humanity is there but must be born in each, and for its birth everything natural, fear included, must do its part. Until love, which is the truth toward God, can cast out fear, it is well that fear hold: it is a bond, however poor, between what is and what creates, which can be broken only by tightening an infinitely closer one. God must be terrible to those far from him, for they fear he will do---is doing---with them what they cannot desire and can ill endure. Such as many men are, and all without God would become, they must prefer a devil, for his supreme selfishness, to a God who will die for his creatures and insists on giving himself to them and on their being unselfish and blessed like himself. They must become what is the power and worth of life, or die; the vague sense of this frightens them. They love their poor existence as it is; God loves it as it must be---and they fear him.

Men of low, undeveloped nature hold such false notions of what is good and what the Power requires that they cannot but fear, and their devotion is lost in sacrifices of ingratiation. God takes them where they are, accepts what they honestly offer, and helps them outgrow themselves, to offer the true offering and know him whom they ignorantly worship. He will not abolish their fear except with the truth of his own being; until they apprehend it, he receives their sacrifices of blood, the invention of their sore need, changing only their modes for the time. He destroys the lie that is not all a lie only by the truth that is all true. Though he loves them utterly, he does not tell them there is nothing in him to fear; that would drive them from him for ever. While they remain as they are, there is much in him that cannot but affright them, and they do well to fear him; it is the only true relation between them. To remove that fear other than by letting them know his love with its purifying fire, which for ages perhaps they cannot know, would give them up utterly to evil. Persuade men, while they still love their own will and are slaves to passionate impulse, that fear is vile and an insult to God, who will have none of it---what follows? They will insult God as a discarded idol, a superstition, a falsehood under whose evil influence they have too long groaned. How much will they learn of him then? Nor would the old fear be long in returning, now before powers once thought inanimate and endowed with souls by fear's imagination: spiritual chaos would come again. God loves righteousness; rather than do an unfair thing he would lay down his Godhead and assert himself in ceasing to be; that his creature might not die of ignorance he died as much as a God could die, divinely more than man can, to give him himself. Such a God may well look fearful from afar to a creature who recognizes no imperative good in himself, fears only suffering, and has no aspiration, only wretched ambition. But as such a creature comes nearer to him in whose likeness he was begun---as the eternal right begins to disclose itself, as he becomes capable of the idea that his kind belongs to him as he could never belong to himself, and nears the capacity to see that his individuality is perfected only in love of his neighbour and his being ends only in oneness with its source (I do not say as he sees these things, but as he nears seeing them)---his terror of the God of his life will abate. Though far from guessing the bliss awaiting him, he draws nearer the goal of his nature, the central secret joy of sonship to a God who loves righteousness and hates iniquity, does nothing he would not permit in his creature, and demands nothing of him he would not do himself.

The fire of God---his essential being, his love, his creative power---differs from its earthly symbol in burning only at a distance. The farther from him, the worse it burns; as we turn and approach him the burning turns to comfort, which will grow to such bliss that the heart cries out, with a gladness no other can reach, that it desires no one in heaven or on earth but him. The glory of being, shining on the corrupt and deathly, must like the sun consume the dead. What it burns in the soul is not of the soul, yet the foul growth feeding on the soul clings so close that the burning is felt through every spiritual nerve. When the parasites are consumed---when the man yields his self and its low world and returns to his lord and God---what he felt only as burning he will feel as love, comfort, strength, an eternal ever-growing life. For now he lives, and life cannot hurt life, only death, which ought to be destroyed. God is life essential and eternal, and death, which is corruption living only in the decay of life's things, cannot live in his sight. If any child of the father finds he is afraid before him, that the thought of God is a discomfort or a terror, let him not stop to dress but rush in his nakedness, a true child, from his own evil and God's terror into the salvation of the Father's arms, the home he was sent from that he might learn it was home. If an evil father is won by the sight of the child he was vexed with running to his embrace, how much more will the Father of our spirits, who seeks nothing but his children themselves, receive him with open arms!

Self, accepted as the law of self, is life's one demon-enemy; God is the only Saviour from it and from all that is not God, for God is life and all else is death. Life destroys death and all of death's kind.

When John saw the glory of the Son of Man, he fell at his feet as one dead. Whether he saw him in what we vaguely call a vision, or as humanly as when he leaned on his bosom, I do not care to ask: it would take all glorious shapes of humanity to reveal Jesus, and he knew the right way to show himself to John. It seems to me that the words spoken can have come from no mere vision, that the very Lord himself spoke them, while John plainly received them as a message to give again. Points in the description of the Lord's appearance commend themselves even to our ignorance by their grandeur and fitness. Something akin to terror overwhelmed the three disciples who saw his glory on the mount; but since then John had leaned on the Lord's bosom, followed him to the judgment seat without denying his name, borne witness to his resurrection, and suffered for him, and was now "in the isle that is called Patmos, for the word of God and the testimony of Jesus." Why should he be afraid? No glory even of God *should* breed terror; when a child of God is afraid, the word *Father* is not yet freely formed by his spiritual mouth. The glory can breed terror only in one who can be terrified by it, and while he is such it is well the terror be bred and maintained until he seeks refuge from it in the only place where it is not---the bosom of the glory.

One point is not distinguishable in the Greek: whether it means "one like unto *the* Son of Man" or "one like unto *a* son of man." The authorized version has the former, the revised the latter. I incline to the former: John saw him like the man he had known so well, and the dazzling glory---nothing blinds so much as light---made him unsure, not any unlikeness mixed with the likeness.

But the appearance of The Son of Man was not meant to breed terror in the son of man to whom he came. Why then was John afraid? Joy to us that he was, for the words that follow---surely no phantasmic outcome of uncertain vision or blinding terror! They bear the best sign of their source: they must be from the heart of our great Brother, the one Man, Christ Jesus, divinely human!

It was only the imperfection of the disciple, unfinished in faith and so in everything a man needs, that caused his terror. The Lord's words imply this: he speaks of the very thing that made John afraid as the thing that ought to have taken all fear from him. For what was the glory John saw---the head and hair pouring out radiance, white as white wool, snow-white as his garments on Mount Hermon; his eyes like a flame of fire in the midst of it, and his countenance as the sun shining in his strength; the feet with a darker glow, like fine brass burning in a furnace, a humbler glory than the head's, as if in memory of his humiliation; the girdle under his breast, golden between the snow and the brass---but the effulgence of his glory who was himself the effulgence of the Father's, the poor expression of the unutterable truth that was the very reason John ought not to be afraid?---"He laid his right hand upon me, saying unto me, Fear not; I am the first and the last, and the living one."

Our terror must be endless until we come heart to heart with the fire-core of the universe, the first and the last and the living one!

But oh, the joy to be told by Power himself, the first and the last, the living one---told what we can then see *must* be true, but are so slow to believe---that the cure for trembling is the presence of Power; that fear cannot stand before Strength; that the visible God is the destruction of death; that the one safety in the universe is the perfect nearness of the Living One! God is being; death is nowhere! He told Paul that strength is made perfect in weakness; here he teaches John that weakness, not strength, is what to fear. All appearances of strength that might rightly move terror are false; the true Strong is the *One*, as the true Good is the *One*. The Living One has the power of life; the Evil One only the power of death, whose very nature is a self-necessity for being destroyed.

But the glory of even the mildest show of the Living One is such that the dearest of his apostles, the best of the children of men, is cowed at the sight. John has not yet learned that glory is part of his inheritance, that nothing in a man made in God's image is alien to the most glorious of heavenly shows; so he falls as dead---when lo, the voice of him who was and is and is for evermore tells him not to be afraid, for the one reason that he is the first and the last, the living one! For what shall be the joy, peace and completion of him that lives but closest contact with his Life?---as close as before he issued from it, only infinitely higher in kind, since now it is willed on *both* sides. He who has had a beginning needs the indwelling power of that beginning to be complete, not merely to his consciousness but in himself, ended where he began, with an "endless ending." Then he is complete as God is, one with the self-existent. John Boanerges, far from trembling because he on whose bosom he had leaned now stood with the glory of his love streaming forth, ought to have felt the more joyful and safe the more the living one's strength was manifest. Jesus was never fit to be trusted because he wore the weakness of the flesh, but because he was strong enough to take that weakness as the garment in which his strength could best work; now that strength shone with its own light. Had John been as close in spirit as in bodily presence, he would have fallen at his feet, but not as one dead: as one too full of joy to stand before the life that was feeding his, to embrace and kiss the feet of him who had now, as in a second resurrection from above, arisen before him in yet heavenlier glory.

It is the man of evil, of self-seeking design, not he who would fain do right and submit at once to the Master's word even in his worst time, who is reasonably afraid of power. When God is no longer the world's ruler and there is one stronger than he, when there is might inherent in evil and making-energy in what is by nature destruction, then will be the time to dread power. Even then the bad man would have no security against being ground up for some no-idea of the Power of darkness. And then the good, instead of trembling, should resolve with the Lord of light to endure all, to let every billow of evil break on him, and do no smallest ill or tell the whitest lie for God, knowing that territory so gained could be only a province of the kingdom of darkness. If there were two powers, one of evil and one of good, as men in ignorance have not unnaturally imagined, duty would reveal who is born of the good power, while one born of the evil could do nothing but be evil. But Good only can create; and were Evil ever so much the stronger, men's duty would remain the same: to hold by the Living One and defy Power to its worst, like Prometheus on his rock defying Jove and for ever dying, thus for ever foiling the Evil. Evil can destroy only itself and its own; at worst it could cause a succession of deaths, from each of which the defiant soul would rise to more victorious endurance, until it laughed Evil in the face and the demon-god shrank withered. For those who believe that good is the one power, and that evil exists only because for a time it cannot help subserving the good, what place is there for fear? The strong and the good are one; and if our hope coincides with God's and is rooted in his will, what should we do but rejoice in the glory of the First and the Last?

The First and the Last is the enclosing defence of the castle of our being; the Master is before and behind, began it and will see it endless. He garrisons it; he is the living, live-making one.

The reason for not fearing before God is that he is all-glorious, all-perfect. Our being needs such a God; children can do with nothing less than the Father, the infinite one. Beyond all the order the intellect can descry, the imagination devise, the hungriest heart long for or the fullest heart thank for, as the heavens are higher than the earth, rise the thought, the creation, the love of the God who is in Christ, his God and our God, his Father and our Father.

Ages before Jesus was born, where even Moses and his law were yet unknown, the suffering heart of humanity saw and was persuaded that its peace lay only with the first, the last, the living one:

*Oh, that you would hide me in the grave... and remember me!... You will call, and I will answer you; you will long for the work of your hands.*

### THE VOICE OF JOB

*The passage:* Job says: if only you would hide me in the grave and keep me secret until your wrath is past, set me a time and remember me! If a man dies, will he live again? All the days of my appointed time I will wait until my change comes. You will call, and I will answer; you will long for the work of your hands.

The book of Job seems to me the most daring of poems: from a position of the most vantageless realism, it assaults the very citadel of the ideal! Its hero sits among the ashes, covered with loathsome boils, scraping himself with a potsherd. Sore in body, mind, heart and spirit, he is the type of humanity in the depths of misery. I mean the individual endlessly repeated: Job is *the human being*, at the centre of pain's assaults and fear's invasions, which sooner or later threaten to overwhelm every man, show him enslaved to the external, and stir him to seek the infinite, where alone he is free. In leaden despair Job cries aloud to the unseen Might, scarcely known, which he still regards as the God of his life. It is no slave's cry, no more than Prometheus' defiance of Jupiter from his rock. Job is more overwhelmed than the Titan, for he is in infinite perplexity as well as pain, but there is no cowardice in his cry. Before the Judge he asserts his innocence and will not grovel, knowing that would insult the holy. He feels he has not deserved such suffering, and will neither tell nor listen to lies for God.

Prometheus is more stonily patient than Job. Job is no Stoic; he bemoans himself like a brave child who thinks he suffers wrong and recoils, bewildered, from the unreason of it. Prometheus faces a tyrant he despises, and endures with unbewailing unsubmission, upheld by knowing he fights humanity's battle against an all but all-powerful Selfishness; endurance is the only weapon that avails. Job is more troubled because the one who has laid on him a torture so heavy that he takes his flesh in his teeth for pain is He who is the head and heart, the beginning and end of things. Job cannot and will not believe him a tyrant; even as he pleads against his dealing with him he loves him, and looks to him as the source of life and gladness. He dares not think God unjust, yet cannot allow that he has done anything to deserve this. Hence his profound perplexity: how can the two be reconciled? It has not occurred to him that what it would be unfair to lay on him as punishment may be laid on him as favour, by a supreme love that would give him blessing beyond all prayer---blessing he would not dare ask if he saw what it requires, but which, once known, he would endure all again to have. So he is sorely divided: he must not think God has mistaken him, yet the seeming mistake forces itself on him at every turn. He had never relaxed his pursuit of a godly life, yet the God he acknowledged in all his ways has raised his hand against him as rarely against any transgressor: his children swept away, his possessions gone, he is poor as the poorest and diseased as the vilest. Worse than all, death is denied him; he is left to linger in self-loathing, meeting at every turn the awful suggestion that God has cast him off. He does not deny there is evil in him---"Do you open your eyes on such a one," he pleads, "and bring *me* into judgment with *you*?"---but he denies he has been a wicked man, a doer of what he knew to be evil, or that there was any guile in him. And who, because he knows and laments the guile in himself, will dare deny that there was once a Nathanael in the world? Had Job been Calvinist or Lutheran, the book would have been very different: his perplexity would have been how a just God could require of a man more than he could do, and punish him as if he were a perfect being who chose the evil he knew in all its enormity. I call no one Master but Christ, and from him I learn that his quarrel with us is that we will not do what we know, will not come to him that we may have life. How much more powerful with men would be expostulation grounded on what they will not do rather than on what they have done!

Job's child-like judgment of God had never been perverted, to the dishonour of the great Father, by the low popular theories that explain God's ways through people who do not understand *him*. Such explanations suit those who do not care to know him, content to stare from afar at the cloud where thunders and voices come from; to those who would arise and go to the Father they are a grievous burden, threatening to sink them to Tophet. The contradiction between Job's idea of God's justice and what has befallen him haunts him, with a sting worse than all his other misery, but it is not fixed in hopelessness by an accepted explanation more frightful than itself. Let the world-sphinx put what riddles she will; she can devour no man while he waits an answer from the world-redeemer. Job refused his friends' explanation because he knew it false; accepting the kind many would give him today would have meant being devoured at once by the monster. He holds to the skirt of God's garment and besieges his door, the one source of true answer. No answer will do but the one God alone can give; for who but God can justify God's ways to his creature?

We must not look for logic in a soul whose very consciousness is contradiction; misery is rarely logical, being itself a discord. Yet it is natural that Job, *feeling* as if God wronged him, should long to see God, to stand face to face with him and confront the One. His one hope is that if he could get God to listen and lay his case clear, God would see how things were, explain the matter, and give him peace: the man in the ashes would know that the foundations of the world still stand, and that God has not closed his eyes or---horror of horrors---ceased to be just. Surely the Just would set the mind of his justice-loving creature at rest!

His friends are good, religious men of the pharisaic type: men who pay court to God instead of coming into his presence as children; men whose traditional theories satisfy their feeble intellects and who therefore think everyone must accept them or perish; men anxious to appease God rather than trust him, who would rather receive salvation from God than God as their salvation. They would persuade Job to confess he was a hypocrite, insisting such things could not come on him but for wickedness, and, since they knew of no open wickedness, for some secret vileness. They grow angry when he will not be persuaded against his knowledge of himself. Nor was Job overweening, for the prologue prepares us: God says to the accuser, "Have you considered my servant Job, that there is none like him in the earth, a perfect and upright man, one who fears God and shuns evil?" God gives him into Satan's hand confident of the result, and at the end approves what Job has said of himself. Yet the very appearance of God is enough to turn Job against himself: his part was to trust God altogether, in spite of every appearance and reality. He will justify himself no more. He sees that though God has not been punishing him for sins, he is far from what he ought to be: "Behold, I am vile; what shall I answer you? I will lay my hand on my mouth."

But let us look more closely at how Job thinks and speaks about God, and how he addresses him---so different from the pharisaic in all ages, in none more than our own.

Growing indignant at the idea that his nature needed such treatment, Job cries, "Am I a sea or a whale, that you set a watch over me?"---*you know I am not wicked.* "You set a print upon the heels of my feet!"---*so that my footprints show the way I have gone.* To his friends he cries: "Will you speak wickedly for God, and talk deceitfully for him?"---*do you not know I am the man I say?* "Will you accept his person?"---*siding with him against me?* "Is it good that he should search you out? or as one man mocks another, do you so mock him?"---*saying what you do not think?* "He will surely reprove you, if you secretly accept persons!"---*even God's own person!*

Such words please the Father of spirits. He is not a God who accepts flattery that puts him above obligation to his creatures, who demands a righteousness different from his own, or who deals ungenerously with his poverty-stricken children. Job is confident of receiving justice. His faith is profound, yet he is always complaining---a strange but natural conflict that is simply the form his faith takes in trouble. Even while he declares the hardness of his treatment, he seems sure that all he needs to set things right is admission to God's presence: to be heard must be to have justice. Used by any living man, his language would horrify the religious of today in proportion to their lack of truth, just as it horrified his three friends, the honest pharisees of the time, whose religion was "doctrine" and rebuke. God speaks no word of rebuke to Job for the freedom of his speech; he has always sought such as Job to worship him. It is those who know only the outsides of religion, who speak of God only as the *Almighty* or *Providence*, who say of a man who goes close up to God and speaks out of the deepest in the nature God made, "he is irreverent." For such men, to utter the name of God in the drama, highest of human arts, is blasphemy. They pay court to God without loving him, treating him as far away rather than as the only home. They accept God's person. "Shall not his excellency"---another thing than what you admire---"make you afraid? Shall not his dread"---another thing than the pagan respect you show---"fall upon you?"

In his desolation, God's truth seems to Job, more plainly than ever, the one thing that holds together the world that came into being by the word of his mouth. If God cannot be reached, nothing is left to the man so lately greatest in the east but despair and hell. Like a child escaping the dogs of the street, he flings the door open and rushes, not looking back, to seek the presence of the living one. Carrying the burden of his death, he cries, "Look what you have laid on me! Shall mortal man, the helpless creature you made, bear a cross like this?" God is the God of comfort, known to man as refuge and life-giver or not known at all. But alas, Job cannot come to him; he sees his face nowhere; God has hidden himself. "Oh that I knew where I might find him, that I might come even to his seat! I would order my cause before him and fill my mouth with arguments. Will he plead against me with his great power? No! he would put strength in me. There the righteous might dispute with him, and I should be delivered for ever from my judge. Behold, I go forward, but he is not there, and backward, but I cannot perceive him; on the left hand and the right I cannot behold him. But he knows the way I take; when he has tried me, I shall come forth as gold."

He cannot find him! Yet he is in God's presence all the time, and his words enter the ear of God his Saviour.

The grandeur of the poem is that Job pleads his cause with God against all the remonstrance of religious authority, recognizing no one but God, and is justified in it. Grandest of all, he implies, if he does not say, that God *owes* something to his creature. This is the beginning of the greatest discovery: that God owes *himself* to the creature he made in his image, since he made him unable to live without him. This highest claim of his creatures upon him is his divinest gift to them. To fulfil it he sent his Son, that he himself, the Father of him and of us, might follow into our hearts. Perhaps the worst thing in a theology built out of man's dull *possible*, and not out of the being and deeds and words of Jesus Christ, is its constant impression that God acknowledges no such obligation. Are we not the clay, and he the potter? how can the clay claim from the potter? We are the clay, but *his* clay, spiritual, live clay, with needs and desires and *rights*---clay worth the Son of God's dying for, that it might learn to consent to be shaped to honour. We can have no merits, but God has given us rights. Apart from him we have nothing, but having come forth from him we have rights toward him---never, never *against* him! His whole labour is to induce us to claim what he gave us the rights to in creating us. We had no claim to be created, which would be absurd, but being made, we have claims on our maker: our needs are our claims. A man who will not provide for his child's hunger is condemned by the whole world.

"Ah, but," says the partisan of God, "the Almighty's relation is very different from an earthly father's; there is no parallel." I grant it. The man did not create the child; he only yielded to an impulse created in himself. God is infinitely more bound to provide for *his* child than any man for his; the relation is divinely closer. Every hunger, aspiration and longing of our nature is to be referred to him, who made all our needs and made us creatures of a thousand necessities---and have we no claim on him? We have innumerable, infinite claims; and his one great claim on us is that we should claim our claims of him.

It is terrible to represent God as unrelated to us in a way that allows appeal to his righteousness. How could he be righteous, or have anything to do as judge of all the earth, if he owed us nothing? He owes us nothing that he does not pay like a God; but it is of the devil to imagine imperfection or disgrace in obligation. So far is God from thinking so that in every act of his being he puts himself under obligation to his creatures. When doubt and dread invade and the voice of love in the soul is dumb, nothing can please the Father better than to hear his child cry to him from whom he came, "Here I am, O God! You made me: give me what you made me needing." The child's necessity, weakness and helplessness are the strongest of his claims. If I am a whale, I can claim a sea; a sea, room to roll and break in waves; a lion, I seek my meat from God; a child, I claim above all that if any need of mine is denied, it be by the love of a father who will let me see his face and plead my cause before him. This must be just what God desires! What would he have but that his children claim their birthright, the divine nature, God himself, which he made them for and has been giving them ever since he began them to be? A created need is a created claim, and the Father's heart delights to acknowledge it. The story of Jesus is the heart of his answer, not primarily to prayers, but to the divine necessities of the children he has sent out into his universe.

Away with the thought that God could have been a perfect, adorable creator and done less than he has done for his children, that any being but Jesus Christ could have been worthy of all-glorifying worship, that his nature could have lacked absolute love and self-devotion, these highest splendours!

In the light of this truth, look at the words at the head of this sermon: "Oh that you would hide me in the grave!" Job appeals to his creator, whom his sufferings force him to regard as displeased with him, though he knows not why. *We* know he was not displeased, but Job had not read the preface to his own story. He asks God to hide him and forget him for a time, so that the maker's desire to see once more the work of his hands may awake in him, and silence, absence and loss may speak for the buried one and make the parent's heart long for the child's face. Then "you shall call and I will answer you; you will have a desire to the work of your hands," and Job will rise in joy to plead the cause of his righteousness with confidence. For God is nearer to a man than anything he has made: what is closer than the making and the made, the willing and the answering that owes its power to answer to the other's will and desire? God must love his creature who looks up to him with hungry eyes---hungry for life, acknowledgment, justice. The whole existence of a creature is one entire claim on his creator: just *therefore*, let him do with me as he will, even seat me in the ashes to scrape myself with a potsherd, I will bring forward my claim all the more, insist on it, assail with it the ear and heart of the Father. Is it not the sweetest music a maker's ear can hear---except the word of the perfect Son, "Lo, I come to do your will, O God!"? We, imperfect sons, shall learn to say the same, and so enter our birthright and share the divine nature; that is why the Son came and died, to slay our selfishness and mean hollow pride and wake our childhood. We are our Father's debtors for our needs, rights and claims, and he will have us pay the uttermost farthing. So true is he that he will even compel us, through misery if need be, to put in our claims, for he knows we have eternal need of them: without the essential rights of his being, who can live?

I protest, therefore, against all teaching that, born of the faithlessness of the human heart, gives the impression that God's exceeding goodness toward man is not the natural and necessary outcome of his being. The root of every heresy popular in the church draws nourishment only from unbelief. The idea that God would be God all the same, as glorious as he needed to be, had he not taken on himself the divine toil of bringing home his wandered children or done anything to seek and save the lost, is false as hell. It is as if God could be less than he is---less than perfect, less than Jesus Christ, less than Love absolute; as if the God of the New Testament were not under his own perfect necessity of loving-kindness, but had made himself better than, by his own nature and love, he needed to be. They would have it that, being unbound, he deserves the greater homage! So he might, if he were not our Father. But to think of the living God not as our Father but as one who has greatly condescended, not at all bound by his own willed grandeur of righteous nature to do as he has done, is killing to all but a slavish devotion, and thinks of him as nothing like the God we see in Jesus Christ.

It will be answered that we have fallen and God is thereby freed from any obligation, if any ever were. That is another lie. No amount of wrong-doing in a child frees a parent from the divine necessity of doing all he can to deliver him; the bond cannot be broken. It is the vulgar, slavish, worldly idea of freedom that it means being bound to nothing. God's idea of liberty is not such! Speaking as a man: the more vital obligation he takes on, the more children he creates with claims on him, the freer he is as creator and giver of life, the essence of his Godhead; to make scope for his essence is to be free. Our Lord teaches that the truth, known by obedience to him, will make us free: our freedom lies in living the truth of our relations to God and man. To be alone in the universe would be to be slave to unspeakable longings and lonelinesses. And speaking humanly again: God could not be satisfied with himself without doing all a God and Father could do for the creatures he made---without doing just what he has done, is doing and will do to deliver his sons and daughters and bring them home rejoicing. To answer the human heart's cry, "Would that I could see him! would that I might come before him and look on him face to face!" he sent his Son, the express image of his person. And lest our understanding of God be limited by the constant presence to our weak, dullable spiritual sense of any embodiment whatever, he took him away. Having seen him, in his absence we understand him better. If we dare, like Job, to plead with him in the heart-eating troubles that come from being unable to love the misrepresentation of him our would-be teachers hold out; if we speak out before him what seems to us right, will he not be pleased with his children's love of righteousness---the truth that will not part him from his righteousness? He will not plead against us with his great power but put strength in us, and instruct us where we are wrong. The heart that wants to think aright, worships him not as a tyrant but as perfectly righteous, will not call unjust things righteous, and clings to the skirt of his garment with pleading eyes is the Father's delight, and he will open to it riches of his being it has not entered that heart to conceive. "O Lord, they tell me I have so offended against your law that as I am you cannot look on me, but threaten me with eternal banishment from you. But if you do not look on me, how can I ever be other than I am? I was born in sin and have never known myself clean: how can I see sin as you see it, or cleanse myself? You must take me as I am and cleanse me. Is it not impossible that I should behold the final goodness of good and final evilness of evil? How then can I deserve eternal torment? Had I known good and evil as you see them and still chosen evil, I know not what I should not deserve; but you know it has always been something good in the evil that enticed my selfish heart, and all my kind's. You require us to forgive: surely you forgive freely! Bound you may be to destroy evil, but are you bound to keep the sinner alive to punish him, even if it makes him no better? Sin cannot be deep as life, for you are the life; sorrow and pain go deeper than sin, for they reach the divine in us: you can suffer, though you will not sin. To see men suffer might make us shun evil but never hate it; we might see that you hate sin, but never that you love the sinner. Chastise us in loving kindness, and we shall not faint. Evil is deep in us, but we are not all evil, for we love righteousness; and are you not yourself, in your Son, the sacrifice for our sins, the atonement of our breach? You made us subject to vanity but have taken your godlike share of the consequences. Could we ever have come to know good as you know it except through the sea of sin and the fire of cleansing? They tell me I must say *for Christ's sake* or you will not pardon, and it takes the heart out of my poor love; I thank you that nowhere in your gospel does one of your servants say such a thing. In spite of our fears and grovelling, our weakness and wrongs, you will be to us what you are, such a perfect Father as no loving child-heart on earth could invent. You will take our sins on yourself and give us your life to live with. You bear our griefs and carry our sorrows; surely you will one day enable us to pay every debt we owe each other. You have given yourself to us that, having you, we may be eternally alive with your life. We run within the circle of what men call your wrath, and find ourselves clasped in the zone of your love!"

But be it well understood that when I say *rights* I do not mean *merits* of any sort. We can deserve nothing from him, in the sense of any right proceeding from ourselves. All our rights are such as the bounty of inconceivable love has glorified our being with, bestowed for the one purpose of being satisfied, and so deep and delicate that they cannot be satisfied until we long for it with our deepest desire. The giver of them came to men, lived with men, and died by the hands of men that they might possess these rights abundantly; God could do no more to fulfil his part, save what he is still doing every hour for every individual. Our rights are rights with God himself at the heart of them. He could recall them only by making us cease; while we exist, by the being that is ours, they are ours. If he could not make us care for the rights he gave out of the depth of his creative being, I think he would have to uncreate us. As to deserving, that is absurd; he had to die in the endeavour to make us listen and receive. "When you have done all that is commanded you, say, We are unprofitable servants; we have done what it was our duty to do." Duty is a thing prepaid: it can never have desert. There is no claim on God that springs from us; all is from him.

But lest any unchildlike soul, in arrogance and ignorance, think to stand on his rights *against* God and demand this or that after the will of the flesh, I will name some of the things he has a right to, perhaps first of all, from the God of his life, because of the divine germ in him. He has a claim on God for any pain, want, disappointment or misery that would help show him to himself as the fool he is; a claim to be punished to the last scorpion of the whip, spared no pang that may urge him toward repentance; even a claim to be sent into the outer darkness, whether what we call hell or something speechlessly worse, if nothing less will do. He has a claim to be compelled to repent: hedged in on every side, with the great shepherd's strong, sharp-toothed sheep-dogs sent after him one after another to thwart every desire, foil every plan and frustrate every hope, until he sees that nothing will ease his pain or make life worth having but the presence of the living God within him, that nothing is good but God's will and nothing noble enough for the heart's desire but oneness with the eternal. For this God must make him yield his very being, that he may enter in and dwell with him.

That the man would enforce none of these claims is nothing: it is not a man who owes them to him but the eternal God, bound by his own will of right toward the creature he made to discharge them. His candle burns dim in the man's soul; it must shine as the sun. For what is the all-pervading dissatisfaction of his wretched being but an unrecognized hunger after his Father's righteousness? The soul God made hungers thus, though the selfish, usurping self that is its consciousness hungers only for low things, ever trying in vain to fill its narrow content with husks. For even the most degraded chamber of the soul, the temple of the deified Self, cannot be filled with less than God; the usurping Self must be miserable until it stops looking at itself in the mirror of Satan and opens its innermost closet to the God who means to dwell there and make peace.

He who has looked on the face of God in Jesus Christ, whose heart overflows even a little with answering love, sees God standing with full hands, and his children hanging back, refusing to take, doubting the God-heart that is absolute in truth and love.

It is not at first easy to see how God answers Job; I find no explanation at all of why he afflicted him. He justifies Job's words, says Job has spoken rightly of him and his friends have not, and calls up before him one after another the works of his hands. The answer, like some of our Lord's, seems addressed to Job himself and not to his intellect: to the revealing, God-like imagination in the man, not to any logical faculty. It sets forth God's power as seen in his handiwork and wondered at by the men of the time, and all that is said of the creatures concerns their show to men's eyes. In what belongs to nature's deeper meanings and her mediation between us and God, her appearances are her truths, far deeper than any scientific discoveries about them. God cares *most* for the show of things, for it is the face of far deeper things; in it we see, as in a glass darkly, the face of the unseen. To know a primrose is higher than to know all its botany, just as to know Christ is infinitely higher than to know all theology, all that is said about his person or babbled about his work. The body of man likewise exists for its outside, the face and form in which revelation dwells, not for its hidden secrets. So Nature exists primarily for her face, her look, her appeals to heart and imagination, her simple service to human need, and not for the secrets to be discovered in her and turned to man's further use. What is our knowledge of the oxygen, hydrogen, nitrogen and ozone of the atmosphere beside the blowing of the wind on our faces, or the analysis of water beside the babble of a running stream? What is any knowledge of things to the heart, beside its child-play with the Eternal! By infinite decomposition we should learn nothing more of what a thing is, for the moment we decompose it, it ceases to be and its meaning is gone. Far more than astronomy does the mere look of the vault over our heads do for us: think what our idea of greatness, God and infinitude would be if we were reared under a flat white ceiling instead of a blue, light-spangled firmament! I would not depreciate science, but its discoveries are unspeakably less precious than the merest gifts of Nature, which we take unthinking from her hands from morning to night. One day, I trust, we shall enter their secrets from within, by natural contact between our heart and theirs. When we are one with God we may understand in an hour what no man of science, investigating from the surface with the keenest intellect, would reach in a lifetime. Whether such power will come in this world or only beyond it matters nothing to me: life is one, God is the same there and here, and I shall be the same, however larger the life he gives me.

The argument implied in the poem, not expressed, seems to be this: Job saw God so far before him in power, and his works so far beyond his understanding that they filled him with wonder---creation's vast might, the times and seasons, the marvels of the heavens, the springs of the sea, the gates of death; the animals with their generations, providing, beauties and instincts; the strange and awful behemoth on land and leviathan in the sea, perhaps now vanished from the living world. Beholding these, he ought to have reasoned that he who could work so grandly beyond his understanding must use wisdom in things that touched him nearer, though they came no nearer his understanding: "Shall he that contends with the Almighty instruct him? He that reproves God, let him answer it." "Will you annul my judgment? Will you condemn me that you may be righteous?" In this world power is no *proof* of righteousness; but was it likely that he who could create should be unrighteous? Did such works foreshadow injustice toward the creature made in his image? Did Job understand even his own being, history and destiny? Might not God's ways with him be beyond him, and might he not trust him to do justice? His maker was so much greater than Job that his ways with him might well be beyond Job's comprehension; God's thoughts were higher than his as the heavens are higher than the earth!

The true child, the righteous man, will trust absolutely, against all appearances, the God who has created in him the love of righteousness.

God does not, I say, tell Job why he afflicted him; he rouses his child-heart to trust. For the rest of Job's life, I imagine, his slowly vanishing perplexities would give him fresh meditations about God and his ways, new opportunities of trusting him, and added means of growing in the knowledge of God. Everything we cannot understand is a closed book of larger knowledge and blessedness, whose clasps the blessed perplexity urges us to open. There can be nothing that is not in itself a righteous intelligibility---whether intelligible to us matters nothing. The awful thing would be anything unintelligible in its nature: that would be the same as *no God*. That God knows is enough for me; I shall know, if I can know. To think God did not know would be death, for it would be to conclude there was no God to know.

How much more than Job are we bound, who know him in his Son as Love, to trust God in all the troubling questions that press on us about the motions and results of things! About the lower animals, and about souls that seem never to wake from the sleep of death or to fall into it again, we will trust him.
