In the confusion of Job's thoughts---how could they be otherwise, with two such facts staring at each other, that God was just yet *punishing* a righteous man as if he were wicked?---he could not yet conceive or receive the thought that approving love itself might inflict or allow the torture, that such suffering is granted only to a righteous man that he may be made perfect. I can well imagine that, as one moment he doubted God's righteousness and the next cried, "Though he slay me, yet will I trust in him," some doubt of God's existence mingled in the chaos. To deny God's existence may, paradoxical as it seems, involve less unbelief than the smallest *yielding* to doubt of his goodness; a man may be haunted with doubts and only grow in faith. Doubts are the Living One's messengers to rouse the honest, the first knock at our door of things not yet understood but needing to be, and they commonly meet the inhospitable reception given angels who do not come in their own likeness. Doubt must precede every deeper assurance, for uncertainties are what we first see when we look into a region unexplored. So Job's longing to see God may well include a mighty desire to be assured of God's being; to acknowledge is not to be sure of God. One great point in the poem is that when Job hears God's voice, though it gives no explanation, it is enough: he knows that God is, and hears his creature's cry. That God is there, knowing all about him, is enough to reconcile seeming contradictions and make the worst ills of outer life endurable. God settled everything when, answering out of the whirlwind, he showed he had not forsaken him. Nothing but a far closer divine presence can ever make life fit for a son of man, for the simplest of reasons: he is made in God's image and must have in him the reality of which his being is the image; without it his conscious self is a mask, a spiritual emptiness. For the present, though, Job, yielding to God, was calmed and satisfied. Perhaps he came to see that if anything God could do would make him doubt God, it was time to be so troubled, that the imperfection of his knowledge of God might be revealed and an earthquake of his being bring to the surface the gold of God in him. To know that our faith is weak is the first step toward strengthening it; to be capable of distrusting is death; to know that we are, and cry out, is to begin to live---to begin to be made such that we cannot distrust, such that God may do anything with us and we shall never doubt him. Until doubt is impossible we lack true, childlike knowledge of God; for either God is such that one *may* distrust him, or to distrust him is the greatest injustice a man can commit, so that if we can distrust him we know him imperfectly or not at all. But all he was required to receive at the moment was the argument from God's loving wisdom in his power to his loving wisdom in everything else. For power is a real and good thing, giving an immediate impression that it proceeds from goodness; however long it may last after goodness is gone, it was never born of anything but goodness. In the deepest fact power and goodness are one.

Seeing God, Job forgets all he wanted to say. He had meant to order his cause before him; he had longed to see him to defend himself, imagining God as well as his friends wrongly accused him; but his speech is gone. To justify himself in the presence of Him who is Righteousness seems to him what it is: foolish, worthless labour. If God does not see him righteous he is not righteous and may hold his peace; if he is, God knows it better than he. All the evils and imperfections of his nature rise before him in the presence of the one pure one, who is right and has no selfishness. "Behold, I am vile; what shall I answer you? I will lay my hand on my mouth. Once have I spoken, but I will not answer; yes, twice, but I will proceed no further." Again, after God has called behemoth and leviathan to witness for him, he replies, "I know that you can do everything, and that no thought can be withheld from you. Who is he that hides counsel without knowledge?" This was the question with which God first made his presence known; in Job's mouth now it is the humble confession, "*I am that foolish man*." "Therefore I have uttered what I understood not; things too wonderful for me." "Hear, I beg you, and I will speak"---henceforth he will cry to be taught, not to justify himself. "I will demand of you, and you declare to me"---he will seek the more diligently to know God's counsel. That he cannot understand will no longer distress him but urge him to fresh endeavour after the knowledge of him who is perfect in all his doings. "I have heard of you by the hearing of the ear, but now my eye sees you. Therefore I abhor myself, and repent in dust and ashes."

Job had his desire: he saw the face of God---and abhorred himself in dust and ashes. He sought justification and found self-abhorrence. Was this punishment? The farthest from it possible. It was the best thing, to begin with, that the face of God could do for him. Self-contempt is a blessed gift when its giver is the visible glory of the Living One. To see there is to partake; to turn from self is to begin to be pure of heart. Job was right that he did not deserve to be punished so for his sins; neither did he deserve to see God's face, yet that crown of all gifts was given him---and it was to see himself vile. By the very sufferings he had cried out against, the living one came near, and he was silent. Oh the divine generosity that lets us be abashed and self-condemned before the Holy, to come so near him as to see ourselves dark spots against his brightness! We must be of his kind, else no sight of him could make us feel small and ugly and unclean. To be rebuked before him is to be his. Good as Job was, he had never yet been right near God; now God has become very real to him; he knows in very deed that God is he with whom he has to do. God laid these troubles on him that through them he might draw nigh and enable him to know him.

Two things are clearly contained in this poem: that not every man deserves to be punished everlastingly from the Lord's presence for his sins; and that the best of men, when he sees the face of God, will know himself vile. God is just and will never deal with the sinner as if he could commit the pure sin; yet if the best man is not delivered from himself, that self will sink him into Tophet.

Any man may, like Job, plead his cause with God, though perhaps not to like justification: he gives us liberty to speak and hears with absolute fairness. But, blessed be God, the one result for all who draw nigh will be to see him plainly, surely right, the perfect Saviour, the profoundest refuge even from the wrongs of their own being, so seeing him they will abhor themselves and rejoice in him. And, as the poem indicates, when we turn from ourselves to him, becoming true---being to God and ourselves what we are---he will turn again our captivity; those who sowed in tears shall reap in joy and come again rejoicing, bringing their sheaves. Then the waters that rise from God's fountains will run in God's channels.

The prosperity that follows Job's submission embodies a great truth. A man must do right even if it send him to Hades, even to hell for ever, yet while the Lord lives we need not fear: *all* good things must grow out of and hang upon the one central good, the one law of life, the Will, the One Good. To submit absolutely to him is the only reason; then circumstance as well as all being must bud and blossom as the rose. What matter whether in this world or the next, if one day I know my life as perfect bliss, with no limitation, hindrance, pain or sorrow more than it can dominate in peace and perfect assurance?

I care not whether the book of Job is a history or a poem; I think it is both, and care not how much of each. It was probably, in the childlike days of the world, a well-known story in the east, which some man whom God had made wise to understand his will and ways took up and told as a poet. Who can say its age? It must be before Moses. I would gladly throw out Elihu's part as an interpolation. One in whom, of all men I have known, I put the greatest trust once told me, in effect, that the language of the rest of the poem and that of Elihu differ as Chaucer's and Shakspere's do.

The poem is difficult, and in the original inaccessible to me; but through all the evident inadequacy of our translation, who can fail to hear two souls, the poet's and Job's, crying with agonized hope that, whatever the evil shows around them, truth and righteousness are the heart of things? Job's faith, even his hope, seems at times on the point of giving way; he struggles like a drowning man, but his courage revives as his head rises. Christians we call ourselves! What would our faith not be, were it as much greater than Job's as the word from Jesus' mouth is mightier than what he heard out of the whirlwind! Here is a book of faith indeed, before the law was given by Moses: Grace and Truth have visited us---but where is our faith?

Friends, our cross may be heavy and the *via dolorosa* rough, but we have claims on God, the right to cry to him for help. He has spent and is spending himself to give us our birthright, which is righteousness. We cannot be saved but by leaving our sins: we shall not be condemned for past sins, but we shall be if we love the darkness rather than the light and refuse to come to him that we may have life. God offers us the one thing we cannot live without, his own self: we must make room for him and cleanse our hearts that he may come in, and do as the Master tells us, who knew all about the Father and the way to him---*we must deny ourselves, and take up our cross daily, and follow him*.

### SELF-DENIAL

*The passage:* Jesus told everyone that whoever would follow him must deny himself, take up his cross daily, and follow; whoever would save his life will lose it, and whoever loses it for his sake will save it (St. Luke ix. 23, 24).

Christ is the way out and the way in: out of slavery, conscious or unconscious, into liberty; out of the unhomeliness of things to the home we desire but do not know; from the stormy skirts of the Father's garments to the peace of his bosom. To picture him we need endless figures, some of them opposed: he is the door of the sheepfold and the shepherd of the sheep, the way and the leader in the way, the rock that followed and the captain of our salvation. We must become as little children, and Christ must be born in us. We must learn of him, and the one lesson he has to give is himself: he first does all he wants us to do and is first all he wants us to be. We must see things as he saw them, take God's will as the very life of our being, and care neither for our own way nor for what is said of us. The world must be nothing to us.

I would not be misunderstood. By *the world* I mean not the world God makes and means, still less the human hearts in it, but the world man makes by choosing the perversion of his own nature, a world opposed to God's. I mean every way of judging and thinking---political, economic, ecclesiastical, social or individual---that is not God's: that leaves him out of account, does not set his will supreme as the one law of life, and cares for the customs of society, the practice of the trade, the usage of the time, rather than what is true and right. From everything against the teaching of Jesus, from the world in the heart of the best man in it, and above all in his own heart, the disciple must turn to follow him. The first thing in all progress is to leave something behind; to follow him is to leave one's self behind. "If any man would come after me, let him deny himself."

Some take this to mean that the disciple must go against his likings because they are his likings, thinking that abstaining from what is pleasant or doing what is disagreeable, thwarting the lower nature, is good in itself. I will not dare say what a man may not get good from, if he does it in simplicity and honesty. When a man, wanting to do right, mistakenly does wrong, God will see that he is shown the better way, perhaps by means of the very mistake. I allow that a mere effort of will, arbitrary and uninformed by duty, may add to a man's power over his lower nature. But in that nature God must rule, not the man, however well he means. From a man's ruling himself in the smallest opposition, however devout, to the law of his being, comes the huge danger of nourishing, through the pride of self-conquest, a far worse self than the unchained animal self: the demoniac self. True victory over self is God's victory in the man; subjugation is not enough, it must be subjugation by God. In whatever a man does without God he must fail miserably, or succeed more miserably. No part of a man can rule another part, for God created it, and the part is greater than the whole. By crossing his natural and therefore rightful inclinations, a man may develop a self-satisfaction that is a root of all sin: doing what God does not require, he becomes not a law but a law-giver to himself, who commands rather than obeys. The diseased satisfaction some feel in laying burdens on themselves pampers, little as they suspect it, the most dangerous appetite of the very self they think they are mortifying. All God's creatures are good, received with thanksgiving; a thing becomes evil only when used where a higher law forbids, or refused for self-discipline where no higher law forbids and God therefore allows it. To be his own schoolmaster is a dangerous position; the pupil cannot be expected to progress, except in the wrong direction. To enjoy heartily and thankfully, and to do cheerfully without when God wills, is the way to live with regard to the lower nature, which must not be confused with the things of *the world*. If anyone calls this dangerous doctrine, I answer that God's law is enough for me; man's laws are false and come of rebellion. God, not man, is our judge.

It is not to thwart or tease the poor self that Jesus speaks; God did not give it to us for that. He tells us to leave it altogether---yield, deny, refuse, lose it---and only so shall we save it and have a share in our own being. The self is given us to sacrifice, so that we, like Christ, may have something to offer. We are to abandon it utterly, not torment it; then it can no more be vexed.

"What can this mean? We are not to thwart, but to abandon? How abandon, without thwarting?"

It means this: we must refuse and deny self altogether as a ruling or originating element in us. It is no longer to be the regent of our action. We are no more to think, "What should I like to do?" but "What would the Living One have me do?" It is not selfish to take what God has made us desire, nor are we very good to yield it when he would take it from us; we would be very bad not to. But to yield it heartily, without struggle or regret, is to deny the Self itself, not just something it would like. The Self is God's making, but it must be the "slave of Christ," that the Son may make it also the free son of the same Father; it must receive all from him and follow him, with Christ as its law. The time will come when it is so possessed and enlarged by the indwelling God, its deepest self, that no enforced denial is needed: it will have learned to receive with thanksgiving, demand nothing, and no longer turn on its own centre. God's eternal denial of himself, revealed in him who took up his cross daily in the flesh, will have developed in the man, whose eternal rejoicing will be in God and his fellows, before whom he will cast his glad self to be a carpet for their walk, a footstool for their rest, a stair for their climbing.

To deny oneself, then, is to act no longer from the standing-ground of self; to allow no private communication between the self and the will; not to let the right hand know what the left hand does. No grasping, no wish to feel worthy, no ambition, no wish to surpass another, no longing for men's praise shall move the will. It is to shrink from no contempt of community or country that goes against the mind of the Living One, and for no love or entreaty of parent, spouse, child, friend or lover to turn aside from following him: we forsake them all as ruling powers in our lives, and do nothing to please them that would not first please him. Right deeds, not the judgment on them, and true words, not their reception, shall be our care. We shall not love money, trust in it, or seek it as the business of life; and whether we have it or not, we must think of it as the gift of God, not a windfall of circumstance. By the uplooking will we must draw our life fresh every moment from the Living One, and not glory in the mere consciousness of health and being. It is God who feeds us, warms us, quenches our thirst; we must know that even our understanding is his, and that we live and feed on him every hour. To know this in the depth of our knowing is to deny ourselves and take God instead; to try after it is to begin the denial, following him who never sought his own. So we must deny all anxieties and fears: when young, not minding what the world calls failure; when old, not being vexed that we forget, cannot do, or grow weak or ill. We have to do with God, who can, not with ourselves, where we cannot; the being we could not make is his care, and our care is to will his will, his to give us all things. This is to deny ourselves. "Self, I have not to consult you but him whose idea is the soul of you, and of which you are as yet all unworthy---the Causing of you, not the caused you. You may be my consciousness, but you are not my being; if you were, what a poor, dingy, weak wretch I should be! My life is hid with Christ in God, with you certainly, but as an obedient servant, not a master. Submit, or I will cast you from me and pray for another consciousness. God is more to me than my consciousness of myself: he is my life, and you are only so much of it as my poor half-made being can grasp at once. Because I have fooled and spoiled you, treating you as my very self, you have dwindled and lessened me till I am ashamed of myself, and I am often disgusted by your paltry, mean face. No! Let me have the company of the Perfect One, my elder brother, not of you! I will not make a friend of the mere shadow of my own being. Good-bye, Self! I deny you, and will do my best every day to leave you behind me."

And in this we must not forget that when Jesus tells us to follow him, come to him, believe in him, he speaks first and always as *the Son* of the Father, in the active sense: the obedient God, not one who claims sonship as ground for further claim. He is the Son who came expressly and only to do the Father's will. When he says *Follow me*, he himself is following the Father; his face is set homeward. Even this is nothing unless we *believe* in him, which is to do as he does. We must believe in him altogether practically, as he believed in his Father: not as one about whom we hold something, but as one we follow out of the body of this death into eternal life. To hold a theory about why he died or wherein lay his atonement is not to follow him; such things are revealed only to those who follow him in his active being, who do as he did and live as he lived. He is all for the Father; we must be too. To follow him is to learn of him, to think his thoughts, use his judgments, see and feel as he did, so that we may be of one mind with his Father. Nothing less, even working miracles and casting out devils, makes a disciple; busy from morning to night doing great things for him on any other road, we should only earn the answer, "I never knew you." When he says, "Take my yoke upon you," he does not mean a yoke he lays on us; it is his own yoke, the Father's will, which he carries and would have us bear with him. Of this yoke he says it is easy, of this burden that it is light---and he says it with Gethsemane before him and the hour and power of darkness waiting. There is no magnifying of himself. He first denies himself and takes up his cross, and then tells us to do the same. The Father magnifies the Son; the Son magnifies the Father.

We must be jealous for God against ourselves, and watch the cunning, deceitful Self---always so until it is thoroughly denied, left quite empty of our will and regard, and God has come into it and made it, not indeed an *adytum*, but a *pylon* for himself. Until then even its denials and its turnings from dear things for Christ's sake will foster its self-regard and breed a deeper self-worship. While it is only thwarted and not denied, satisfaction with conquered difficulty may minister yet more to its self-congratulation. When the Self cannot have honour for its gifts, its conquests, and the "golden opinions bought from all sorts of people," it will please itself with its abnegations and forsakings. It may not *call* itself a saint, but it will soon *feel* itself one, a superior creature looking down on the foolish world, walking on high "above the smoke and stir of this dim spot," and worshipping itself the more intently because it has given up the approval of others and no longer needs them to assure it of its merits. In a thousand ways Self will delude itself. Christ sought nothing but his Father's will; we have to grow diamond-clear, true as the white light of morning. A hopeless task, were it not that he offers to come himself and dwell in us.

I have wondered whether "take up his cross" was already a common phrase when the Lord said it, since he had not yet told them he would be crucified. I can hardly believe crucifixion so common that bearing the cross had passed into ordinary speech. As the Lord's idea was new to men, so I think was its image. I grant that, being so hateful to the Jews, it *might* have come to stand for the worst human misery, but would they use as a figure something that so sorely showed their slavery? I hardly think so. Certainly it had not come to stand for the self-abnegation he had just brought to light, hardly yet to the light, only the twilight; and nothing less, it seems to me, can have suggested the terrible symbol.

But although the denial of self is an entire and absolute idea, it has to be done *daily*. It is deeper and harder than any single effort of the most herculean will, for the will itself is not pure or free until the Self is absolutely denied. The water of life from the well within us takes long to permeate every part of our spiritual frame, until at last, reaching the outermost folds of our personality, it casts out disease, our bodies are redeemed by indwelling righteousness, and creation is delivered from the bondage of corruption into the liberty of the glory of the children of God. Every day till then we must take up our cross, and every hour see that we are carrying it. A birthright may be lost for a mess of pottage, and what Satan calls a trifle must be a thing of eternal significance.

Is there not many a Christian who, having begun to deny himself, spends much strength in the vain and evil effort to accommodate matters between Christ and the dear Self, seeking to save what he must so certainly lose, and in how different a way from the Master's! It is one thing to have the loved self devoured of hell in hate, horror and disappointment, another to yield it to the conscious possession of the living God, who will then first raise it to its true individuality, freedom and life. With its cause within it, it shall indeed be saved. Here is the promise to those who leave all and follow him: "Whosoever shall lose his life, for my sake, the same shall save it"---in St. Matthew, "find it." What speech of men or angels can shadow this dimly glorious hope! To lose ourselves in the salvation of God's heart! To be no longer a care to ourselves, but to know God taking divinest care of us! To be a resting-place for divine love, a branch of the tree of life for the dove to alight on, an open air of love, a thoroughfare for the thoughts of God and all holy creatures! To know one's self by the reflex action of endless brotherly presence, yearning for nothing from anyone, pouring out love by the natural motion of the spirit! To revel in the hundredfold of everything good we left for his sake, above all in the unsought love of those who love us as we love them! To know that God and we mean the same thing, that we are pleasing to the Father; to live nestling at his knee, climbing to his bosom, blessed in the simple being that is one with God! What a self to receive again for the one we left! We left it paltry, low and mean; he took up the poor cinder of a consciousness, carried it back to the workshop of his spirit, made it a true thing, radiant, clear, fit for eternal companying and indwelling, and restored it to us for ever!

All high things can be spoken only in figures, which cannot *fit* intellectually matters too high for them; they can be truly interpreted only by those who have the spiritual fact in themselves. Speaking of a man and his soul implies a self and a self reacting on each other, and we cannot divide ourselves so; the figure suits imperfectly. The Lord never meant to explain things to our understanding; what we need is a word to think with about high things, and a true figure, though far from perfect, gives us that. But the imperfection of his figures cannot lie in excess: any symbol falls short of the meaning it holds. It is the low stupidity of an unspiritual nature that reads his meaning as less than his symbols; the true soul sees that his words always represent more than they present. There is no joy belonging to human nature as God made it that will not be enhanced a hundredfold for the man who gives up himself, though he may seem to yield the very essence of life. To yield self is to give up grasping at things in their second causes, as men call them, which are merely God's means, and to receive them from their source, seeing where they come from. The careless soul takes the Father's gifts as if things had a way of dropping into his hand. So he is a slave of chance and his own blundering: for the good that comes he gives no thanks, for who is there to thank? At his disappointments he grumbles, for there must be someone to blame! What power would not be worse than squandered in sustaining him in his paltry, low-aimed existence? No world could be built on such an idea. It is the children who shall inherit the earth; those who will not be children cannot possess it. The hour is coming when all that art, science and nature can give, in ennobling subjugation to the higher as man is subject to the Father, shall be the endless delight of the sons and daughters of God. To the man to whom God is all in all, God can give these things; another cannot receive them. We are not to love God for what he can give; we cannot love him except because he is our God and altogether good and beautiful. But neither may we forget what the Lord does not forget: in the end, when truth is victorious, God will answer his creature in the joy of his heart. For what is joy but the harmony of the spirit? The good Father made his children to be joyful; only, before they can enter his joy they must be like him, ready to sacrifice joy to truth. No promise of such joy appeals to selfishness; every reward Christ holds out enters the soul only as a death to selfishness. His heaven is a loving of all, a forgetting of self, each dwelling in all and all in each. A joyful child is rarely selfish, even in the nursery; it is not selfish to be joyful. What could keep him who sees the face of God from joy? That bliss lies behind all other bliss. The one bliss of the universe is the presence of God, which is God being to the man, and felt by him as being, what in his own nature he is: the indwelling power of his life. God must be to his creature what he is in himself, for only by his essential being can he create; his presence is the unceasing call and response of father to child. Where is the selfishness in being so made happy? It may be deep selfishness to refuse to be happy. Is there selfishness in the Lord's seeing the travail of his soul and being satisfied? Selfishness is taking bliss from another; finding one's bliss in another's is not selfishness, and the greater the joy so reaped, the farther from it. The one bliss next to the love of God is the love of our neighbour. If anyone says, "You love because it makes you blessed," I deny it: we are blessed because we love. No one could reach the bliss of loving his neighbour who was selfish and sought it from love of himself. Love is unselfishness. In the main we love because we cannot help it; there is no merit in it, but neither is it selfish. Many confound righteousness with merit and say, "If it makes you happy to love, where is your merit? It is only selfishness!" There is no merit, I reply, yet the love born in us is our salvation from selfishness and the very essence of righteousness. That a thing is joyful does not mean I do it for the joy of it, and when the joy is in others it is pure. That *certain* joys should be joys is the very denial of selfishness; the man whom love itself did not make joyful would be demoniacally selfish. It is selfish to enjoy content while beholding others lack; even in the highest spiritual bliss, to sit careless of others would be selfishness, and the higher the bliss, the worse. But that bliss is surely right of which a great part is labour that others may share it, and I do not doubt such labour will be a great part of our heavenly gladness; the redeeming Father will find plenty of like work for his children. Those who think that where all are good things must be dull have little Christian imagination; it is because there is so little good in them yet that they know so little of the beauty of life divine. There will be interest and variety enough, not without pain, in helping those still wearily toiling up the heights of truth, perhaps unwilling to part with the miserable self which, while they cherish it, they are not yet worth being or capable of having.

Some of the things a man may have to forsake in following Christ are not forsaken because of what they are in themselves. Nature, art, science and fit society are not among the things he loses in forsaking himself: they are God's and have no part in the world of evil, false judgments, low wishes and unrealities that make up the conscious life of the self that must be denied; that life will never be restored to him. But in forsaking himself to do his true work in the world, a man may find he must for a time leave some of God's things---not as bad things, but because they draw his mind from the necessities of the true life in himself or others, and there is no room for them until those of paramount claim have been so heeded that these no longer impede but further them. Then he who knows God will find that knowledge opens his understanding to all else; he will behold things from within instead of searching wearily into them from without. This gave King David more understanding than all his teachers. Then the things he left will be restored a hundredfold. So it is in forsaking friends. To forsake them for Christ is not to forsake them as evil or to cease to love them---"for he that loveth not his brother whom he hath seen, how can he love God whom he hath not seen?"---but to let their love cast not even a shadow between us and our Master, and to be content to lose their approval, company, even affection, where the Master says one thing and they another. It is to learn to love them in a higher, tenderer, truer way, which keeps all that was genuine in the former and loses all that was false. We shall love *their* selves, and disregard our own.

I do not forget the Lord's word about *hating father and mother*. I have a glimpse of its meaning, but dare not try to explain it now. It is all against the self, not against the father and mother.

There is another kind of forsaking that may fall to some, and may be very difficult: forsaking the notions of God and his Christ they were taught in youth, which they held, and could not help holding, when they began to believe. They have begun to doubt whether they are true, but casting them away feels like parting with every assurance of safety.

There are so-called doctrines long accepted by good people which I find hard to understand how any man can hold and love God, except by closing the spiritual eyes tight. If a man cares more for opinion than for life, it is not worth persuading him to renounce his opinions; he would only put others in the same place of honour, a place that can *belong* to no opinion. What such a man believes matters nothing, for he is not a true man: holding with a school he supposes right, he props himself up with the worst unbelief of all, opinion calling itself faith. But for him who is in earnest about God's will it is of endless consequence to think rightly of God; he cannot come close to him or truly know his will while his notion of him is in any point that of a false god. If he seems to himself to give up even his former assurance of salvation by yielding ideas of God unworthy of God, he must nonetheless take up that cross also. He will come to see that he must follow *no* doctrine, however truly man's word could state it, but the living Truth, the Master himself.

Many good souls will one day be horrified at what they now believe of God. If they have not thought about it but have given themselves to obedience, they may have done little harm so far; but they can make little progress in knowing God while, even passively, holding evil things true of him. If they do think about them and find no obstruction, they must be far from any true knowledge of God. But some find them a terrible obstruction and yet imagine, or fear, them true. These must take courage to forsake the false in *any* shape, to deny their old selves in the most seemingly sacred prejudices, and follow Jesus not as the tradition of the elders presents him but as he presents himself, with his apostles and the spirit of truth. There are "traditions of men" after Christ as well as before him, and far worse, as "making of none effect" higher and better things; we have to look to it *how we have learned Christ*.

### THE TRUTH IN JESUS

*The passage:* Paul says his readers did not learn Christ that way, if they heard him and were taught in him as truth is in Jesus: as regards their former way of life they are to put off the old man, who is going to ruin through the love of the lie (Eph. iv. 20-22).

How have we learned Christ? It ought to be a startling thought that we may have learned him wrong, which is far worse than not learning him at all, for a false Christ, hard to exorcise, then takes his place. The point is whether we have learned Christ as he taught himself, or as men taught him who thought they understood but did not; whether we know him with fleshly notions and low human fancies, or after the spirit, as God knows him. The Christian religion, being higher and wider than the Jewish, has been open to even greater corrupt misrepresentation. Have we learned false statements about him, or *himself*? Is our brain merely full of things concerning him, or does he dwell in our hearts, a lesson ever being learned, the power of our life?

I have been led to what I am about to say by an utterance of one in the front rank of those who assert that we can know nothing of the "Infinite and Eternal energy from which all things proceed." It is this:

"To visit on Adam's descendants, through hundreds of generations, dreadful penalties for a small transgression they did not commit; to damn all who do not use an alleged way of forgiveness most have never heard of; and to reconcile by sacrificing a perfectly innocent son to satisfy an assumed need for a propitiatory victim: these are acts that, ascribed to a human ruler, would call forth abhorrence, and ascribing them to the Ultimate Cause of things must become impossible, even if not felt to be full of difficulties."

I quote this not to oppose either clause, for I entirely agree with it, and I do not mean to address its writer or anyone who holds with him. I have never heard a word from that way of thinking that touched anything I hold. One of my earliest recollections is of beginning to be at strife with the false system here assailed, and I scorn such paganism as heartily in the name of Christ as in the name of righteousness. Rather than believe a single point involving its spirit, even with the assurance of the salvation it offers, I would join those who "know nothing," and set myself, hopeless of heart, to what I am now trying with infinite hope in the pure originating One: to get rid of my miserable mean self, comforted only by the chance that death would either end thought or reveal something of the Ultimate Cause that it would be no insult to him to hold. Even such a chance alone might enable one to live.

I will not now inquire how the writer comes to offer these so-called beliefs as Christianity, when so many who believe in Christ with true hearts, some of higher rank in literature than he, hold none of them and abhor them as much as he does. He would probably answer that he had to deal with the ruling notions of religious society; and that these are held by the bulk of Christians, educated and uneducated, however many think an explanatory clause here and there turns away the reproach, I am sorry to be unable to deny. Mitigated forms of expression change nothing: a brief examination would show these ideas to be not merely analogous to the originals but ultimately identical.

But if I had to do with the writer, I would ask how, refusing these dogmas as abominable, yet knowing they are attributed to men whose teaching has done more to civilize the world than any other, he has not inquired enough to satisfy a man of his faculty that this was not their teaching; that it was so different and so good that even the forced companionship of such lies has not destroyed its regenerative power. He will allow that a man named Jesus died for the truth he taught: can he believe he died for that? Would it not be well to inquire what he really taught, from the primary sources? I mention him only to make my position plain to those to whom I do speak: those who call themselves Christians.

If I asked these Christians how such opinions come to be held concerning the Holy One whose ways they take on them to set forth, most would answer, "He tells us these things himself in his word; we have learned them from the Scriptures." Many would offer explanations that seem to them to remove all cause for reprobation, and others would say that better ideas, though widely held, have not yet had time to show themselves in the belief of the nation's thinkers. Of those who present doctrine as the quotation describes, some are content that it should be so, and some find it grievous but do not see how to get rid of it. To the latter it may be a little comfort to hear one who has studied the New Testament for many years and loves it beyond words declare his conviction that there is not an atom of such teaching in the whole lovely, divine utterance; it is altogether men's invention, honest in part at least, but not true. Thank God, we are nowise bound to accept any man's explanation of God's ways, however good the man, if it does not commend itself to our conscience; to accept what we cannot see to be good would be sin.

But it is no part of my object now to set forth what I believe or do not believe; a time may come for that. I want to address those who call themselves Christians, and expostulate with them thus:

Whatever your *opinions* on the greatest of all subjects, is it well that the impression of Christianity on your generation should be that of your opinions, and not of something beyond opinion? Can Christianity be represented by opinion, even the best? If it could, how many of us are such as God would choose to represent his thoughts by our opinions of them? If you answer, "The opinions I hold are those of the Bible," I reply that none can understand, still less represent, another's opinions but those of the same mind with him, certainly not those who suppose *opinion* to be the object of any writer in the Bible. Is Christianity a system of articles of belief, however correctly worded? Never. I would rather have a man who held, as numbers of you do, the most obnoxious untruths and irreverent opinions, if he *lived* in the faith of the Son of God---trusted in God as the Son of God trusted in him---than a man with whose every formula of belief I agreed but who knew nothing of a daily walk with God. The one, holding doctrines of devils, is yet a child of God; the other, holding the doctrines of Christ and his Apostles, is of the world, yea, of the devil.

"What! A man hold the doctrine of devils, and yet be of God?"

Yes; for to hold a thing with the intellect is not to believe it. A man's real belief is what he lives by, and the man I mean lives by the love of God and obedience to his law so far as he has recognized it. Those hideous doctrines are outside him; he *thinks* they are inside, but they cannot really be inside any good man. They are sadly against him, for he cannot love to dwell on them, yet he lives in a measure like the true God. What a man believes is what he does. He would shrink with loathing from actions he thinks God justified in doing; like God, he loves and helps and saves. Will the living God let such a man's opinions damn him? No more than he will let the correct opinions of another, who lives for himself, save him. What I insist on is that, supposing your theories right and containing all that is to be believed, they are not what makes you Christians; with not a few of you they are just what keeps you from it. For when you say that to be saved a man must hold this or that, you leave the living God and his will and trust in a notion about him. Some of you say we must trust in the finished work of Christ, in his merits, the atonement, the blood he shed. All these statements repudiate the living Lord *in whom* we are told to believe, who by his presence with and in us, and our obedience, lifts us out of darkness into light and into the liberty of the sons of God. No belief *about him* is the faith of the New Testament. Unless the light of the knowledge of the glory of God in the face of Christ Jesus makes a man sick of his opinions, he may hold them to doomsday for me, for no opinion is Christianity and no plan of salvation is the gospel of the living God. Many will grant this heartily, yet the moment you talk with them you find they insist that to believe in Christ is to believe in the atonement, meaning only their special theory of it. If I say we must believe in the atoning Christ and cannot believe in any theory of atonement, they denounce me: "He does not believe in the atonement!" If I explain it otherwise than they do, they say I deny it, and count it of no consequence that I believe in the atoner with my whole heart, soul, strength and mind. This they call *contending for the truth*! Because I refuse an explanation that is not in the New Testament, though they think it is, and seems to me as false in logic as detestable in morals, I am not a Christian! What wonder that men such as the one I quoted refuse the Christianity they suppose such "believers" to represent! A potent faith in the Lord himself may mingle with this folly, but the importance they place on theory obstructs true faith even more than the theories themselves: while the mind is occupied in asking,

"Do I believe or feel this thing right?"---the true question is forgotten: "Have I left all to follow him?" To the man who gives himself to the living Lord every belief will come right; the Lord himself will see that his disciple believes aright about him. If a man cannot trust him for this, what faith has he? It is because he has little faith that he clings to preposterous, dishonouring ideas, the traditions of men about his Father instead of the teaching of Christ and his apostles. The living Christ is to such people a shadow, and no soul can thoroughly believe in the all but obliterated Christ of their theories: the disciple of such a Christ rests on his work, or his merits, or his atonement!

What I insist on is that a man's faith be in the living, loving, ruling, helping Christ, devoted to us as ever he was, with all the powers of the Godhead for the salvation of his brethren. What saves a man is not faith that he did this or his work wrought that, but faith in the man who did and is doing everything for us; without it he cannot heal us spiritually, any more than he healed physically when present to men's eyes. If you ask, "What is faith in him?" I answer: leaving your way, your objects, your self, and taking his and him; leaving your trust in men, money, opinion, character, atonement itself, *and doing as he tells you*. It is the one terrible heresy of the church that it has always presented something else than obedience as faith in Christ. The work of Christ is not the Working Christ, any more than his clothing is his body. If the woman who touched the hem of his garment had trusted in the garment and not in him who wore it, would she have been healed? Those who believe *about* Christ rather than in him get what comfort they do because, touching the mere hem, they cannot help believing a little in the live man inside the garment. No wonder they are so often miserable: they lie down to sleep with only the skirt of a robe in their hand, a robe never his, only supposed his by them, when they might sleep in peace with the living Lord in their hearts. Instead of knowing Christ in them saving them, they waste themselves in soul-sickening self-examination---whether they believe, whether they truly trust the atonement, are sorry enough, are of the elect, have an interest in the blood shed---the way to madness of brain and despair of heart; while the man who died for them waits to begin to save them from every evil, first from this self that consumes them with trouble about its salvation. He will set them free and take them home to the Father's bosom, if only they mind what he says, which is the beginning, middle and end of faith. If instead of searching the mysteries of corruption in their own charnel-houses they would awake and arise from the dead and come into the light he waits to give them, he would at once begin to fill them with the fulness of God.

"But I do not know how to awake and arise!"

I will tell you: get up and do something the Master tells you, and so make yourself his disciple at once. Instead of asking whether you believe, ask whether this day you have done one thing because he said, Do it, or once abstained because he said, Do not. It is absurd to say you believe, or want to believe, in him if you do nothing he tells you. If you can think of nothing he said that has had an atom of influence on your doing or not doing, you have good ground to think yourself no disciple. Do not waste your time trying to convince yourself otherwise. What if you succeeded, and he said after all, "Why did you not do the things I told you? Depart from me; I do not know you!" If it is true that you are his disciple, you can make it truer; if not, you can begin at once to *be* one, by obeying him in the first thing you can think of in which you are not obeying him. We must learn to obey him in everything, and so must begin somewhere: let it be at once, in the very next thing at the door of conscience! Oh fools and slow of heart, if you think of nothing but Christ and do not do his words, you build on sand. What have those teachers not to answer for who have turned your regard from the Lord's own words, which are spirit and life, to plans of salvation tortured out of his apostles' words, even were those plans as true as they are false! There is but one plan of salvation: to believe in the Lord Jesus Christ, that is, to take him for what he is, our master, and his words as if he meant them, as he did. To do his words is to enter into vital relation with him; obedience is the only way to be one with him. The relation is absolute and can begin to *live* only in obedience: it is obedience. Have I the poorest notion of a God, and dare I enter into relations with him the first of which is not that what he says I will do? The thing is eternally absurd. I know what the father of lies whispers to those who dislike this teaching: "It is the doctrine of works!" But one word of the Lord humbly heard will send all the demons of false theology into the abyss. He says the man who does not do what he tells him builds his house to fall in ruin; he tells his messengers to baptize all nations, "teaching them to observe all things whatsoever I have commanded you." It is faith he requires, the highest act of which the human mind is capable; but faith in what he is and says, a faith that can exist only in obedience, a faith which *is* obedience. For this, men have substituted a belief *about* him, faith in some supposed design of his coming in the flesh. It was himself and God in him that he manifested; but they make faith in him thus manifested secondary to accepting a paltry contrivance of juggling morality which they attribute to God and Christ and call the atonement and the plan of salvation. Do you put faith in *him*, I ask, or in the doctrines and commandments of men? If you say, "In him," then do you not see that you are bound above all things to obey him? You find it too hard to trust him as you would; it will remain so while you will not even try the things he tells you that you can do, and are not true to him. The very thing that would make you able to trust him you turn your back on: obedience you decline or neglect. You say you do not refuse? I care not whether you refuse, while you do not obey. Remember the parable: "I go, sir, and went not." What have you done this day because it was the will of Christ? Have you dismissed an anxious thought for the morrow, ministered to a need without letting your right hand know, forgiven your enemy, given to one who asked, sought God's kingdom first? Tell me something you have done, are doing, or are trying to do because he told you. If you do nothing he says, no wonder you cannot trust him, and are driven to take refuge in the atonement, as if something he did, and not he himself in his doing, were the atonement. "That is not as you understand it?" What does it matter how you understand it, so long as you are not of one mind with the Truth, not *at one* with God? Knowing you do not heed his word, why should I heed your explanation of it? You do not do his will, and so cannot understand him; you do not know him, and so cannot trust him. Your common sense ought to know itself unequal to the task; only the heart of the child can understand the Father. Would you have me think you guilty of the sin against the Holy Ghost, that you *understand* Jesus Christ and yet will not obey him? That were too dreadful; I believe you do not understand him. No man can yet do aright what he tells him, but are you trying? Obedience is not perfection, but trying. You count him a hard master and will not stir. Do you suppose he ever gave a commandment knowing it could not be done? He tells us a thing knowing we must do it or be lost; not even his Father could save us except by getting us at length to do everything he commands, for not otherwise can we know life or learn the holy secret of divine being. He knows you can try, and that in your trying and failing he will help you until you do God's will as he does it himself. He takes the will in the imperfect deed and at last makes the deed perfect. Correctest notions without obedience are worthless. Doing God's will is the way to oneness with God, which alone is salvation. Even clasping the Father's knees at the gate of heaven, you could not be at peace unless your whole being were in every vital movement one with the living God; with one brooding thought not a joy in him, or one desire you could not leave to his will, you would not be saved, and so could not feel saved. God, all and in all, ours to the fulfilling of our very being, is the religion of the perfect, son-hearted Lord Christ.

Well I know it is faith that saves us, but faith in God himself, not in any work of God. If I did not believe God as good as the tenderest, purest, most unselfish human heart could imagine him, an infinitude better and higher than we as the heavens are higher than the earth---believe it not as a proposition, but with the responsive condition of my whole nature; if I did not feel every fibre of heart, brain and body safe with him because he is the Father who made me---I would not be saved, for this faith is salvation: God and the man one, the vital energy flowing unchecked from creator into creature. The poorest faith in the living God revealed in Christ Jesus, if vital and true, that is obedient, is the beginning of the way to know him, and to know him is eternal life. A faith of any different kind will not save you. A faith, for instance, that God forgives me not because he loves me but because he loves Jesus Christ cannot save me, because it is a falsehood against God: if true, it would preach a God who was not love, in whom was no salvation, to know whom could not be eternal life. Such a faith would damn a man, binding him to a God who was anything but perfect. Such assertions, going by the name of Christianity, are the poor remnants of paganism, believed only by the part of our nature not yet Christian, so far as a lie can be believed. We must forsake all our fears and distrusts for Christ, and receive his teaching heartily, not letting the interpretation attributed to his apostles turn us aside. What they teach is never against what Christ taught, though the exposition of it often is, from the grievous fault of those who would understand, and even explain, rather than obey. We may be sure that no man will be condemned for any sin that is past. If he is condemned, it will be because he would not come to the light when it came to him, would not cease to do evil and learn to do well, hid his unbelief in the garment of a false faith, imputed to himself a righteousness not his, and preferred imagining himself worthy to confessing himself everywhere wrong and repenting. We may be sure also that if a man becomes Christ's disciple, Christ will not leave him ignorant of what he has to believe; if we do what he tells us, his light will go up in our hearts, while till then we could not understand even if he explained. If you cannot trust him to let you know what is right, but think you must hold this or that before you can come to him, then I justify your doubts in what you call your worst times, but which I suspect are your best, when you come nearest the truth: those in which you fear you have no faith.

So long as a man will not set himself to obey the word of the Lord Christ, whether spoken, written or printed, I would not take the trouble to convince him that the most obnoxious doctrines are false. It is those who would fain believe but are hindered by such doctrines that I would help. Disputation about things hides the living Christ, who alone can teach the truth, who is the truth. I write for those whom false teaching has driven away from God---as well it might, for the God so taught is not worth believing in. A stick, a stone or a devil is all some of our fellow men have to believe in; whoever believes in a God not altogether unselfish and good, who does not do all he can for his creatures, belongs to the same class, and his is not the God revealed in Christ. If a man sees any darkness in God, and especially if he defends it, I cannot but think his blindness has followed his mockery of "*Lord! Lord!*" Had he strenuously obeyed Jesus he would by now have received the truth that God is light and in him is no darkness, a truth not acknowledged by calling the darkness attributed to him light, and the candle of the Lord in man's soul darkness. It is one thing to believe that God can do nothing wrong, quite another to call right whatever presumption attributes to him.

The whole secret of progress is doing the thing we know. There is no other way of progress in the spiritual life, or in the understanding of it: only as we do, can we know.

Is there anything, then, you will not leave for Christ? You cannot know him, yet he is the Truth, the one thing that can be known! Would you rather keep this or that, with imperfection, than part with it to be perfect? You cannot know Christ, for the principle of his life was the simple absolute relation of realities: his one idea was to be a perfect child to his Father. He who will not part with all for Christ is not worthy of him and cannot know him, and the Lord, being true, cannot acknowledge him. How could he receive into his house a man who prefers something to his Father, who strikes a bargain and says, "I will give up so much if you will spare me"? Such a man counts it too much to yield all to him who gave us everything, even himself, by life and death. He will have to be left to himself, to find what it is to be without God! Those who know God, or have caught a far-off glimmer of him, find life insupportable unless God is All in all, the first and the last.

The duty of Christians to their fellows is to let their light shine, not to force on them their interpretations of God's designs. If you who explain the theory of Christianity had set yourselves instead to do the Master's will, the one object for which the Gospel was preached to you, how different would be the part of the world you touch! Had you given yourselves to understanding his word in order to do it, and not to quarrying material from it to buttress your systems, in many a heart the name of the Lord would be loved where now it is unknown. Men, undeterred by explanations you were not forcing on them, and attracted by your behaviour, would say to each other, as Moses at the burning bush, "I will now turn aside and see this great sight!" They would come to see how these Christians loved one another and were just to everyone: their goods the best, their weights surest, their prices most *reasonable*, their word most certain; no jealousy or emulation in their families, no worship of mammon, no selfishness as the ruling principle; children as diligently taught to share as some are taught to save or to spend on self; religion nowhere one thing and daily life another; the churchman not thinking first of his church, nor the peer of his privileges.

What do I hear you say?---"*How then shall the world go on?*" The Lord's world will go on, and without you; the devil's world will go on, and with you. The objection is another overwhelming proof of your unbelief. Either you do not believe his word that if we seek first the kingdom of God and his righteousness all things needful will be added to us, or what he undertakes does not satisfy you and you prefer the offers of Mammon. You want what you call a fortune, the freedom of the world, and would not live under such restrictions as the Lord might lay on you if he saw something precious might be made of you. You would inherit the earth, but not by meekness; you would leave God to look after eternal life, if only you might be sure of not sharing with the rich man when you die. But unable to trust him for this world, you cannot trust him for the next. Refusing to obey him in your life, how can you trust him for your life? Hence your substitutes for faith: you hold him to his word, bind him by his promises, appeal to the atonement and the satisfaction made to his justice, while you take no trouble to fulfil the one reasonable and imperative condition---condition and means in one---on which alone he can deliver you from the burden of life into its strength and glory: that you be true and obedient children. You say "Christ has satisfied the law," but you will not satisfy him! He says, "Leave everything behind you, and be as I am towards God, and you shall have peace and rest," and you turn away muttering about *figurative language*. If you had been true, living *the* life, you would have drawn the world after you, however little. In your churches you would be receiving truest nourishment, thinking far less of serving God on Sunday and far more of serving your neighbour in the week. The sociable vile, the masterful rich, the deceitful trader and the ambitious poor, whom you drew in with the offer of a salvation other than deliverance from sin, would not be lording it over you and dragging you down; you would be cleaner and stronger without them. Publicans and sinners would have been drawn instead and turned into true men and women; and the Israelite indeed, in whom is no guile, who is repelled more by your worldliness than by your misrepresentations of God, would have hastened to the company of the loving and true, eager to learn what made them so good, happy, unselfish, free of care, ready to die and willing to live, careless to possess. Finding you hold, from the force of false tradition, such things as you do, he would have said, "No! such beliefs can never account for such mighty results!" You would have answered, "Search the Scriptures and see." He would have searched and found, not what you imagine there, but things infinitely better and higher that do account for what he wondered at: a truth he would hold for ever as the only gladness of his being. So you would have had your reward for being true Christians in spite of what you were taught, and been taught the truth in return by him whom your Christianity had enticed, sent to the fountainhead free of the prejudices that disabled your judgment. Delivered from the false notions that stunted your growth, how rapid it would have become!

If any of you tell me my doctrine is presumptuous, contrary to the New Testament and to what the best of men have always believed, I will not therefore defend even my beliefs, much less my opinions. I appeal to you instead whether I have spoken the truth about our paramount obligation to do the word of Christ. If you say I have not, there is no other ground on which we can meet. But if you allow it is a prime duty, even if not *the* prime duty, then I insist that you do it, recommending the knowledge of him so and not otherwise. I do not try to change your opinions; if they are wrong, the obedience I insist on can alone set them right. I assert that only so can you fit yourselves to understand the mind of Christ. None but he who does right can think right; you cannot *know* Christ to be right until you do as he does and tells you to do, nor set him forth until you know him as he means himself to be known. If you serve and trust Mammon, how can you know the living God, who alone is to be trusted? If you do not admit it is a man's duty to do the word of Christ, or admitting it do not care to do it, why should I care to convince you my doctrine is right? If I could convince your judgment while your hearts remain as they are, I should only add to your condemnation. The true heart must see at once that, however wrong I may be in other things, I am right in this: Jesus must be obeyed, and at once, in the things he did say; it will not long imagine it obeys him in things he did not say. If a man does what is unpleasing to Christ, believing it his will, he still gains by it, for it gives the Lord a hold of him which he will use; but before he can reach liberty he must be delivered from that falsehood. The man who does not choose to see that Christ must be obeyed will be left to the teaching of the Father, who brings all that hear and learn of him to Christ. The Father will leave no man to his own way. The Lord did not die to provide a man with the wretched heaven he may invent for himself or accept from others, but to give him life and bring him to the heaven of the Father's peace, for the children must share in the essential bliss of the Father and the Son. This has been the Father's work from the beginning: to bring us into the home of his heart, where he shares the glories of life with the Living One, in whom was born the life that lights men back to the original life. However a man refuses this destiny, it is useless to kick against the goads of God's love. The Father is goading him, or will goad him if needful, into life by unrest and trouble; hell-fire will have its turn if less will not do, and who can need it more than those who neither enter the kingdom of heaven themselves nor allow those to enter who would? The old race of the Pharisees is by no means extinct; they were St Paul's great trouble, and are found in every religious community under the sun.

The one thing truly to reconcile all differences is to walk in the light. So St Paul teaches in Philippians iii. 16. After setting forth the loftiest idea of human endeavour in declaring the summit of his own aspiration, he says not, "This must be your endeavour also, or you cannot be saved," but, "If in anything ye be otherwise minded, God shall reveal even this unto you. Nevertheless whereto we have already attained, let us walk by that same." Observe what widest scope the apostle gives to honest opinion, even in things of grandest import! The one essential point with him is that what we have seen to be true, *we walk by that*. In such walking only will love and truth grow; the soul, then first in its genuine element and true relation to God, will see into reality that was before a blank to it, and he who has promised to teach will teach abundantly. Faster and faster will the glory of the Lord dawn upon his people so walking, and fast and far will knowledge of him spread, for truth of action, before and after truth of word, will prepare his way. The man walking by what he has attained will be able to think aright; the man who does not think right is unable because he has not been walking right. Only when he begins to do the thing he knows does he begin to think aright; then God comes to him in a new and higher way and works along with the spirit he has created. The soul without its heaven above its head, its life-breath around it, its love-treasure in its heart, its origin one with it, cannot think to any real purpose, nor ever would. When man joins with God, all impotence and discord is cast out; until then God is in contest with the gates of hell that open in the man, and can but hold his own. When the man joins him, Satan is foiled. For then first nature receives her necessity, the law of all laws: that God and man are one. Until they begin to be one in reality as in the divine idea, nothing can go right with the man, and God can have no rest from his labour in him. As the greatest orbs in heaven are drawn by the least, God himself must be held in divine disquiet until every one of his family is brought home to his heart, to be one with him in a unity too profound to be understood by any but the God from whom it comes, yet guessed at by the soul from the unspeakableness of its delight when it is at length with the *only* one that can be its own. For God is the heritage of the soul in the ownness of origin; man is the offspring of his making will; God himself is his birth-place; God is the self that makes the soul able to say *I too, I myself*. This absolute bliss of the creature is that for which the Son died and the Father suffered with him. Then only is life itself, right, one, and as designed by the eternal life-outgiving Life.

Whereto then we have attained, let us walk by that same!

## Third Series

::: center
*TO MY WIFE*
:::

| Sun and wind and rain, the Lord
|     Is to seed his Father buried
| For he is the living Word,
|     And the quickening Spirit.

| BORDIGHERA:
|     *May* 3, 1889.

### THE CREATION IN CHRIST

*The passage:* Everything was made by him, and nothing that has been made was made without him. In him was life, and that life was the light of men (John i. 3, 4).

Anyone who loves the gospel and thinks about it, especially someone used to putting thoughts into words, has probably felt that something is wrong with the end of the third verse in English. It limps, and seems unworthy of the rest. That it is merely redundant is cold comfort to anyone hoping for perfection in the words of the man closest to the Lord. The last phrase is so useless it sounds foolish: "All things were made by him, and without him was not anything made *that was made*."

So I was hopeful when I saw in the Greek that moving one full stop would remove the redundancy. Punctuation has no authority for anyone who knows what scribes, editors and printers do to texts, and a precious meaning would be something to thank God for. It did reveal a truth, one that brought the wording into line with the apostle's highest thought. I was so glad that it hardly mattered that the best manuscripts and translations back the change; the case for the usual reading seemed to me worthless.

Let us look at the passage as I think it should be translated, and then at the meaning it was written for. That meaning belongs to the truth as it is in Jesus and doesn't depend on this passage, but John expresses it here magnificently and uniquely, if I am right about the interpretation that came to me the moment I saw how the words probably relate.

"All things were made through him, and without him was made not one thing. That which was made in him was life, and the life was the light of men."

Notice the contrast between *through* and *in*.

This great statement seems to me to hold, more than in shadow, the seed of creation and redemption: of everything divine in its bearing on everything human.

I write not to provoke controversy, which I loathe, but in the hope of showing those ready to see it the glory of the truth of the Father and the Son, as this first of seers expressed it. I care as little for a name for orthodoxy as for championing novelty. To the untrue, truth itself must seem unsound, because the light in them is darkness.

I believe that Jesus Christ is the eternal Son of the eternal Father: from the very first he is the Son, because God is the Father. That is an imperfect statement, as any human thought must be that reaches toward the unknown roots of our existence, but I believe it too firmly not to say it, even in words that cannot be right. So I believe the Father is the greater, and that without the Father there could be no Son. I won't argue this by logic, and state it only for what follows. Paul implies the same when he speaks of the Lord handing the kingdom to his Father, that God may be all in all. I worship the Son as the human God, the only Man, who draws his being and power from the Father. He is equal to his Father, and subject to him, as a son is, and has made himself his Father's equal in what is most precious in Godhead: Love. That is the essence of the statement I am dealing with, higher than making the worlds, which he did by the Father's power and not his own. So John, to whom the Lord must have said things he did not say to the rest, writes that all things were made not *by* the Son but *through* him.

We shouldn't explain things away into nothing; we should picture them as best we can, even if the pictures are only shadows of the heavenly. Our being and understanding are poor shadows, but they are shadows of his, cast by him, and no more our own doing than his creative power is. So, in shadow-speech, I say: when the Father brought the visible out of the unseen, he made essential use of the Son, so that everything was created *through* him. Who can understand how the Father's part differs from the Son's? Perhaps we will one day see a little into it, for I dare hope that through chosen sonship we will come much nearer to creating ourselves. To call the finest work of human genius "creation" seems to me a mockery of humanity, itself still being created.

Read the verse again. All things were made *through* him, but "that which was made *in* him was life." See it? The power by which he made the worlds was given him by his Father, but he had a greater power in himself. Something was made not through but in him, by himself: here he creates, in his grand way, within himself, as the Father did.

What life does the apostle mean? Many forms of life came through the Son, but they were results, not the life made in him. The Father could use him in creating only because of the life that was in him.

What God's life is to himself, we can only know that we cannot know, and even that is not plain ignorance: to see you cannot understand something is already to approach it. What it is to us we do know: the cause of everything we call life, of everything that is. The one endless mystery (I take the two to be one) is how God can be self-existent and how he can make others exist. It will stay a mystery for ever, not because God won't explain it but because he couldn't make us understand. A creature cannot know its own creation, since its cause comes before it, nor understand what it can do nothing like. To understand how we were made we would have to make ourselves, which takes being God; and the idea that I, with this cramped, self-dissatisfied consciousness, could have caused myself is the most dismal of all. Yet if I am a child of God I must be *like* him even in creative energy; something in me must answer, in a childlike way, to his eternal might. But I run ahead. The question now is what that life was, made *in* the Son, by him inside himself: not *through* him but *in* him, his own as God's life is God's own.

It was, I answer, the act in him that corresponds, as Son, to his Father's self-existence. What is deepest in God? Not his power. A being who was only power would be so far from divine that we could give him only fear, not righteous worship; however justly he judged, he could not be God. (Evil, of course, cannot create one atom.) The God we love could not be righteous unless he were something deeper than we usually mean by the word. In one word, God is Love. Love is the deepest depth in him, the root of all his being. He could not be God without creatures to be God to, but love is more: the heart and hand of his creation, his right and his power to create. Nor could he be righteous, meaning fair to his creatures, if love had not made them. His perfection is his love, and all his divine rights rest on it. He is not the great monarch! The simplest peasant loving his cow is more divine than a monarch whose glory is his monarchy. If God refused to punish sin, or punished it for anything but love, he would not be the Father of Jesus Christ. So what in Christ corresponds to God's creative power? It must also come from love, but in the Son the love is toward one already existing. Because of that eternal love the Father must have the Son; God could not be love without things to love. Jesus has God to love, and his love answers the Father's. The answer to self-existent love is self-giving love, and Jesus' refusal of himself corresponds to God's creating. His love creates by renouncing self: the death of self as a motive, self drowned in the life of God, where it lives only as love. What is life in a child but perfect response to his parents, oneness with them? A child at strife with them is as a child dead, stiff and contorted; see him yield to a will rightly above his own, and life flows back into his limbs and light fountains in his eyes!

Negatively, Christ does and cares for nothing for his own sake; positively, he cares with his whole soul for his Father's will and pleasure. Because his Father is his Father, he chooses to be his child. The truth in Jesus is his relation to the Father; his righteousness is fulfilling it. Born of God, he is alive, but by giving himself perfectly, choosing to die to himself and live to God, he creates in himself a new and higher life. Standing on it, he can wake in us, his brothers and sisters, a divine shadow of that life. We came from the same home, the heart of his God and ours, but without our elder brother to go first we would never have chosen the self-giving that is life. To will, not from self but with the Eternal, is to live.

This choice of his own being, made in full knowledge, this willing to be the perfectly obedient Son, is what in Jesus answers to God's self-existence. By putting himself under the Father's will as his only good, the only *reason* he exists, he rose at once to the height of his being and sat on the throne of his nature. When he died on the cross, in the wild weather of his outlying provinces, he did what he had always done at home in glory and gladness. From the infinite beginning (speaking only in contradictions) he completed the eternal circle of his being by saying, "Thy will, not mine, be done!" He made himself what he is by *deathing* himself into the will of the eternal Father, through which he was the eternal Son, plunging into the fountain of his own life, the everlasting Fatherhood. This is the life made *in* Jesus. Because he willed it, it is possible, and essential, for every man, woman and child the Father has sent into the outer world to return to the inner, his heart. As the Father's self-existent life gave us being, so Jesus' willed devotion lets him give us eternal life like his own, by enabling us to do the same. There is no other life for anyone: a disciple must live by the same absolute devotion of his will to the Father's, and so his life is one with the Father's.

We have come out of the divine nature, which chooses to be divine, so we must *choose* to be divine, one with God, loving and living as he does, or perish. People cannot originate this life; it must be shown them and they must choose it. God is the Father of Jesus and of us, of every possibility of our being, but Jesus is the father of our *sonship*: in him was made the life that is sonship to the Father, the acknowledgment in fact and life of the Father's claim on his sons and daughters. We cannot be true sons unless our will wills his, our doing following his making. Jesus was the true Son not because he couldn't help it but because he willed to be the son he was in the divine idea; so with us. We are sons and daughters by God's claim and must be so by our will, for we aren't made to be what we cannot help being. Only by choosing God as the Father he is and doing his will can we be saved into the original necessity and bliss of our being. Working this out must be painful, and handing it on to those below us always painful, but the eternal form of God's will for us is intense bliss.

"And the life was the light of men."

The life I have described became light for people when he in whom it began appeared. It became light so that they could see it and choose it for themselves, choosing to live that way.

There is always something deeper than anything said; all words, human and divine, all figures and pictures, are only outer layers through which the central reality shines more or less clearly. Light itself is the poor outer form of something deeper and better: life. Life is Christ himself; light is his body. Light is what we *see* in him; life is what we may *be* in him. Life "is a light by abundant clarity invisible," the unspeakable unknown, and it must become light people can see before they can know it. So the obedient human God appeared as an obedient divine man, humbly doing his Father's works among unfriendly brothers.
