The Son took form in flesh to be seen and so become the light of men---not that they might have light but that they might have life: seeing what they could never originate, they might begin to hunger for the life they are capable of, so that the life in them longs for him who is its life, as root and stem thirst for the flower they exist for. So the child of God may become the son of God by beholding *the* Son, the life revealed in light; his radiant heart may be sunlight to his fellows; the idea may be drawn out by the presence of the Ideal, the perfect Son of the Father, sent to his brothers and sisters.

The Son's devotion could never have been without the Father's, who seeks his own glory no more than the Son does and is devoted to the Son and all his sons and daughters with perfect, fathomless unselfishness. All that Jesus was and did on earth is what he has always been as the blessed Son-God of the Father-God: that life shining out so that people could see it, being like God, doing God's will and works, and so unveiling the Father in the Son. It is the Son's prayer to the other sons to come back to the Father and be reconciled. He seems to me to say: "I know your Father, because he is my Father; I have been with him from eternity. You don't know him, and I have come to tell you he is just like me, only greater and better. He alone is the true, original good; I am true because I seek nothing but his will. He alone is all in all; I am not, but he is my Father, and I am the son in whom his heart of love is satisfied. Come home with me and sit on the throne of my obedience. Together we will do his will and be glad, for his will is the only good. Do with me what you like; I won't defend myself. My witness is true, and I will stand by it whatever comes. If I held to it only until danger came close, then prayed for twelve legions of angels, I would be saying the Father will do anything for his children until it hurts him. I bear witness, in the face of death, that my Father is like me, and I dare death to prove me wrong. Kill me, do what you will: my Father is true, and I am true in saying so. Death can kill only my body; it can't take me prisoner. Father, your will be done! The pain will pass; it is only for a time. I will gladly suffer so that people know I live and that you are my life. Be with me, Father, so that it is not more than I can bear."

Friends, if you suppose anything less than this could save the world, or bring joy to a single child of God's making, you know neither the Son nor the Father.

What holds the universe together, the one active unity, reconciling every form and wandering desire and returning love, the fact at the root of every vision, showing that "love is the only good in the world" and selfishness the one hateful thing, is the Son's devotion to the Father. It is the life of the universe. What makes the universe a whole is not that God created everything, but that the one through whom he created it loves him perfectly and is content to exist because his Father is with him. Nor is it that God is all in all, but the Son's love for the Father. Unity cannot come from oneness: for unity to begin there must be two. Without Christ, then, there could be no universe. The reconciliation Jesus brought is not the first source of the world's unity and safety; it was the necessary working out of the prior eternal fact that God and Christ are one, Father and Son, each loving the other as only a Father or only a Son can. The Lord's prayer for unity between people, the Father and himself springs from love's eternal need. Without the Father and the Son, no two would care a jot for each other. It might be right for creatures to love because they exist, but what two creatures would ever have started the loving? I can't believe it would have been I; even with an inclination to love, selfishness would soon have overcome it. But if the Father loves the Son, if the music behind life's harmony lies not in a theory of love in the Father's heart but in the burning fact of it in the hearts of Father and Son, then glory to the Father, the Son and the spirit of both, who draw us up into the glory of their joy, to share their thoughts of love, of delight and rest in each other, of joy in all the little ones. The life of Jesus is the light of men, showing them the Father.

But light is not enough; it is for the sake of life. We too must have life in ourselves, and we can live only as Jesus lived, in the way life was made in him: by giving up our life, the one supreme act open to us for making life in ourselves. Christ did it of himself and became light to us so that we could do it after him. We must do it ourselves; all his help, the light and the Spirit's working in our hearts, is so that we can. Until then we are not alive. The Son's whole agony with every person is to get him to die as he died. Preaching that doesn't aim at this builds with wood and hay and stubble. If I cannot say with my whole heart, "Father, do with me as you will, only help me against myself and for you"; if I cannot say, "I am your child, a part of you, glorious in you but grown poor in me: let me be your dog, your horse, anything you will, yours in any shape, and my own or anyone else's only as yours"---if we cannot give ourselves like that, we have not yet grasped what Christ has grasped us for. The faith a person may, and must, put in God reaches beyond the farthest star the universe could hold. But the question now is not moving mountains, which will one day be easy, but waking and rising from the dead now.

When someone truly says "Thy will be done" with Jesus, as Jesus said it, he closes the everlasting circle of life, and the life of the Father and the Son flows through him as part of the divine organism. Then the Lord's prayer is fulfilled in him: "I in them and thou in me, that they may be made perfect in one." The Christ in us is the perfect child's spirit toward the perfect Father, our true nature flowered by the Lord, whose life is the light of men so that it may become their life, for our true nature is childhood to the Father.

Friends, if you know or suspect these things are true, let us get up and live, even in our darkest spiritual dullness, when hope itself sees nothing to hope for. Let us not dwell on the cause of our earthliness, unless it is some wrong in us, but go straight to the Life. Never let us take comfort in the thought that our deadness is physical: what comfort is it that a deadened body overpowers a spirit that should master it and inspire it to obey? Let us find comfort in the Father and the Son: as long as the Son loves the Father with all the love the Father can welcome, all is well with the little ones. God is all right, so why mind standing a minute in the dark outside his window? We miss being inside, but waiting has its own bliss. Suppose the rain falls and the wind blows, and we stand alone, or with someone dear sharing our *outness*, and the window is dark behind curtains of inscrutable good. We can tell ourselves, or our friend, "God is; Jesus is not dead; nothing can be going wrong, however it looks to hearts unfinished in childness." And we can say to the Lord, "Jesus, are you loving the Father in there? Then we out here will do his will and wait patiently till he opens the door. Perhaps you are telling the Father, 'Your little ones need some wind and rain; their buds are hard and the flowers won't open; I can't make them blessed without a bit more winter.' And perhaps the Father will say, 'Comfort them, my son Jesus, by remembering your patience when you were missing me, and how sure you were of me when everything around you seemed so unlike me.'" In a word, let us be at peace, because peace is at the heart of things: perfect satisfaction between the Father and the Son, which they call us to share and promise we will, when they have had their good way with us.

Ahead lies a happiness beyond words or human invention for every child who falls in with the Father's perfect imagination. His imagination is one with his creative will: what God imagines exists. When the created one falls in with the will of him who "loved him into being," all is well, and the great creation goes on in him on ever higher levels. Your will, O God, be done! Everything else is loss and decay. There is no life but that born of the life the Word made in himself by doing your will, which is the light of men. Through that light the life of men is born, the same life that first came to be in Jesus. As he laid down his life, so must we lay down ours, that as he lives we may live. That which was made in him was life, and the life is the light of men; yet his own, to whom he was sent, *did not believe him*.

### THE KNOWING OF THE SON

*The passage:* You have never heard God\'s voice or seen his form, and his word does not stay in you, for you do not believe the one he sent (John v. 37, 38).

One day we shall know how near the New Testament comes to the Lord\'s very words. That we have them with a difference I cannot doubt. For one thing, I do not believe he spoke Greek. He was sent to the lost sheep of Israel, and the thoughts of God would have come from his heart in the mother tongue of the simple men he spoke to, not one they knew secondhand. He may have spoken Greek to the less simple Jews of Jerusalem, but I see no ground to think so.

Are we bound to believe that John, who understood him best, could give us in his gospel, after so many years, the *very* words of the simplest profundities ever heard on earth, supposing the Lord spoke Greek to his disciples? I do not say he could not; I ask whether we must believe he could. When the divine presence in their hearts made the disciples able to understand the Lord, they remembered things they had forgotten, perhaps even the very words. But must we believe the evangelists always recorded his words exactly? The small differences between their records are answer enough. John\'s gospel is the fruit of years of remembering and pondering, one understood thing recalling another; whatever memory can do at its best, with God in the man, I do not believe John always gave the very words. God has not cared that we should have assurance of his very words anywhere---perhaps because his children are prone to word-worship, but also because he would not have them oppressed by words. Being human, words are only partly capable; they could not absolutely contain what the Lord meant, and even he must rely on the spirit of his disciple to be understood. The letter cannot give life, so it should not be throned with power to kill; it should only be the handmaid that opens the door of truth to the mind that is *of* the truth.

\'Then you believe in an individual inspiration for anyone who chooses to claim it!\'

Yes---for everyone who claims it from God, not for everyone who claims from men the recognition of having it; he who has a thing does not need it recognized. If I did not believe in a special inspiration for every man who asks for the holy spirit, I would have to throw out the whole tale as an imposture, since in it the Lord promises it to those who ask. An objector without this spirit knows nothing of the words that are spirit and life; his objection is worth less than a savage\'s to a chemist\'s assertion, and his assent is an idle blowing of a horn.

\'But how is one to tell whether it is really the spirit of God speaking in a man?\'

You are not called on to tell. The question for you is whether you have the spirit of Christ yourself: Am I alive with the life of Christ? Everyone who desires to follow the Master has his spirit, and will receive more, to follow closer. He need not prove to anyone that he has the light of Jesus; he has to let it shine. That does not make him a teacher, or able to put large truths into true forms. But when the strength or joy or pity of the truth urges him, let him speak it unafraid, content to be condemned for it, and comforted that if he is mistaken the Lord himself will condemn him and save him \'as by fire.\' Men\'s condemnation, even in Christ\'s name, will not hurt him. If he speaks true, the Lord will say \'I sent him\'; for all truth is of him, and no one sees a true thing to be true except by the Lord, the spirit.

\'How am I to know that a thing is true?\'

By *doing* what you know to be true, calling nothing true until you see it to be true, and staying silent until the truth opens your mouth. If you are meant to be silent, woe to you if you speak.

\'But if I do not take the words the evangelists attribute to him as the certain, absolute, very words of the Master, how am I to know that they represent his truth?\'

By seeing in them what answers to the plainest truth he speaks and to the power working in you to make you a true man; by their appeal to your judgment and their rousing of your conscience. If they do not seem true to you, either they are not the Master\'s words or you are not true enough to understand them. Words of his that do not show their truth to you are not yet the word of God to you, for they are not spirit and life in you. They may be the nearest words can come to the truth, and may have brought many to God\'s heart, but for you they remain sealed. A true heart will revere them as probably carrying the Master\'s meaning; to you they are the rock in the desert before Moses spoke to it. If you wait, your ignorance will not hurt you; if you presume to reason from them, you are a blind man disputing about what he never saw. To reason about truth from words that do not show us they are true is to walk into the mire, the presumption of Pharisaical *hypocrisy*, which only the untrue are capable of. Humble mistake will not hurt us: the truth is there, and the Lord will see that we come to know it. Even when we think we know it with scarcely a glimpse, the error of a true heart will not be allowed to ruin it; it would not have mistaken the truth but for the untruth still in it, and he who casts out devils will cast out that devil.

In the saying before us I see enough to believe its words embody the mind of Christ. If I could not, I would say, \'I have not yet recognised his mind in this, so I have not understood it, for to understand what is true is to know it true.\' I have yet to see any words credibly reported as Jesus\' about which I dared to say, \'His mind is not in them.\' A man\'s mind can receive a word only so far as it is Christ\'s word and he is one with Christ; to him who truly receives it, it is a power, not of argument but of life. The Lord\'s words are for the spiritual logic that reasons from divine thought to divine thought, not for the logic that handles words as if they were things.

No thought, human or divine, can pass between people except through the symbolism of creation. The heavens and the earth are around us so that we can speak of the unseen by the seen, for the outermost husk of creation corresponds to the deepest things of the Creator. He is not a God who hides himself but one who made in order to reveal. An enemy has meddled with some things, but with more he could not; through these we may speak of God, for once he is revealed they show so much of his nature that we use them as spiritual tokens to carry to others what we have seen of the unseen. The words I now want to look into belong to this kind of mediation.

\'And the Father who sent me has himself testified of me. You have never heard his voice or seen his shape, and his word does not abide in you, for you do not believe the one he sent.\'

If Jesus said these words, he meant more than lies on the surface. They cannot merely assert what everybody knew, nor can their repeated negations be tautology; he was not telling the Jews a fact they would never have denied. Which of them would claim to have heard God\'s voice or seen his shape? John himself says, \'No man hath seen God at any time.\' The tone here is reproach. Then he reproaches them for not seeing God, when no man has, and Paul says no man can! Is there a paradox? There cannot be the sophism: \'No man has seen God; you are to blame for not having seen him; therefore all men are to blame.\' And if we read it, \'No man has seen God, but some ought to have seen him,\' there is no hope for the race in being told that not one who could see him ever has.

One utterance is John\'s, the other his master\'s. If they contradicted, John\'s would of course be thrown away; but there can hardly be contradiction, since the man who says the one records the other from the master he belonged to and loved as no man ever loved man.

The word *see* has one sense in one statement and another in the other: *with the eyes* in the first, *with the soul* in the second. One is said of all men; the other to certain Jews of Jerusalem about themselves. No man has seen God with bodily eyes; these Jews ought to have seen him with spiritual eyes.

No man has ever seen God in any outward, visible form of his own; he is revealed in no shape but his son\'s. But multitudes have seen something of God with the eye of the heart, and perhaps every man might and ought to. We cannot follow God into the minute workings of his spirit any more than into the life-forces beyond the microscope; he may be working in a savage\'s heart in ways no wisdom of his wisest child can see. Many who have never beheld God\'s face have caught a glimpse of the hem of his garment, or seen the vastness of his shadow; thousands who have never felt the warmth of its folds have been startled by

| No face: only the sight
| Of a sweepy garment vast and white.

Some have dreamed his hand laid on them who never knew themselves gathered to his bosom. The Lord\'s reproach is to men who ought to have had an experience they had not had. Let us look nearer at his words.

\'Ye have not heard his voice\' might mean \'*you have never listened*\' or \'*never obeyed*\'; but the next phrase, \'nor seen his shape,\' keeps us to the primary sense of *hear*: \'*You have never heard his voice so as to know it for his*,\' and \'*You do not know what he is like*.\' He plainly implies, \'*You ought to know both*.\' \'You have not his word abiding in you\' means \'*The word in you from the beginning, the word of God in your conscience, you have not kept; the scripture in which you think you have eternal life, which you accept as Moses\' witness, is not at home in you. It comes to you and goes from you; you hear, heed not and forget. You do not dwell with it, brood on it and obey it. You are not of its kind, not among those whose ears are ready and who hunger after the word of the Father*.\'

What is the sign in them of this ignorance of God, on which the Lord bases his accusation?---\'You do not believe the one he sent.\'

\'How so?\' the Jews might answer. \'Have we not asked you for a sign from heaven, and have you not flatly refused it?\'

The argument carried little weight with those it applied to most, since the more it applied the less they could see that it did; but it would carry great force with some listeners whose minds were open and whose hearts were drawn to him. His argument was this: \'If you had ever heard the Father\'s voice, imagined a God anything like him, or cared for his will so that his word was at home in your hearts, you would have known me when you saw me. The least acquaintance with God, such as any true heart has, would have shown you by the light of his word in you, by the shape you had glimpsed however vaguely, and by the likeness of my face and voice to my father\'s, that I came from him; and you would have listened. The family feeling would have been awake, the holy instinct that makes you know your elder brother. That you do not know me is because you do not know your own father, even my father: all your lives you have refused to do his will, and so have not heard his voice; you have thought of him only as a partisan of your ambitions. If you had loved my father, you would have known his son.\' I think he might have added, \'If you had even loved your neighbour, you would have known me, neighbour to the deepest and best in you.\' If the Lord appeared in England today as once in Palestine, he would not come in the painters\' halo, or in that wintry shine of effeminate beauty and sweet weakness in which they helplessly portray him; nor probably as a carpenter, mason or gardener. He would come in a form and condition bearing the same relation to England, Scotland and Ireland now as his then bore to motley Judea, Samaria and Galilee. Coming so unlooked for, who would recognize and receive him? The idea involves no absurdity; he is not far from us at any moment, if the old story is more than the best of the world\'s fables. Now as then, the first would be the childlike in heart, the truest, the least selfish---not the highest in any church\'s estimation, for the childlike are not yet the many, and perhaps not even those who know most about his former visit and have pondered every word of the Greek Testament. The first to cry \'It is the Lord!\' would be neither \'good churchman\' nor \'good dissenter,\' nor anyone who imagines him caring about stupid outside matters. It would not be the man who holds by the mooring-ring of the letter, fast in the quay of what he calls theology, and from his rotting deck abuses those who go down to the sea in ships---who lets the wind of the spirit blow where it will, but never lets it blow him out among its wonders in the deep. Nor he who obeys a command without caring to see its reason, nor he who from barrenness of soul cannot receive the Master\'s meaning and so makes his word of none effect. If anyone, it would be those likest the Master, who did the will of their father and his, who built on the rock by hearing and doing his sayings. But are there any enough like him to know him at once by voice and face? Multitudes would be taken by a false Christ fashioned after their fancy, and would reject the Lord as a poor impostor. One thing is certain: those who first recognized him would be those who most loved righteousness and hated iniquity.

But I would not forget that foolish forms often cover a live heart, warm toward everything human and divine; the ugliest robe may hide the loveliest form. Every covering is not a clothing. The grass clothes the fields, and the glory surpassing Solomon\'s clothes the grass; but the traditions of even the worthiest elders will not clothe a soul, much less those of the unworthy. A soul\'s true clothing must grow out of the soul itself. Some naked souls need only the sight of truth to rush to it, as Dante says, like a wild beast to its den; others, heavily clad in the scribes\' leftover garments and afraid of tearing what is fit only to be trodden underfoot, circle it warily like a shy horse that fears a hidden enemy. But let each be true in the way possible to him, and he shall have the Master\'s praise.

If the Lord appeared, the many who take the common presentation of a thing or person for the thing or person could never see the new vision as another form of the old. The Master has been so misrepresented by those who claimed to present him---above all in the relation between him and his father---that they could see no likeness. For my part, I would rather believe in no God than in the God generally offered. How far those may be to blame who, righteously disgusted, throw the idea away without asking whether something in it may be true though most must be false, on the chance that some who call themselves his prophets have taken spiritual bribes

| To mingle beauty with infirmities,
| And pure perfection with impure defeature---

how far those may be to blame, it is not my work to inquire. Some would gladly grasp the hope that this might prove a fact; others do not care to discern, on the palimpsest covered but not obliterated, a credible tale of a perfect man revealing a perfect God: they are not true enough to want that to be fact which would demand they model their lives on a perfect idea and found every hope on it.

*But all of us, beholding the glory of the Lord, are changed into the same image*.

### THE MIRRORS OF THE LORD

*The passage:* All of us, with open face beholding the glory of the Lord as in a glass, are changed into the same image, from glory to glory, as by the spirit of the Lord (II. Corinthians iii. 18).

This passage shows how Paul received the Lord, and how he understands the Lord\'s life to be the light of men and so their life also.

Of all writers I know, Paul seems to me the most determinedly practical. His so-called mysticism is sometimes a power of seeing, as by spiritual refraction, truths not yet risen above the human horizon; sometimes a wide-eyed habit of noting analogies between the concentric regions of creation. It is a divinely alive poetic imagination, whose part is to foresee and welcome approaching truth, find the same principle in unlike things, and embody its discoveries in new forms and symbols that present the deeper truths they exist for.

I find in Paul the same artistic fault, with the same difficulty, that I find in Shakspere. It springs from the admirable fact that the man is much more than the artist: he tries to say too much at once, pouring a soul swelling with life and thought through the too narrow neck of human utterance. So we are sometimes bewildered between two or more equally good meanings, unsure of his line of reasoning; but the uncertainty is always intellectual, never practical. What Paul cares about is plain to the true heart, though not to the man whose wish to understand outruns his obedience, who assumes Paul meant to teach a system, to explain rather than help us see God---a God revealed only to childlike insight, never to the keenest intellect. Paul, like his master, set out to rouse men to seek the kingdom of God over them and his righteousness in them; to drop the lust of possession and passing pleasure; to look on the glory of the Father and turn to him from all he hates; to see the brotherhood of men and the hideousness of whatever is unfair, unloving or self-exalting. He taught no plan of salvation but obedience to the Lord of Life. He knew nothing of the so-called Christian systems that change the glory of the perfect God into the likeness of low intellects and dull consciences, a worse corruption than representing him in human shape. What soul would not prefer the Apollo of light, the high-walking Hyperion, to the self-cherishing monarch, law-dispensing magistrate or cruel martinet born of pagan Roman arrogance and accepted by the world in the church as the portrait of its God! Jesus Christ is the *only* likeness of the living Father.

Let us see what Paul teaches here about the life that is the light of men---his way of bringing to bear the truth John announced.

When Moses came out from speaking with God, his face was radiant, a wonder and a power to the people. The radiance began at once to fade, as was natural, since it was not Moses\'s own, so he veiled his face that they might not see it fade. Whether that was right or wise I leave aside. Entering the tabernacle he took the veil off to speak with God open-faced, and put it on again coming out. Paul says that this veil now lies on the hearts of the Jews, so that they cannot understand Moses; but when they turn to the Lord, going into the tabernacle with Moses, it will be taken away and they will see God, and understand that the glory on Moses\'s face has faded before the glory that excels it, the glory of Jesus. Here I can hardly help asking: Would not Moses have done better to let them see that his glory depended altogether on the glory within the veil, where they were not worthy to enter? Did that veil hide only his face? Did he not, however unintentionally, lay it on their hearts, where it hid God, so that they stormed at the idea that their prophet\'s glory must yield to the greater than Moses? Might they not, without it, have realized that his was a glory that must pass, whose glory was to prepare the way for a glory that must extinguish it? Moses put the veil for ever from his face, but they clutched it to their hearts, and it blinded them---a fine symbol of the wilful blindness of the old Mosaist or modern Wesleyan, who admits no light his Moses or his Wesley did not see, and so loses even what of the light he saw and reflected.

Paul says the sight of the Lord will burn that veil from their hearts. His presence gives liberty: where he is, there is no more bondage, wilderness or Sinai. The Son makes free with sonship.

Now comes the passage I want to make clearer:

\'But we all,\' having this presence and this liberty, \'with open face beholding as in a glass the glory of the Lord, are changed into the same image\'---the Lord\'s---\'from glory to glory, even as of the Lord, the spirit.\'

\'We need no Moses, no earthly mediator, to come between us and the light and bring out a little of the glory. We go into the presence of the Son revealing the Father. Our mediator is the Lord himself, the spirit of light, not sent by us to bring back God\'s will, but come from God to bring us himself. As Moses stood with uncovered face receiving the glory of God full upon it, so we---you and I, Corinthians---stand with open face in the full light of that glory, only how much more radiant! It is no reflected light we see, but the glory of God shining out of the face of Christ. Israel saw the fading reflection of God\'s glory on Moses\'s face; we see the glory itself in the face of Jesus.\'

But in what follows, the revised version misses the meaning almost as much as the authorized, when instead of \'beholding as in a glass\' it gives \'reflecting as a mirror.\' The first is wrong; the second is far from right. The figure is a poet\'s, not a scientist\'s: the poet deals with the outer show of things, which is deeper in its relation to truth, and more useful, than analysis. Paul thought of the mirror not as throwing back light but as receiving, taking into itself what is presented to it---here filling its bosom with the glory it looks on. When I see my friend\'s face in a mirror, the mirror seems to hold it in a liquid embrace; the face is *there*, down in the depth. True, it shines out, but Paul\'s thought is the *visual* fact, which Wordsworth says the poet always seizes, of the mirror holding the face.

That a poet or prophet---Paul was both---would think of it so, especially in his age, I can make more probable from a passage in Dante, whose time, though much farther from the apostle\'s than ours from Dante\'s, was in many ways liker Paul\'s than ours.

The passage is this:---Dell\' Inferno: Canto xxiii. 25-27:

| E quei: \'S\'io fossi d\'impiombato vetro,
|   L\'immagine di fuor tua non trarrei
|   Piu tosto a me, che quella dentro impetro.\'

Here Virgil says to Dante, of his power to read his thoughts:

\'If I were of leaded glass\'---glass backed with lead, a mirror---\'I should not draw thy outward image to me more readily than I gain thy inner one\'---than I now know your thoughts.

So the true, simple and most literal word for the Greek seems to me the verb *mirror*, and the sentence so far would run: \'But we all, with unveiled face, mirroring the glory of the Lord,---.\'

I must now unfold the idea at work in the apostle\'s heart. A correct translation is nothing unless it brings a deeper or fresher insight; I have nothing to do with the man who cares for words apart from what the writer meant by them, until he stops \'passing for a man\' and becomes one. The prophet-apostle seems to me to say, \'We all, with clear vision of the Lord, mirroring his glory in our hearts as a mirror takes his face into itself, are thereby changed into his likeness, his glory working our glory, by the present power in our inmost being of the Lord, the spirit.\' Our mirroring of Christ is one with the presence of his spirit in us. The idea is not the radiating of Christ\'s light on others, though that would be a lawful figure, but the taking into us, and having in us, of him working to change us.

That the thing signified outreaches the figure is no discovery; the simile is only a ladder to a higher stratum, and the climber who reaches it \'then unto the ladder turns his back.\' So what the mirror stands for has properties far beyond leaded glass or polished metal: it is a live soul understanding what it takes into its deeps, holding it and conscious of what it holds. It mirrors by its will to hold. Unlike its symbol, it holds not only the outward resemblance but the inward likeness of the person revealed, and is open to the influence of what it embraces and can co-operate with it, mirror and mirrored being of one origin and nature. Paul\'s idea is that when we take into our understanding, heart, conscience and being the glory of God, Jesus Christ as he shows himself to us, he works on us, and keeps working till we are changed into the likeness we have mirrored; for with his likeness he comes himself and dwells in us. He will work until that likeness is perfected in us, the image of the humanity of God in which we were first made, which could never develop in us except by the indwelling of the perfect likeness. By the power of Christ thus received and at home in us, we are changed: the glory in him becomes glory in us, his glory changing us to glory.

But we must beware of receiving this or any symbol *after the flesh*, of taking it in any merely physical, psychical or spirituo-mechanical way. The symbol deals with things beyond the region symbols are drawn from. The indwelling of Jesus in the soul of man, who shall declare! But let us note that his dwelling in us is the power of the spirit of God upon us; \'the Lord is that spirit,\' and with him dwelling in us we are changed \'even as from the Lord the spirit.\' When we think Christ, Christ comes; when we receive his image into our spiritual mirror, he enters with it. Our open, receiving thought is his door; when our hearts turn to him, holding up our mirror, he comes in, not by our thought only or in our idea only, but himself and of his own will, as we could not take him but as he can come, enabling us by his very coming to receive the one welcome guest of the whole universe. So the Lord, the spirit, becomes the soul of our souls, becomes spiritually what he always was creatively; as our spirit gives shape to our bodies, his soul gives shape to ours. This is nothing unnatural or at conflict with our being. It is only that the deeper soul that willed and wills our souls, the infinite Life, rises up into the Self we call *I* and *me*, which lives directly from him and is unspeakably more his than ours, and works with his creation at higher levels, making the *I* and *me* more and more his and himself more and more ours; until the glory of our existence flashes upon us, we face full to the sun that lights what it sent forth, and know ourselves alive with the Father\'s own infinite life. We know that our existence is not the moonlight of mere consciousness of being, but the sun-glory of a life justified by becoming one with its origin, thinking and feeling with the primal Sun of life, from whom it was dropped away that it might know itself and return to circle for ever in exultant harmony around him. Then indeed we *are* and have life: the life of Jesus has, through light, become life in us; the glory of God in the face of Jesus, mirrored in our hearts, has made us alive; we are one with God for ever and ever.

What less than such a splendour of hope would be worthy of the revelation of Jesus? Filled with the soul of their Father, men shall inherit his glory; filled with themselves, they cast him out and rot. Only the company of the Lord, soul to soul, his life of God-devotion, saves his brethren with life; they must receive the Son, and through the Son the Father. What it cost the Son to get so near that we could say *Come in* is the story of his life. He stands at the door and knocks, and when we open he comes in and dwells with us, and we are transformed to the same image of truth, purity and heavenly childhood. Where power dwells there is no force; where the spirit-Lord is there is liberty. By free, potent communion with their inmost being, he will change his obedient brethren till in every thought and impulse they are like him: unselfish, neighbourly, loving the Father perfectly, ready to die for the truth, caring for nothing but the will of God, which is love, harmony, liberty, beauty and joy.

I do not know if we may call this having life in ourselves; but it is the waking up and perfecting in us of the divine life inherited from our Father, who made us in his own image and whose nature remains in us, which makes it the deepest reproach to a man that he has neither heard his voice nor seen his shape. He who would thus live must, as a mirror draws an outward glory into its bosom, receive into his \'heart of heart\' the inward glory of Jesus Christ, *the Truth*.

### THE TRUTH

*The passage:* The Lord says, "I am the truth" (John xiv. 6).

The man of the five senses uses "truth" only for something historical or scientific that has been proved a fact. He may grant that a higher truth could exist, but his lower powers can't prove it, so he acts as if a wise man would ignore any appeal to the highest part of his nature. But in the word of *the* man, truth means more than fact, more than the relations of facts or persons, more than the loftiest metaphysical abstraction: it means being and life, will and action. For he says, "*I am the truth*."

I want to help whoever I can to understand what *the truth* means, not through definitions or logical distinctions but so that the right idea comes to mind when they hear the word from the Lord, and not an empty sound or a vague or false notion. If he says "I am the truth," it is good to know what he means by it. He can mean nothing merely intellectual that could be stated and left there; he means something vital, which governs all its necessary relations and includes whatever goes by the same name on any lower plane. Let us climb toward it step by step.

When something is so, the word that says it is so is the truth. But the fact may be worth nothing in itself, and so may our knowing it. Whether I walked on one side of the street or the other one morning can't matter in itself, though it might matter to someone to know. So it would sound wrong to say "It is a *truth* that I walked on the sunny side"; the right word is *fact*. Whether a statement about it is correct is still a question of fact. But when we ask whether the statement is true or false, we are looking at it as a person's assertion, and have risen to another plane. Which side I walked on may not matter, but it matters vitally to the witness, and to anyone who loves him, whether he believes what he says: whether he himself is true or false. About the thing, the question is of *fact*; about the man, it is of truth. He is either a pure soul or a false one, capable and guilty of a lie, and it makes no difference whether he stated the fact; if he meant to, he remains a true man.

I anticipate here: there are *truths* as well as *facts*, and lies against truths as well as against facts. The Pharisees who said *Corban* lied against the truth that a man must honour his father and mother.

Now let us rise from casual facts to constant ones that we cannot change, which involve what we call *law*. These have more dignity, and statements about them matter more. Whether the water in my jug froze one morning is a small matter; that water always freezes at thirty-two degrees Fahrenheit is of great importance. Is that a *truth*? I think not. No principle we can see is involved in the changeless fact. The principle at its root in God's mind must be a truth, but to us the fact is still only a fact, and the word truth should be kept for higher things. Some call the facts of nature truths, but however wide a law you make, you have gone no higher than saying that such and such is always so. Call it a law of nature, not a truth. It can't be a truth to us until we see why it exists, its relation to mind and intent and ultimately to self-existence: tell us why it *must* be so, and you state a truth. When we see that a law is what it is because it embodies an eternal thought, a fact of the being of God (and only God's facts are truths), it becomes an embodied truth to us, a way God would have us think of him. When a law of nature makes us say, "I see why it ought to be; it is just like God," it rises, not to the dignity of a truth in itself, but to the truth of its own nature: a revelation of God's character and will, a picture of something in him, far nearer a truth than anything below it. Without the solidity of matter, what notion would we have of the unchanging, or of God and truth and law?

There is a region, perhaps lower by scientific standards, where the word truth begins to apply. I believe every fact in nature is a revelation of God, there as it is because God is as he is, and I suspect all its facts impress us so that we learn God without knowing it. We can see any one fact this way only by finding its soul, its fact of God; but from our first contact with the world it has shown us God through things seen, by which we come to know the unseen. What idea of God could we have without the sky, a visible sphere that is also a formless infinity? The truth of the sky is what it makes us feel of the God who sent it to our eyes. Light, in its discovered laws, seems to me to be as it is because God is as he is: its so-called laws are the waving of his garments, waving as they do because he is thinking and loving and walking inside them.

We are now in a region far above the one commonly claimed for science, open only to the heart of the child and the childlike man and woman. The poet is at home here, and often has to go there to fetch his things. For things as they are, not as science deals with them, are God's revelation to his children. To be clear: every fact and law of science, even a tentative one, has God's will in it and may reveal him, but none is there for its own sake. Each is a means; the intent, and God in it, are found in the finished end, not in the laws except as his means. That is why human science cannot discover God: it unpicks the tapestry of God's science backward, working with its back to him, always leaving his intent, his finished work, behind. At best it reaches his back, never his face, while those who would reach his heart, who like Dante are returning to where they already are, will find the springhead of his science too. Analysis is well, as death is well; analysis is death, not life. It finds a little of the way God walks to his ends, but leaves the end behind. I don't say the scientist does so, but the process of his work does, following God's footsteps backward without, too often, appreciating what they were for. To rise from the finished work is the swifter and loftier ascent. Even if a man could find out why God worked as he did, he would discover God, but not the best and deepest of him, for means cannot be as great as ends.

Ask a man of mere science what the truth of a flower is, and he will pull it to pieces and show you its parts and how each serves its life, and tell you where it grows and what climate and insects do to it. Ask the poet, and he will say, "Why, the flower itself, the perfect flower, and what it cannot help saying to anyone with ears to hear." The truth of a flower is not the facts about it, however accurate, but the shining, gladdening, patient thing on its stalk that draws smiles and tears from child and prophet. The man of science laughs at this because he is only a man of science and doesn't know what it means; the poet and the child care as little for his laughter as the birds of God (Dante's name for angels) for his treatise on aeronautics. The children of God must always be mocked by the children of the world, in the church or out of it, who have sharp eyes and ears but dull hearts; those who hold love the only good understand them, smile, and can do without what they have to offer. In the higher state their love is leading them to, they will soon outstrip the men of science, for they have what lies at the root of science, what God's science exists to reveal. What does it profit a man to know everything and lose the bliss, the sense of well-being, that alone gives his knowledge value?

God's science in the flower exists for the flower's relation to his children. If we understand the flower, are at one with it and love it, we have what the science is for, and only that equips us to search truly for the means by which God's idea of the flower was worked out. The idea of God *is* the flower, not its botany, which is only ways and means, like canvas and brush to the picture in a painter's mind. The mere intellect can't find out what owes its being to the supreme heart; its relation to what is born of the heart is false unless humble. God thought the flower, invented its means, and sent it, a gift of himself, to the eyes and hearts of his children. That even the ignorant and degraded love flowers suggests they have a place in the history of the world, as written in heaven's archives, that science could never understand. Watch that child! He has found one of his silent, motionless brothers, with God's clothing on it and God's thought in its face. What a smile breaks out as the divine understanding passes between them! Watch his mother when he takes it home to her, no nearer understanding it than he. It is no old association that brings tears to her eyes, but God's thought, unrecognized as such, holding communion with her; she weeps with inexplicable delight. It is only a daisy! But to her it is no mere fact or law of nature; it is the truth of a flower, a perfect thought from the heart of God, a truth of God: not an intellectual truth but a divine fact, a dim revelation, a movement of the creative soul. Who but a father could think flowers up for his little ones? We are close now to the region where the Lord's word is at home: "I am the truth."

What is the truth of water? That it is made of hydrogen and oxygen? Does this precious thing exist for the sake of that fact? Did God put the two gases together only so that man could pull them apart, as a father makes toys only to be pulled to pieces? That is a school examiner's idea of a toy's best use, not a father's! Show us what in the two gases fits them to be honoured by forming this lovely thing, and you will give us a revelation about more than water, about the God who made them. There is no water in oxygen or hydrogen. It comes bubbling fresh from the imagination of the living God. Water itself, which dances and sings and slakes that wonderful thirst (a picture of the draught the woman of Samaria asked Jesus for), is its own truth, and so a truth of God. Let anyone who wants to know the maker's love get painfully thirsty and drink from a brook, and then lift up his heart, not just then to the maker of oxygen and hydrogen but to the inventor and mediator of thirst and water, to foresee a little of what his soul may find in God. If he doesn't then pant like a deer for water-brooks, let him go back to his science and its husks, which will make him thirsty at last, like the Persian's victim in the dust-tower. Resolving water into its elements tells no more about it than "thirst and water" would tell about the joy of drinking. Let a man go to the hillside and let the brook sing to him until he loves it, and he will be far nearer the fountain of truth than the chemist's triumphal car will ever carry its shouting, half-understanding followers. He will draw from the brook the water of joyous tears, and worship him who made heaven and earth, the sea and the springs of water.

So the truth *of a thing* is its blossom, what it was made for, the topmost stone set on with rejoicing. Truth in a man's imagination is the power to recognize that: wherever we see the glory of God in anything he has made, sky or flower or human face, a true imagination is seeing a truth of God. Now to a still higher plane.

When truth touches a man not as something outside him that his intellect reflects, but as a being of action, so that knowing it affects or ought to affect his sense of duty, it becomes far nobler. A fact of no value in itself becomes a matter of moral life and death when he must choose to be true or false about it. Seeing the truth of a thing, its heart and crown, brings him near the fountain of truth it came from; perceiving God in what is, he becomes more of what he was meant to be, with new relations to the universe. But doing the least duty raises him far higher; by it he begins to be a true man. A man may delight in the vision and glory of a truth and not be true himself; the man with weak sight who does what he sees, as far as he sees, and wants to see farther, is true. Knowing what is so and saying it isn't makes a liar. Whoever recognizes the truth of a human relation and neglects its duty, or knows the laws of nature and ignores them, is not a true man, and the more he teaches those laws the less true he is. He may obey every one and still be the falsest of men, because of higher and closer duties he neglects: whoever looks after himself and not his brother and sister is false. A man may be a poet, aware of the beauty that is a thing's final cause and of the creative loveliness behind it; he may refuse to lie, steal or slander; and still not be a true man: he has not come to the flower of his own being, or reached the truth of *a thing* in his own case, that for which he exists, the idea in the mind that made him, and he is not striving toward it. There are relations closer than those with the facts around him, and plainer than those that seem to bring the maker near, which he fails to see, or sees and fails to acknowledge, or acknowledges and fails to fulfil. A man is a man only in doing the truth, and a perfect man only in doing the highest truth, which is fulfilling his relations to his origin. But his relations with his fellow men are infinitely closer than with the things around him, and to many far plainer than his relation to God; the nearer is plain so that he can step on it and rise to the higher. These relations, of thought to thought, being to being, duty to duty, spring from how his being began and are one with his nature. To the intellect alone they are facts of his nature; that they belong to it makes them truths, and fulfilling them a duty. By fulfilling them he is a true man, and by fulfilling them perfectly he is himself a living *truth*. He is made to understand them at first better than he can love them, so that he can choose them purely because they are true; choosing them, he chooses to love them, and by doing them is enabled to love and truly see them. Then they stop appearing as duties and show as what they are: absolute truths, essential realities, eternal delights. The man who chooses duty is a true man; the perfect man at last never thinks of duty and forgets its name. Jesus' duty was to do in lower forms what he loved and did perfectly in the highest as well; so he fulfilled all righteousness. One who went to the truth by mere impulse would be a holy animal, not a true man. Relations, truths and duties are shown to a man as lying beyond him so that he may choose them and be a child of God, choosing righteousness as God does. Hence the whole sad, victorious human story, and the glory yet to be revealed!

The moral philosopher who treats duties only as facts in his system is essentially a liar, and so is the man who sees them as truths, essential realities of his humanity, and goes no further. Each is a man in possibility, not yet a real one. To recognize these relations is to be obliged to fulfil them. A man who doesn't undoes the right of his own existence and destroys his *raison d'être*; he makes himself a living reason why he should not live, since nothing on those terms could ever have begun. His presence is a claim on his creator for destruction.

The facts of human relation are truths indeed, and most awful: "Anyone who hates his brother is a murderer, and no murderer has eternal life in him." The man who hunts pleasure instead of working the fields of duty, who thinks of himself as alone on earth, is himself a lie. Instead of the man he was made to be, he lives as the beasts seem to, except that they, I trust, are rising and he is sinking. But he can't be allowed to sink beyond God's reach; that is why the holy (healing) miseries come on him, which he complains of as hard and unfair. They are a painful persuasion to be himself, to be a truth.

Suppose that a man fulfilled all the relations to his fellows that I have described, and was toward them at least a true man. He would still feel, all the more, that something necessary to his well-being was missing. Like a living flower, he would feel he had not yet blossomed and could not tell what the blossom ought to be. The Lord's words to the young man, "If you would be perfect," point this way. His existence would not yet be justified to itself. He would stay unsatisfied, because a relation in him, the deepest, closest and strongest, would not yet be a living fact, a truth in him. Not being true toward it, he would not be himself, not ripened into the divine idea that alone can content itself. He is like a child with a child's heart who doesn't know he has a father yet misses him with his whole nature, if not his awareness. The relation has not yet begun to be fulfilled in him enough for the coming blossom to send ahead the patience and hope to live by faith without sight. When the flower begins to come, the human plant begins to rejoice in the glory of God not yet revealed, the inheritance of the saints in light; with raised stem and forward-leaning bud it waits for the hour when the lily of God's field will know itself alive, with God himself for its heart and air, the hour when God and the man are one and all God cares for is the man's. But I am forgetting my plan.

To the intellect the highest truth is the abstract one: the relation man has to the source of his being, his will to the will that made his a will, his love to the love that kindled his power to love, his intellect to the intellect that lit his. If a man treats these only as objects of thought, ideas to be analysed and arranged, he deals with them as facts, not truths. He is no better for it, probably worse, for he knows something and is false to what most deserves his faithfulness.

But sooner or later the soul (or heart, or spirit, whatever you call the man himself as distinct from his body) becomes aware that it needs someone above it to obey, to rest in, and to deliver it from what in himself is despicable, disappointing and unworthy even of his own interest. He becomes aware of an opposition in him that is not harmony, something he hates that is present in him and seems to be himself. He calls it the old Adam, the flesh, his lower nature, his evil self, or sometimes sees it simply as the part of him where God is not. Then he is in the region of truth, beginning to come true. He will soon find there is no part of him he would be at strife with if God were there, if that part were true to what it ought to be in right relation to the whole. Whatever its name, the old Adam or the animal in him, it would then fulfil its part holily, subject to the higher, a good horse, dog or tiger.

When a man bows before a power that can account for him, to whom he is no mystery as he is to himself, that knows where he came from and where he is going, why he loves this and hates that, and where he began to go wrong, and that longs to set him right and make him someone he can look up to without doubt or fear, confident as a child led by the hand toward the heights of happy-making truth, knowing his father is right where he is wrong; when he feels his whole being held in the embrace of a father who takes full responsibility for him, then he is bursting into flower. The truth of his being, his real nature, his idea, born in God and answering to God's truth, begins to show itself, and his nature is almost in harmony with itself. Obeying the will that is the cause of his being, and is righteousness and love and truth, he begins to stand on the peak of his being and know himself divine. He begins to feel free. It is the truth known not to his intellect but in his own sense of being true, in his essential awareness of his divine condition, that has made him free. No abstract truth, not even truth itself held by the purest metaphysical insight, can make anyone free; only the truth done, loved and lived, the truth that is *of* the man and not merely *in* him, the honesty that makes him a child of the honest God.

When a man loves and wills the truth with his whole nature, he is a living truth. But he did not originate this in himself; he saw it and strove for it. The one living, visible source of truth, embracing all truths in all relations, is Jesus Christ. His truth, chosen and willed by him, the ripeness of his being and flower of his sonship, the crown of his one supreme relation, is complete obedience to his Father. The obedient Jesus is Jesus the Truth, the root of all truth in people and of all its growth. However far from truly human a person is, his being is the undeveloped Christ in him. His likeness to Christ is his truth, as a flower's full meaning is the truth of the flower. Every man must come to the truth of God's idea of him: as Christ is the blossom of humanity, so the blossom of every man is the Christ perfected in him. The life-force of humanity working in him is Christ, his root, who generates and perfects his individuality. The stronger a man's pure will to be true, the freer and more active his choice, and the more distinct his individuality, the more he and all he has is Christ's. Without Christ he could not exist; existing, he could not have become capable of truth; capable of truth, he could never have loved it; loving and wanting it, he could not have reached it. Only the Lord's presence in the heart, his deep human sympathy, and whatever deeper thing passes between the creating Truth and the answering soul, could keep a man hoping until he forgets himself and leaves the door open for God to come and go. Christ gives us the will to will, the power to use it, and whatever extra help we need; but we must will the truth ourselves, and the Lord waits for that, for the victory of his Father in his child's heart. Only there can he see the result of his soul's labour and be satisfied. When the blossom breaks out in us, the more it is ours the more it is his, because the Father's highest creation, above all through the Son, is a being who can, like Father and Son, will what is right of his own accord. The groaning and labour, the blossom and the joy, belong to the Father, the Son and us. The will, the power of willing, may be created, but the willing itself is begotten. Because God wills first, man wills also.

When my being is consciously and willingly in the hands of the one who called it to live and think and suffer and be glad, given back to him by perfect obedience, I share the breath and life of God himself. Then I am free, because I am true, which means one with the Father; and freedom knows itself. A true man could not be miserable even in hell, for he is right with himself, being right with the one he came from. To be right with God is to be right with the universe, one with the power, love and will of the mighty Father, the cherisher of joy and lord of laughter, who owns all glories and hopes, loves everything, and hates nothing but selfishness, which he will not have in his kingdom.

Christ, then, is the Lord of life. His life is the light of men, and that light, reflected in them, changes them into the likeness of him, the Truth; so the Truth, the Son, makes them free.

### FREEDOM

*The passage:* The truth will make you free. Everyone who commits sin is a servant of sin. A servant does not stay in the house forever, but a son does. So if the Son makes you free, you will be free indeed (John viii. 32, 34-36).

I could not make sense of this passage as it stands. A man cannot be in the Father's house by being sin's servant, yet here the servant is in the house, and it is the Father's, not sin's. The saying looks muddled and the reasoning faulty; he must be there on some other ground. Left to myself I would have given up, suspecting the Lord's words had been misreported, or that I could not follow an argument resting on several basic truths. But most such puzzles, I am sure, began with a scribe who wrote a worthless note in the margin to explain what he did not understand. The next copyist took it for a missing line and put it into the text, corrupting the saying and obscuring its meaning. How much we owe the scholars who went back and found what was really written! Dr. Westcott's notes give another reading that he thinks reasonably likely to be the true one. The difference looks small, but it swaps doubtful ore for fine gold. Given the choice, I must hope for what is logical and bright over what is illogical and dull.

The reading I take to be true goes: 'Everyone who commits sin is a slave. But a slave does not stay in the house forever; a son does. So if the son makes you free, you will really be free.' The Authorized and Revised versions follow the reading that adds 'of sin.' That is true in itself, but I think useless for the argument that follows. I suspect my reading is what suggested to Paul the opening of Galatians 4---words of spirit and life, though misused to draw the doctrine of adoption, which is poison to a child's heart. The Lord is not saying that the sinner is sin's slave, true as that is, but that he is a slave---to God, as the argument shows. The two fit: no slavery to sin can stop a man being as much God's slave as God in his mercy chooses. Sin makes him a slave instead of a child; slavery to sin is his ruin, slavery to God his only hope. God hates slavery and wants children, but he may keep a slave in his house a long time, hoping to wake the slavish nature to the sonship that is its birthright. The slave cannot stay forever, and a father need not keep his son a slave because the foolish child prefers it.

Anyone who will not do what God wants is a slave whom God can compel, however patiently he waits. A man who obeys because he knows this, or fears punishment, is still a slave, though one within earshot of his master. Far above him are other slaves: all for whom God is not everything. They may commit no great sins and may be trying to do right, but while they 'serve' God from duty without knowing him as their father and the joy of their being, they are good slaves, but slaves, and bad ones if they stopped trying. They are far less servile than those who obey from fear, yet being slaves they can never do any duty perfectly; they must keep trying, wearily and painfully, knowing that if they stop they are lost. They prove they are slaves by being glad to be adopted by their own father! Where are the sons, then? I know no one yet wholly a son or daughter. There may be such people, but I have not known them, or was not fit to recognize them. I do know some who are enough sons and daughters to be at war with the slave in themselves. Nothing I have seen comes near the glory of sonship, but thousands, a remnant so far, side with the father of their spirits against themselves and all that divides them from the one they have never left, for in him they live and move and have their being. These are not slaves but true, if imperfect, children, fighting beside God against the evil separation and breaking down the dividing wall. Only the rings of their fetters remain. They know the dying slave in them, hate it and struggle to be rid of it. The real slave has no wish to be a child or end his slavery, thinks the child's claim presumption, clings to the traditional, approved service of forms and ceremonies, does not know the will of the maker of the seven stars and Orion, let alone care to do it, and never asks, 'Father, what do you want me to do?' Such people give themselves away by complaining. 'Do we not well to be angry?' they say, like Jonah, and being slaves I do not see how they can help it. Sons and daughters stop complaining of life's hardships---aches, poverty, hunger, rejection by what is called Society---for anyone who believes in a perfect father can hardly blame him for what he dislikes. Ah, friend, you and I may be slaves, but there *are* sons and daughters of the kind I mean.

Slaves of sin seldom grumble at that slavery. They grumble only at their slavery to God, and at the painful messengers he sends to free them from both. They must be sons or slaves; they cannot get rid of their owner. Whether they deny God, mock him by acknowledging and ignoring him, treat him as a king of whims and formalities, offer him dull services he cares nothing about without troubling to learn what pleases him, or strain to appease him with some yoke the Son never wore or asked them to wear, they are slaves, no less for being God's, and so thoroughly slaves that they do not want to become sons and daughters and find the good of life where alone it lies. Could a creator make a creature whose well-being did not depend on him? And if he could, would the creature be greater? The slave at heart says at once, like Milton's Satan, that the one furthest from his maker is freest. That makes existence itself a slavery, with a creator and as many slaves as he cares to make, whose refusal to obey is an unwitting protest against their own nature. To them creation is an injustice: God had no right to make beings less than himself, and since he could not make equals, he should not have made any! Yet they do not complain of being created, only of being expected to act justly. They refuse to obey yet take every advantage they can from the one who made them. They want a freedom unlike God's, and unknowingly seek a more complete slavery. There is no middle way between perfect harmony with the Father and slavery, obedient or rebellious; and the rebel rebels by the Father's strength in him. Divine in essence, he flings his existence in the face of his own nature.

Yet their rebellion is in a way his own spirit rising in them against their lies about him. They do not see that if they, his work, are such a joy to themselves, the life that works them could be a greater joy still, for it is nearer to them than they are to themselves and yet reaches infinitely beyond them. No bond is as close as that between creator and created. Where it is denied the split is widest; where it is lived the closeness is beyond words. The rebel's protest against slavery comes from the truth of God in him, which he cannot wholly shake off, and from a slavishness too low to love truth, a meanness that takes everything and admits nothing, as if his existence shamed him. God's liberty is in contest with the slavery of the creature who cuts his stem from its root so he can call it his own and love it, and who balances on the wobbly wall of his own being instead of the rock it is built on. He calls ruling himself---the greater governed by the lesser, since the conscious self is smaller than the whole self---a freedom beyond the whole range of God's universe. If he says, 'At least I have it my own way!' I answer: you do not know which way is yours. You have no idea where your urges and likings come from: diseased nerves, the roar of a wandering bodiless devil, an infant hatred in your heart, an ancestor's greed or lawlessness, or a far-reaching chord from a heavenly orchestra. The moment one reaches your awareness you call it your own way and glory in it! Two devils, one at each ear, could soon have that lordly *me* willing opposites from one moment to the next, then drive you mad when you found you could not choose a way and its opposite together. It all turns on the relation of creator and created, which few yet feel. To live without the eternal creating life is impossible; freedom from God can only mean blindness to the facts of existence and to the glory of a creature who joins his creator in making him, who wants the loving will that gave him life and choice to finish making him and draw him into the creating heart, so that he lives not by his own poor power but as one with the life behind his life. He knows the Father's life as the flame of his own, and rejoices that in all that is done in the universe the Father will give him as full a share as perfect generosity can. If you call that irreverent, I doubt you have seen the God revealed in Jesus. But all will be well: the little god of your poor contentment will starve your soul to misery, and terror of eternal death creeping up on you will drive you to seek a perfect father. Hidebound Christians! The Lord is not cramped; you are cramped, in your narrow, unwilling souls. Some of you need shaming; some need the fire.

A reader may cry out, like a child waking from a dream of endless seeking, 'I am bound like Lazarus in his grave-clothes! What can I do?' The Lord's answer, drawn from this saying---which, like a parable, speaks to the conscience and heart rather than the intellect---is this: 'You are a slave, and a slave has no hold on the house. Only sons and daughters have a lasting home with their father. You must be freed from your slavery, and that is why I am here. If I make you free, you will be free indeed, because the only way I can free you is to make you what you were meant to be: sons, like me. But you must become sons. You must want it, and I will help.' It is as if he said: 'You will have the freedom of my Father's universe, because once free of yourselves you will be free of his heart. Yourselves are your slavery, the darkness you loved more than light. You have honoured yourselves and sought honour from people, not from the Father, so even in his house you have been only passing slaves. We in his family are all one, with no party spirit and no self-seeking. Join us and you will be as free as we are.'

If the poor starved child cries, 'How, Lord?', the answer depends on what he means. If he means 'What is your plan for cutting my bonds?', the answer may be darker still, and the bonds tighter. If he means 'Lord, what do you want me to do?', the answer comes at once: 'Give yourself to me. Do what I tell you, understand what I say, be my good, obedient little brother. I will wake in you the heart my father put there, the same kind I have, and it will grow to love the Father completely, as mine does, until you are ready to be torn to pieces for him. Then you will know you are at the heart of the universe, of every secret, of the Father. Only then will you be free, and then free indeed.'

Christ died to save us, not from suffering but from ourselves; not from injustice, still less from justice, but from being unjust. He died so that we might live as he lives: by dying to ourselves, as he died to himself, to live to God. Without that we cannot live to God, and whoever does not live to God is dead. 'You will know the truth,' the Lord says, 'and the truth will make you free. I am the truth, and you will be as free as I am. To be free you must be sons like me, what you were created to be: answer as sons to the Father who calls you, fear nothing but evil, care for nothing but his will, and trust him absolutely, for he alone can be trusted.' Jesus showed us the Father not only by acting and loving as the Father does but by his joy in him and total obedience: the devotion of a perfect son. He is the Son of God because he and the Father are one in thought, mind and heart. The universe hangs on this truth---not the doctrine, but Jesus' truth to his Father itself---and the freedom of the children, the redemption of their whole world, hangs on their recognizing it, which means becoming true as he is. 'I and the Father are one' is the central truth of the universe, and the truth at its outer edge is 'that they also may be one in us.'

So the only free man is a child of the Father. He serves everyone but can be made the slave of none, being a son of the lord of the universe. By his own truth he is free, a king in himself. The Son rests his claim to royalty on this: he was born, and came into the world, to witness to the truth.

### KINGSHIP

*The passage:* Pilate asked, 'So you are a king?' Jesus answered, 'You say I am a king. For this I was born and came into the world: to bear witness to the truth. Everyone who is of the truth listens to my voice' (John xviii. 37).

Pilate asks whether Jesus is a king, after hearing that his kingdom is not of this world. Jesus answers that he is indeed a king, but not as the world understands it. He could have had its rank and rule: with his disciples and twelve legions of angels he could have thrown the Romans into the abyss after the nations they had already tumbled in. He could easily have been the just king the Jews dreamed of---never seen anywhere, yet always haunting the hopes of the poor and their helpers---ruling the nations under Rome from Jerusalem, and not only handing out what people call justice but forcing atonement. But he did not care for government; it would not serve his Father's aims or satisfy his own heart. What was perfect empire to the Son of God, compared with helping one person love his neighbour and be good like his Father, for that person's joy and the Father's glory? That was where true kingship began. He would rather wash his tired brothers' feet than be the one perfect monarch the world ever had. It was empire he rejected when he told Satan to get behind him like a dog to heel.

What then is the kingdom he cares to reign over, since he says he came to be a king? A kingdom of kings and no other: only where every person is a king does he care to reign, in his Father's name. As no European king would want to rule cannibals, savages or animals, he has no wish to rule anything this world calls a nation: a king must rule his own kind. He is king not by conquest, inheritance or election but by what he essentially is, and his subjects must be kings in the same way, by nature. In his kingdom no one tries to be above another; ambition is the dirt of the world's kingdoms. He says: 'I am a king, born to bear witness to the truth. Everyone of my kind, who is of the truth, hears my voice; he is a king like me, and one of my subjects.' Pilate, like most Christians today, asks for a definition of truth, in set terms for his intellect, instead of setting about being true. But at once, whether sure the inquiry is useless or planning to resume it once Jesus is freed, he goes out and tells the people he finds no fault in him. Whatever we make of that, he is far less to blame than 'Christians' who, instead of working to be pure 'even as he is pure,' to look after their brothers and sisters, and to serve God by being honest at work, 'serve' him by going to church or chapel, condemning their neighbours' opinions, or teaching others what they ignore themselves. Neither Pilate nor they ask the one true question: how am I to become a true man, a man worth being? The Lord is a king because his whole life---thought, imagination, will and every smallest act---is true: true to God, because he is altogether his; true to himself, because he forgets himself altogether; true to others, because he will bear whatever they do to him and never stop declaring himself the son, messenger and likeness of God. They will kill him, but it doesn't matter: the truth is as he says!

Jesus is a king because his business is to bear witness to the truth: all of it, every true relation in the universe, first that his Father is perfectly good, and that the crown and joy of life is to want and do the will of the source of all will and life. That deals hell's power its death-blow. For hell has one principle: 'I am my own, my own king and subject, the centre my thoughts go out from and return to. My glory and pleasure are my chief concern; my ambition, to draw all eyes to myself. My kingdom is all I can make acknowledge my greatness; my judgment is the faultless rule of things; my right is what I want. The more I am everything to myself, the greater I am: the less I admit I owe anyone, the more I forget I did not make myself, the more self-sufficient I feel, the greater I am. I will be free to do whatever I feel inclined to do, wherever the inclination comes from. To do my own will, as long as I feel anything to be my will, is to be free, is to live.' To this principle of hell and of the world, whether preached or only lived, the king gives the flat lie. It is as if he said: 'I ought to know: I have been from all eternity the son of the one you come from, whom you call your father but will not have as one. I know all he thinks and is, and my perfect freedom, my pure individuality, rests on my having no will but his, because his will is right. He is righteousness itself, love, fairness and self-giving, and he wants his children to be like him. I was born to bear witness to the truth by being it in my own person, the likeness and visible form of the God who is true. My very being is his witness: he is the truth, and I am the truth. Kill me, but while I live I say: as I am, he is. If I said I did not know him I would be a liar, and should a king who has come to say what is true turn his back for fear of people? I say my Father is like me, and you hate to hear it because you are not like him. I look low to you because you judge by show, so you call it blasphemy; but it would be blasphemy to say he is what you imagine, since you love show, power and people's praise. I do not, and God is like me. Kill me and I will rise again; you cannot keep me dead. Death is my servant; you are its slaves, because you will not be true and let the truth make you free. Bound and in your hands, I am as free as God, because God is my father. I know I will suffer to the death, and if you knew my Father you would be ready for it too. He is my strength. My Father is greater than I.'

Remember, friends, I said 'It is as if he said.' I am daring to offer a shadow of the Lord's witness, surely cast by his deeds and words; if I am wrong, he will forgive me. I fear only that, seeing and writing these things, I may fail to witness and prove a castaway: a talker, not a king; an arguer about the truth, not a disciple ready to die with him; a hater of lies about God who is himself a liar, speaking the truth but not doing it.

So the Lord witnessed to the one God, the Truth, by standing as he was before the eyes and lies of men. The true king stands up true, speaks the truth, and will die rather than lie. His robes may be rags or purple; it makes no difference, though rags are likelier. The Lord was most royally dressed when his robes were a mockery. Of those who witnessed before Christ, some were sawn apart and some conquered kingdoms; it made no difference which.

The truth is God; the witness to it is Jesus. Its kingdom is human hearts. Human happiness is the true God. God's thought is the truth of everything, and all well-being lies in true relation to him. Whoever answers this with his whole being is of the truth. Whoever knows these things and sees them to be true, but does not run his life, actions, judgments and love by them, is the worst kind of liar: hand, foot and face scorn what his tongue confesses.

The sons of Zebedee and their ambitious mother little knew what the throne of Christ's glory was, which they begged to share.
